To Dr. John Elliotson

My Dearest Doctor,

Twelve months back, just as I thought the curtain was finally falling on my little drama, a true pal rolled up his sleeves and dragged you right into my sickbed. Honestly, I'm convinced I'd still be a permanent resident in that cot today if it hadn't been for your tireless hovering and your magic touch. I simply adore remembering how magnanimous and sweet you were then (along with a whole cast of other folks who showed a baffling amount of loyalty and warmth), especially when those virtues were about as rare as black swans.

Since you refused any payment other than a heartfelt "thank you," allow me to put these sentiments down in ink, signing off on behalf of me and my entire family.

With deepest gratitude and sincere devotion,

W. M. Thackeray.

If this jumble of words, the fruit of two frantic years' labor, fails to please you artistically—as it surely must, given how difficult true art is—it at least offers the virtue of a certain raw honesty. A polished novel might lose this edge, smoothing out the rough truths until nothing remains but sugar-coated lies. By speaking directly to you, I am forced to drop my mask and blurt out exactly what my heart dictates. There are likely a thousand typos, hasty phrases, and printer's errors scattered through here that I'd love to scrub away if I could see the whole thing again without blinking. Think of this less as a published masterpiece and more as a late-night confession, a rambling chat where I inevitably expose my own foibles, vanities, and quirks.

Just as you wouldn't judge a friend solely by one bad mood or a single offhand remark, you shouldn't judge me by this draft alone. The question isn't "Is this perfect?" but rather, "Does the fellow behind the page mean to tell the truth?" Am I just a charlatan shouting sentimental nonsense to sound deep? Or am I chasing fame with cheap tricks? I cannot pretend otherwise. Fate has thrown me a lucky break; I've attracted far more readers than I ever dreamed possible. But I won't tell you to shut your eyes to flaws or let your eyelids droop over boring passages. All I ask is that you trust me when I say I'm trying to get it straight. Without truth, there is no story worth telling.

Now, for the thrill-seekers among you: yes, this book *did* start with a grand, blood-chilling scheme. Ladies and gentlemen, you were going to read about high-stakes drama! Imagine a ruffian with a charming streak of virtue hanging out in St. Giles's, constantly shadowed by a debutante from Belgravia! Picture the clash of worlds—the street slang battling against polite drawing-room chatter, the hair-raising chases, the fistfights, and potentially some murder! Even as recently as nine this morning, my dear old pal Colonel Altamont was scheduled for the gallows. I only spared his neck at the last minute when he popped up at the window, looking rather cheerful about it.

Alas, I scrapped that plan (much to the relief of the publishers, who are usually quite eager for a bit of sensationalism). Why? Because I tried to write it and realized I knew absolutely nothing about convicts. Having never spent time with a jailbird or a criminal, I couldn't possibly compete with the likes of Monsieur Eugène Sue. To paint a real scoundrel, you have to make him so terrifyingly vile that you can't bear to print his face. Unless you are brave enough to show the ugly truth, perhaps you should stay home and draw flowers instead.

And then there's the matter of "Gentlemen" like myself. This story attempts to sketch one such soul—not better, not worse, just average. Yet, we dare not show them as they truly are, with their glaring selfishness and the petty vices bred by their education. Since the great Henry Fielding wrote *Tom Jones*, no decent novelist in England seems allowed to portray a real man with full force. Society demands we wrap our heroes in silk, forcing them to wear that fake, polite smile known as the "conventional simper." Several gentlewomen have sent angry letters, and some subscribers have dropped their names, simply because I dared to describe a young man struggling with temptation.

My original aim was simple: to show that a man possesses the passions to feel deeply, yet also the courage and kindness to master them. I promise you, you won't hear the gritty, messy details of what really drives people in clubs, colleges, or mess rooms. You certainly won't learn the actual conversations of your sons. I have tried to inject a little more bare-knuckle frankness than is usual, hoping for no harm and with no evil intent. If the truth sometimes tastes bitter, well, it is still the best medicine. Whether you sit in a library reading serious philosophy or sitting by the fire listening to a storyteller wind down, the goal remains the same: to speak plainly and bid you farewell.

It was a brilliant morning in the height of the London Season, and Major Arthur Pendennis, true to his habits, marched from his lodgings to breakfast at a certain exclusive club in Pall Mall. To the uninitiated, this establishment was merely a gathering of gentlemen; to the Major, it was his kingdom. As the premier connoisseur of vintage wines in England and a man whose spirit burned with an active, dominating curiosity, he had been elevated to the Committee, effectively serving as the institution's manager. The stewards and waiters bowed to him with the reverent solemnity usually reserved for Dukes or Field Marshals.

By twenty minutes past ten, the Major made his entrance. He was a study in impeccable military fashion *en retraite*. His boots were the best blacked leather in London, topped with a checked morning cravat that remained crisp until dinner time. His buff waistcoat boasted buttons crowned with the royal insignia, and his linen shirts were so flawlessly white that Beau Brummell himself—had fortune not driven him abroad—might have envied the laundrywoman who tended them. From his perfectly coiffed rich brown hair (factitious, though) to his Wellington-pattern nose and long, white wrists adorned with rings bearing the Pendennis arms, he was a specimen of aristocratic vanity. Though seen from behind, he appeared no older than thirty; a closer inspection revealed the faint lines around his somewhat faded eyes, the tell-tale signs of a man who worried too much about appearances.

His domain was a specific corner table by the fire, near the window, a throne no interloper dared disturb. In previous years, a couple of mad wags had attempted to usurp his seat, but the Major's quiet, intimidating dignity as he settled in and surveyed the room ensured their retreat. Here, amidst the crackling hearth, lay his correspondence. Young bucks often stared in awe at the sheer volume of his mail, the array of seals and frankings indicating the gravity of his social standing. Whether it concerned etiquette, marital alliances, or the exact age of a Duchess, everyone turned to Pendennis. Marchionesses would drive up specifically to deliver notes or summon him for tea. He was universally affable, a walking lord who tipped his hat to everyone, thereby elevating every passerby to nobility.

Now seated, while the waiters bustled with toast and newspapers, the Major donned his gold-rimmed spectacles and began his daily audit. He carried the glasses with a jaunty air, examining a stream of invitations. There were solemn dinner cards promising heavy conversation and multiple courses, delicate confidences whispering of female entreaties, and a formal missive from the Marquis of Steyne inviting him to Richmond for a soirée where French would be spoken—a tongue the Major mastered perfectly. Another request came from the Bishop of Ealing and Mrs. Trail, seeking his presence at Ealing House. He read them all with graceful satisfaction, particularly enjoying the spectacle as Glowry, the Scotch surgeon sitting opposite, glared with jealous hatred. Glowry, who received no such honors, watched the flood of invitations with a mixture of envy and disdain.

Having finished his perusal, the Major consulted his pocket-book, cross-referencing his free evenings against the calendar of hospitality. He mentally crossed off Cutler, the East India Director, opting instead for Lord Steyne's French party at the Star and Garter. He accepted the Bishop, reasoning that despite the slow service, dining with prelates was essential. With his schedule arranged to his liking, he settled in for breakfast, scanning the Gazette and fashionable news to ensure his name featured prominently among guests. Between bites, he engaged in cheerful chatter with his acquaintances, the air thick with the hum of high society.

Yet, buried within this mountain of fashionable correspondence, there sat a single, solitary letter. It stood apart from the glittering world of London, marked by a country postmark and a humble seal. Written in a delicate, feminine hand, it bore the word "Immediate" scrawled across the top with a dash of anxious urgency. For reasons of his own, the Major had neglected this humble rural petitioner, allowing her to wait in the social ante-chamber beneath the slop-basin while the grandees of her acquaintance were served.

Finally, it was her turn. The Major broke the seal, which read "Fairoaks," and opened the envelope before diving into the letter itself.

"My dear Major Pendennis," the letter began, "I beg and implore you to come to me *immediately*."

Pendennis paused. *Immediately?* Likely a summons to Steyne's dinner. He continued reading, however, his smile slowly fading into confusion.

"I am in the very greatest grief and perplexity," the letter continued. "My dearest boy... is grieving me *dreadfully*. He has formed—an infatuation!" The Major grinned internally at the dramatic phrasing. "For an actress who has been performing here. She is at least twelve years older than Arthur—who will not be eighteen till next February—and the wretched boy insists upon marrying her."

"Hay! What's making Pendennis swear now?" Glowry muttered to himself, interpreting the Major's sudden stillness as rage.

"Do, my dear friend," the letter pleaded, "come instantly... entreat, command, the wretched child to give up this most deplorable resolution." Signed with deep sorrow, it ended with "your unhappy affectionate sister, Helen Pendennis."

"Fairoaks, Tuesday," the Major murmured, finishing the page. "A d--d pretty business at Fairoaks, Tuesday. Now let us see what the boy has to say."

He turned to the second sheet, written in a frantic, floundering boy's hand. It was sealed with the massive signet of the Pendennises, encircled by supplementary wax sputtered in agitation.

"FAIROAKS, Monday, Midnight.
In informing you of my engagement with Miss Costigan..."

The rest followed in a torrent of youthful desperation. Arthur described Miss Costigan, daughter of J. Chesterfield Costigan, Esq., also known professionally as Miss Fotheringay of the Theatres Royal. He acknowledged the shock this caused his family, noting his mother's profound grief. Yet, he argued passionately. Her family, he claimed via Mr. Costigan, was as ancient and noble as their own, tracing roots back to the time of King Richard II. He dismissed his mother's objections as impossible to overcome with logic, insisting that Miss Costigan possessed "irreproachable character and lineage" and devoted her "splendid talents" to maintaining her family.

"A love like mine, Sir, I feel, is contracted once and for ever," the boy wrote, his voice trembling with conviction. "As I never had dreamed of love until I saw her—I feel now that I shall die without ever knowing another passion." He addressed the age gap as a mere illusion, a "barrier" suggested by Miss Costigan's own delicacy, which he now deemed unworthy of consideration. "If I hesitated to abide by my passion... I should despise myself, and be unworthy of my name as a gentleman."

He pressed for a speedy marriage, arguing that delay implied doubt, a sentiment unworthy of a man who loved fully. "Why, then, wait?" he cried. "I entreat you, my dear Uncle, to come down and reconcile my dear mother to our union." He signed off as "Your affectionate Nephew, ARTHUR PENDENNIS, JR."

When the Major finished the letter, his countenance transformed. Rage and horror contorted his features. Glowry, sensing a storm, reached instinctively for his lancet in his card-case, fearing his friend might swoon. The news was catastrophic enough to shake the foundations of Pendennis's orderly world. His headstrong nephew, barely eighteen, intent on marrying an actress ten years his senior. And the mother, obviously, had spoiled the rascal with nothing but sentimental nonsense and romantic rubbish.

"My nephew marry a tragedy queen!" the Major groaned inwardly. "Gracious mercy, people will laugh at me so that I shall not dare show my head!"

The cost of this scandal was immediate and severe. He would have to forfeit Lord Steyne's dinner at Richmond, sacrificing sleep and comfort in favor of an "abominable tight mail-coach." Worse, he would have to cancel every other engagement for weeks to come. Who knew how long this domestic crisis might drag on?

With a heavy heart, the Major quitted his breakfast table for the adjoining writing-room. There, with a rueful pen, he drafted refusals to the Marquis, the Earl, the Bishop, and every other host who had sought his company. He then summoned his servant to book tickets for the evening mail-coach, ordering that the exorbitant fare for the seats be charged directly to the account of the widow and the young scapegrace whom he was sworn to protect.

In the reign of King George III, known to the romantics as "George the Magnificent," resided in a diminutive West Country hamlet named Clavering a gentleman of the name Pendennis. To those who still retained the sharp memories of pre-industrial England, his lineage was visible on a signboard above a humble shop in Bath: a gilt pestle and mortar hovering over the entrance, where John Pendennis, a surgeon and apothecary, practiced his trade. Here he tended to the sick, assisted ladies in delicate circumstances, and—with a generous condescension—sold brown-paper plasters to a farmer's wife or peddled toothbrushes and lavender water to passersby. These facts were attested to by the local gentry of Clavering, whose recollections were far more tenacious than those of the bustling metropolis.

Yet, beneath the guise of a penny-selling apothecary, John Pendennis was a man of considerable education and ancient blood. His Cornish pedigree traced the Pendennises back to the era of the Druids, intermarrying with the Normans in later centuries and forging ties with the great houses of Wales and Brittany. Though he possessed a university degree from Cambridge, destiny had intervened. In his second year, his father died insolvent, forcing young Pen to abandon his scholarly dreams for the grim reality of the pestle and apron. He harbored a profound, lifelong hatred for the trade, entering it only through sheer necessity and the machinations of his mother's brother, a London apothecary of dubious reputation who had lowered the family standards by marrying John's father.

Upon completing his apprenticeship, John severed ties with his coarse-minded relative and established himself independently in Bath. Poverty was his constant companion for some time; it strained his resources to maintain the shop's gilt ornaments and provide comfort for his bedridden mother. Fortune, however, smiled upon him serendipitously. One day, Lady Ribstone, accompanied by an intoxicated Irish chairman who smashed through her carriage and splintered a pink bottle in the surgeon's window, collapsed outside his door. Accommodated in his shop, she was revived with cinnamon and sal-volatile. Captivated by Mr. Pendennis's uncommonly gentle and gentlemanlike manners, Lady Ribstone appointed him personal physician to her extensive household.

His fortunes shifted dramatically thereafter. When Master Ribstone returned from Eton suffering from a fever, Pendennis treated him with such skill and tenderness that he secured the enduring favor of the Codlingbury family. From that point forward, prosperity followed. The fashionable circles of Bath patronized him, particularly the ladies, who adored and admired him. First, his modest shop transformed into a smart establishment; soon, he deemed the sale of toothbrushes unworthy of his ancient lineage. Eventually, he shuttered the retail side entirely, retaining only a private surgery staffed by a genteel assistant. He acquired a gig, and before his mother's passing, the joyous sight of her rolling to her window revealed her beloved John stepping into a private one-horse carriage, proudly displaying the Pendennis arms upon its panels. "What would Arthur say now?" she lamented, referring to her younger son who, despite his poverty, had never visited her. "Captain Pendennis is with his regiment in India," John replied gently, requesting that he not be called "Johnny" before the young man, Mr. Parkins.

Tragically, the day came when she ceased to address him with any term of endearment. Her voice, once kind though querulous, fell silent. John altered his nightbell to the chamber where she had grumbled for decades and claimed the great bed in solitude. Over forty, he had yet to experience war or the throne of George IV, but what mattered to him was his pedigree. He displayed it prominently in his drawing-room, flanked by paintings of Codlingbury House and St. Boniface's College, retrieving it from a trunk with the same ceremonial gravity as a swordsman drawing a blade.

Around the time of his mother's demise, another patient, the virtuous Lady Pontypool, also passed away at Bath. Attended for five years by Miss Helen Thistlewood, a distant relative of the Pontypools and daughter of a naval lieutenant slain at Copenhagen, Lady Pontypool had subjected the young lady to a tyranny known only to women. Yet, Helen bore the old lady's insults with angelical sweetness. As they traveled in a mourning coach to Bath Abbey, Pendennis, observing her pale, sweet face, resolved to ask a question that made his pulse race. He was twenty years her senior and rarely ardent, perhaps having strangled a youthful romance in infancy, but at thirty-four, he was a collected, quiet gentleman. Following the funeral, he called upon her, holding her hand as he asked where she would reside now that the Pontypool estates were being dismantled, boxed, and carted away.

Tears filling her eyes, Helen confessed she knew not where to go, possessing only a thousand pounds left by her patroness. Looking deeply into her face, Pendennis proposed that she live with him. Though older, he boasted of his good birth, principles, and temper. Alone in the world, he needed a constant companion whom he intended to make happy. Reciting a speech composed that morning and perfected in his carriage, he offered his hand. Perhaps Helen, having dreamed of better things, hesitated, but recognizing his worthiness, prudence, and honor, she blushed, curtsied low, and asked to consider his proposal.

They married during the dull Bath season, which coincided with the height of the season in London. Securing lodgings in Holles Street, Cavendish Square, Pendennis transported his wife in a chaise and pair, introducing her to the theaters, parks, and royal chapel. He showered her with the pleasures of the town and distributed calling cards to his former patrons, including Lord Pontypool and the Earls of Bareacres. While Bareacres ignored the card, Lord Pontypool called, admiring Mrs. Pendennis and arranging a future concert. The Ribstones, too, invited them to an entertainment of which Mr. Pendennis would boast until the day of his death.

Mr. Pendennis's secret ambition had always been to become a genuine gentleman. Through frugality, prudence, and a stroke of luck involving copper-mining shares, he amassed enough capital to purchase a house and small estate near Clavering. He sold his Bath business to Mr. Parkins, retiring with a handsome annuity. By this time, his son, Arthur, was eight years old, a fact that allowed the elder Pendennis to largely forget the days of compounding pills and dirtying his hands. He abandoned the black breeches and stockings, adopting the attire of an English squire: a bottle-green coat, brass buttons, and drab gaiters. He stood at his lodge gate, bowing to coaches, and founded the Clavering Book Club and the Samaritan Soup and Blanket Society. He redirected the mail route through his village and became an active vestryman and market-goer, inspecting livestock with a knowing air.

Though his income of eight hundred pounds a year prevented him from mingling with the county's aristocracy, he cultivated a comfortable circle of the "second-best" sort. Their society smelled faintly of gentility, featuring moonlight dinners and festivals. Nearby, the ruins of Clavering Park, once the seat of the powerful Sir Francis Clavering, lay desolate, its palatial halls crumbling and moss-covered. From the lawn of Fairoaks, Pendennis's estate, one could behold a picturesque scene: the golden tinge of the setting sun upon the lawns, the noisy river Brawl, and the purple silhouette of Clavering Abbey's towers. Here, young Arthur and his mother would walk, casting long blue shadows, reciting poetry and exchanging embraces, for to love and pray were Mrs. Pendennis's primary duties.

At home, John Pendennis commanded absolute respect, his orders obeyed with the rigor of the Medes and Persians. Meals were served punctually, prayers recited, and stables inspected at regular intervals. After dinner, he napped with *The Globe* newspaper and a yellow bandanna handkerchief, gifts from his brother, Major Pendennis, who had returned from India. The Major, a retired soldier on half-pay, was a frequent visitor, bringing news of the London fashion and tales of his refusal to marry the wealthy Miss Hunkle, preferring the company of the titled over the accumulation of wealth. The family adored the Major, viewing him as the link to the great world, while Mrs. Pendennis, a woman of tranquil beauty and innate dignity, was revered by all.

Mrs. Pendennis's influence extended to her son, Arthur. In childhood, he viewed her as a supernatural being of wisdom and love, a perfection that enraged lesser country ladies. However, nature permits no human to be faultless; the very virtues of Mrs. Pendennis generated a vice: pride. She regarded her husband with the reverence due a pope and her son with an idolatrous ardor. This "idol-worship," as the narrator termed it, proved to be the genesis of Arthur's future misfortunes.

At Greyfriars School, Arthur was neither a dunce nor a scholar, performing exactly what was required. He excelled in verse composition, writing just enough to meet regulations, unlike his more prolific peers. He devoured novels and plays outside school hours, spent his money lavishly on tarts, and lived on credit when necessary. Though physically aversive to fighting and Greek grammar, he was kind-hearted, accepting a

Arthur Pendennis was about sixteen when his reign officially commenced. To the uninitiated eye, he possessed a "dumpy" figure, though his mother insisted on styling it "neat." His hair, a healthy brown that shimmered like gold in the sun, framed a round, rosy, and freckled countenance radiating good humor. Nature eventually granted him the whiskers he had so ardently coveted, painting them in a distinctly reddish hue. While not conventionally beautiful, his frank expression and merry laughter, delivered from honest blue eyes, secured him the status of the county's pride in Mrs. Pendennis's estimation. Between the ages of sixteen and eighteen, he grew from five feet six to five feet eight inches, pausing abruptly at this altitude. His mother, however, remained baffled by the mathematics of his growth, noting with concern that he had outstripped his father by a full three inches. One might reasonably ask if any man possesses the audacity to tower so significantly above his progenitor.

School proved a dismal failure for young Arthur. The discipline of the establishment clashed violently with his spirit, and he harbored a profound preference for domestic comfort. Although the question of his return was vigorously debated, and his uncle advocated for a return to the classroom, the verdict was sealed by medical authority. The Doctor argued that a thorough knowledge of Greek plays was essential for future success, but Pen adroitly exploited his mother's sensitivities. He painted a vivid picture of Greyfriars School as a den of dangerous wildlings, causing the timid soul to immediately capitulate to his plea to remain at home.

Consequently, Pen's uncle offered to leverage his influence with His Royal Highness, the Commander-in-Chief, who was notably kind to the boy. The proposal was to secure a commission in the Foot Guards. Pen's heart leapt with joy; he had recently attended the band playing at St. James's Park on a Sunday outing. There, amidst the crimson and gold of the regiments, he had witnessed Tom Ricketts, a former tormentor from the fourth form who usually wore jackets so comically tight they served as targets for older boys. Now, Tom stood aglow in scarlet, topped with an immense bearskin cap, staggering beneath the weight of the colors. Having once cut Tom over the back with a hockey-stick last term, Pen now saw him rallying around the national flag, surrounded by bayonets and warriors with chin-tufts and Waterloo medals. Tom recognized him and bestowed a patronizing nod. Standing in the center of the square, talking familiarly to giants, Pen realized what he would give to wear such epaulettes and join such a glorious service.

However, Helen Pendennis, when the prospect of the army was broached, adopted a visage of pure terror. She declared that while she respected differing opinions, a Christian had no right to make the army a profession. She reminded Pen that Mr. Pendennis would never have allowed his son to become a soldier and expressed that she would be deeply unhappy if he pursued such a path. Pen, possessing a generosity that bordered on self-abnegation and a determination never to deliberately distress his mother, instantly surrendered his vision. He presented her with a gift of sorts: the forfeiture of his red coat, his epaulettes, and his fervent ardor for military glory.

Her reaction was to deem him the noblest creature in the world. Meanwhile, Major Pendennis, upon acknowledging and refusing the commission via letter, replied with curt anger, dismissing his nephew as merely "spooney." Yet, peace returned when the holidays arrived. During Christmas, when the Major descended to Fairoaks, Pen showcased his talents in hunting. He rode a spirited mare with uncommon pluck and grace, clearing fences with cool judgment rather than reckless bravado. He wrote enthusiastic letters to his schoolmates detailing his top-boots and exploits across the country, secretly beginning to envision a scarlet coat. His mother, though she believed it would become him remarkably well, spent hours in anguish during his absences, expecting to receive news of him arriving home on a shutter.

Despite these diversions, Pen did not neglect his intellectual pursuits entirely. He possessed a natural taste for literature that extended far beyond his curriculum, refusing to drink from the waters of knowledge only when forced. At home, he devoured everything from *Inchbald's Theatre* to *White's Farriery*. At Clavering, he unearthed an old cargo of French novels, reading them with intense devotion. He frequently perched atop the highest step of Doctor Portman's library, a folio balanced on his knees, whether tackling Hakluyt's Travels, Hobbes's *Leviathan*, Augustine's works, or Chaucer's poetry. His friendship with the Vicar was strong, and it was from the Reverend that Pen inherited his lifelong appreciation for port wine. As for Mrs. Portman, who was not jealous despite her husband's admitted affection for Mrs. Pendennis, her sole sorrow was that her daughter, Minny, was too old for Pen. At that juncture, Miss Myra Portman was only two years younger than Pen's mother and weighed as much as both mothers combined.

One might consider these details insipid, but reflect upon your own youth. How was it? I cherish the image of a well-nurtured boy: brave, gentle, warm-hearted, loving, and gazing upon the world with kind, honest eyes. Life wears brighter colors then, and the enjoyment is profound, though often forgotten until the time has passed.

To prevent the idleness predicted by the Doctor of the Cistercians, Mr. Smirke, the Vicar's curate, was hired at a liberal salary to accompany Pen daily. Smirke was a man of impeccable manners, sporting a curl on his fair forehead and tying his cravat with a melancholy grace. He was a competent scholar and mathematician, teaching Pen as much as the boy wished to learn—which was surprisingly little. Pen quickly measured his tutor, finding him absurdly inept at horseback riding. When Smirke mounted Pen's mare, Rebecca (named for Pen's favorite heroine), he turned his toes out with ridiculous flair, leaving a gaping space between his knees and the saddle. Any lad with a sense of humor could not possibly respect such an equestrian. Pen nearly terrified Smirke to death by forcing him onto Rebecca and leading him over a common where the county fox-hounds, led by the staunch sportsman Mr. Hardhead, happened to be hunting. Smirke, astride Rebecca, astounded the hounds and disgusted the huntsman, laming one of the dogs by persisting in riding among the pack and earning a lecture from the huntsman so energetic it rivaled any oration he had heard since leaving the bargemen on the banks of the Isis.

Smirke confided his Latin and English poems to his pupil, presenting Mrs. Pendennis with a volume of his English verse, printed at Clapham. Together, they rattled through ancient poets at a brisk, galloping pace, a stark contrast to the slow, methodical grubbing of the Cistercian scholars who dissected every word. Pen, unwilling to halt, compelled his tutor to construe whenever he stumbled, thereby galloping through the *Iliad*, the *Odyssey*, the tragedians, and the wicked Aristophanes, whom Pen declared the greatest poet of all. Though he covered considerable ground, he forgot it almost entirely in later life, retaining only a vague recollection akin to a Member of Parliament keeping two or three quotations or a reviewer hinting at a smattering of Greek for decency's sake. Despite this, the superstition of a gentleman's education was faithfully transmitted from generation to generation.

Beyond the ancients, Pen approached English literature with great gusto. Smirke sighed and shook his head regarding Byron and Moore, but Pen was a sworn devotee. He memorized them, taking little Laura into the window to recite lines like "Zuleika, I am not thy brother" in tones so tragic that the solemn little maid widened her eyes in astonishment. She sat sewing at her mother's knee, listening to Pen read aloud through the night, failing to comprehend a single word. He read Shakespeare to his mother (who claimed to like it but clearly did not), Byron, Pope, and his favorite *Lalla Rookh*, which pleased her indifferently. However, Bishop Heber and Mrs. Hemans, above all, caused her to melt into her pocket-handkerchief as Pen recited them in his kind, boyish voice. The *Christian Year*, which appeared around this time, became a shared secret between mother and son, whispered to with awe. The faint, solemn church-music echoed in his memory, filling him with hope and devoid of doubt as bells rang on Sunday mornings.

During this era, Pen debuted in the Poets' Corner of the *County Chronicle* with verses of which he was perfectly satisfied. These included "To a Tear," "On the Anniversary of the Battle of Waterloo," "To Madame Caradori," and "On Saint Bartholomew's Day"—a vehement denunciation of Popery warning Protestants against Catholic emancipation. These masterpieces, signed 'N.E.P.', are presumably preserved by Mrs. Pendennis today alongside his first socks, haircut, and bottles. He rode Rebecca over Dumpling Downs or into the county town of Chatteris, spouting his own poetry with a Byronic afflatus.

His genius at this time was decidedly gloomy. He brought his mother a tragedy in which he slaughtered sixteen characters before the second act, a performance so hilarious it caused him to throw the manuscript

Mrs. Haller

Having retreated to the George, Mr. Foker and his companion sat down to a feast fit for a king, or at least a very well-fed vicar. Mr. Rincer delivered the first course with the solemn gravity of a courtier awaiting the monarch, bowing low enough to suggest he might have been serving the Lord-Lieutenant himself. Mr. Foker attacked the turtle and venison with a ferocity that belied his recent history of feasting off ginger-beer and contrabund sausages, displaying a level of gastronomic expertise that commanded Pen's immediate, if slightly bewildered, respect. Pen found himself nodding sagely as Foker critiqued the vintages, declaring the champagne "condemned gooseberry" and giving the port a knowing wink, proving it to be the genuine article. The waiter, a man known personally by his Christian name along with his entire family tree, was treated to a lecture on the virtues of honest trade as the London coaches rattled up to the door. Foker flung open the window, bellowing greetings to the guards and ostlers, mimicking the shrill toots of their horns with such theatrical flair that the horses' cloths seemed to dance in sympathy as the carriages departed.

"A bottle of sherry, a bottle of sham, a bottle of port, and a shass caffy!" Foker announced, wiping crumbs from his chin after devouring a mountain of nuts and fruit. "It ain't so bad, hay, Pen?" He immediately pronounced it time to "toddle." Pen sprang up, his eyes bright and his face flushed with the blood of good wine, and they marched toward the theatre, paying their money to a wheezy old lady who seemed perpetually asleep behind the ticket box. "Mrs. Dropsicum, Bingley's mother-in-law, great in *Lady Macbeth*," Foker noted, clearly enjoying the gossip.

Inside, the theatre was a study in contrasts. Despite the playbill's promises of "galvanic thrills," the benches were sparsely populated, save for a scattering of rustics in the pit, a few noisy souls in the galleries, and a dozen free-admission guests in the boxes. Among them sat Lieutenants Rodgers and Podgers, and young Cornet Tidmus, who treated the actors as if they were personal acquaintances, applauding them by name whenever they spoke. Manager Bingley, the star of the house except for the occasional visit from London's greats, took the role of 'The Stranger.' He was a vision of theatrical decay: tight pantaloons, Hessian boots, a drooping cloak adorned with a hearse feather that barely concealed a magnificent, buckled brown wig. He wore the stage jewelry with the confidence of a thief, sporting a massive fake diamond on his little finger that he twiddled openly in front of the pit, a favor he extended to his young comedians who adored the ring's mysterious provenance, rumored to belong to the legendary George Frederick Cooke.

Reading from a stage-book that looked as though it had been born in a rouge pot, Bingley performed his soliloquies with dramatic flair, pointing at the text and wagging his head ominously while gazing upward for divine inspiration. When 'The Stranger' spotted the young gentlemen in the box, he turned his attention directly to them, calculating the effect of every ornament—from the gilt volume to the jeweled finger—with surgical precision. His servant, Francis, watched with a mixture of pride and pity, remarking that nature held no charms for his master, who had not smiled in three years. Yet, as the scene progressed, the manager's eyes shifted from the script to the audience, specifically locking onto Pendennis. He played the character entirely for Pen, marking pages carefully to ensure the act continued uninterrupted, determined to subjugate the young man's emotions. When the scene ended, Foker boomed, "Bravo, Bingley!" and the house, including the Dragoons in the opposite box, joined in a thunderous ovation.

But the real storm was about to break. Servants bustled about the stage, setting up chairs and tables, and Foker whispered, "That's Hicks and Miss Thackthwaite... Pretty girl, ain't she, Pen? But stop—hurray—bravo! Here's the Fotheringay." The pit erupted, umbrellas waving like flags of truce, as a volley of applause tore through the gallery. From the wings emerged Mrs. Haller, her face initially downcast, but instantly rallied by the roar of the crowd. She sank into a magnificent curtsey, folding her hands across her breast, and the applause became deafening. Pen, inflamed by wine and the sheer magnitude of the moment, clapped with a fervor that drowned out everyone else, singing "bravo" louder than the officers.

Mrs. Haller, the tall, dark-haired beauty of twenty-six (or nineteen, depending on whether she was telling the truth), swept the house with her presence. Her forehead was vast, crowned with waves of black hair that cascaded in braids worthy of the Louvre Venus. Her eyes, deep-fringed and luminous, held a tenderness and mystery that seemed to trap the soul within their gaze. Dressed in flowing black robes that revealed only her surprisingly large sandals, she moved with a grace that made the air itself seem to fold around her. It was her hands, however, that captivated the room; when she folded them over her bosom in resignation or raised them in command, they became the focal point of existence. She beckoned, repelled, and embraced the entire house, charming it with a virtue that was both her own and inherited from her brave father.

"I say, Pen, isn't she a stunner?" Foker asked, leaning forward.

"Hush!" Pen whispered, mesmerized.

Mrs. Haller began her lines in a deep, sweet voice. While the words of 'The Stranger' were often nonsense—a sham performance akin to the fake book and the artificial wig—the underlying current of love, children, and forgiveness cut through the balderdash with devastating reality. With smothered sorrow and gushing pathos, she delivered the lines about her dead husband and imaginary children, causing the little first fiddler in the orchestra, Old Mr. Bows, to bury his face in a blue handkerchief. Foker wept piteously, pulling out a yellow bandanna, but Pen was lost. He followed her movements with a feverish intensity, watching her at the side-scenes, seeing her father remove her shawl, and witnessing the final reconciliation where she collapsed onto the manager's shoulders. When the curtain fell, Pen felt as though a pall had descended upon him. He did not hear the manager's announcement of the next play, nor did he register the general call for Miss Fotheringay until the curtain rose again, revealing her arm-in-arm with Bingley, flowers thrown upon her by the officers. Her hair had fallen down, her eyes met Pen's, and then she vanished.

"She is a crusher, ain't she now!" Foker exclaimed.

Pen could only answer vaguely. A wild, maddening joy surged through him, a fever of longing that left him speechless. As the curtain rose for the ballet, Pen stopped caring about the dance itself, his mind fixated on the actress who had played the maid in the previous scene. Time slipped away as he checked his watch and realized it was time to leave. Foker urged him to stay for *The Bravo of the Battle-Axe*, boasting of Bingley's acrobatics, but Pen's eyes scanned the bill in vain for a name that wasn't there. He had to go. He squeezed Foker's hand, choked back a scream of emotion, and fled the theatre.

Outside, he walked frantically through the town, unsure of how long, before mounting his horse at the George and racing homeward. As he burst into the yard at Fairoaks, the clock tower chimed one. He dashed into his room and pulled the covers over his head, falling into a sound sleep instantly, the fatigue of the day and the shock of the experience knocking him unconscious. But soon, the voice of Love, personified by the figure of Mrs. Haller, jogged his shoulder. "Come, my man, no more of this laziness," she cooed. "I am Love! I bring fever and passion, wild longing, maddening desire."

Pen awoke in the early morning, the birds of the rookery cawing outside. Immediately, the image of the actress flooded his mind. Just as his father had given him a gold watch years prior, which he had polished and treasured, Pen now possessed a new treasure. He felt a sudden, overwhelming maturity, realizing with certainty that he was as deeply in love as any hero in a romance. He instructed John to bring his shaving water with the utmost confidence and dressed in his finest attire, descending to breakfast with an air of grandeur. He patronized his mother and little Laura, who had been practicing music lessons for hours, wondering at his transformation. When Laura asked what the play was about, Pen laughed it off, declining to speak. When she questioned his fine waistcoat, he blushed and concocted a tale of visiting a learned tutor at Baymouth to discuss college courses, a lie easily spun to cover his heart.

Helen Pendennis, however, looked at her son with troubled eyes, sensing the same vague terror that had haunted her since the news of his absence. Arthur Pendennis, meanwhile, defied his mother's gaze, his eyes burning with resolve. During breakfast, Pen behaved with haughty

Mrs. Haller at Home

Rebecca the mare did not slacken her pace; instead, she seemed to gather new momentum, thundering down the lane toward Baymouth. Pen, riding with the reckless abandon of a boy who believes he is invincible, put her up at the inn stables and bolted directly to Mr. Foker's lodging. These apartments sat precariously over a chemist's shop, where the stock of cigars and soda-water vanished with alarming speed, courtesy of his youthful tenants.

Upon arriving, Pen discovered only Mr. Spavin, Foker's friend and co-owner of the tandem carriage that had recently ploughed through Chatteris. Spavin was smoking a cigar with the air of a man who had conquered the world, while simultaneously teaching a diminutive terrier tricks with a scrap of biscuit.

The contrast was jarring. Pen's face was flushed a vibrant, healthy red from the gallop, glowing with vitality. Beside him, Spavin's features were waxy, pale, and distinctly debauched. Spavin glanced up, his eyes narrowing. "Who's that man?" he muttered internally, comparing Pen's robust energy to his own lethargy. "He looks as fresh as a bean. His hand doesn't shake in the morning. I'd bet five to one."

"Mr. Pendennis!" Spavin announced, peering at the card Pen produced with some pride. "A fair tap of ale, I assure you."

Having failed to find Foker, who was nowhere to be seen, Pen felt a sudden, hollow disappointment. He walked down to the beach, biting his nails as he stared at the vast, bright expanse of the sea. The blue waters rolled into the bay, foaming and roaring hoarsely. Pen looked at them with blank, unfocused eyes, hardly registering the noise. Inside, however, a tidal wave of frustration was crashing against his mind, and he possessed no power to hold it back. He flung stones into the surf, but the ocean kept coming. Rage at not seeing Foker boiled within him. He *must* see Foker.

"Suppose I go on," Pen thought, "along the Chatteris road, just to catch him."

Rebecca was saddled within the hour. Galloping along the grass verge of the Chatteris road, Pen reached the point where the Clavering branch usually appeared. Naturally, the horse turned, but Pen cut her sharply over the shoulder, forcing her onto the turnpike. He rode on, scanning the horizon for the black tandem and its distinctive red wheels, finding nothing but empty road.

At the turnpike, logic suggested continuing. So, Pen rode to the George Inn. The hostler confirmed that Mr. Foker was indeed present, having made a "tremendous row" the previous night—drinking, singing, and attempting to fight a postboy, though, according to the hostler, likely losing that bout. With a satirical grin directed at Foker's domestic, Stoopid, the hostler summoned Pen.

Stoopid emerged carrying Foker's immaculately brushed clothes, looking grave despite the chaos. "Show Mr. Pendennis up to 'un."

Inside the apartment, amidst an immense feather bed and towering bolsters, Mr. Harry Foker lay reposing. Only a sallow face and a red silk nightcap peeked out from the mountain of pillows.

"Hullo!" cried Pen.

"Who goes there? Brother, quickly tell!" sang out the voice from the depths. "What! Pendennis again? Is your Mamma acquainted with your absence? Did you sup with us last night? No stop—who supped with us last night, Stoopid?"

"There was the three officers, sir, and Mr. Bingley, sir, and Mr. Costigan, sir," Stoopid replied with perfect, unblinking gravity.

"Ay, the cup and merry jest went round," Foker chorused, yawning and throwing his lean arms over his head. "We chanted. I remember I wanted to fight a postboy. Did I thrash him, Stoopid?"

"No, sir. Fight didn't come off, sir."

"Do it some other day." Foker yawned again. "But there was chanting. Bingley chanted, I chanted, the General chanted—Costigan I mean. Did you ever hear him sing 'The Little Pig under the Bed,' Pen?"

"The man we met yesterday," Pen stammered, trembling slightly, "the father of—"

"Of the Fotheringay—the very man. Ain't she a Venus, Pen?"

"Please sir, Mr. Costigan's in the sitting-room, sir," Stoopid interrupted, pointing the way. "Says you asked him to breakfast. Called five times, sir, but wouldn't wake you on no account. Has been here since eleven."

"How much is it now?"

"One, sir."

"What would the best of mothers say," cried the little sluggard, "if she saw me in bed at this hour? She sent me down here with a grinder. She wants me to cultivate my neglected genus." Foker burst into a boyish laugh, then beckoned Pen to follow him into the sitting room to talk to the General while he dressed. "Ask him to sing you 'The Little Pig under the Bed'; it's capital."

Pen fled in great perturbation to meet Mr. Costigan. Meanwhile, Foker began his toilette.

To understand Foker, one must look to his lineage. One grandfather was a brewer, the other an Earl, both sources of immense wealth. The Fokers had attended Cistercian School from father to son. There, young Foker had been dreadfully bullied for his trade, his uncomely countenance, his inaptitude for learning, and his gluttony. But freedom from school transformed him. Within months, he blossomed from a silent sneak into the humorous, sarcastic, brilliant Foker who now inhabited the room. Though a dunce by academic standards, he was now a dandy, scented and arrayed in fine linen, perfectly splendid.

In the sitting room, General—or Captain, as he preferred—Costigan sat by the window with a newspaper held at arm's length. His eyes were dim, and he spelled the words with his lips, a rare and difficult occupation for a man whose brain seemed permanently muzzy. His hat was cocked jauntily on one ear, revealing a quantity of lank, iron-grey hair that fell over his pate. His boots were shabby, a testament to poverty attacking the extremities first. Whisky had spoiled whatever complexion he once possessed, lending his face a copper tinge. He wore a high stock, scarred and stained, and a dress coat tightly buttoned where the fabric hadn't surrendered to wear.

As soon as he saw Pen, the Captain descended from the window seat. He saluted with a military flourish, conveying a couple of fingers covered by a broken black glove to his hat before removing it altogether.

"The young gentleman to whom I had the honour to be introduced yesterday in the Cathedral Yard," the Captain said, offering a splendid bow. "I hope I see you well, sir. I marked ye in the theatre last night during me daughter's performance; and missed ye on my return." He recounted the jollity of the night, pulling out an old snuff-box to present to his new acquaintance.

Arthur Pendennis was flurried. This shabby-looking buck was her father. Perfumed with the recollection of last night's cigars, the Captain twisted the tuft on his chin as jauntily as any young dandy.

"I hope, Miss F--, Miss Costigan is well, sir," Pen stammered, flushing. "She—she gave me greater pleasure, than—than I—I—I ever enjoyed at a play. I think, sir—I think she's the finest actress in the world," he gasped.

"Your hand, young man! For ye speak from your heart," cried the Captain. "Thank ye, sir, an old soldier and a fond father thanks ye. She is the finest actress in the world. I've seen the Siddons, sir, and the O'Nale—they were great, but what were they compared to Miss Fotheringay? I do not wish she should assume her own name while on the stage. Me family, sir, are proud people; and the Costigans of Costiganstown think that an honest man, who has borne Her Majesty's colours in the Hundred and Third, would demean himself, by permitting his daughter to earn her old father's bread."

"There cannot be a more honourable duty, surely," Pen agreed.

"Honourable! Bedad, sir, I'd like to see the man who said Jack Costigan would consent to anything dishonourable." The Captain's voice dropped to a pathetic whisper. "I have a heart, sir, though I am poor; I like a man who has a heart. You have: I read it in your honest face and steady eye. And would you believe it"? he added, "that that Bingley who has made his fortune by me child, gives her but two guineas a week: out of which she finds herself in dresses, and which, added to me own small means, makes our all?"

The Captain's means were invisible, yet he managed affairs with a mystique that baffled outsiders. He had begun brilliantly with a pair of colours, a fine person, and one of the most beautiful voices in the world. Even now, he sang Irish ballads with admirable pathos, crying at their own melanch

For a stretch of time following the previous tumult, Cicero and Euripides ceased to occupy Mr. Pen's weary mind, leaving the honest Mr. Smirke to glide through his lessons with an unusually effortless grace. Yet, amidst this academic peace, Rebecca suffered the most grievous torment. While Pen occasionally announced his intention to travel to Chatteris for fencing lessons—a journey undertaken openly when three clear hours lay ahead, accompanied by his mother's knowledge—the young rascal frequently bolted for the city. He would vanish into Prior's Lane, driven by a frenzy that matched Richard III's despair at Bosworth when his horse was slain beneath him. When Rebecca grew lame, Pen's distress was so profound he delved deep into the medical manuals of the stable keeper at Chatteris, seeking cures for his own imaginary ailments and those of others.

Under the guise of reading a Greek drama with Smirke, this young reprobate would occasionally slip away, timing his departure perfectly to catch the "Competitor" coach. He would linger in Chatteris for two hours before returning via the "Rival" line at ten o'clock. On one occasion, his secret nearly unraveled thanks to the Curate's simplicity. When Mrs. Pendennis asked if they had read much the night prior, Smirke hovered on the brink of the truth, admitting he hadn't seen Mr. Pen at all. Just as the lie was about to surface, Pen's boot-heeled ground heavily onto Smirke's toe beneath the table, a silent, violent warning that saved the curate from betrayal.

Naturally, the pair had discussed the tender subject. It is excellent sport, for anyone not personally entangled, to overhear two lovers speaking. Under solemn vows of secrecy, the Curate confessed Pen's condition, trembling slightly as he asked if the object of Pen's affections was "unworthy"—perhaps an "unlawful attachment." If so, the poor fellow felt compelled to break his vow and inform Pen's mother, fearing a quarrel and the loss of his chance to see what he loved most in the world.

"Unlawful, unworthy!" Pen exploded, bouncing out of his chair. "She is as pure as she is beautiful! I would give my heart to no other woman. I keep this matter secret in my family for reasons of a weighty nature which I am not at liberty to disclose. But any man who breathes a word against her purity insults both her honor and mine—and, by Jove, I shall not stand it!"

Smirke, offering a faint, nervous laugh, merely said, "Well, well, don't call me out, Arthur, for you know I cannot fight." With this compromise, the wretched curate remained entirely at the mercy of his pupil, causing Greek literature and mathematics to suffer accordingly.

Had the Reverend Gentleman possessed discernment and inspected the 'Poet's Corner' of the County Chronicle arriving in the Wednesday bag, he might have spotted verses titled 'Mrs. Haller,' 'Passion and Genius,' and 'Lines to Miss Fotheringay.' These appeared weekly, glowing with gloomy, thrilling passion. However, as these poems were no longer signed 'NEP' by their artful composer, but rather subscribed 'EROS,' neither the tutor nor Helen—the good soul who diligently cut her son's verses from the paper—suspected that Nep was the very same flaming Eros singing the praises of the new actress.

Eventually, Mrs. Pendennis asked, "Who is the lady whom your rival is always singing about in the County Chronicle? He writes somewhat like you, dear Pen, but yours are far superior. Have you seen Miss Fotheringay?"

Pen admitted he had. That night, he had gone to watch "The Stranger," where she played Mrs. Haller. He noted she was preparing for a benefit performance as Ophelia. "Suppose we go?" he suggested. "Shakespeare, you know, Mother. We can get horses from the Clavering Arms." Little Laura sprang up with delight, her heart racing at the prospect of a play.

Pen invoked "Shakespeare" because the deceased Father Pendennis, true to his character, had professed an uncommon reverence for the Bard of Avon, claiming his works contained more poetry than all Johnson's poets combined. Though Mr. Pendennis himself rarely read these volumes, he had often instructed Pen to peruse them, promising future joys when the boy reached a proper age to accompany him and his wife to the theater.

Tears welled in the kind mother's eyes as she recalled these speeches. She kissed her son fondly and agreed to go. Laura jumped for joy. Was Pen happy? Was he ashamed? Holding his mother, he longed to confess everything but kept his counsel. He intended to observe her reaction; the play itself would serve as the test, much like Hamlet testing Gertrude.

Helen, in a mood of high spirits, invited Mr. Smirke to join the party. The ecclesiastic, raised by a fond parent at Clapham who forbade dramatic entertainments, had never seen a play. But Shakespeare! To ride in Mrs. Pendennis's carriage and sit beside her for an entire night! He could not resist the allure of such pleasure. He delivered a feeble speech, weaving themes of temptation and gratitude, before accepting her generous offer. As he spoke, however, he bestowed upon her a lingering look that rendered her exceedingly uncomfortable. She had seen that gaze many times lately, persistently following her. He became positively odious in the widow's eyes with every passing day.

We shall not dwell extensively on Pen's courtship of Miss Fotheringay, for the reader has already sampled her conversation, much of which requires no repetition. Pen sat with her hour after hour, pouring forth his honest, boyish soul. He recounted everything he knew, hoped, felt, read, or imagined. He never tires of talking and longing. Her role in these tête-à-têtes was not to converse, but to feign understanding (a difficult feat, given the quantity of nonsense the youth blurted out) and to maintain an appearance of exquisite handsomeness and sympathy. In reality, while Pen delivered twenty-minute tirades, intoxicated by his own eloquence, the lovely Emily—who could comprehend less than a tenth of his ramblings—used the time to arrange her thoughts. She planned how to dress the cold mutton, how to turn the black satin, or how to fashion a bonnet from her scarf à la Miss Thackthwaite. Pen spouted Byron and Moore; passion and poetry. Her business was simply to lift her eyes, fix them on his face, and cry, "Oh, 'tis beautiful! Ah, how exquisite! Repeat those lines again." And off the boy would go, while she returned to her simple domestic concerns regarding the hashed mutton.

Indeed, Pen's passion was not long a secret from Miss Fotheringay or her father. Upon his second visit, his admiration was evident to both. As he departed, the old gentleman winked at his daughter over a glass of grog, remarking, "Faith, Milly darling, I think ye've hooked that chap."

"Pooh, 'tis only a boy, papa dear," Milly replied lightly. "Sure he's but a child." Pen would have been delighted to hear this phrase; he was currently galloping home wild with pleasure, shouting her name as he rode.

"Ye've hooked 'um any how," the Captain insisted. "Let me tell ye he's not a bad fish. I asked Tom at the George and Flint, the grocer, about his mother. Fine fortune, drives in a chariot, possesses Fairoaks Park, the finest grounds. Only son, property all his own at twenty-one. Ye might go further and not fare so well, Miss Fotheringay."

"Them boys are mostly talk," Milly said seriously. "Ye know at Dublin how ye went on about young Poldoody. I have a whole desk full of verses he wrote me from Trinity College, but he went abroad, and his mother married him to an Englishwoman."

"Lord Poldoody was a young nobleman; in their cases, it's natural. And ye weren't in the position ye're in now, Milly dear. But ye mustn't encourage this young chap too much, for, bedad, Jack Costigan won't have any trifling with his daughter."

"No more will his daughter, papa, you may be sure of that," Milly assured him. "A little sip more of the punch—sure, 'tis beautiful. Ye needn't be afraid about the young chap—I think I'm old enough to take care of myself, Captain Costigan."

Thus, Pen visited day after day, rushing in and galloping away, growing more wild with every encounter. Sometimes the Captain attended, but trusting his daughter implicitly, he preferred to leave the young couple to themselves, cocking his hat over his eye and strutting off on some errand when Pen entered. How delightful those interviews were! The Captain's drawing-room was a low, wainscoted space with a large window overlooking the Dean's garden. There, Pen sat and talked endlessly. Emily sat at her work, looking beautiful and calm, bathed in sunlight streaming through the great windows, illuminating her superb features. Mid-conversation, the great bell would boom, causing Pen to pause, smiling and silent until the vast music faded away—or the rooks in the cathedral elms erupted into noise at sunset—or the sound of the organ and choristers drifted over the quiet air, gently hushing Pen's chatter.

Incidentally,

Major Arthur Pendennis arrived at Fairoaks exactly when the schedule demanded, emerging from a journey that had tested the limits of human endurance. For twenty-five hours, he had been packed like sardines into a mail-coach moving at eight miles an hour. His companions included a burly fellow who swelled with layers of greatcoats and snored with the volume of a collapsing cathedral, a widow who treated the vehicle as a private distillery, filling the air with the acrid perfume of Jamaica rum and water, and two schoolboys heading home for the midsummer holidays. These lads, sitting beside the Major's servant on the roof, had somehow managed to transform the coach into a floating pantry, devouring a cargo of ham, eggs, pies, pickles, tea, coffee, and boiled beef with a ferocity that left the Major, sipping weak tea with a rheumy eye, wondering if Lord Steyne was dining at that very moment on something far superior.

Yet, despite the jolting, the horn blasts at turnpike gates, the crushing weight of neighbors, and the Widow's feet constantly nudging the Major's toes, the travelers remained merrily alive. When the coach finally ground to a halt at the little lodge-gate of Fairoaks, the Major descended with a yellow complexion, a bristly beard, a wig that had lost its curl, and joints that screamed with gouty grief. The porteress and gardener's wife bowed deeply, and his man, Morgan, performed his duties with respectful silence, though the Major suspected the man of keeping secrets regarding the wig situation.

Inside, Helen watched from her window. Knowing her brother's vanity, she did not rush to meet him. Arthur Pendennis, even in his prime, required a certain preparation before appearing before his family; he was notoriously particular about his dentition and his coiffure. Once inside his apartment, the transformation began. He groaned, wheezed, and cursed Morgan profusely as he struggled to retrieve his missing front teeth and restore his hair to a state of "majestic" gravity. Finally belted, curled, and set, he emerged into the drawing-room with an air of grave business, looking every inch the man of fashion and commerce.

However, Pen was not there. Only Helen and little Laura sat sewing at her knees. To Laura, the Major offered only a single forefinger, a gesture that caused the girl to tremble, drop the digit, and flee the room in terror. Arthur, wishing to avoid the child entirely, retreated to the orchard, where he found Pen pacing furiously alongside Mr. Smirke. Smirke, Pen's tutor, was so engrossed in the young man's fiery discourse that he failed to notice Laura approaching until she interrupted with a clear cry.

"Come in, Pen!" she urged, gripping his hand. "There's somebody come; Uncle Arthur's here."

"He is, is he?" Pen replied, his grip tightening. He glanced at Smirke with a look of fierce readiness, as if daring anyone to challenge him. Smirke sighed gently and nodded.

"Lead on, Laura," Pen commanded, his voice a mix of comedy and deep, internal anxiety. "Say I wait upon my uncle." He laughed, a nervous sound, as he prepared himself for the ordeal ahead.

Over the preceding forty-eight hours, Pen had confided everything to Smirke, speaking endlessly of Miss Fotheringay—Emily, the goddess of his dreams. Smirke, a man smitten by the same enchantment, listened with rapt attention. He had been convinced by Pen's passionate rhetoric and poetic flourishes that the plan was not only feasible but prudent: Emily settling at Fairoaks, Captain Costigan in the yellow room, and Pen married at eighteen. Even Pen's mother, the Widow Pendennis, had nearly been swayed by her son's indignant logic, though she harbored lingering fears about the consequences of a union between a sixteen-year-old aristocrat and a thirty-year-old actress who had long ceased to blush.

While Pen composed verses late into the night, his mother crept quietly past his door. Hearing the rhythmic tossing and the murmuring of lines, she entered softly. Finding him buried in manuscripts, surrounded by scattered papers, she sat beside him, taking his hot hand. "Why don't you go to sleep, my dear?" she asked tenderly.

"I couldn't sleep," Pen confessed, flinging his arms around her neck. "I love her, Mother! I love her!"

His mother, a woman of immense kindness, soothed him with a strange mixture of pity and nostalgia, recalling how recently he had been a child in that very bed. Later, when the Major, fully restored, confronted the Widow, he discovered her distress was compounded by fear for her son's happiness. She begged him to be gentle, citing Pen's high spirits and the rough treatment he had received from Dr. Portman.

The Major, however, played the role of the seasoned diplomat perfectly. With a gallant kiss on her hand, he feigned shock at her alarm. "Bon Dieu!" he thought internally, marveling at how easily the boy had charmed the woman. "If I were a king, I'd lock this boy away." He pressed her hand, forcing a smile, and then launched into a lecture on the perils of mismatched unions.

"My dear madam," he declared, "we cannot allow the head of the house to marry a strolling mountebank from a booth. We shall not marry into Greenwich Fair."

"But if the match breaks off suddenly..." Helen interjected, her eyes lowering.

"The issue shan't be marriage," the Major countered resolutely. "My dear sister, I've no doubt Arthur will suffer terribly before he gets over this little disappointment. But consider my own brother. He courted a wealthy girl, faced obstacles, and ended up in India as secretary to Lord Buckley. They parted ways, exchanged letters, and eventually, she became a baronet's wife. We suffered, yes, but we recovered."

"And look at my brother Pen," the Major continued, gesturing broadly. "He had a chance with Miss Balls, the apothecary's daughter. Difficultities arose. Regrets? None. Look at this hand!" He pressed Helen's fingers again, emphasizing the point.

"The marriages between people of such different rank and age," Helen sighed, "produce so much unhappiness. Think of Laura's father."

"Injudicious!" the Major cut in sharply. "To marry a woman of low station is to invite misery. Imagine a wife attached to a mother who drops her 'h's and calls Maria 'Marire!' You cannot introduce her into society. I have seen men cut from the best circles solely because of their wives' vulgar connections."

He painted a vivid picture of social ruin, citing examples of Lady Snapperton rejecting a druggist's daughter and the tragic history of Lord Clodworthy, whose wife looked like his mother. "Marry at eighteen an actress of thirty?" he scoffed. "Bah! I would as soon see him marry the cook."

As the conversation wound down, Helen, overwhelmed by the weight of these warnings and her own youthful regrets, realized that the matter of little Laura needed to be addressed separately. The stage was set: the Major, armed with cynical wisdom and a flair for dramatic storytelling, had successfully delayed the inevitable collision, leaving the young lovers in a state of suspended animation, waiting for the storm to break.

In Which Pen is Kept Waiting at the Door, While the Reader is Informed Who Little Laura Was.

Once upon a time, a certain young gentleman of Cambridge University descended upon a quiet village to spend the long vacation. He sought out the home of young Helen Thistlewood, who resided with her mother—the widow of a lieutenant felled at Copenhagen. This visitor was the Reverend Francis Bell, a fellow of a college renowned for his learning and tutoring skills. By blood, he was the nephew of Mrs. Thistlewood and the cousin of Miss Helen, making his presence in her modest household entirely proper. There he remained, surrounded by three or four pupils who had followed him from the university.

Before long, the two kinswomen discerned that the reverend gentleman was engaged, merely awaiting a suitable college living to formalize his union. His intended bride was the daughter of another parson, who had served as Mr. Bell's private tutor in his youth. Indeed, the romance had begun when Bell, a boy of seventeen or eighteen, had knelt in the garden before Miss Martha Coacher, pledging his eternal affection while helping her pick peas.

Miss Coacher was significantly older than the impetuous youth, and her heart, though wounded by numerous past disappointments, remained open. Her father, the apothecary, had failed to guide her wisely; indeed, three of his pupils and a local dragoon officer had previously tested her affections with varying degrees of success before fleeing or breaking hearts. Yet, despite her skepticism—"Law, Bell, I'm sure you are too young to think of such things"—she secretly harbored hope. A lock of her auburn hair, tied in a blue ribbon, eventually signaled her consent. Though she had gifted similar tokens to faithless suitors before, she believed Francis Bell to be different.

For a time, Francis proved worthy. He labored prodigiously at college, winning honors and prizes, which he sent to old Coacher and the silver declamation cup to Miss Martha. Their correspondence was tender and frequent, and Bell attributed his academic triumphs to her influence. However, by the time Francis returned as a Fellow and Tutor at twenty-six, Miss Coacher was thirty-four. Time had not softened her temper or improved her manners. Having relaxed his studies, Bell found himself bound by a thousand letters to a coarse, ill-favored, and middle-aged woman.

An altercation ensued, leading Francis to refuse a visit to Bearleader's Green, where his former fiancée lived. Instead, he traveled to his aunt's village, a place he had not seen since little Helen sat upon his knee. Now, Helen was a beautiful young woman. For four months, from June to October, the cousins shared walks, mornings, and books read aloud by candlelight. Helen knew everything of his past; he had shown her the portrait of his fiancée, along with letters that were hard, eager, and cruel. Yet, the days grew kinder, filled with more confidence and pity. Then, in October, Francis departed for college, leaving the poor girl with a heart that felt it following him.

Francis, too, awoke from a midsummer dream to the harsh reality of his predicament. He gnashed his teeth against the chain binding him, desperate for freedom. Could he confess, offer his savings, and beg for release? He temporized, knowing few livings would fall for years. Meanwhile, the betrothed woman complained bitterly of his altered tone, while the cousins exchanged sad but fond letters. Finally, the new attachment surfaced. Francis admitted it, rebuking Martha for her violent temper and inferiority. Her response was dramatic: if he broke his promise, she would expose his perjury to the courts and then commit suicide.

Desperate to avoid scandal, Francis arranged a final interview with Helen, whose mother had recently died. They resolved that he must fulfill his duty to honor his vow, effectively making two honest people miserable. The living fell to him sooner than expected, and by the time he was inducted, he was already grey and worn. Helen wrote him a brief, sorrowful letter on his marriage, returning his letters and the lock of hair save for a small fragment.

Years passed. Francis Bell, now a chaplain, secured a position on Coventry Island. He married a colonial lady he loved, but she died in childbirth. Following her death, Bell gave up completely, sending his own daughter home to Helen Pendennis and her husband, John. The child, dressed in black and accompanied by a soldier's wife, wept at parting but quickly found solace under Helen's motherly care. Around her neck hung a locket containing hair Helen had given to the dead Francis decades prior. She named the girl Helen Laura, though John Pendennis, suspicious of the orphan's lineage, insisted she be known simply as "Laura." To everyone, she was Mrs. Pendennis's daughter, a connection viewed with suspicion by both John and his brother, the Major.

Now, let us introduce Mr. Pen, who had been standing outside all this while, steeling his nerves for a confrontation with his formidable uncle. Determined to face the "old man of the world" with the dignity of his famous family, he flung open the door. He entered with a severe, warlike expression, armed cap-a-pie, ready for battle.

However, the Major, observing the scene, could barely suppress a grin at the boy's pompous simplicity. Seeing that the widow was already sympathetic and that threats would fail against Pen's stubborn seriousness, the Major dropped his authoritative demeanor instantly. With a good-humored smile, he shook Pen's passive fingers and said, "Well, Pen, my boy, tell us all about it."

Helen, delighted by the Major's generosity, was caught off guard by Pen's reaction. The boy, expecting a tragedy, felt his grand entrance baulked and ludicrous. Blushing with mortified vanity, he stammered, "I—I—I didn't know that you were come till just now... is—is town very full, I suppose?"

The Major, fighting to keep from laughing, glanced comically at Mrs. Pendennis, who also found the scene absurdly sentimental. After kissing Mr. Pen, the atmosphere lightened. The guardian declared, "What a couple of fools they are," urging them to minimize sentiment and focus on the facts.

Pen, regaining his heroic air, recounted his tale: a most beautiful and virtuous lady of high family, reduced circumstances, whom he loved above all else. He acknowledged the difficulties of age and station but claimed his affection was so great he could surmount them. "She has consented to unite her lot with mine," he declared.

"How much is that, my boy?" the Major asked. "Has anybody left you some money? I don't know that you are worth a shilling in the world."

"You know what I have is his!" cried Mrs. Pendennis.

"Good heavens, madam, hold your tongue!" the guardian muttered, though he maintained his temper. "No doubt, you would sacrifice anything for him. But it is, after all, *your* fortune which Pen is offering, of which he wishes to take possession at eighteen."

"I know my mother will give me anything," Pen said, looking disturbed.

"Yes, my good fellow, but there is reason in all things," the Major interjected gently. "If your mother keeps the house, it is fair that she should select her company. When you transfer her banker's account to support Miss Costigan, don't you think you should have consulted my sister as a principal party? You are her dependent; wouldn't it have been more generous to wait and ask her leave?"

Pen lowered his head, realizing his romantic gesture was perhaps a selfish piece of folly. "I did it in a moment of passion," he admitted, though sincerely. "Now it is said, and I stand to it. I'll die rather than recall it."

"But will she take you on those terms?" the Major pressed. "Don't you suppose your position as a young gentleman of ancient birth makes your address welcome?"

"I'll die, I say, rather than forfeit my pledge!" Pen doubled his fists, turning red.

"Who asks you?" answered the imperturbable guardian. "No gentleman breaks his word freely given. But you can wait. You owe something to your mother, to your family, and to me."

"Oh, of course," Pen said, feeling relieved. "As you have pledged your word to her, give us another, will you, Arthur?"

"What is it?" Arthur asked.

"That you will make no private marriage—that you won't be taking a trip to Scotland."

"That would be a falsehood. Pen never told his mother a falsehood," Helen interjected.

Pen hung his head, tears filling his eyes. How could he explain that the whole intrigue had been a lie to a tender, confiding creature? Giving his uncle his hand, he promised, "On my word of honour, as a gentleman, I will never marry without my mother's consent!" Offering Helen a bright look of unchanging affection, he retired to his study.

"He's an angel—he's an angel," the mother cried.

"He comes of a good stock, ma'am," said the Major, pleased with his diplomacy. "Three years ago, Lady Ferrybridge sent for me regarding her son, Gretna, who had eloped with a Scotch clergyman's daughter. I extracted a promise from Master Pen that he would not repeat such errors."

"He hasn't,—that is one comfort," answered the brother-in-law.

Like a wary man of the world, the Major did not press further immediately. Later, over wine, he discoursed on the

To any soul who truly loves the glittering machinery of London society—the exclusive clubs, the opera stalls, the carriage drives along Rotten Row, and the whispered intrigues behind the scenes of the great houses—let them admit that Major Pendennis was a man of staggering, almost tragic generosity. In May, he surrendered it all. He traded the vibrant chaos of town life for a quiet, dusty country cottage. He exchanged the thrill of a bow from a Marquis for the silence of a parson's study; he swapped the roar of the Opera House for the hum of a single candle flickering over a modest table. He gave up the "select" circles, the secret festivals, and the endless stream of invitations to lock himself away with a grieving widow, a son who had barely learned to shave, a curate who sighed too often, and a ten-year-old girl.

And what a sacrifice it was! Few outside his immediate circle realized the depth of his renunciation. Down in the country, his letters arrived, franked and heavy with the news of the season. To Helen, they were merely polite notes from distant cousins; to young Pen, they were portals to a universe of grandeur. The Major would hold up a letter, his face etched with a melancholy pride, and sigh over the name at the top. "Look at that," he'd murmur, "Lady Grampound Bowser! Or the Duchess of St. Albans!" Young Pen, wide-eyed and respectful, would stare at the superscripts, listening with bated breath to the tales of the fashionably wicked and the nobly virtuous.

The old Major's mind was a vast library of gossip, a repository of scandal and virtue alike. With the eloquence of a man recounting his own battles, he poured these stories into Pen's eager ears. "You must start your genealogical studies immediately, my boy," he would advise, pacing the terrace or settling over a glass of wine. "Read Debrett every day. Don't worry about the history; focus on the alliances. I once knew a young man, Mr. Suckling, who ruined his chances because he didn't know who sat next to whom at a dinner. He insulted Admiral Bowser without realizing the Admiral's wife was sitting beside him and her husband's son was across the table. A terrible mistake, I assure you."

These lectures, though tedious to the bewildered Mrs. Pendennis, were the lifeblood of young Pen's existence. The Major, a man of honor with eyes that were perpetually "open," viewed the world through a lens of practical morality. He believed that virtue lay not in abstract ideals, but in social survival. "A gentleman on his estate must set an example," he preached, insisting that Pen should attend church, not just for piety, but for the sheer necessity of appearing respectable to the local yokels. "You are the first man in the parish now that the Claverings are gone. Play your cards right, and you might even represent the town."

Pen, naturally pliable and awestruck by his uncle's authority, swallowed these doctrines whole. He found Captain Costigan, his intended father-in-law, increasingly repulsive—a drunken, unshaven brute who smelled of punch and trouble. Under the Major's gentle prodding, Pen admitted that Emily, his beloved, lacked accomplishment and possessed only a "very good average intellect." When pressed further, Pen confessed he had received but three trite letters from her. The Major nodded sagely, offering a humble apology for his intrusive questioning, all the while ensuring Pen understood that he was doing it for the sake of his nephew's future honor.

Back at Chatteris, the atmosphere shifted palpably. Emily, sensing the storm clouds gathering with the arrival of the Major, grew anxious. "I suppose you mean to leave me now?" she fretted. "He'll carry you off!" Yet, when Pen visited, surrounded by the Captain's boisterous hospitality and the sharp tongue of Miss Rouncy, he stood firm. In the presence of his father-in-law's trophies and the Captain's dirty embrace, Pen vowed that no other woman could ever compare to Emily. He fled Chatteris with a mixture of relief and guilt, muttering internally about the sacrifices he was making for a girl who remained, to his mind, cold and mistrustful.
