However, the Major's surveillance was relentless. Through his faithful valet, Mr. Morgan, a London professional of impeccable discretion, the Major constructed a dossier on Pen and his love interest. Morgan, a man of the streets, scoured the local inns and theaters, extracting secrets from waiters and ostlers. He discovered that Captain Costigan was a man of questionable finances and habits, frequently drunk and often in debt, banned from the officers' mess by Colonel Swallowtail. Yet, when it came to Emily, the picture changed. The theater staff and the local community spoke highly of her father's protective nature. Despite his drinking, Captain Costigan ensured Emily never ventured out alone. Morgan noted that when officers like Sir Derby Oaks or the dashing Mr. Foker came to take fencing lessons, Emily usually retreated to her lodging or stayed close to her father, rarely engaging with the suitors directly.

Doctor Portman, the vicar, shared the Major's growing disquiet. While the Major lamented that Pen's attachment to an actress was a product of "romantic notions" bred by women, the Doctor argued for a pragmatic approach. "If I were his guardian," Portman mused, "I would command him to break it off. A virtuous attachment to a lady of his rank and fortune would secure his future." But the Major, cynical and experienced, countered that trying to force Pen to abandon his infatuation would only drive him deeper into it. "Time is what we lack," the Major observed. "We must do our best with what we have."

Seeing no other way forward, the two elderly gentlemen hatched a plan. On a day when Pen was expected to dine at home, Portman and Pendennis drove to Chatteris. Like jolly bachelors escaping the confines of propriety, they dined at the George Inn and proceeded straight to the theater to witness *Hamlet*. The stage was familiar ground; both gentlemen had seen Miss Fotheringay perform countless times, yet tonight they watched with fresh intensity.

As Ophelia swept her curtsy to the sparse audience, the Major leaned forward, squinting through his opera-glass. "Gad," he muttered, his voice tight, "the young rascal hasn't made a bad choice." The Doctor, clapping his hands in genuine appreciation, agreed. "She is a clever actress, Major, endowed with considerable personal attractions."

"But that young officer in the box," the Major pointed out, gesturing to a dragoon who was applauding with fervor. "He looks quite taken with her, doesn't he?"

"She certainly seems sweet upon him," the Doctor conceded.

The Major closed his glasses, pocketing them with a decisive click. "Very well," he said, standing up. "Enough observation for tonight." Nor did he linger for the afterpiece. He walked home toward the George, his mind already turning to the strategy ahead, leaving the Doctor to return to his duties at the Deanery. The game, the Major realized, required patience, but the pieces were moving exactly as he had anticipated.

Major Pendennis ambled home, his boots thudding rhythmically against the cobbles, until the familiar façade of the George Inn loomed ahead. There, stationed like a sentinel at the threshold, stood his devoted valet, Mr. Morgan. Seeing his master approach with a candle tucked under his arm, ready for the bedroom, the valet interceded with his characteristic air of knowing deference.

"I think, sir," Morgan intoned, "if you'd venture into the coffee-room, there sits a young gentleman you'd be pleased to encounter."

"Is Mr. Arthur here?" the Major demanded, his brow furrowing in genuine irritation.

"No, sir, but his bosom buddy, Mr. Foker," the valet corrected gently. "Lady Agnes Foker's lad is ensconced there. He's been nodding off in that coffee-room since lunch, just rang for his coffee, and I daresay you'd find a word or two to exchange with him."

Morgan ushered the door open, and the Major stepped inside. Indeed, there sat Mr. Foker, the sole inhabitant of the dim chamber. He was vigorously rubbing the sleep from his eyes, seated before a table cluttered with empty decanters and the skeletal remains of a heavy dessert. He had intended to attend the theatre, but a copious repast and subsequent somnolence had stolen away his theatrical ambitions; instead, he had propped his legs upon the bench and succumbed to a deep nap.

The Major, pondering how to initiate the interview, found himself preempted by the youth.

"Wish to peruse the evening papers, sir?" Mr. Foker offered, already holding *The Globe* aloft with an affable grin. "Here, let me oblige."

"The Major bowed gratefully. "Much obliged. If my memory serves, I am addressing Mr. Henry Foker, son of Lady Agnes. I hold the honor of knowing her ladyship, and you certainly wear the visage of a Rosherville."

"Hullo!" Mr. Foker exclaimed, lowering the paper. "Pardon me, I mistook you for some commercial sort." He paused, regaining his composure. "But truly, to whom do I have the privilege?"

"To a relation of a friend and schoolchum of yours," the Major explained, settling onto a chair. "Arthur Pendennis, my nephew, speaks of you in high regard. I am Major Pendennis, the guardian mentioned in his letters. Might I share your soda-water? I recall dining at your grandfather's table with great pleasure."

"Sir, you flatter me," Foker replied with exaggerated politeness. "So, you are Arthur's uncle?"

"And guardian," the Major added.

"He's a capital fellow," Foker declared warmly. "And sharp, too—I'm a simpleton, I admit, but I spot the clever ones and like 'em."

"You display both taste and humility," the Major observed. "Arthur praises your talents highly."

"Not book-smart," Foker confessed, shaking his head. "Never could crack the codes. Pendennis, however, could churn out verses for half the lads. Yet—" the youth broke in, his candor unguarded, "since you're his guardian, I'll be blunt: I think he's what we call 'flat.'"

The Major was instantly seized by the thrill of a secret. "Flat, how?" he asked, smiling.

"About the ladies, sir," Foker whispered conspiratorially, winking. A wink so bold it might have been bestowed upon the Duke of Wellington himself, born of a state of absolute absence, candor, and fearlessness induced by a couple of bottles of wine. "You know Pen's flat. It's mostly my fault. One night we hit the play—I was reading for my small role in *The Long*, though I dash over from Baymouth often in my drag. Pen got smitten all of a heap with Miss Fotheringay. Her real name is Costigan; she's a beauty, I assure you. Next morn, I introduced him to the General, whom we dub 'Old Scamp' due to his affinity for whisky-and-water. Now, Pen's besotted, and I'm blessed if he hasn't popped the question."

Slapping his hand down on the table, he sent the remaining dessert rattling. "What! You know the score?"

"Don't I? And the gossips, too!" Foker laughed. "We were chaffing Derby Oaks at mess yesterday until the Baronet was raving like a hatter. Know Sir Derby Oaks? We dined together recently. Remember when you slipped past us to dinner?"

"I recall Sir Thomas Oaks, his sire, back when he dwelt in Cavendish Square and served Queen Charlotte."

"The young one is spinning money fast," Foker noted. "And is Sir Derby Oaks another suit?"

"Another what?" Foker queried innocently.

"Another suitor?"

"Lord bless you! We call him Monday, Wednesday, and Friday, while Pen claims Tuesday, Thursday, and Saturday. Mind you, no harm done! Miss F. is far too wide-awake for that trick. She plays one off against the other; she's strung with two bows."

"Pendennis laughed. "You seem quite wide-awake yourself, Mr. Foker."

"Pretty well, thanks, sir," Foker replied imperturbably. "Not clever, perhaps, but 'downy.' Friends say I keep track of the hour fairly well. Shall I tell you the time?"

"On my word," the Major blurted, delighted, "you could be of immense service. A man of the world, easy to deal with. Our family is, alas, not amused by this absurd intrigue."

"Fancy that!" Foker agreed. "Connection ineligible. Too much beer brewed on the premises. No Irish need apply. That seems to be your drift."

"It is precisely so," the Major admitted, though he failed to grasp Foker's deeper irony. He pressed the youth for particulars on the amiable Costigan household. To his credit, Foker spoke favorably of their morals. "The General loves a jovial bowl," Foker conceded, "so I wouldn't bet on his pockets, but he watches his daughter like a hawk. Neither he nor she stands for anything but honor. Pen's attentions are the talk of the company. I hear from a young lady close to Miss F., whose family I occasionally tea with. This Miss Rouncy says Sir Derby Oaks has hung around ever since his regiment dropped anchor here, but Pen has cut him out, driving the Baronet nearly to propose. Wish he would! Then you'd see which one Miss F. would leap at."

"I thought as much," the Major said, relieved. "You bring me joy, Mr. Foker. I wish I'd met you sooner."

"Ain't liked sticking my oar in," Foker shrugged. "Don't speak till asked. Heard your man was hankering after my maid-servant; didn't know the full tale till Miss F. and Miss R. had a row over the ostrich plumes. Miss R. spilled the beans."

"Miss Rouncy, then, was the confidante?"

"Confidant? I believe so. She's twice as clever as Fotheringay, literary and all, whereas Miss Foth can barely read."

"She can write," the Major reminded him, recalling the contents of Pen's breast pocket.

Foker burst into a sardonic "Hee-hee! Rouncy pens her letters," he declared. "Every single one! Since they feuded, she doesn't know how to function. Miss Rouncy's a stunning hand, unlike the old girl, who makes dreadful work of spelling when Bows aren't nearby. Rouncy has been setting her copies lately; her script is beautiful."

"I assume you know it well," the Major teased archly.

Foker winked again. "I'd pay a fortune for a sample of her handiwork," the Major continued, his eyes gleaming. "Surely you could spare me one?"

"No, no, that would be wicked," Foker protested. "Perhaps I shouldn't've said so much. Miss F.'s writin' isn't terrible, I dare say; she just had Miss R. do the first letter and hasn't stopped since. But mark my words: till they're friends again, the letters halt."

"I hope they never reconcile," the Major said sincerely. "As a man of the world, you must see how fatal this step is for my nephew's future. How eager we are to liberate him from this pair of schemers! Love on her side is out of the question."

"If Pen hadn't two thousand a year when he turned twenty—"

"If Pen hadn't what?" the Major gasped.

"Two thousand a year! Doesn't he have two thousand? The General swears he does."

"My dear friend!" the Major shrieked, seizing the revelation with desperate fervor. "Thank you! Thank you! I see now! Two thousand a year! His mother earns but five hundred. She'll live to eighty, and Arthur has not a shilling save what she allows."

"What! Not rich, then?" Foker asked.

"On my honor, not a penny more."

"And you're leaving him nothing?"

The Major and Captain Costigan were veterans of the road, men who had stared down enemies without flinching, so their composure remained unshaken. Yet, the atmosphere in Cos's sitting-room shifted palpably with Pendennis's sudden appearance. Miss Fotheringay's heart, usually a steady drum, began to stutter; a flush, bright and unfeigned, climbed her cheeks as Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks fixed a squinting glare upon her. Meanwhile, the tiny, bent figure perched in the window-seat—the theater composer who had abandoned his pen to watch the fencing match—lifted his gaze with renewed vigor as the Major, sporting impeccably blacked boots, swept into the room with a cascade of bows that would have done justice to a courtier.

"My daughter—my friend, Mr. Bows—my gallant young pupil and companion, I trust, Sir Derby Oaks!" boomed Costigan, waving a hand that seemed to conduct an orchestra of introductions. "In a trice, Major, I am at your service!" With this, the old soldier vanished into his adjoining chamber. In a blur of motion that defied physics, he twisted his lank hair with an ancient brush, ripped off a threadbare stock, swapped it for a pristine new one crafted by Emily, tucked in a crisp collar, and donned the brand-new coat ordered specifically for Miss Fotheringay's benefit night. All this metamorphosis occurred within sixty seconds.

Sir Derby followed close behind, emerging moments later encased in his 'shell-jacket,' a garment that hugged his large frame with uncomfortable tightness—a look, Cos suspected, that Milly, Emily, and perhaps Pen, found utterly adorable.

While the actress and the newcomer exchanged pleasantries regarding the weather, Costigan re-emerged, fully transformed into his new suit. "Need I apologize to ye, Major, for receiving ye in shirt-sleeves?" he asked, his voice dripping with a courtesy that masked a lifetime of rough trade.

"An old soldier finds no nobler employment than instructing a youth in the art of the sword," the Major replied, standing tall. "I recall hearing tales of your prowess, Captain Costigan."

"Why, you've heard of Jack Costigan, Major?" the Captain exclaimed, feigning deep offense.

The Major had, in fact, pried details from his nephew regarding this Irish officer. Whether he truly remembered the man or simply wanted to sound authoritative, he proceeded with absolute conviction. "I distinctly recollect meeting Mr. Costigan at Sir Richard Strachan's table during the Walcheren campaign. A fine singer, I'm told."

At this, Miss Fotheringay looked bewildered. "Perhaps we shall discuss these matters later," the Major suggested smoothly, shifting his focus back to the actress. "I sought you today to pay my respects." He executed another bow, so elegant it would have graced a duchess, causing Milly to rise immediately. She retrieved an unripped satin gown from a nearby seat, carried it over, and presented the empty chair to the Major with a curtsy so sharp it nearly sliced the air.

"You speak highly of my performances from my nephew," the Major noted, settling into the chair. "But Arthur is but a boy, a wild spirit whose enthusiasm often outpaces reality. I wished to judge for myself. Permit me to say, your performance astounded me. I have seen the greatest stars, and yet, you surpass them all. You possess a majesty akin to Mrs. Siddons."

"Faith, I always did say so!" Costigan winked at his daughter, gesturing for the Major to sit. "Milly, draw a chair." As soon as the hint fell, Milly produced the seat.

"You are equally pathos-filled," the Major continued, "reminiscent of Miss O'Neill. Those songs echoed Mrs. Jordan in her prime. And your bearing? It called to mind Mars. Have you ever seen the God of War, Miss Fotheringay?"

"There were two Mahers in Crow Street," Emily chimed in. "Fanny was passable, but Biddy..."

"The Major speaks of the deity, Milly, my dear," Costigan interjected quickly.

"It is not that Mars I refer to, though Venus might forgive thoughts of him," the Major smiled, directing his gaze squarely at Sir Derby Oaks, who had returned in his jacket. However, the compliment failed to soothe the young officer. Sir Derby, who clearly did not grasp the nuance of classical mythology, received the remark with a stiff, sullen silence, scowling at Miss Fotheringay as if to ask, *What devilry is this?*

Pendennis, however, was not perturbed. The ill-humor delighted him. "Ah," he mused, "a rival enters the arena." He silently vowed that Sir Derby might win this contest, whatever the cost to Pen and Emily.

"I fear I interrupted your fencing lesson," the Major said lightly. "My stay at Chatteris is brief, but I needed to introduce myself to Captain Costigan and see a lady who charmed me from afar. Last night, Miss Fotheringay, many hearts were captivated. A reverend friend of mine returned home in a state of ecstasy over Ophelia, and I saw Sir Derby Oaks toss a bouquet—an offering no actress deserves less." He pointed to the flowers in a glass of water on the mantel. "Had I known what awaited me, I would have brought one. Are not these the same blooms?"

"I adore flowers," Miss Fotheringay murmured, casting a languid glance at Sir Derby, who remained sullen.

"Sweets to the sweet," Pendennis quoted, striving for warmth. "'Pon my life, I haven't a clue," Sir Derby muttered. "Not a literary man."

"Do you not share your father's love for letters?" the Major asked, feigning shock. "He was a remarkable scholar."

"He saved my life," the Major added solemnly.

"Oh, really?" Miss Fotheringay gasped, rolling her eyes first toward the Major, then at Sir Derby with gratitude. But the young officer was immune to her glances. Far from being pleased that his father, the apothecary, had spared the Major's life, Sir Derby looked as though he wished the accident had gone differently.

"My father was a decent doctor," Sir Derby replied coldly. "I'm not in medicine. Good morning, sir. Cos, goodbye. Miss Fotheringay, good morning." Despite the young lady's imploring smiles, the dragoon bowed stiffly and marched out, his sabre clattering down the creaking stairs. His voice rang out in anger as he cursed little Tom Creed, kicking a spinning top into the street with an oath.

The Major did not smile, despite the amusement the scene provoked. "A monstrously handsome youth," he told Costigan. "One of the finest-looking soldiers I have ever seen."

"A credit to the army and humanity itself," Costigan agreed. "Refined manners, princely fortune, sixteen stone of muscle."

"A perfect champion," the Major laughed. "All the ladies must admire him."

"He's pleasant enough, considering his weight," Milly admitted dryly, "but he lacks conversation."

"He shines best on horseback," Mr. Bows observed. "Third in the steeplechase on a horse named Tareaways."

The Major nodded slowly, realizing Milly's lack of wit was matched by her acting genius.

With typical Irish hospitality, Costigan pressed refreshments upon his guest. The Major, though not hungry after a hypothetical feast, feigned a craving for biscuits and Madeira, knowing that accepting small kindnesses flattered the donor and softened hearts. "Some of the old Madeira, Milly," Cos whispered.

The girl nodded intelligently, slipped out, and summoned her little messenger, Tommy Creed, downstairs. She handed him coins and sent him running to the Grapes for a pint of wine and sixpence-worth of biscuits, instructing him to hurry back.

Downstairs, Miss Costigan sat with Mrs. Creed, whispering about the Major upstairs—"a soft-spoken gentle giant"—and how Sir Derby had stormed out in jealous rage.

"She keeps the cellar key, Major," Costigan announced as the girl departed.

"By Jove, you have a magnificent butler!" Pendennis replied gallantly. "Plainer women served us well in our day, Captain."

"And lucky is the man who secures her," Costigan insisted, turning to Mr. Bows. "Ask my friend here if Miss Fotheringay's mind is not superior to her beauty, if she lacks a cultivated intellect and an amiable disposition."

"Bah," Mr. Bows murmured drily. "Here comes Hebe, blushing from the cellar."

"Don't you think it's time to go to rehearsal, Miss Hebe?" Bows asked, gathering his papers. "A fine late arrival costs a penalty." He shot her a look suggesting they should depart, leaving the elders alone.

Miss Fotheringay, looking uncommonly pretty and good-natured, obeyed. Bows shuffled after her, retrieving his hat and cane.

"Must you go?" the Major asked, stepping forward. "Before you leave, allow an old fellow to shake your hand. I am proud to have made your acquaintance and earnestly desire to become your friend."

She curtsied deeply. The Major followed her to the door, squeezing her hand with a paternal warmth that baffled Bows, who wondered if the Major truly intended to marry

A shooting match was proposed, or at least, a duel that smelled faintly of gin and bad decisions. The architect of this chaos was Captain Costigan, a man whose reputation for honor was as thin as his pockets, currently vibrating with the tremors of a mind intoxicated by "spirits-and-water." To execute his grand design, he required a heavy-handed ally, and he summoned Mr. Garbetts, the Principal Tragedian.

Garbetts was a mountain of a man, a jovial creature of irregular habits who shared a convivial club with Costigan at the Magpie Hotel. They were friends of a sort, bound by mutual loans of signature and a deep-seated hatred for Miss Fotheringay, the Captain's daughter. While Garbetts had once wooed the maiden and been soundly rejected, favoring instead the Captain's wife—a union that caused significant jealousy among the Garbett household—it mattered little now. Garbetts arrived promptly, delivered by a nimble errand-boy named Tommy, carrying two hot glasses of whisky-and-water that acted less as refreshment and more as liquid courage for a man planning to break pokers in sport.

Once fortified, the Captain dictated a letter to Major Pendennis. It was a document of pure theatrical melodrama, drafted in Costigan's finest style and sealed with the family's only remaining piece of silver plate. The contents were clear: the Major was to offer no obstruction to the marriage of his niece, Miss Fotheringay, to Arthur Pendennis. If he failed to comply, the Captain warned, he would be given "satisfaction" via a horsewhip—or, failing that, by the ancient dueling pistols lying on the desk, which had claimed many a "pretty fellow" in Dublin.

But the plan hit a snag almost before it began. Garbetts returned within twenty minutes, his face a mask of anxiety. He had seen Major Pendennis, but he could not deliver the missive. Standing in the Major's drawing room was a man named Tatham, a "minion" whom the Captain despised.

"I'll have nothing to do with fighting!" Garbetts declared, tossing the letter onto the floor with a flourish that defied gravity. "I'm a family man, Captain. Take back your letter."

The Captain, roaring like a bear denied honey, accused his friend of cowardice. "You're no better than a coward!" he shouted, channeling Sir Lucius O'Trigger. Yet, despite the Captain's fury and the presence of his murderous flintlock pistols, Garbetts stood firm. He refused to drag anyone into a brawl involving Tatham, and the two men parted in ill humor, the duel averted for the moment.

Back at the Costigan residence, the atmosphere was thick with the scent of alcohol and impending disaster. The Captain paced his apartment, surrounded by the debris of his intoxication: empty goblets and a teaspoon that had lost its utility. When Miss Fotheringay returned from rehearsal, accompanied by her faithful servant, Mr. Bows, she found her father in a state of agitated delirium.

"My child!" the Captain cried, seizing her in a grip that threatened to bruise. "Prepare yourself! A conspiracy! Both the Pendennis uncle and nephew are infernal traitors!"

Emily, usually the picture of grace, pushed back her sire's hands. "You're tipsy again, Papa," she chided gently. "You promised me no spirits before dinner."

"It is to drown my cares!" the Captain wailed, his eyes bloodshot. "That soft-spoken gentleman in the wig—he's a viper! He's tricked me! And that boy, Arthur... he's a beggar!"

Here lay the crux of the tragedy. The Captain, drunk on suspicion, believed Arthur Pendennis was a pauper who had deceived his daughter into loving him without financial security. He recounted how the Major had allegedly revealed Arthur's poverty, suggesting the youth was dependent on his mother's five hundred-a-year allowance. The Captain's rage was absolute; he spoke of tearing off the Major's wig and dragging him through the streets of London, or forcing him to draw sword in the field.

"But what has the poor fellow done?" Emily asked, her voice steady amidst the storm.

"He has outraged my feelings!" the Captain roared. "He represents himself as a man of property, and it turns out he is nothing! A pauper! An impostor!"

Emily sat down, retrieving a pair of ex-white satin shoes she intended to polish with breadcrumbs later. With a calmness that belied the chaos around her, she addressed the absurdity of her father's wrath.

"If he has no money," she reasoned, "there is no point in marrying him, Papa. But surely he wasn't a liar?"

"Poor lad!" the Captain lamented, clutching his pistol case. "He's a deceiver!"

"And if he isn't rich," Emily pressed, "then why does he look at me like that? Why does he bring me flowers and verses?"

Her father, fueled by drink, insisted that Arthur's lack of funds proved his treachery. He vowed to send a message to the Major demanding a showdown, or at the very least, a public humiliation in front of the county.

"Stop and take dinner, Bows," Emily suddenly announced, breaking the tension. "We have a beautiful beef-steak pudding."

Mr. Bows, a fiddler with a sharp tongue, watched her closely. He wondered if she was already plotting some clever dodge to avoid the duel. Indeed, Emily was not a romantic dreamer; she understood the practicalities of life far better than her hysterical father. She knew that if Arthur truly had no money, the match was dead anyway. And if he *did* have some secret fortune, his uncle's accusations were likely lies born of envy.

Over dinner, the Captain, now sobered slightly by the prospect of food, tried to maintain his stance. But Emily, with the cool judgment of someone unburdened by a strong imagination, dismantled his arguments. She pointed out that her father had deceived himself, projecting his own insecurities onto the boy. She ate her pudding with admirable satisfaction, pitying the poor Arthur in her heart, even as she prepared to play the role of the pragmatic parent.

"The boy is worthy," she said softly. "But if he cannot provide, we cannot go forward. And if he can, then perhaps..."

She rose from the table, walked to a drawer, and retrieved a bundle of letters and poems Arthur had written her. They were filled with Latin, fevers, and lonely nights, all penned with a generosity of spirit that cost nothing. She wrapped them carefully in paper, tying them with a neat string as if they were parcels of sugar.

"They are mine," she said, her voice devoid of emotion. "And when the time comes, I shall send them back. For now, they are just nonsense."

As she sat down to make tea, completely placid, she left behind the boy who was probably yearning over those same pages ten miles away, hugging her image to his soul. The Captain, meanwhile, rubbed his chin, wondering if he should really pull the Major's nose in public, or if the pudding had calmed his nerves enough to let the matter rest. The duel, it seemed, was postponed indefinitely, leaving the future of the love match hanging in the balance between a father's drunken fears and a daughter's quiet resolve.

While the household at Fairoaks grew restless, waiting for the Major's return, Dr. Portman and his wife paused at Helen's lodge-gate on their journey home to Clavering. With them was a brief note from Major Pendennis, penned at the precise moment when the battle with the dragon turned decisively against him. The Major explained that he intended to stay another day at Chatteris to consult with Messrs. Tatham, the lawyers, but conspicuously omitted any mention of the morning's disastrous transaction.

Young Pen, however, found no pleasure in venturing into town while his guardian was present. He dreaded the notion of his uncle spying upon him from the Dean's grass-plat while he attempted the art of courtship in Miss Costigan's drawing-room. The "man of the polished boots" would inevitably ruin the simple joy of a solitary walk. Pen's affections were a quiet, internal storm; they could manifest only through the violent ogling of eyes, remaining utterly silent and dumb in the presence of third parties. After all, the talk of lovers is often the vocabulary without the key, a lamp without the flame. To the uninitiated, it sounds merely silly. Yet, consider the old love letters one keeps hidden away. How blank they appear in retrospect! What spellbound infatuation once made that nonsense beautiful? One wonders how anyone could live happily on six absurd lines, until the inevitable reactionary period sets in, leaving one restless and miserable without a fresh supply of folly. That is why we respectfully decline to publish any of Mr. Pen's verses from this era; they are too spooney and wild for young ladies to read in cold blood. Bide your time, fair maidens; perhaps you will compose similar ravings on your own account soon. For now, let us tie up Mr. Pen's first outpourings with Miss Fotheringay's string and seal them with Captain Costigan's great silver seal.

The Major emerged from his interview with Captain Costigan in a state of concentrated fury, radiating a terror that made him difficult to approach. "The impudent bog-trotting scamp!" he muttered, thinking. "Dare to threaten me! Dare to suggest that his damned Costigans might marry with the Pendennises! Send me a challenge! If the fellow can find a gentleman to carry it, I shall not balk him.—Psha! What would the world say if I parried with a tipsy mountebank over a row with an actress in a barn?" Consequently, when Dr. Portman approached, asking anxiously about the outcome of his battle with the dragon, the Major feigned caution. He claimed the affair was ugly, disagreeable, and far from over, strictly enjoining the doctor and Mrs. Portman to say nothing about the business at Fairoaks. He contented himself with dispatching the aforementioned note and retreated to his hotel, where he vented his wrath upon Mr. Morgan, his valet, cursing loudly upstairs and downstairs.

Morgan relayed the news to his master, Mr. Foker. Having finished his breakfast around two in the afternoon, Foker, eager to learn the result of the interview between his two friends, donned his brocade dressing-gown and embroidered skull-cap. He knocked on the Major's door. Inside, the Major was engaged with papers and red tape, consulting with old Mr. Tatham, the lawyer who had managed his late brother's affairs. Seeing the modest youth clad in such splendor, Mr. Foker hesitated to intrude, but the Major, spotting him, smiled and beckoned him in. Foker removed his embroidered tarboosh, bowed gracefully, and seated himself, spreading out his crimson skirts with excessive kindness. "You seem to like my dressing-gown, sir," he remarked to Mr. Tatham. "A pretty thing, isn't it? Neat, but not in the least gaudy."

"There is that in Foker's manner," the narrative observes, "which would have put an Inquisitor into good humor, smoothing even the wrinkles under the Major's head of hair." The Major recounted his unsatisfactory interview with the "Irishman"—Captain Costigan—who had dared to call his nephew a liar and hinted at cowardice. Mr. Tatham, a Dissenter who avoided theaters, expressed shock at the tale of his nephew's loves, questioning why Mrs. Pendennis had not married a serious man to keep this unfortunate boy from perdition. Mr. Foker chipped in with his usual familiar commentary, noting he had known uncommon good people in the booths. However, Mr. Tatham remained firm regarding Costigan's character: a wine-bibber, an idler, and a notorious insolvent. Indeed, a writ had been issued against Costigan and a play-actor for a bill given to Mr. Skinner, a respectable grocer, but neither defendant was deemed "worth powder and shot."

As Tatham spoke, a third knock startled them. An athletic gentleman in a shabby braided frock entered, bearing a letter with a large blotched red seal. "I am the bearer of a mission from my friend Captain Costigan," the man began, pausing as he caught sight of Mr. Tatham's grave countenance. "Hullo, Garbetts, speak up!" cried Mr. Foker, delighted. "Why, bless my soul, it is the other party to the bill!" exclaimed Tatham. Garbetts, turning pale like Macbeth at Banquo's ghost, gasped inarticulate words and fled. The Major's gravity dissolved instantly into laughter, shared by Foker and the attorney. "I don't think there'll be any fight, Major," young Foker predicted, mimicking the tragedian. "If there is, the old gentleman may send the bailiffs to separate the men." The Major, relieved by the ludicrous resolution, admitted that Foker always arrived to put him in good spirits.

Later that afternoon, Foker visited Captain Costigan, who was absent, having been encouraged by his daughter to frequent the convivial club at the Magpie Hotel. Instead, Foker found Miss Fotheringay at home, washing tea cups while Mr. Bows sat opposite. "Just done breakfast, I see," Foker popped in, earning a sharp retort before launching into a harlequinade of spins and bows. Miss Fotheringay laughed with all her heart, slapping Foker's hand, a gesture that would have terrified Pen, who trembled at the thought of a slap.

The trio discussed the morning's events. Foker praised the Major's veracity and honor, describing him as a "tip-top swell" who would never submit to deceit, especially concerning a charming young woman. He delicately touched upon the marriage question, revealing a perhaps justified contempt for Mr. Pen's high-flown sentimentality. "He's too young for you: too green," Foker noted, predicting the match would fail because Pen was "poor as Job." Miss Costigan, though admitting the absurdity of the arrangement due to her father's stories and the age disparity, lamented that she could not simply reject two thousand a year. "It all comes of believing Papa's silly stories," she sighed, though the image of Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks briefly crossed her mind.

Despite the odds, Miss Costigan declared the Major a proper gentleman, smelling of lavender and as neat as a pin. Mr. Foker invited them to dinner at his apartment at the George, hoping to mend fences after the morning's "shindy." Though Mr. Bows initially declined, Miss Costigan, possessing an honest appetite, accepted. Thus, at seven o'clock, the Major found himself seated with Mr. Bows, a professional fiddler, and Miss Costigan, whose father had recently threatened to blow his brains out. To complete the tableau, Foker fetched the intoxicated General from the Magpie, where he was singing a pathetic song. The General, tearful and effusive, embraced the Major as a dearest friend, calling him his salvation. The next day, the Major called on the General with great cordiality, expressing his desire to serve Miss Costigan on any occasion.

Back at Fairoaks the following day, the Major returned in silence regarding his adventures. He engaged Mr. Smirke to stop for dinner, displaying a constrained hilarity and unusual gracefulness toward his nephew. He gave Pen an emphatic "God-bless-you" before the lad retired, and later, in the breakfast room, pointed to a letter under the newspaper. "Will you come into the drawing-room?" he asked Mrs. Pendennis. "I want to speak to you."

In private, the Major revealed the truth: the affair was at an end. He had dictated a dismissal letter to Costigan, revealing Pen's poverty, which Costigan's father had misrepresented. "She withdrew at once," the Major insisted, "without any persuasion of mine. She is ten years older than you, perfectly unfit to be your wife, and knows it." When Pen, furious and wounded, demanded to speak to Miss Fotheringay himself, his mother interjected, urging him to forget her and comfort her. "Be a man," she pleaded, embracing him as the Major wisely shut the door upon them.

Having secured a complete victory, the Major retrieved Pen's letters from Chatteris, returning them to Costigan along with the promissory note that had caused so much trouble, which he promptly settled. Pen, devastated, rushed to Chatteris in vain. Every attempt to see Miss Fotheringay ended in rejection; the General cut him in the street. Refusing his mother's and uncle's advice to travel abroad, Pen stayed, riding to the theater whenever she performed. One night,

The theater in Chatteris was thinner than a winter mouse, so sparse that the house itself seemed to be holding its breath. Mr. Manager Bingley, straining to breathe life into the corpse of *Pizarro*, played his famous 'Rolla' to an audience that consisted mostly of echoes and the occasional cough. Yet, amidst the Spanish battles and Peruvian processions, the air was thick with a singular, suffocating heat. Poor Pen sat alone in the boxes, his eyes bloodshot, leaning over the ledge as if trying to pry the world open. To him, the stage was a blur of costumes and shouting; the only focus was Cora. He later claimed he wished he'd grabbed a pistol right then, so mad was he with a love that had curdled into pure rage. Had it not been for his mother, watching silently from afar with a sympathy that felt like a cold blanket, he might have ended his days prematurely in front of the local gaol. He simply sat, miserable, staring at her back.

And she? She looked like a goddess carved from moonlight and leopard skin, dressed in white raiment with a golden sun pinned to her chest. Her bracelets, a tawdry but dazzling display, caught the light as she moved. Her eyes, the ones that had once nearly undone Pen's soul, rolled and gleamed with a ferocious vitality. But tonight, they were not fixed on him. Pen didn't notice, nor did he realize that the moment two gentlemen entered Box Number Three, the entire theater shifted its axis.

First, the atmosphere changed. With fewer than a dozen souls in the house, the first act had dragged on like molasses, and there had even been whispers of returning tickets. Actors yawned openly, talking over their lines, while even the formidable Mrs. Bingley spoke under her breath. Then, suddenly, the mood snapped. Mrs. Bingley began to bellow like a bull of Bashan; Bingley, usually listless, darted across the stage yelling like a man possessed. Every actor, from Garbetts to Miss Rouncy, poured their charm, swagger, and scowls directly at the two men in Box 3.

One was a quiet little man in black with a grey, shrewd face. The other, however, was a spectacle. He was tall and portly, draped in velvet coats adorned with the richest frog-braiding, his wrists heavy with jewels and his fingers encased in white kid gloves. When he unfolded his yellow pocket-handkerchief, a cloud of musk and bergamot drifted through the stale air, drowning out the smell of cheap tobacco. This was Mr. Dolphin, the great impresario from London, accompanied by his faithful secretary, the sardonic old gentleman Mr. William Minns.

Before ten minutes had passed, the whole troupe knew why they were there. They weren't putting on a show anymore; they were auditioning. Even Miss Fotheringay, whose heart was usually as dull as a brick, felt a strange flutter. She had little to do but look beautiful and strike picturesque poses around a prop child, and she did it to perfection. The actors tried everything to catch the eye of the "Stage Sultan"—hand gestures, dramatic flourishes, sheer volume—but Mr. Dolphin merely took a pinch of snuff from his great gold box.

Nothing worked until the final scene. Rolla stumbles onto the stage, staggering under the weight of his infant son, a monstrous toddler named Master Talma Bingley. Cora rushes forward, screaming, "Oh God, there's blood upon him!" At that precise moment, Mr. Dolphin clapped his hands, breaking into an enthusiastic bravo. He turned to his secretary, slapped him on the shoulder, and declared, "By Jove, Billy, she'll do!"

Old Billy, however, couldn't resist a jab. "Who taught her that dodge?" he asked. "I remember her at the Olympic, and hang me if she could say 'bo' to a goose." It was revealed to be little Mr. Bows in the orchestra who had coached her. As the curtain fell, the entire company swarmed around Miss Fotheringay, mixing congratulations with a distinct undercurrent of hatred.

But how had the mighty Mr. Dolphin descended to a dusty provincial theater on the verge of ruin? His London Museum Theatre was failing fast. Despite advertising perpetual triumphs and victories of old English comedy, the box office remained stubbornly empty. The legendary Hubbards had failed to pay anyone but themselves; the famous Crawdors had bombed in tragedy; Herr Garbage's lions and tigers had drawn briefly until one bit off Herr's shoulder, forcing the Lord Chamberlain to shut them down; and his grand Lyrical Drama, though spectacular, had almost crushed him financially. He was dragging out his season on half-salaries and feeble comedies, waiting for the day the newspapers announced his bankruptcy.

His salvation arrived in the form of the Most Noble the Marquis of Steyne. Lord Steyne, a refined patron of the arts, rarely attended plays unless it was for the ballet, but when he did, he commanded the proscenium box. Surrounded by a circle of friends, including the ever-joking Mr. Wagg and the serious Mr. Wenham, Lord Steyne often amused himself by reading letters from Major Pendennis, who was currently absent in the country.

"The secret is out," Mr. Wenham had recently declared, reading a letter aloud. "There's a woman in the case."

"Why, d—— it, Wenham, he's your age," Lord Steyne had chuckled.

"Pour les âmes bien nées, l'amour ne compte pas le nombre des années," Mr. Wenham had replied gallantly. "For my part, I hope to be a victim till I die."

"And yet," Lord Steyne had mused, "I'm told there is a young friend of Pendennis's who has gone wild about a certain Miss Fotheringay. She is as handsome, and apparently as good an actress, as ever put on rouge. I want a London engagement for her. Can't you get your friend Dolphin to come and see her?"

So, Mr. Wenham became the emissary. He slipped past the stagehands, who were currently cursing the corps-de-ballet, and found Mr. Dolphin. The manager's curses instantly died on his lips as he saw the visitor. He smoothed his coat, wiped his brow, and greeted Mr. Wenham with a smile that suggested he had never known anger in his life. He followed the messenger immediately to pay his respects to the Great Man.

Back in Chatteris, the news spread faster than gossip. Pen read the announcement in the local paper, where the editor lavishly praised Miss Fotheringay's talent and beauty, predicting her meteoric rise in the metropolis. Bingley, seizing the opportunity, began advertising "The Last Night of Miss Fotheringay's Engagement."

As the date approached, the town split into two camps. Sir Derby Oaks, sitting in the stage box, threw bouquets and caught glances with the enthusiasm of a schoolboy. Pen, meanwhile, remained in the almost deserted boxes, looking haggard and wretched. It seemed the only ones who truly cared if she stayed or left were Pen, Sir Derby, and perhaps Mr. Bows of the orchestra.

One evening, Mr. Bows emerged from his seat and ventured into the box where Pen sat. Holding out a gnarled hand, he invited Pen to walk. They strolled down the street and sat on Chatteris Bridge under the pale moonlight, discussing the girl.

"We may sit on the same bridge," Mr. Bows said, puffing on a pipe. "We've been in the same boat for a long time. You aren't the only fool who's made a mess of himself over that woman. And I have less excuse than you, being older and knowing her better. She has no more heart than the stone you're leaning against. If either of us—or the stone—fell into the water, she wouldn't care. Yes, she would care for me, because she needs me to teach her. She can't get along without me, so eventually, she'll send for me from London. But if she didn't need me? Well, she has no heart, no head, and no sense. She likes her dinner, and she's pleased when people admire her. That's all."

"And you do?" Pen asked, startled by the brutal honesty of the crabbed old man.

"It's a habit, like taking snuff or drinking drams," Bows replied calmly. "I've been taking her these five years, and I can't do without her. If she doesn't send for me, I'll follow her. But I know she will. Someday she'll marry and fling me over, just as I discard the end of this cigar."

With a flick of his wrist, the little flame dropped into the dark water below and vanished. Pen rode home that night, and for the first time in months, his thoughts drifted to someone other than himself.

So long as the enemy lingered outside the gates, Major Pendennis swore he wouldn't stir from Fairoaks. He didn't bother to micromanage Pen's every step, yet the lad remained under the hawk-like gaze of the Major and his network of spies; young Arthur's movements were a matter of record to his vigilant guardian.

Let us pause here to address the universal tragedy of the novelist's hero: surely no man who has ever dipped into fiction escapes the cruel twist of fate regarding love. Whether thwarted by circumstance, betrayed by the fair sex, or undone by his own foolishness, the worthy friend must recall his own bruised heart to understand Mr. Pen's agony. Ah, the weary nights! The sickening fevers! Those mad desires battering themselves against the unyielding granite of indifference, only to bounce back shattered. Imagine tonight, in the heart of London, a ledger of groans and curses from tossing lovers. Who among the males of the metropolis will not be awake at two or three, counting the hours like a monk in a monastery, rolling from pillow to pillow, restless and heart-broken? It is a terrible pang. I have never seen a man literally die of love, though I have watched a twelve-stone giant shrink to nine stone and five under the weight of a disappointed passion—a quarter of his mass perishing in the process. Later, he regains his weight, perhaps grows larger; likely, a new affection has wrapped around his ribs like a warm blanket. Young Pen will find comfort eventually, just as the rest of us do. We say this to spare the ladies premature tears or undue alarm. His mother worried, naturally, but what maternal instinct won't invent fears? "Depend on it," the Major would gallantly assure her, "the boy will recover. Once we move her from the country, we'll whisk him away to see some life. For now, ease your mind. Half the pain of losing a woman stems from vanity, not love. Being abandoned by a woman is the devil, to be sure, but consider how easily *we* abandon them."

Mrs. Pendennis, however, remained in the dark. Such complexities lay far beyond her simple sphere. She disliked the subject, having weathered her own private storm and recovered, possessing little patience for the passions of others—with the singular exception of Arthur's. She absorbed his sufferings as her own, feeling perhaps more acutely in his feverish deliriums than he did himself. She watched him through this present grief with a jealous, silent sympathy, though he kept his troubles hidden from her.

One must grant the Major considerable merit and forbearance, displaying a truly creditable degree of familial affection. Life at Fairoaks was profoundly dull for a man accustomed to the whirlwind of London, where he could bow in three or four drawing rooms nightly. Now, his pleasures were limited to a dinner with Doctor Portman or a neighboring Squire, and a dreary game of backgammon with the Widow, who strained her best to amuse him. He longed for the mail bag bearing London letters and devoured every word of the evening papers. He medicated himself assiduously, believing a regimen of quiet living suited him post-London banquet, dressing laboriously twice daily and taking regular exercise along the terrace. Armed with his cane, his meticulous toilette, his medicine chest, his backgammon box, and his newspaper, this worldly philosopher constructed a fortress against ennui. Though he failed to improve every shining hour like the bees in the widow's garden, Major Pendennis succeeded in rendering his captivity merely tolerable, passing moment by moment. Eventually, he grew fond of steering conversations toward the American War, the Massacre of Wyoming, and the exploits at Saint Lucia, largely because he possessed two volumes of the 'Annual Register' in his bedroom which he studied with religious devotion. Thus does a well-regulated man accommodate himself to fortune, showing a calm superiority.

Pen, meanwhile, sometimes sat in for the backgammon box or listened to his mother's simple summer melodies, yet he remained restless and wretched. He was frequently up before dawn, descending to the carp-pond in Clavering Park. It was a dreary pool choked with whispering rushes and green alders, the site where a milkmaid had drowned herself in the time of the Baronet's grandfather, and where her ghost was rumored to still wander. Pen did not attempt suicide, as his mother suspected. Instead, he enjoyed fishing there, thinking deeply as the float quivered in the little eddies and fish flapped nearby. A bite excited him greatly, and in this manner he occasionally hauled in carps, tenches, and eels, which the Major cooked in the continental fashion.

Beneath his favorite tree by this pond, Pen composed a collection of poems suitable to his melancholy state—verses over which he later blushed, wondering how he could ever have invented such rubbish. As for the tree itself, it holds a hollow where he used to stash his tin-box of ground-bait and fishing tackle; it is there, we are led to believe, that he later hid something else, but matters advance quickly. Suffice it to say, he wrote poetry and relieved his soul significantly. When a gentleman's grief reaches this stage, it may be loud, but it is rarely severe. When a man struggles to find a rhyme for sorrow beyond "borrow" and "tomorrow," his woes are nearer an end than he realizes. So it was with Pen. He suffered hot and cold fits, alternating between sullen peevishness, blank resignation, and occasional mad paroxysms of rage and longing. During these fits, his imaginary mistress, Rebecca, would be saddled and galloped fiercely through the countryside or into Chatteris, her rider gesticulating wildly atop her, startling carters and turnpike men as he cried out the name of the false one.

Mr. Foker became a frequent and welcome visitor to Fairoaks during this era, his good spirits and oddities amusing the Major and Pen, while astonishing the Widow and little Laura. His tandem caused quite a sensation in Clavering marketplace, where he overturned a stall, sliced Mrs. Pybus's poodle over its shaven quarters, and drank raspberry bitters at the Clavering Arms. Everyone in the little place knew who he was and consulted their Peers to verify his title. So young, and their books so ancient, that his name appeared in only a few volumes; his mother, now quite an antiquated lady, was listed as Lady Agnes Milton, progeny of the Earl of Rosherville. Yet his youth, wealth, and honorable lineage spread rapidly through Clavering, where, one assumes, poor Pen's little transaction with the Chatteris actress was discussed with equal fervor.

Viewing the tiny old town of Clavering St. Mary from the London road, past the lodge at Fairoaks, with the rapid, shining Brawl winding down from the town and skirting the woods of Clavering Park, alongside the ancient church tower and peaked roofs rising among trees and old walls, backed by sunshiny hills stretching westward toward the sea—it appears so cheery and comfortable that many a traveler's heart must have yearned for it from the coach-top, imagining it a calm friendly nook to shelter in at life's end. Tom Smith, driver of the Alacrity coach, often pointed to a tree near the river commanding a fine view of the church and town, telling his companion on the box, "Artises come and take hoff the Church from that there tree—It was a Habby once, sir." Indeed, it is a pretty view, recommended to Mr. Stanfield or Mr. Roberts for their next tour.

Yet, like Constantinople seen from the Bosphorus, or Mrs. Rougemont viewed from the opposite box, Clavering is rather prettier at a distance than on close acquaintance. The town so cheerful a few furlongs off looks very blank and dreary. Except on market days, the streets are empty. The clack of pattens echoes through half the place, and you may hear the creaking of the rusty old ensign at the Clavering Arms without disturbance from any other noise. There has not been a ball in the Assembly Rooms since the Clavering volunteers gave one to their Colonel, the old Sir Francis Clavering; the stables that once housed that brilliant, now-defunct regiment are now cheerless and empty, save on Thursdays when farmers put up there with their tilted carts and gigs, making a feeble show of liveliness, or on Petty Sessions when magistrates attend in what used to be the old card-room.

On the south side of the market rises the church, with its great grey towers, wherein the sun illuminates delicate carving, deepening the shadows of huge buttresses and gilding glittering windows and flaming vanes. Centuries ago, the image of the Church's Patroness was wrenched from the porch; such statues of saints as were within reach of stones and hammers during that period of pious demolition are now maimed and headless. Of those spared from fire, only Doctor Portman knows their names and histories, for his curate, Smirke, is not much of an antiquarian, and Mr. Simcoe, incumbent and architect of the Chapel of Ease in the lower town, considers them the abomination of desolation.

The Rectory is a stout, broad-shouldered brick house of Anne's reign, communicating with the church and market by different gates, standing at the opening of Yew-tree Lane, where the Grammar School (Rev. Wapshot) resides, Yew-tree Cottage (Miss Flather), the butchers' slaughtering-house (

Word of Pen's latest escapade spread faster than wildfire, sparking a mix of fury and amusement across the household. To the staid and sensible Doctor Portman, the boy's behavior was a scandalous breach of decorum; to the boisterous Major Pendennis, it was mere youthful frolic, a charming bit of drama. But for Mrs. Pendennis, the news sent her into a flutter of genuine distress. Her imagination ran wild, conjuring scenes of sin, shame, and ruin. She viewed the entire episode as a catastrophic failure, convinced that nothing short of removing Pen from Chatteris could save his soul. "I must get him away!" she cried, wringing her hands. "Anywhere but here! Away from the woman who tangled him in this mess!"

When the loving mother and the stern doctor finally confronted Pen, expecting tears and repentance, they were met with something far more formidable: the heavy, self-important gravity of a teenager convinced of his own righteousness. Pen listened to their rebukes with the air of a man accused of high treason. "I am truly sorry," he declared, his voice dripping with dramatic flair, "but I was provoked without cause! A gentleman cannot stand idly by when his honor is touched. I had to defend myself with dignity and appeal to my uncle."

" *Vous allez trop vite,* " Uncle Pen interjected, feigning confusion despite his obvious amusement. "Between men of the world, perhaps, you might have a case. But between two schoolboys, this sort of theatrical posturing is simply ridiculous, my dear boy."

"It is wicked!" Mrs. Pendennis sobbed, her eyes wet with worry. "So unlike him!"

Pen, sensing an opening, leaned in for a hug, puffing out his chest. "Women, dearest mother, simply do not understand these matters of honor! I placed myself entirely in the hands of Foker; it was the only path a gentleman could take."

Major Pendennis chuckled, shaking his head. "Progress, I see. Great progress." Meanwhile, Mrs. Pendennis lamented the influence of Mr. Foker, calling him a "wicked, horrid little wretch." She confessed that she had a sudden, fierce urge to forbid Pen's university attendance entirely. Yet, duty and tradition held her back. Her husband had always planned this future, and the college was where he himself had briefly flourished. So, reluctantly, she acquiesced, though a shadow of dread lingered.

Indeed, the arrangement was final. Pen would depart at the next October term. Mr. Foker had vouched for him, promising introductions to the elite circles. The Major waxed eloquent on the benefits of this networking. "Mr. Foker knows the very best young men," the Major boasted, listing names like the Young Marquis of Plinlimmon and Lord Magnus Charters. "He is connected to Lady Runnymede, who will surely invite Pen to Logwood. And consider his friendship with the son of Lady Agnes—it is a stroke of luck, a blessing disguised as a risk."

Helen, however, remained skeptical. Though she admitted Foker had been kind in the Costigan affair, a gnawing fear persisted. The noise of quarrels and the glare of worldly ambition terrified her for her son's safety.

Doctor Portman, conversely, was firm. "College is essential," he reasoned. "If he reads and mixes with the best society, he will shine. Smirke speaks highly of his intellect, and I have heard him recite classics with commendable skill." Pen, usually prone to gloom over his private heartaches, agreed grudgingly, distracted by the whirlwind of recent troubles.

As summer waned into August and September, Chatteris buzzed with the annual assizes and horse races. Miss Fotheringay, the local actress, performed her farewell seasons, drawing crowds who barely noticed her impending departure. To the polite locals, she was just another performer, a fleeting novelty. Yet, amidst the spectacle, Miss Costigan held her ground. With a stoicism born of simple temperament, she ate, drank, slept, and worked with a comforting regularity, avoiding the emotional turbulence that plagued others. In her quiet resilience, she proved that sometimes, a lack of sensitivity is a profound gift.

Pen, meanwhile, wandered the theater and the town with a restless air that worried his mother. But the Major, ever the strategist, interpreted this restlessness as a sign of recovery. "Look at him," the Major observed. "He writes verses! He prowls the shrubberies spouting poetry! He even eats with a decent appetite." The discovery of a leather-bound volume filled with Latin and English verse in Pen's study confirmed the Major's suspicions. The boy was channeling his turmoil into art. "Gad, I wish I could eat like that," the Major mused, thinking of his own dietary struggles. "The boy sleeps soundly."

Lacking a mother for intimate confessions, Pen turned to the Curate, Smirke. Their bond deepened, transforming into a secret, passionate friendship. Pen poured his heart out, treating Smirke as a confessor. He wrote sonnets, penned Latin verses, and showered the Curate with gifts: his finest books, a gold chain, and even an offer of his double-barreled gun. He bought a gold pencil-case on credit just to give to Smirke, inscribing it with vows of eternal devotion. For Smirke, the situation was a delicate trap. The prospect of Pen leaving for college threatened to sever his lifeline to Fairoaks. Without Pen, his daily visits to the widow would vanish. Could he hope for more? The widow's recent kindnesses—a compliment on his sermon, thanks for a melon, warm words for Pen—had flattered his hopes. Was she hinting at something deeper? Or was it all innocent gratitude? The stakes were terrifying: if he pushed too hard and was rejected, the gates of Fairoaks might close forever.

While Pen navigated his stormy emotions, Smirke found solace in the company of Madame Fribsby. The eccentric spinster, formerly a milliner from London and a devotee of romantic fiction, lived a life steeped in melodrama. She believed everyone in love, interpreting every glance as a proposal and every silence as a tragedy. She convinced Smirke that the widow, Mrs. Pendennis, was deeply, silently in love with him. "She is so lonely," Madame Fribsby cooed, speaking in broken French. "When Master Pendennis leaves, she will have no one. You must tell her, Monsieur le Curé!" Smirke, shy and hopeful, enjoyed these sessions, where the Milliner's deaf mother sat in silent ignorance of the heated confessions.

Back at the Rectory, the atmosphere grew thick with gossip. The village, fueled by the tongues of Mrs. Pybus, Mrs. Speers, and Captain Glanders, spun tales of scandal. They whispered that Pen had nearly killed someone over a girl, that the whole Fotheringay affair was a cover-up involving blackmail, and that the Pendennis family was on the brink of total disgrace. These rumors stung Pen's pride, turning him into a local pariah. Even the clergyman, Simcoe, preached fiery sermons against "theatrical sins" and "lost youth," clearly targeting Pen.

Amidst the chaos, the real scandal simmered beneath the surface: the alleged romance between Smirke and Mrs. Pendennis. Madame Fribsby, unable to contain her theories, eventually spilled the beans to Mrs. Pybus. Soon, the story was everywhere. Mrs. Portman, the Rector's wife, rushed to confirm the tale with Doctor Portman. "What a sly fox!" the Doctor fumed. "To think of the widow taking a fancy to that milksop!" He was so incensed that he considered firing Smirke immediately.

"But he is too young for her," Mrs. Portman gently countered, noting the age gap. "She might be his mother."

"Nonsense!" the Doctor snapped. "A matron with a grown son will never remarry!"

Yet, the Doctor's anger quickly gave way to a secret. After calming his wife with a joke about ghosts dancing at weddings, he revealed the truth: Smirke was actually engaged to a Miss Thompson living on Clapham Common. The story of the romance with the widow was pure fabrication, invented by the gossips.

Feeling relieved but determined to stop the rumors, the Doctor decided to intervene personally. He invited Smirke to dinner, hoping to steer him away from the scandal. Just as he prepared to visit the Curate, he caught sight of Smirke emerging from Madame Fribsby's house. The Curate was impeccably dressed, his silk stockings gleaming, his cravat stiff and perfect, smelling faintly of eau-de-cologne.

" *O gracilis puer* ," the Doctor called out, trying to sound paternal. "Where are you bound? I wanted you to dine with me."

"I am engaged to dine... at Fairoaks," Smirke replied, his cheeks flushing pink as he tucked his scented handkerchief away. Mounting his pony, he trotted away, heading straight for the Pendennis estate, unaware that the storm of gossip he had helped create was already brewing around him, and that his dinner invitation from the Doctor was about to turn into a serious intervention.

The Curate had departed on his usual errand to Fairoaks and was currently ensconced in Pen's study, ostensibly reading with his charge, when the afternoon of that same day brought a surprise. Mrs. Portman, having concluded her business with Mrs. Pybus, found the weather so gloriously fine that she decided to extend her stroll all the way to Fairoaks to pay a visit to her dear friend. During their conversation, the Rector's lady revealed a grand secret concerning the Curate, Mr. Smirke: namely, that he harbored a very old, deeply hidden attachment.

"And on whom has Mr. Smirke bestowed his heart?" inquired Mrs. Pendennis, feigning a superior air while harboring a distinct internal alarm.

"Why, my dear," replied the other lady, "when Mr. Smirke first arrived and dined at the Rectory, rumors flew that we coveted him for Myra, forcing us to withdraw our inquiries. Others claimed he was smitten elsewhere, but I always denied it, insisting that *you*..."

"That I!" cried Mrs. Pendennis, indignant. "People are dreadfully impertinent, I assure you. Mr. Smirke came here solely as Arthur's tutor, and I am shocked that anyone should dare speak so freely—"

"'Pon my soul, it is a trifle excessive," interjected the Major, lowering his newspaper and adjusting his double eye-glass.

"I've lost all patience with that Mrs. Pybus," Helen continued, her indignation rising.

"I told her there was no truth to it," Mrs. Portman explained. "I always maintained so, my dear. Now, however, it emerges that my demure gentleman has been secretly engaged to a young lady—Miss Thompson of Clapham Common—for quite some time. I am truly delighted, especially on Myra's account, for an unmarried curate can be a tiresome presence in one's household. Of course, the matter must remain strictly private, but I thought I ought to inform you, lest it cause further unpleasantness. However, mind you: not a single word of this story, if you please."

Mrs. Pendennis expressed her utmost delight at the news, hoping sincerely that the kind and amiable Mr. Smirke would soon secure a deserving wife. Once her visitor departed, Helen and her brother discussed the matter with immense satisfaction. The kindly lady chided herself for her recent haughty behavior toward Mr. Smirke, whom she had lately avoided, realizing now that she should have been grateful for his constant attention to Arthur.

"Gratitude to this sort of person is commendable," the Major remarked, "but familiarity is absolutely out of the question. This gentleman instructs and accepts payment like any other master. You are far too humble, my good soul. Distinctions between ranks must be observed. I warned you before that you were too kind to Mr. Smirke."

Yet Helen did not share her brother's skepticism. With Arthur preparing to depart, she recalled with fresh appreciation how polite Mr. Smirke had been, how he had run errands for her, delivered books, copied music, taught Laura, and gifted her with kindness. Her conscience stung her for her past ingratitude. Consequently, when Mr. Smirke descended from the study with Pen, looking eager for the hall prior to his leaving, she stepped forward, shaking his hand with a slightly flushing countenance, and invited him into her drawing-room—a sanctuary where they had rarely met. Given the prospect of a substantial dinner that day, she extended an invitation for him to partake, and it is safe to assume he was overjoyed to accept such a delightful summons.

Relieved of her former doubts and misgivings regarding the Curate, Helen proved exceedingly kind and gracious throughout the meal, perhaps doubling her attentions because the Major remained notably high-minded and reserved toward his nephew's tutor. When Pendennis requested wine, he addressed Smirke as if speaking to a sovereign addressing a lowly retainer, a manner so condescending that even Pen chuckled, though he himself was quite capable of being as conceited as most youths.

However, Smirke cared little for the Major's impertinence so long as his hostess treated him with such warmth. He spent a delightful time at her side, deploying all his conversational skills to please her. He discoursed with equal ease on the Fancy Bazaar, the Great Missionary Meeting, the latest novels, and the Bishop's excellent sermon on fashionable parties in London—an account he had gleaned from the newspapers. In short, he employed every art by which a college divine, possessing both sprightly and serious talents, a taste for the genteel, an irreproachable conduct, and a susceptible heart, endeavors to become agreeable to the object of his affection.

Not long after his sister and little Laura had retired from the apartment, the Major emerged from the dining-room yawning. "What an unsufferable bore that man is, and how he did talk!" he complained.

"He has been very good to Arthur, who is very fond of him," Mrs. Pendennis noted. "I wonder who the Miss Thompson is whom he intends to marry?"

"I always suspected the fellow was looking in another direction," said the Major.

"And in what?" asked Mrs. Pendennis, quite innocently. "Toward Myra Portman?"

"Toward Helen Pendennis, if you must know," answered her brother-in-law.

"Toward me! Impossible!" exclaimed Helen, who knew perfectly well that this had indeed been the case. "His marriage will undoubtedly be a very happy affair. I only hope Arthur does not imbibe too much wine."

Indeed, Arthur, flush with the pride of holding the cellar keys and anticipating several more dinners shared with his dear friend Smirke, had procured a liberal supply of claret. When the elders and little Laura departed, the two topplers began to pour the vintage freely. One bottle quickly yielded up the ghost; another shed more than half its blood within half an hour. Pen, with a hollow laugh and voice, drained a bumper to the falsehood of women, declaring sardonically that wine, at least, was a mistress who never deceived and was certain to offer a warm welcome.

Smirke gently countered that he knew of some women who embodied truth and tenderness. Casting his eyes upward toward the ceiling and heaving a sigh as if summoning some dear and unmentionable entity, he raised his glass and drained it. The rosy liquor began to suffuse his cheeks.

Pen recited some verses he had composed that morning, informing the listener that the woman who had slighted his passion was unworthy of winning it. He declared himself awakening from love's mad fever and proceeding to abandon a heartless deceiver. Though he would never again be the happy, careless boy of months past, nor possess the heart it had been before passion and grief nearly killed it, he hoped to demonstrate he was a man worthy of his race. Someday, he vowed, the false one would learn the magnitude of the treasure and nobility of the heart she had discarded.

Pen, a highly excitable individual, rolled out these verses in his rich, sweet voice, trembling with emotion. He possessed a habit of blushing in such states, and his large, honest grey eyes displayed a sensibility so genuine, hearty, and manly that Miss Costigan, if she had a heart, surely would have softened toward him. Very likely, she was altogether unworthy of the affection he lavished upon her.

The sentimental Smirke, caught by the emotion agitating his young friend, grasped Pen's hand over the dessert dishes and wine-glasses. "These verses are beautiful," he proclaimed. "You are a poet, a great poet, and by Heaven's permission, destined for a magnificent career." "Go on and prosper, dear Arthur," he cried. "The wounds you currently suffer are merely temporary; the very grief you endure will cleanse and strengthen your heart. I have always prophesied the greatest and brightest things of you, once you correct certain failings and weaknesses of character that presently afflict you. But you will overcome these, my boy; you will. And when you are famous and celebrated, as I know you will be, will you not remember your old tutor and the happy early days of your youth?"

Pen swore he would. With another vigorous handshake across the glasses and apricots, he declared, "I shall never forget how kind you have been to me, Smirke. I don't know what I should have done without you. You are my best friend."

"Am I, really, Arthur?" Smirke asked, peering through his spectacles, his heart beating so violently he feared Pen could hear it throbbing.

"My best friend, my friend forever," Pen repeated. "God bless you, old boy." He consumed the final glass of the second bottle of the famous wine, which his father had laid in, his uncle purchased, and Lord Levant imported. Like a slave indifferent to his fate, it now ministered pleasure to its present owner, granting delectation to its young master.

"We'll have another bottle, old boy," Pen announced. "By Jove, we will. Hurray!—Claret goes for free. My uncle told me he saw Sheridan drink five bottles at Brookes's, plus a bottle of Maraschino. This is some of the finest wine in England, he says. So it is, by Jove. There's nothing like it. *Nunc vino pellite curas*—cras ingens iterabimus aequo—fill your glass, Old Smirke. A h

Every man, regardless of whether his time at school was brief or glorious, tends to look back on his university days with a soft, nostalgic warmth. Life is just starting for the young man; the leash has been snapped, and the air tastes sweet with the promise of liberty. He knows nothing yet of worry, ill-health, deceit, poverty, or tomorrow's disappointments. The play hasn't been staged enough to tire him. While the hangover of later years turns sour and bitter, that first sip of pleasure remains crystal clear and brilliant. How eagerly the boy rushes to the cup, draining it with wild intensity! Even old connoisseurs, restricted to poached eggs and plain water, find a strange joy in watching others feast with appetite. And just as it is delightful to watch one's children enjoy a pantomime, I hope no amount of age or wisdom ever hardens a man's heart so completely that he cannot smile at the sight of happy youth.

Returning recently from Oxford, where my friend Arthur Pendennis spent some of his early years, I traveled by train alongside a young student of Saint Boniface. He had somehow secured permission to leave and was determined to spend a day in London. From the moment we boarded until the journey ended—which felt far too short—I never ceased to chatter and laugh with him. His honesty and cheerful wit were infectious, and I found myself exhausted simply by listening to his jokes. Upon arriving at the terminus, nothing satisfied him but a hansom cab, so he could rush into town and dive straight into whatever pleasures awaited him. Off he went, whirlwind-like, joy illuminating his open face. As for me, carrying a mere carpet-bag, I perched on the exterior of an omnibus, sitting contentedly sandwiched between a Jewish peddler smoking foul cigars and a footman attending to a poodle dog. Once we gathered our "fated" complement of passengers and luggage, the coachman drove off leisurely. We were in no particular haste to reach the city, nor did either of us feel eager to rush into that nearby "smoking Babylon," dine at a club, or dance at the casino. Yet, in a few years, my young friend from the railway would likely feel no different.

Railroads did not exist when Arthur Pendennis attended the famous University of Oxford; instead, he traveled there in a well-appointed coach, crammed inside and out with dons, gown-wearers, fresh undergraduates, and their guardians. A portly old gentleman from the City, dressed in grey stockings, sat beside Major Pendennis. This man held his pale-faced son opposite and was terrified when he learned the coach had been driven for several miles by a young Mr. Foker of Saint Boniface College. Mr. Foker, known as the "friend of all men"—including coachmen—could drive as well as anyone else, and perhaps better. Pen sat atop the coach, examining the vehicle, the passengers, and the countryside with immense delight and curiosity. His heart leaped with pleasure as the university appeared on the horizon, revealing a magnificent vista of ancient towers, soaring pinnacles, towering elms, and the gleaming river.

Before setting out, Pen had spent a few days with his uncle at the Major's lodgings in Bury Street. The Major believed Pen's wardrobe needed refreshing, and Arthur was anything but resistant to plans involving new coats and waistcoats. The self-sacrificing uncle made endless concessions for his nephew's benefit. London felt desolate; the Pavement of Pall Mall was empty, even the red-coated guards had departed, and scarcely a face could be seen in the bow-windows of the clubs. The Major escorted his nephew into one or two of these deserted mansions and inscribed the lad's name onto the candidate list of a club. Pen's pleasure at this gesture of respect from his guardian was overwhelming. He read his name and title in the parchment volume—"Arthur Pendennis, Esquire, of Fairoaks Lodge..."—proposed by the Major and seconded by "Viscount Colchicum," feeling a thrill of intense gratification. "You will come in for ballot in about three years, by which time you will have taken your degree," the guardian told him. Pen longed for those three years to pass instantly, surveying the stucco halls, vast libraries, and drawing-rooms as if they were already his private domain. The Major chuckled softly at the pompous airs of the simple young fellow as he strutted out of the building. Later, the Major and Foker drove down in the latter's cab to Grey Friars to renew acquaintance with some old comrades. Boys swarmed around the cab near the gates, admiring the chestnut horse and the gravity of Stoopid, the tiger. As the bell for afternoon school rang, they swaggered about the playground chatting with old friends. Then the dreadful Doctor appeared, holding his grammar book. Foker slunk away uneasily at his presence, but Pen approached, blushing slightly, and shook the dignitary's hand. He laughed inwardly, remembering how well-remembered Latin Grammars had boxed his ears many times before. Generous, good-natured, and, in a word, perfectly conceited and satisfied with himself, Pen embodied the spirit of the season.

Next, they drove to the parental brewery. Foker's "Entire" consisted of an enormous pile of buildings near Grey Friars, with the name gilded on countless public-house signs tended by local vassals. The venerable junior partner and manager honored the young lord of the vats and his friend, serving them silver flagons of brown stout so strong that it seemed both the young men and Mr. Harry Foker's horse, Stoopid, were affected by its potency. Indeed, Stoopid rushed home to the west end at a frantic pace, endangering pie-stalls and pedestrians, causing the cab steps to collide with posts, and swinging wildly on his boards.

The Major was thoroughly pleased to see Pen with his young acquaintance, listened intently to Mr. Foker's artless stories, treated the two boys to a fine dinner at a Covent Garden coffee-house, and then proceeded to the theater. However, he was above all delighted when Mr. and Mrs. Agnes Foker, who happened to be in London, invited Major Pendennis and Mr. Arthur Pendennis to dinner at Grosvenor Street. "Having obtained entry into Lady Agnes Foker's house," he told Pen with an affectionate solemnity fitting the occasion, "it behooves you, my dear boy, to keep it. You must mind and never neglect to call there when you return to London. I recommend you to read carefully in Debrett the alliances and genealogy of the Earls of Rosherville, and if you can, make some trifling allusions to the family—something historical, neat, and complimentary—that you, with your poetic fancy, can execute well. Mr. Foker himself is a worthy man, though not of high extraction or extensive education. He always ensures some of the family porter is served round after dinner, which you will on no account refuse, and which I shall drink myself, though all beer disagrees with me confoundedly." And the heroic martyr did indeed sacrifice himself, as promised, on the day of the dinner, enduring the effects while old Mr. Foker, seated at the head of the table, delivered his usual joke about "Foker's Entire." I am sure we would all have enjoyed witnessing the Major's grin as the worthy old gentleman cracked his time-honored joke.

Lady Agnes, wrapped entirely in her devotion to Harry and considered the fondest of mothers (albeit one of the most good-natured but not the wisest), received her son's friend with great cordiality. She astonished Pen by detailing the rigorous course of studies her darling boy was pursuing, expressing fears that it might injure his delicate health. Mr. Foker the Elder burst into a horse-laugh at some of these remarks, and the heir winked knowingly at his friend. Lady Agnes then recounted her son's history from his earliest days, listing his miraculous survival of measles and whooping cough, his narrow escape from drowning, and the terrifying tyrannies practiced upon him at the horrid school where Mr. Foker had sent him because he, too, had been raised there. She vowed never to forgive that disagreeable doctor, prattling incessantly for an hour about her son. Finally, she voted the two Messieurs Pendennis to be most agreeable men.
