When pheasants arrived with the second course—which the Major correctly identified as coming from Logwood—her ladyship hoped they would both visit there at Christmas or during the holidays when dear Harry was home.

"My dear boy," Pen said to Arthur as they lit candles in Bury Street for bed, "you made that little allusion to Agincourt, where one of the Roshervilles distinguished himself, very neatly and well, although Lady Agnes did not quite grasp it: but it was exceedingly well for a beginner—though you ought not to blush so, by the way—and I beseech you, my dear Arthur, to remember through life that with an entrée—with a *good* entrée, mind—it is just as easy for you to have good society as bad, and that it costs a man, when properly introduced, no more trouble to maintain a footing in the best houses in London than to dine with a lawyer in Bedford Square. Bear this in mind when you are at Oxford, and for heaven's sake be very particular in the acquaintances you make. The premier step in life is the most important of all—did you write to your mother today? No? Well, do, before you go, and ask Mr. Foker for a frank—they like it.

Our friend Pen felt a distinct relief when his mentor finally departed, leaving the young gentleman on the second day after their arrival at Oxford. If Pen was relieved, the Major was undoubtedly ecstatic to have discharged his duty and secured a respite from it. For over three months, that martyred Major had devoted himself entirely to his nephew—a sacrifice so profound one wonders if any other man, save perhaps a woman accustomed to it, would endure. While a man might lay down his head or risk his honor, asking him to surrender his ease or his heart's desire is a trial few survive. Yet, the Major deserved praise for his conduct; he had earned his holiday. Foker and Pen saw him off in the coach, with the younger gentleman giving specific instructions to the driver to ensure the Major's comfort. The elder Pendennis delighted in watching his nephew accompanied by a young fellow who would introduce him to the University's finest circles. With the Major rushing off to London and subsequently to Cheltenham to descend upon local estates for hunting and company, the stage was set.

A quarter of the serial space has been dedicated to this single episode of Pen's career, a testament to the fact that his adventures cannot be chronicled with equal minutess. Unless a descendant of the chronicler takes up the pen at his death, the full scope of Pen's life remains untold. We shall not traverse his academic journey with such granularity. Alas, the lives of such boys do not lend themselves to total narration. As long as one's "honor" appears clear, the mind feels relatively secure. However, let us admit the candor that women are generally pure and unselfish, whereas men are rarely so. My list of "spotless" acquaintances is short; I struggle to find ten saints among them.

During Pen's first term, he attended classical and mathematical lectures with reasonable diligence. However, discovering quickly that he lacked the genius for exact sciences—and annoyed by being thoroughly beaten in the lecture-room by several vulgar youths wearing shoes so thick and coarse they required straps—he abandoned mathematics. He announced to his indulgent parent that he would henceforth devote himself exclusively to Greek and Roman literature. Mrs. Pendennis was wholly satisfied with this shift, urging only that he not ruin his health through over-study. She had heard harrowing tales of students consumed by brain-fever and perishing prematurely. Pen, though unaware of any latent disease, promised not to sit up reading late, adhering to this pledge with a tenacity that belied his usual remissness.

Soon, however, Pen realized he learned little of value in the classics. His fellow students were dull, and his tutor, Mr. Buck, possessed no more scholarly insight than a fifth-form boy at Grey Friars. Mr. Buck might have some humdrum notions about the meter of Aeschylus, but his understanding of poetry was as shallow as Mrs. Binge's. Pen grew weary of watching dull students and a mediocre tutor stumble through lines of plays he could read in a fraction of the time they spent. Private reading, he concluded, was the only truly profitable study. He informed his mother of this plan, intending to read significantly more privately and less publicly. The excellent woman, knowing no more of Homer than algebra, accepted his arrangements with contentment, confident he would merit his eventual position.

Pen did not return home until after Christmas, a slight disappointment to his mother and to Laura, who missed him for building snow fortifications. Instead, he was invited to Logwood, the residence of Lady Agnes Foker, where private theatricals and a gay Christmas party of "very fine folks" awaited. These were precisely the company the Major would want his nephew to avoid neglecting, though Pen stayed at home for the final three weeks of the vacation. Laura noted the quantity of fine new clothes he brought, and his mother admired his improved, manly appearance.

At Easter, he returned again, bringing even smarter attire: wonderful shooting jackets with remarkable buttons, and in the evenings, gorgeous velvet waistcoats paired with richly embroidered cravats. Peering into his room, his mother discovered a beautiful dressing-case with silver mountings, filled with lovely rings and jewelry. Most notably, he had acquired a new French watch and gold chain, replacing the big old chronometer inherited from John Pendennis—the watch by which the late doctor had felt countless pulses. Just prior to college, Helen had given him this timepiece, unwinding it from her trinket-box with a solemn speech honoring her husband's virtues and the proper use of time. Now, Pen declared the portly chronometer "out of date," comparing it disrespectfully to a warming-pan, and relegated it to a drawer alongside soiled primrose gloves and outdated cravats. He also swapped his old mare, Rebecca, for a more powerful steed, paying a heavy sum for the exchange, much to Laura's tears.

Furthermore, Pen returned with a large box of cigars bearing exotic names like *Colorados*, *Afrancesados*, and *Telescopios*. He began consuming them not only around the stables and greenhouses, where they proved beneficial for Helen's plants, but also in his study. Initially disapproving, his mother eventually relented. Pen claimed he was working on a prize poem and could not compose without tobacco, citing Lord Byron. When Helen entered the room one afternoon and found him engrossed in a recent novel—cultivating light literature for both his nation and others—she did not disturb him. Instead, retrieving a light-box and cigar-case from his adjoining bedroom, she gently placed a cigar in his mouth and lit the match. Pen laughed, kissing her hand as it hovered protectively over the back of the sofa. "Dear old mother," he remarked, "if I were to tell you to burn the house down, I think you would do it." Indeed, the foolish woman likely would have burned it down for him.

Beyond English "light literature," Pen imported boxes of French fiction. Helen, dipping into these volumes, read passages that opened her eyes with wonder, though Pen insisted it was his duty to keep up with contemporary authors like Paul de Kock, just as much as Swift or Molière. While Mrs. Pendennis sighed in perplexity and acquiesced, Miss Laura was strictly warned off the books by both her anxious mother and the rigorous moralist, Arthur Pendennis himself, who deemed such reading inappropriate for a young lady.

During this long vacation, Pen drained the bin of claret his father had stored, famously remarking that there was not a headache in a hogshead. Upon exhaustion, he wrote to "Messrs. Binney and Latham" of Mark Lane, following the advice of the late Doctor Portman, who had recommended a reputable London house over Oxbridge tradesmen for their deleterious and expensive liquor. Hinting that the university account should accompany the domestic bill, the poor widow was frightened by the amount. Pen laughed at her old-fashioned views, insisting the bill was moderate and that everyone drank claret and champagne now. The widow paid, dimly sensing her household expenses were swelling beyond her narrow income, though she consoled herself that these were only occasional expenditures. In the brief moments he was home, surely they ought to make him happy?

Arthur's allowances were remarkably liberal, far exceeding those of many wealthier peers. Years ago, the thrifty John Pendennis had established an "Education Fund," adding sums annually. Even after his death, the widow continued to augment it, so that by the time Pen entered Oxford, the fund was substantial. The Major's philosophy was clear: let the boy be liberally allowanced, enter the world as a gentleman, and take his place among men of rank. It would be his own duty to maintain it. To stint a boy or put him below his fellows' standard was bad policy; he would soon face the world and fight for himself. Thus, young Pen, the only son of an estate-gentleman with a good allowance and a gentlemanlike bearing, appeared far more consequential than he truly was.

To the authorities, tradesmen, and undergraduates, Pen was viewed as a "young buck" of the aristocracy. His manner was frank, brave, and perhaps slightly impertinent, fitting for a high-spirited youth. Generous and free-handed, he seemed plentiful in funds. He loved joviality, possessed a good singing voice, and while boat-racing had not yet reached its later fever pitch, riding and tandem-driving were his fashions. He rode well to hounds, appeared in pink, and managed to run up significant bills at Nile's livery-stable and other quarters. He was fond of books, particularly rare editions with beautiful bindings, filling his shelves with tall copies featuring gilding and marbling. His taste in art was keen, favoring Strangers and Rembrandt etchings over the tawdry racing prints that delighted his predecessor, Mr. Spicer. He was also partial to rings, jewelry, and fine raiment, dressing with such care for dining at friends' rooms as if courting a mistress. Rumors circulated that he wore rings over his kid gloves, a folly he stoutly denied, though youth perpetuates many such absurdities with admirable gravity.

In his second year, coinciding with Miss Fotheringay's triumph in London, Pen hung a print of her in his bedroom and confessed to his circle how "awfully, wildly, madly, passionately" he had loved her. Recalling this fatal period, his brow darkened, eyes rolled, and chest heaved with emotion as he described his woes. These verses were copied, sneered at, admired, and circulated from coterie to coterie. Few things elevate a lad in the estimation of his peers more than a reputation

Just prior to Mr. Foker's departure from Oxford, a gentleman arrived at Boniface College bearing the distinguished name of Horace Bloundell. Though he hailed from the ancient Suffolk lineage of Bloundell-Bloundell Hall—a title the undergraduates affectionately shortened to "Bloundell-Bloundellshire"—his entry into Oxford was far from smooth. Having previously clashed with the authorities at Cambridge, where he had been expelled, Bloundell had been rejected by St. George's and several other institutions. Yet, owing to his pedigree and the fact that Dr. Donne, the Master of Boniface, was also a Suffolk native and possibly a distant relative, Bloundell found a welcoming home at Boniface.

Behind this social maneuvering lay a pressing financial imperative: a family living was waiting for someone to claim it. Originally designated for Bloundell's third brother, the position fell vacant when that brother succumbed to illness while serving in a dragoon regiment. Seizing the moment, Bloundell abandoned his crimson pantaloons and sable shako for the sober black coat and white cravat of the clergyman. Although his initial plans at Cambridge had stumbled, the former soldier proved resilient, undeterred by failure, and determined to secure his clerical future elsewhere.

By Pen's second year, the atmosphere within the college had shifted. Major Pendennis visited his nephew briefly, introducing him to a motley assembly of university peers: the gentle Lord Plinlimmon, the open-hearted Magnus Charters, the sly Harland, the intrepid Ringwood—who earned the nickname "Rupert" due to the bravery displayed in his blunders—and Broadbent, a Bristol dissenter known as "Barebones" for his republican zeal. Finally, there was Mr. Bloundell, who had quickly ascended to the select circle of the university.

While the Major found delight in the company surrounding Pen, save for one individual who affected the airs of a worldly man, the morning following the party revealed a friction between uncle and nephew regarding these guests. Over breakfast, the Major recounted anecdotes with the gusto of a seasoned raconteur, receiving honest sympathy and boisterous laughter from the lads. However, Mr. Bloundell wore a mask of scorn, occasionally betraying familiarity with the stories. On one occasion, he even interrupted the Major to correct a detail, citing a version he claimed was authentic based on conversations at the club. The students marveled at this boldness, watching as the Major, with a melancholy grace, graciously accepted the correction. Subsequently, Bloundell openly disparaged "Old Pen," labeling him a notorious bore and a heavy eater at Gaunt House.

"The sympathies of men are usually reciprocal," the Major observed later, noting that if a friend accused someone of slandering him, it was a safe bet that the slanderer held a misliking for the accuser as well. "We cannot explain why, but generally, if we detest Dr. Fell, he detests us."

Regarding Pen's recent dining exploits, the Major praised the carving and the hospitality, noting Lord Plinlimmon's amiability and Lord Magnus's temporary republicanism. Yet, he drew a firm line at Mr. Bloundell. "That young man, Bloundell, strikes me as entirely ineligible. A man may possess a splendid coat of arms and descend from Richard the Lionheart's Blondel, yet remain a tiger, my boy. I suspect he deserted his regiment in bad odor. Look at the jewelry; 'Scamp' is written plainly across his countenance."

"Oh, bless my soul, sir!" Pen laughed. "Bloundell is the most popular fellow in the university. We elected him to the Barmecides immediately! He comes from an excellent family, the Suffolk Bloundells."

"A good pedigree does not inoculate against vice," the Major countered, chipping his egg. "Avoid him. By the way, your dinner was exquisite, though perhaps slightly too lavish. You need not worry about the extra costs; you have a sufficient income."

Poor Pen, however, remained oblivious to the warning. The Major scarcely realized how frequently these extravagant meals occurred, fueled by Pen's desire to project an air of sophistication. To the young mind, a deep knowledge of wines and cuisine signaled a mature, worldly gentleman. Pen, aspiring to be the "Admirable Crichton" of dining, wasted little effort learning the arts of gastronomy, trusting blindly in the college cook's judgment. Soon, his reputation as a gourmet vanished, replaced by the sight of creditors gathering outside his door, and the unfortunate cook falling victim to Pen's blind trust.

Despite the Major's counsel, Pen could not resist Bloundell's allure. Young men are naturally drawn to those who seem to possess a "knowledge of life"—a rogue or a lover of adventure who has tales to tell. It is a dangerous trait, yet it persists. Even today, countless youths admit to reading *Don Juan*, unaware of the corruption it seeds. Bloundell alone was sufficient to lower the moral tone of the college, rendering the students unruly and ungenteel. While the dignitaries of St. George's remained unmoved by Bloundell's flashy charms, others like Broadbent dubbed him "Captain Macheath," predicting a hanging. Even the cautious Foker, speaking with characteristic ambiguity, advised Pen to avoid betting against Bloundell at cards, hinting that the Captain played with a reckless hand and limited funds.

Pen, driven by an insatiable appetite for pleasure, embraced the lifestyle wholeheartedly. He championed the maxim of "seeing life," quoting Terence and Shakespeare to justify his hedonism. He seemed poised to become a complete *roué* if the pace continued.

One evening, after a supper party where a game of *vingt-et-un* had been played, the group departed. As they removed their caps, Bloundell, displaying a practiced hand, produced a pair of dice from his waistcoat pocket. He placed them into a green wine glass, gave the glass a theatrical swirl, and rolled the ivory cubes onto the table, calling "seven" the main. He repeated the ritual, mesmerizing the onlookers. Pen, who had only ever played backgammon at home, watched with wide eyes.

Bloundell, possessing a robust voice, began to sing the chorus from *Robert the Devil*. Pen, buoyed by his recent winnings, joined in enthusiastically. Before long, the entire party sat around the table engaged in the game of hazard. The green glass circulated, and eventually, Pen shattered it after throwing six mains.

From that night forward, Pen plunged into the delights of gaming with feverish intensity. Bloundell would visit Pen's rooms post-breakfast, and time seemed to dissolve as the dice rattled. They hosted clandestine parties behind closed doors, employing a felt-lined box designed to silence the telltale roll of the bones, shielding them from the sharp ears of the tutors. On one occasion, they narrowly escaped detection by Mr. Buck, who, overhearing "Two to one on the caster" through an open window, burst in to find them engrossed in Homer. Pen, maintaining a grave demeanor, explained he was coaching his friends on the state of the River Scamander, asking gravely if it was navigable.

Yet, the lessons extended beyond gambling. Captain Macheath, a master of fabrication, spun tales of garrison conquests and Parisian extravaganzas that captivated Pen. He boasted of waking in his chambers to find twelve thousand francs under his pillow and a boot stuffed with Napoleons. Pen, entranced, believed these fantasies were facts, sighing that such wealth would solve all his troubles.

Furthermore, Macheath dismantled Pen's romantic illusions regarding Miss Fotheringay. When Pen confessed his devotion, Macheath scoffed, revealing that Miss Fotheringay was a notorious actress of questionable character, surrounded by admirers who spent lavishly on her. "Marry her?" Macheath sneered, comparing her favorably to the most infamous stage divas. "She hasn't a shred of character." Pen, a man of candid disposition who believed everything he was told, was devastated. How could his pure flame be directed toward an impostor? He found it impossible to doubt the stories of a man who spoke so poorly of women.

During an Easter vacation, Pen and Bloundell traveled to London, staying at a hotel in Covent Garden where Bloundell maintained a "tick." They frequented a military club, where Pen mingled with gallant youths sporting spurs and mustaches, drinking pale ale and beating the town by night. It was here, amidst the roar of theaters and singing houses, that Pen encountered the Major inadvertently. Sitting in the boxes at the Museum Theatre, Pen watched the performance of *The Fotheringay*, which was currently triumphant in London.

Though he had not seen her since their separation at Chatteris, Pen recognized the actress instantly. A sudden, feeblish thrill ran through him as the applause thundered and Ophelia entered with her familiar curtsey. For a moment, jealousy flared; he felt as though the entire house scrutinized him. He fled to the stage door, hoping to catch a glimpse, but she slipped past unnoticed. Returning the next night, he stayed quietly for the afterpiece, and on the final night, he attended *Don Giovanni* alongside the celebrated dancer Taglioni.

By the end of the trip, the illusion had evaporated. She was beautiful, but the light in her eyes was extinguished, and her voice lacked the magic that once thrilled his bosom. The accent sounded coarse and false, a mere mechanical imitation rather than

To any soul versed in the arcane arts of Heraldry and the Peerage, it is common knowledge that the noble house to which Lady Helen Pendennis belongs displays, upon their crest, a nest teeming with tiny pelicans, diligently pecking at the bleeding bosom of a larger, maternal bird. By the legends of heraldry, this great mother feeds her brood upon the blood she willingly sheds. Now, surely, female pelicans enjoy such self-mutilation beneath the greedy beaks of their offspring? It is a certainty that human women do likewise. There exists some peculiar delight, unknown to the masculine sex, that accompanies the agony of being scarified; indeed, I suspect some ladies would endure actual torture rather than deny it. They derive a strange joy from sacrificing themselves for the objects their instincts dictate they love. Whether it be a reckless husband, a profligate son, or a darling rogue of a brother, their hearts are instantly opened to pour out their finest treasures for the sake of the cherished individual. And what a feast of such sacrifice we men on the other side are prepared to serve these gentle creatures! Scarce a man reading this has not, at some point, administered such pleasure to his womankind, treating them to the luxury of forgiveness. They care naught for their own living standards; but when the prodigal returns, they hold a jubilee, slaughter the fattened calf, and prepare their festival. At the mere whisper of the sinner's return, the kind angels set the table, and Mercy and Forgiveness smile out to greet him. May it ever thus remain for all of us; if Justice is all we have to look to, Heaven help us!

In the latter days of Pen's tenure at Oxford, his Uncle Major's partiality for the lad had swelled to intoxicating proportions. The Major, a proud bachelor of the old school, adored Arthur for his high spirits, frank demeanor, and genuine gentility. It delighted the old London lion to see Pen strolling with the young patricians of the university. Though his stinginess was a byword among the club wags—who envied his endless engagements yet refused to acknowledge his poverty—he was charmed to treat his nephew and the young lords to snug dinners at his lodgings, regaling them with choice claret and his finest *bons mots*. Some of his tales were so delicate that repetition would injure them, while others were so crude that repeating them would benefit no one. Yet he poured them out, paying court to their parents through the boys and courting himself through their company. He visited Oxford frequently, where the young fellows amused themselves by entertaining the elderly gentleman, throwing parties and breakfasts to both joke him and honor him. He plied them with stories, becoming juvenile and hilarious in their presence. He attended Pen at a grand debate at the Union, crowing and cheering, tapping his stick in rhythm with the students, and marveling at the boy's fiery eloquence. He believed he had secured a young Pitt for a nephew. His affection bordering on the paternal, he wrote letters brimming with playful advice and town gossip. He boasted at his clubs, introducing Arthur with great pleasure, claiming, "Egad, the young fellows are putting the old ones to the wall!" He spoke of Young Lord Plinlimmon, a friend of his boy, and Young Lord Magnus Charters, a chum of his scapegrace, predicting they would eclipse their fathers' achievements. He begged permission to bring Arthur to a grand fête at Gaunt House, watching with ineffable satisfaction as he danced with the sisters of those young noblemen. He labored mightily to secure invitations for the lad, acting as if he were a mother seeking a marriage match for a daughter, rather than a half-pay officer in a wig. Everywhere he boasted of Pen's towering talents and oratorical prowess, writing repeatedly to Helen to confirm that Lord Runnymede would appoint him to an embassy or the Duke would seat him in Parliament. And Helen, for her part, was too eager to believe any tale spoken in favor of her son.

Yet, all this pride and affection from uncle and mother had been trampled into dust by Pen's wicked extravagance and idleness! One cannot envy Pen's feelings as he contemplated his actions. He had slept, and the tortoise had won the race. He had ruined at the start what might have been a brilliant career. He had dipped ungenerously into a generous mother's purse, basely and recklessly spilling her small cruse. Oh, it was a coward's hand that could strike and rob a creature so tender! If Pen felt the wrong he had done others, surely a vain young gentleman felt the shame he had brought upon himself even more keenly. Let us be assured that no remorse is more cruel, and no groans more piteous than those of wounded self-love. Like Joel Miller's friend, the Senior Wrangler, who bowed to the audience from his box merely because he and the King entered the theater simultaneously—a fatuity by no means agreeable to himself—poor Arthur Pendennis was convinced that all England would remark the absence of his name from the examination lists and gossip endlessly about his misfortune. His wounded tutor, the many debt collectors ("duns"), the skip and bed-maker who served him, the undergraduates of his own time and those below whom he had patronized or scorned—how could he bear to look any of them in the face? He fled to his rooms, locking himself inside, and there penned a letter to his tutor, overflowing with thanks, regards, remorse, and despair. He requested his name be struck from the college books and intimated a wish that death would swiftly end the woes of the disgraced Arthur Pendennis.

Then he slipped out, scarcely knowing where he was going, mechanically taking the unfrequented lanes behind the colleges until he cleared the university precincts and reached the banks of the Cam, now deserted but once alive with boat races and cheering gownsmen. He wandered on until he found himself miles from Oxford, or rather, was discovered by acquaintances leaving the city. As Pen climbed a hill, a drizzling January rain battering his face, his ragged gown fluttering behind him—he had not removed his academic garb since morning—a post-chaise rattled up the road. A servant sat on the box, while within, or rather half-out of the window, sat a young gentleman smoking a cigar and loudly encouraging the postboy. It was our young acquaintance from Baymouth, Mr. Spavin, who had earned his degree and was driving home in triumph in his yellow post-chaise. He spotted the figure, gesticulating madly as it struggled up the hill, and beheld poor Pen's pale, ghastly face as the chaise whirled past.

"Wo!" roared Mr. Spavin to the postboy. The horses halted in their frantic career, and the carriage drew up some fifty yards before Pen. Presently, he heard his own name shouted and saw the upper half of Mr. Spavin's body thrust out of the side window, beckoning Pen vehemently.

Pen stopped, hesitated, nodded his head fiercely, and pointed onward as if wishing the postilion to depart. He said nothing, but his countenance must have looked terribly desperate. Young Spavin, staring with an expression of blank alarm, jumped out, ran toward Pen, and grasping his hand, exclaimed, "I say—hullo, old boy, where are you going, and what's the row now?"

"I'm going where I deserve to go," said Pen, with an imprecation.

"This ain't the way," smiled Mr. Spavin. "This is the Fenbury road. I say, Pen, don't take on because you're plucked. It's nothing when you're used to it. I've been plucked three times, old boy—and after the first time I didn't care. Glad it's over, though. You'll have better luck next time."

Pen looked at his early acquaintance—who had been plucked, rusticated, and who, despite repeated failures and learning to read and write correctly only late in life, had attained the honor of a degree. "This man has passed," he thought, "and I have failed!" It was almost too much to bear.

"Good-bye, Spavin," said he. "I'm very glad you are through. Don't let me keep you; I'm in a hurry—I'm going to town tonight."

"Gammon," said Mr. Spavin. "This ain't the way to town; this is the Fenbury road, I tell you."

"I was just going to turn back," Pen said.

"All the coaches are full with the men going down," Spavin noted. Pen winced. "You'd not get a place for a ten-pound note. Get into my yellow; I'll drop you at Mudford, where you have a chance of the Fenbury mail. I'll lend you a hat and a coat; I've got lots. Come along; jump in, old boy—go it, leathers!" Thus, Pen found himself in Mr. Spavin's post-chaise, riding with that gentleman as far as the Ram Inn at Mudford, fifteen miles from Oxford, where the Fenbury mail changed horses, and where Pen finally secured a seat to London.

The next day, immense excitement swept through Boniface College, Oxford. For some time, a rumor had terrified Pen's tutor and tradesmen: Pendennis, maddened by the loss of his degree, had made away with himself. A battered cap, in which his name was almost discernible, together with a seal bearing his crest of an eagle looking at a now-extinct sun, had been found three miles on

The Major's epistle, a document of dire import, acted as a lightning rod for Doctor Portman. With the alacrity of a man rushing to administer a painful but necessary extraction, he hastened to Fairoaks. He viewed the situation not with malice, but with the grim efficiency of a dentist pulling a bad tooth; the wisdom required was simply to get it out, and quickly. While he lamented the youth's descent into perdition, he maintained a professional demeanor, comparing his own vigorous application of the forceps to a necessary duty. After reading the letter aloud to Myra and Mrs. Portman, accompanied by a torrent of damnable commentary regarding the "young scapegrace" spiraling further into sin, the Doctor departed. The ladies, armed with their usual precision and speed, immediately disseminated the intelligence through the Clavering society before striding purposefully toward Fairoaks to deliver the final blow to the widow.

However, Helen Pendennis had already received the news via Pen's own correspondence, and strangely, it brought relief rather than devastation. A heavy, gloomy presentiment had hung over her for months, whispering of inevitable ruin. Now, knowing the worst, she felt a strange sense of closure. Her darling boy had returned, repentant and tender-hearted. Could she desire more? When the Rector attempted to lecture her, his words grounded in common sense yet bolstered by ancient tradition, failed to stir any true indignation within her heart. She harbored no anger, save for a singular sorrow that *he* might be unhappy. To her mind, the academic degree was a meaningless fuss; why did the Rector and the Doctor insist on shipping the boy to a den of temptation with so little hope of redemption? Why not keep him at home, safe with his mother? Regarding his debts, she dismissed them as trivialities—"his debts!" she cried internally. Wasn't his father's money essentially his own? Didn't he possess the inherent right to spend it? Thus, the widow confronted the virtuous Doctor, and remarkably, none of his righteous arrows pierced her gentle bosom.

Meanwhile, a peculiar shift in domestic dynamics had occurred between Pen and his younger sister, Laura. For years, following a childish custom of brotherly affection, Pen had greeted Laura with a familiar kiss. Upon returning from college after months away, however, he found not the simple girl he remembered, but a tall, slim, and striking young lady. The old ritual was impossible; he could not proffer the customary kiss, and she received him with a gracious curtsy, a proffered hand, and a flush that climbed from her neck to her cheek, precisely where Pen had once saluted her.

The narrator, admitting a lack of skill—and perhaps inclination—to describe female beauty, insists that virtue outweighs fleeting charms. Yet, Miss Laura Bell, at sixteen, possessed a distinct presence. Standing five feet four inches, she was deemed "gawky" or a "Maypole" by smaller peers, though adorned with beautiful roses around her head. Pale usually, with a faint rose tint, her cheeks bloomed violently into deep crimson when emotion demanded, leaving lingering stains long after the feeling had passed. Her eyes, large since childhood, shone with an innate brilliance that defied description, requiring two pairs of veils in the form of the finest black lashes to mitigate their glare. Critics claimed she "ogled" everyone, but the truth lay in Nature's design; they simply shone. Her complexion rivaled Lady Mantrap's, unassisted by powder. Her nose remained to the reader's imagination, but her smile revealed pearly teeth, and her voice was a low, sweet music. Despite rumors of large feet due to her long dresses, her overall grace suggested a harmony of proportion.

Though outwardly sweet, Laura's internal compass had shifted. Having matured significantly in mind and person over the last two years, she was only half-pleased with Pen's recent conduct. His letters to home had grown sparse and brief. When Helen pleaded for constancy, citing his studies and engagements, Laura countered sharply. "It is better that he loses a prize than forget his mother," she argued, insisting he stay home rather than vacation at his friends' grand estates where no one loved him as deeply as she did. "As I do?" Helen sighed, feigning offense. Laura, however, stood firm, denying any personal jealousy, yet refusing to accept Helen's defenses. She insisted that Pen must forge his own path, cultivate influential acquaintances, and navigate the complex webs of male ambition that women could not comprehend. Whether Helen truly believed these excuses or merely endured them for Pen's sake, the result was a comforting maternal delusion: the certainty that a woman's love, once given, remains an untiring fortress of forgiveness.

Furthermore, Laura found Pen's recent behavior shocking. He spoke lightly and laxly of women, neglecting the courtesies expected of a gentleman. Smoking his horrid pipes indoors, refusing to accompany his mother to church or social calls, and lounging in dressing-gowns yawning over novels while she returned from her duties, he was a stranger to the hero of her infancy. The boy she had spent countless nights praising for his virtues, love, and bravery was replaced by a bold, brilliant, and sarcastic young man who seemingly scorned the simple devotions and pleasures of the women around him.

The catalyst for her outrage, however, was the Fotheringay affair. Learning of it first through the Major's sarcasm and then from neighbors at Clavering, Laura was thunderstruck. A Pendennis throwing himself away on an actress! Helen's boy galloping daily to kneel before a stage performer and drink with her infamous father! "I would have run away," Laura declared passionately, "if I had to walk barefoot through the snow." When Helen asked if she would abandon her mother, Laura immediately retracted her statement, and the two women embraced in a display of warmth typical of their natures.

Yet, where did Laura's indignation stem from? Was it ignorance of the reciprocal nature of human weakness, or misinformation from the bitter Claverings and the vengeful Mrs. Portman? Or perhaps, as often alleged, a hidden jealousy? Regardless of the cause, her fury toward Pen did not extend to Helen. Instead, she devoted herself to the widow with a fierce, girlish affection—a passion bordering on folly. She showered Helen with caresses and tender epithets, a scene too intimate for sober historians to recount with propriety.

But when Laura learned of Pen's actual misery and shame, her wrath evaporated instantly, replaced by a tender, unreasonable compassion. He was the Pen of old days again—the frank, generous, and tender-hearted boy. She sided immediately with Helen against the Doctor, dismissing the "enormity" of Pen's transgressions. Debts? They were trifles; he had been thrust into expensive society by his uncle's orders and was forced to emulate his companions. Failed to obtain his degree? The poor boy was ill during the exams, distracted by financial woes, perhaps bullied by jealous tutors. Others, she reasoned, must have been cruel and unfair. With flushing cheeks and eyes bright with righteous anger, she stood before the Doctor, seizing Helen's hand and kissing it openly, daring him to speak a word against her "darling mother's Pen."

Discomfited and amazed by the obstinacy of the women, the Doctor eventually departed. Laura renewed her embraces and arguments with tenfold fervor. Helen, sensing the cogency of her daughter's points, suspected foul play among the examiners. Together, the announcement of misfortune vexed them very little. Pen, plunged in shame and grief in London, tormented by remorse for his mother's sorrow, would have marveled at how lightly they bore the calamity. Calamity, after all, is welcome to a woman if it promises to bring a truant lover home. If one reduces their mistress to a crust, they will happily share it, provided the partner eats the remainder in their company.

Once the Doctor left, Laura ordered fires lit in Mr. Arthur's rooms and his bedding aired. By the time Helen finished a tender, affectionate letter to Pen, Laura had prepared the apartments. Smiling fondly, she led her mother into the warm room where fires blazed, and the two sat on the bed, talking endlessly of Pen. Laura added a postscript to Helen's letter, addressing him as "my dearest Pen," urging him to come home instantly with two emphatic dashes under the command, promising happiness with his mother and affectionate sister.

Late that night, after reading Bibles and casting a glance into Pen's room, Laura, whose head occasionally occupied the pillow once pressed by the late Pendennis, suddenly cried, "Mamma, are you awake?" Helen stirred, confirming she had not slept a wink, her gaze fixed on the night-lamp in the chimney, her thoughts consumed by Pen for hours. Laura, similarly awake and plotting, revealed a notable scheme born in her busy brain. She proposed that, having lived with Helen for ten years without touching her purse, she owed the widow a sum equal to ten years' schooling costs—five hundred pounds. They would visit the banker, Mr. Rowdy, retrieve the funds, secure two additional sums to repay, and send everything to Pen. This would clear his debts without harming anyone, allowing them to live happily ever after. Helen responded with a jumble of incoherent ejaculations and embraces, and the two women slept soundly until dawn.

When the sun rose gloriously over the purple hills, Helen woke to find Laura sleeping beside her, her face sweet, her bosom heaving gently as if happy dreams swept over it. Helen felt a

The residents of Fairoaks drifted through their daily routine with a heavy, sleepy lethargy, content to let the clock tick away the hours. Meanwhile, atop the hill on the opposite bank of the River Brawl, the great house was violently shaking off a century-and-a-half-long slumber. It seemed to be stretching and yawning with renewed vigor, signaling that something extraordinary was about to wake within its walls.

Just as Arthur Pendennis was so deeply mired in his own recent misfortune that he barely noticed a sparrow falling from a tree, a rumor exploded through the local press like a cannon shell. The word spread faster than gossip at a village fair: Clavering Park was going to be inhabited again. From the bustling markets of Clavering to the quiet greens around Gooseberry Green, the news traveled on carriages and bicycles, in the conversations of vicars and the whispers of schoolboys. Everyone from the squire to the poorest laborer knew the same tale: the great, moss-covered mansion was returning to life.

Five years prior, the county papers had announced the marriage at Florence of Francis Clavering, the only son of Sir Francis Clavering, to Jemima Augusta, a wealthy widow from Calcutta. Back then, the locals believed the scandalous story that a ruined baronet had somehow wed a rich Indian widow to save his skin. Rumours followed the couple like ghosts. They were seen in Italy, living luxuriously in Florence, then wandering to Germany and Switzerland, playing games and drinking champagne wherever money could buy them a bed. Their final stop before returning home was Paris, where they lived in dizzying splendor after the death of the widow's father, leaving behind a fortune that reportedly included twenty thousand pounds a year.

However, the background of Sir Francis Clavering was far from respectable. Born to an outlaw father in a gloomy chateau near Bruges, he had attempted a career in the dragoons but failed spectacularly. Ruined by gambling debts, he spent time in the Fleet Prison and eventually fled to Belgium to join his father, an exiled gout-ridden aristocrat. For years, he was a notorious figure in continental watering holes, lurking in billiard rooms and riding steeplechases on borrowed horses. It was in a boarding house at Lausanne that he executed his "lucky coup"—marrying the widow Amory. Following her father's death, she inherited a massive sum, which she promptly poured into buying a title for herself and securing her future.

Yet, before her return to England, Lady Clavering's reputation was already tarnished. She was viewed with suspicion by the proper English abroad; her manners were considered rough, and her origins were murky. Her father had been a disreputable lawyer involved in indigo smuggling, and her first husband, Amory, had been merely the mate of an Indiaman. Even the stern Old Sir Jasper Rogers, former Chief Justice of Calcutta, had hinted at a "queer story" about Lady Clavering's past, though he refused to share the details.

Despite this shadowy past, Lady Clavering managed to dazzle Parisian society in the winter of 183—. With an annual income rumored to be twenty thousand pounds, she swept through the Faubourg St. Germain. Even the most austere figures, like Lady Elderbury and the Countess of Southdown, found themselves drawn to her generosity. She funded schools, supported the Armenian Patriarch, and donated to missionary causes ranging from Father Barbarossa to the Baptists of Quashyboo. Her purse was open for everyone, from the persecuted Jesuits to the Reverend J. Ramshorn, who needed funds to convert the Pope.

By 1837, arrangements were made with her creditors, and Sir Francis returned to England without fear of arrest. He arrived at Clavering House accompanied by his lawyer friend, Mr. Tatham, and a companion named Captain Strong. The housekeeper, Mrs. Blenkinsop, greeted them with trembling emotions, expecting to see her old master. However, the tall, mustachioed gentleman in furs burst into laughter. "You've saddled the wrong horse!" he proclaimed, revealing that he was not Sir Francis, but his friend, Captain Strong. The real Sir Francis, a pale and languid man, stepped out weakly from the carriage.

Mrs. Blenkinsop, flustered, apologized, but Sir Francis reassured her kindly. He and Captain Strong toured the vast, gloomy mansion, marveling at its size. While Sir Francis remained silent and resigned, treating the house like a dusty museum, Captain Strong was energized. He saw potential everywhere, envisioning theaters in the libraries and grand balls in the saloons. He dismissed the idea of restoring the gardens, focusing instead on the interior. "Leave it to me," he declared, outlining plans to furnish the rooms with tapestries, armor, and exotic furniture. He insisted that Sir Francis take the north wing to escape the noise of Miss Amory's piano, joking that the piano player was "screeching from morning till night."

As they explored, Captain Strong revealed his true nature. Unlike the reserved Sir Francis, Strong was a man of boundless energy and charm. He had served in the Hungarian Uhlans, survived the Battle of Missolonghi, and fought in Spain. He spoke fluently in multiple languages and possessed a collection of medals, including the Cross of the Redeemer and the White Eagle of Poland. He was the life of the party, telling wild tales of adventure and danger to anyone who would listen.

Back at Fairoaks, the news of the Claverings' return sent waves of excitement. Laura Pendennis eagerly discussed the prospect of meeting Miss Amory, describing her as a "muse" and a "femme incomprise." She painted a picture of a mysterious, artistic beauty who could paint, write poetry, compose music, and ride like Diana Vernon. Arthur Pendennis, initially skeptical, found himself intrigued by the tales of the "beau cousin." Meanwhile, Captain Strong became a fixture at Fairoaks, regaling the ladies with stories of Greek captives and Spanish nuns. His presence transformed the somber house into a hub of activity.

Soon, preparations for the Claverings' arrival were underway. Furniture vans rolled in from London and Chatteris, loaded with goods overseen by Captain Strong. New staff arrived, including two melancholic footmen, Messieurs Frederic and James, and a flamboyant French chef, Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant. Mirobolant, formerly the Chef of the Duc de Borodino, treated the kitchen with theatrical flair, composing menus in his dressing gown and playing the piano before meals. Under Strong's direction, the house was transformed, ready to welcome its new inhabitants.

On the eventful day, a grand procession arrived. An open carriage and a traveling chariot, drawn by foaming horses, thundered up the driveway. Inside were Sir Francis, looking solemn and unmoved; Lady Clavering, radiant in bright black eyes and a good-humored countenance; their young son, Master Francis; and Miss Amory, who held Captain Strong's arm. The house buzzed with anticipation as the family entered, greeted by the crackling fires and the bowing servants.

The following Sunday, the Claverings took their place in the ancient pew of Clavering Church. The congregation gathered in droves to catch a glimpse of the newcomers. Lady Clavering stood out in her finery, adorned with shawls, jewels, and ribbons, while Miss Amory appeared modest in dove-colored dress. Young Master Francis, unused to church decorum, caused a scene by yelling and running around the pew, eventually being carried out by a footman. Despite the chaos, the atmosphere was one of delight and curiosity.

Afterward, the bond between the Pendennises and the Claverings deepened. Miss Amory, whose real name was Blanche, formed an instant connection with Laura. They shared secrets, music, and poetry in Blanche's charming room, filled with books and flowers. Blanche, who claimed to have "suffered" greatly despite her privileged upbringing, resonated with Laura's gentle spirit. She showed Laura her drawings and played her compositions, creating a friendship that blossomed overnight.

Arthur Pendennis also found himself drawn to the circle. He discovered a mutual interest in fishing along the River Brawl, where he frequently encountered Miss Amory. Their encounters sparked a playful rivalry, with Arthur teasingly wondering if Miss Blanche was the "pretty little fish" he was trying to catch. He grew fond of the healthful exercise and the company, finding himself laughing and chatting more than usual.

The evenings at Clavering Park became a highlight for the Pendennises. Music filled the air, with Laura singing alongside Blanche and Captain Strong providing a booming bass accompaniment. Sir Francis, though often absent-minded, enjoyed the games and card-playing, while Strong proved to be a loyal and generous friend. Yet, beneath the surface of the lively gatherings, there were moments of introspection. Helen Pendennis, watching her friends enjoy their newfound happiness, reflected on her own lost loves and the passage of time. As the moon rose over the river, casting a silvery glow on the water, she felt a profound sense of loss, remembering the last look of her deceased lover.

Despite the sadness lingering in Helen's heart, the new era at Clavering Park brought joy and camaraderie to the Pendennis family. The strangers had become friends, the old house had been reborn, and the river flowed on, carrying the stories of the past and the hopes of the future.

A Little Innocent

There is a universal law of domestic architecture: every house possesses a hidden beam, a splinter, or a skeleton in the closet. For the fortunate, this discovery offers a comforting sort of vicarious misery; it allows them to sigh that their neighbors, despite their wealth, harbor their own private storms. Into this category fell the "Little Innocent" Muse of Blanche. To the untrained eye, she was a radiant sprite, singing sweetly and speaking softly, seemingly capable of turning dust into gold wherever she tread. Yet, to the inhabitants of Clavering House, she was the Nemesis, the shadow, the walking disaster. Consider how a single pebble in a boot can ruin a journey; so does a single irritant in a household destroy the peace of all within it. Who could have imagined that a smiling fairy like Blanche Amory was the architect of such discord?

One afternoon, following dinner, the Baronet and Captain Strong sat over the billiard table, cigars clutched between fingers like weapons of mass destruction. The Baronet, a man of blunt sentiments, broke the silence with a startling confession. "Strong," he said, puffing smoke, "I wish to the doose your wife was dead."

Captain Strong, a man of infinite patience, chuckled. "So do I. It's a mighty cannon, by Jove. But she won't die; she'll live forever—you see if she doesn't. Pray tell me, Frank, why do you wish her off the hooks?"

"Because then you might marry Missy!" the Baronet drawled, his tone dripping with malice. "She isn't bad-looking. She'll have ten thousand pounds, and that is a godsend for a poor old devil like you."

"And gad, Strong," the Baronet continued, his face contorting, "I hate her worse and worse every day. I can't stand her, I truly can't."

"I wouldn't take her at twice the price," Captain Strong laughed, wiping his cigar ash onto the floor. "I've never seen such a little devil."

"I should like to poison her," the Baronet declared solemnly. "By Jove, I should."

"Why, what mischief has she been up to now?" Strong inquired.

"Nothing particular," the Baronet replied, grimacing. "Only her old tricks. That girl has a knack of making everyone miserable; it's quite astonishing. Last night, she sent the governess sobbing away from the dinner table. Later, as I passed Frank's room, I heard the poor boy howling in the dark. His sister had terrified his soul out of his body by telling him tales of a ghost haunting the house. At lunch, she turned my lady inside out. Though my wife is something of a fool, she is a good soul—I'm hanged if she isn't."

"What exactly did Missy do to her?" Strong asked, amused.

"She began talking about the late Amory, my predecessor!" the Baronet grinned. "She produced some picture from *The Keepsake* and swore it looked like her dear father. She demanded to know where his grave was. Hang her father! Whenever Miss Amory mentions him, Lady Clavering bursts into tears. And the little devil does it on purpose, just to spite her mother."

He paused, recounting a further indignity. "Today, when she started again, I flew into a confounded rage. I told her I was her father, and then... and then, sir, she actually threw a shy at me!"

"And what did she say about you, Frank?" Strong queried, still chuckling.

"Gad!" the Baronet roared. "She said I wasn't her father, that I wasn't fit to comprehend her, that her father must have been a man of genius and fine feelings, whereas I had married her mother purely for money."

"Well, didn't you?" Strong pressed gently.

"It don't make it any the pleasanter to hear because it's true, you know," Sir Francis Clavering admitted, looking wounded. "I'm not a literary man, but I'm not such a fool as she paints me. I don't know how she does it, but she always manages to put me in the hole. She turns the whole house around her with her quiet ways and her confounded sentimental airs. I wish she was dead, Ned."

"It was my wife you wanted dead just now," Strong pointed out, maintaining perfect good humor.

Upon hearing this, the Baronet's candour returned instantly. "Well, when people bore my life out, I do wish they were dead. And I wish Missy were down a well, with all my heart."

From this candid exchange, it becomes evident that our accomplished little friend possessed peculiarities rendering her unpopular. She was a young lady of genuine genius, exquisite sympathies, and considerable literary attainments, living, like many another genius, with relatives who could not comprehend her. Her mother and stepfather were not men of letters; the Baronet read *Bell's Life* and the *Racing Calendar*, while Lady Clavering still wrote like a schoolgirl of thirteen, with an extraordinary disregard for grammar. Sensing her intellectual superiority, Miss Amory lost no opportunity to broadcast the inferiority of her circle. She was a martyr, yet she ensured everyone knew she was suffering. If she wept, as she believed she must, were we to wonder? Without sympathy, life is nothing. Would it not be a lack of candour to feign cheerfulness when one feels so deeply misunderstood? If a poetess may not bemoan her lot, of what earthly use is her lyre? Blanche struck hers only to the saddest tunes, singing elegies over dead hopes and dirges for nipped buds of affection.

Her actual distresses were not severe, but her griefs lay in her own soul. Like most, she was sad and habitually dissatisfied. *Mes larmes* trickled from her eyes at command; she possessed an unlimited supply of tears, a faculty honed by practice. Sentiment, like the disease Horace mentioned, increases through indulgence; the more one cries, the more one desires to do so.

Missy had begun to gush at a very early age. Lamartine was her favorite bard from the moment she could feel. She subsequently improved her mind by devouring the novels of Balzac and George Sand. By sixteen, she had read every romance of the French masters. Though she sympathized little with her kin, she claimed friends in the spirit-world: the tender Indiana, the passionate Lelia, the amiable Trenmor, the high-souled convict Stenio, and countless other heroes. She had fallen in love with Prince Rodolph and Prince Djalma while still at school, settling questions of divorce and the rights of woman with Indiana before leaving off pinafores. The impetuous little lady played at love with these imaginary worthies as she once played at maternity with her doll.

Novelists, supposedly knowing the secrets of female hearts, reveal that at eleven, Mademoiselle Betsi (as Miss Amory was then called) felt tender emotions for a Savoyard organ-grinder she believed to be a prince. At twelve, an old, hideous drawing-master agitated her heart. At thirteen, attending Madame de Caramel's boarding-school, she engaged in a correspondence by letter with two young gentlemen of the College of Charlemagne.

Note that Miss Amory, known as Missy at home, had originally been christened Betsy. She assumed the name Blanche of her own fantasy, crowning herself with it. The weapon her stepfather held over her was the threat to call her publicly "Betsy," a menace that occasionally kept the young rebel in order.

Regarding dolls, Miss Blanche displayed a similar girlish inconstancy. She had a host of "darlings" and a treasure-chest full of locks of hair gathered during her sentimental progress. Some friends married, others moved schools, and one beloved sister she had lost, only to find later working the books in a grocery shop in the Rue du Bac. She viewed these estrangements and disillusionments as the penalties of genius. Meanwhile, she managed to make her mother uncomfortable and caused her stepfather to wish she were dead. Only Captain Strong, with his invincible good humour, proved immune to her sarcasms. The little lady ruled the house with her tongue, correcting her mother's speech errors and terrifying the good soul into greater nervousness.

The arrival of the family at Clavering Park had inspired great curiosity in the town. While many watched, Madame Fribsby, the local milliner, remained unmoved until the Park ladies appeared in church. She noted every article of toilette, from bonnets to brodequins, taking a survey of the maids' attire as well. Despite the sermon of Dr. Portman, Madame Fribsby was undisturbed. Within days, she secured an interview with Lady Clavering's confidential attendant, presenting cards in French and English claiming to receive the latest fashions from Paris and offering court dresses to the nobility. These cards were favorably received.

Mrs. Bonner, the lady's maid, soon became a frequent visitor to Madame Fribsby's drawing-room, enjoying meals of green tea, scandal, hot Sally-Lunn cakes, and novels. However, she found less time for these pleasures than her junior officer, Miss Amory's maid, who was worked as hard as a factory girl by the inexorable little Muse, her mistress.

The Muse loved to be dressed becomingly, possessing a lively fancy for change. She altered her attire daily. Her maid, P

If you've read the previous pages, you likely know Sir Francis Clavering's honest, if slightly uncharitable, assessment of his wife. And honestly, the Baronet wasn't entirely off-base. Lady Clavering, far from being a woman of profound wisdom or extensive learning, had spent exactly two years in "Ackney" (a suburb of London she refused to admit was Hackney) before being shipped off to join her father in Calcutta at the tender age of fifteen. That voyage aboard the *Ramchunder*, commanded by the formidable Captain Bragg, was where fate intervened. On board that very ship, two years prior, she had met her first husband, Mr. Amory, the ship's third mate. A rebellious soul, Amory had earned the Captain's ire so thoroughly that he was locked in irons until the Cape, abandoned there, and eventually left behind by a furious Captain Bragg who simply delivered the ward to her father in Calcutta.

What followed was a whirlwind of mercantile disaster. Amory returned to Calcutta, married a wealthy attorney's daughter, became an indigo planter, failed spectacularly; became an agent, failed again; became an editor of the *Sunderbund Pilot*, failed once more. Throughout this gauntlet, he quarreled incessantly with his father-in-law and wife. His final act was a financial crash that forced him to flee for New South Wales. Somewhere amidst this chaos, he likely crossed paths with Sir Jasper Rogers, the esteemed judge, and it appears he managed to squander his remaining fortune by misusing his father-in-law's name—a crime that ultimately sealed his fate.

Finding life in India stifling and uncomfortable, the family arranged for Lady Clavering to return to Europe with her four-year-old daughter, Blanche (known as Betsy). They were joined by Mrs. Bonner, the loyal nurse introduced in the last chapter, and settled near Captain Bragg's residence in Pocklington Street. However, the summer proved a miserable, rain-soaked affair. Captain Bragg, pompous and seemingly eager to shed the "Indian lady," created an atmosphere of gloomy surveillance. Believing the entire court and the East India Company were gossiping about her husband's ruin, Lady Clavering resolved to flee. She packed her bags, escaping the bully Bragg, and embarked on a grand tour of Europe.

Her travels were legendary. She called Hackney "Ackney," spoke with a charming foreign twang, and dressed with flamboyant flair. She was a gourmand of epic proportions, preparing curries and pilaws wherever she stayed, and her generosity knew no bounds. She brought triple portions of champagne, fowl, and ham to picnics, bought endless theater boxes and tickets for masked balls to distribute freely, and prepaid months' rent for boarding houses. She became a beacon of joy for impoverished officers and dowagers alike, tramping through Brussels, Paris, Milan, Naples, and Rome. It was in Rome, where Sir Francis Clavering was also staying (struggling to pay his hotel bills alongside the Chevalier Strong), that news of Amory's death reached her. With no genuine grief for her "scapegrace" of a husband, she married the descendant of the ancient Clavering line. Thus, we arrive at the present moment: Lady Clavering, now Mistress of Clavering Park, portrayed by the celebrated painter Pinckney with her little boy beside her.

Young Miss Blanche followed her mother's peripatetic lifestyle, acquiring a robust understanding of life. Though she had a governess briefly and later attended Madame de Caramel's pension in the Champs Élysées, her life shifted dramatically when the Claverings returned to England. Following her grandfather's death and the birth of her younger brother, Blanche realized her status had plummeted. While she was still technically an heiress, her stepfather, the absent-minded Sir Francis, and her mother, who dropped her H's and lacked refinement, meant she was overshadowed by little Frank, the heir apparent. Frank was a spoiled, quarrelsome urchin who constantly trod on Blanche's toes, upset her dinner on her dresses, and kept her from inheriting the full glory of the estate. Feeling misunderstood by her dull stepfather and a mother who seemed to prefer Frank, Blanche's heart naturally yearned for something outside the family circle.

This longing manifested as a desire to settle down, prompting Sir Francis's wish, mentioned in the last chapter, that his friend Mrs. Strong might pass away so he could take Blanche as a second Mrs. Strong. Since that was impossible, any suitor was welcome. Enter Arthur Pendennis, a smart, well-educated young gentleman. He was theoretically free to propose and would have been welcomed with open arms by Lady Clavering as a son-in-law. Yet, Mr. Pen hesitated. Burdened by shame over his recent failures, his idle existence, and the poverty he'd brought upon his mother, he suffered from a debilitating case of vanity-induced shyness. He couldn't imagine proposing to the brilliant Blanche Amory, surrounded by a dozen servants, while his mother scraped by on meager meals at Fairoaks. Despair and dallying won out over gallantry.

However, the situation was complicated by the presence of Laura Pendennis. Miss Amory, charming and lively, fascinated Laura with her arts and graces. But Laura's mother, Helen, saw the devil in the details. Helen suspected Blanche of being light-minded, frivolous, and lacking reverence, detecting a selfish worldliness beneath her pretty words. Laura and Pen initially defended Blanche against Helen's accusations, with Pen laughing off his mother's jealousy ("Psha, mother! All women are jealous"). But as weeks turned into months, Helen watched with maternal anxiety as the intimacy deepened. Seeing the pair meeting daily, her heart sank. Terrified of losing Pen forever, she finally confessed her fears, revealing that she felt broken and wished to see her children united before her death. Pen, ever the patronizing comforter, kissed and soothed her, imagining Laura as his ideal partner—fair, open, and pious. He pictured her bringing roses, and the image of the two women remained etched in his mind, a tear-jerking vision of potential bliss.

Yet, reality soon intruded. Before long, Laura was forced to concede to Helen's judgment. Blanche revealed her true colors: she was selfish, unkind, and inconstant. She complained endlessly about her family's inability to understand her, mocked her mother's lack of education, and described Sir Francis as a weak, cigar-smelling oaf. Most painfully, she detailed how her mother's affection had shifted entirely to little Frank. Blanche painted a picture of isolation, claiming she was "alone, alone, alone in the world."

These grievances, though heart-rending, failed to sway Laura regarding Blanche's specific actions. For instance, when little Frank upset a glass of water on Blanche's drawing, she boxed his ears and hurled opprobrious names at him in both English and French. Similarly, she treated the boy's governess with imperial disdain, sending her on trivial errands to fetch books or pocket-handkerchiefs. Laura, accustomed to gratitude for service, found Blanche's imperious demands jarring. It struck Laura that the "fairy" she admired was merely a mortal, and not a particularly amiable one. This realization was cemented after the incident with Frank, where Blanche, reciting a poem about her "pretty baby brother" and vowing eternal devotion, proceeded to slap him. It was a harsh lesson in cynical philosophy, mirroring a similar dynamic Laura had observed in Pen's own home.

By this point, Laura grew tired of admiring Blanche. The illusion shattered, replaced by a quiet sense of superiority. Blanche, sensing this shift, became peevish and dissatisfied. The tension culminated in a "battle-royal" on a fateful day. Blanche, in a wicked mood, had been insolent to her mother, savage to Frank, cruel to the governess, and abysmal to Pincott, her attendant. Unable to attack her powerful friend directly, Blanche lashed out at everyone else, particularly Pincott, driving the poor girl from the room in tears.

Laura, hearing of this, erupted in indignation. She lectured Blanche on her wicked conduct, telling her she ought to kneel and ask forgiveness from Heaven. In a flurry of emotion, Laura fled home across the park, leaving Blanche behind. Upon returning to her mother, Laura confirmed Helen's suspicions, declaring she gave Blanche up forever. Both women embraced, and Helen secretly rejoiced, believing this separation would save Pen from a "flighty creature."

However, the true motive lay hidden. Earlier that day, while sitting together, Blanche had tried to probe Laura about Pen's past with the actress, Mrs. Pybus. Laura, embarrassed, claimed ignorance, insisting Pen had acted generously. Blanche pressed, asking if he had written Laura any poems. Laura admitted to knowing only two translations and a prize poem she found "prosy." Then, with an arch smile, Blanche produced two crumpled papers from her desk—love verses dedicated to "Undine" and a "Naiad." Laura read them, finding them vague and indistinct, and asked if Blanche had replied. Blanche laughed, saying, "No! Not for worlds, dearest!" and tucked them back into the desk.

Following this, Blanche played a few Rossini songs on the piano, her voice flexible and

The frost of the quarrel melted faster than the snows of winter. Laura, a woman of quick forgiveness, sighed and let the matter slide, while Miss Blanche, whose grudges were as fleeting as a summer shower, found the accusation of wickedness oddly flattering rather than stinging. Vanity, after all, rarely bruises under charges of moral frailty; Blanche was merely pleased by her friend's theatrical indignation, a performance that required no real cause. Thus, Laura confessed that the romantic chapter of their early friendship had closed, leaving Blanche worthy only of ordinary regard.

Blanche, however, was a poet at heart. Instantly composing verses of "desertion" and "disenchantment," she lamented the coldness of love and the neglect of fidelity. Just as her sorrow needed an outlet, new neighbors arrived from London, bringing daughters who became her eternal sisters. With them, Blanche shared her woes. Meanwhile, the tall footmen ceased delivering notes to Laura, and the pony-carriage rarely ventured to Fairoaks. When Laura finally visited Blanche, the latter adopted a look of suffering martyrdom, which Laura, in turn, laughed at with a good-humored disrespect.

Yet, if Blanche found consolation in female friendship, she discovered others of the fair sex who offered her sympathy. Whenever she encountered a young man—even for ten minutes in a garden or a drawing-room window—she would deploy her beautiful eyes, speak in tones of tender appeal, and retreat to repeat the drama for her next suitor. At Clavering Park, when the house was quiet, Pen enjoyed the monopoly of her glances. The local county, however, was sparse in eligible young men; the nearest rivals were a curate or two and a rustic squire with ill-made clothes. The local dragoons, belonging to the Baronet's own regiment, were off-limits due to past squabbles over horses, accounts, blind-Hookey, and white feathers.

But the autumn session and the London season changed things. Families from the counties descended upon Baymouth and filled Mr. Bingley's theatre at Chatteris. The old, shrewish families of Drummington and Tozely Park, previously shy of the Claverings, suddenly took notice. Tales circulated wildly about Sir Francis, his wife, and especially Miss Amory and Captain Strong. Then came the heavy hitters: the Earl of Trehawk and the Countess Dowager of Rockminster. Their arrival in state triggered a cascade of carriages, filling the avenues of Clavering Park.

With the house crowded, Mirobolant, the valet, found distraction in his duties, forgetting the pangs of love. Footmen were too busy to ferry notes to Fairoaks. Blanche wandered new paths, leaving the riverside where Pen usually fished. Pen, however, remained by the stream, whipping it with empty lines. The "fish" refused to bite, and the "Peri"—Miss Amory—did not appear.

Recalling his old haunt, a hollow tree where he once wooed Miss Fotheringay, Pen transformed it into a post-office. Beneath moss and stone, he hid little poems addressed to an "Undine." Occasionally, a reply arrived on pink-scented paper, a flower, or a faint word of acknowledgment. These exchanges were playful, half-jocular, half-sympathetic. But when the great folks invaded Clavering Park, the routine broke. The white paper stayed buried under the stone; the pink notes ceased. The tree reflected in the stream as the Brawl rolled by. Pen, baulked in his game, grew serious. He demanded an explanation.

He found the Hall full of visitors, or Miss Amory absent at a ball, or walking in state accompanied by the Bishop and Bishopess of Chatteris, who scowled at him with dignified disapproval. The Right Reverend Prelate remembered the little transaction in the Dean's garden. "You're a sad young man," Lady Clavering whispered kindly. "How is your dear Mar?" Pen, vexed by the narrowness of his means and the humiliations of country life, tried to catch her eye.

"You never come to the river now," he said, trying to be poetic.
"I can't," Blanche replied. "The house is full of people."
"Undine has left the stream," Pen pressed.
"She never ought to have gone there," Miss Amory answered firmly. "It was only play... Besides, you have other consolations at home." She dropped her eyes, offering a final chance. Yet, thinking of Laura's purity and his mother's hopes, Pen hesitated. "Blanche!" he started, then stopped as she fled back to the company, leaving him biting his nails in the moonlight.

Later, Laura sat with Mr. Pynsent, Lord Rockminster's son, exchanging a sharp, impertinent nod typical of Oxford men. Pynsent, a tall, gaunt dandy with red whiskers, discussed Laura's estates with amused curiosity. When Laura jokingly revealed they owned "nine hens and a cock, a pig, and an old pointer," Pynsent laughed, mistaking her for a country gentlewoman unaware of the city's ways. Soon, Blanche joined them, singing a duet that drew Pynsent away, though he later called her a "lean, scraggy humbug" to a friend.

Two days later, Pen returned from a fruitless fishing trip to find Pynsent and his companion, Mr. Wagg, in his mother's drawing-room. Pynsent, striding over chairs, discoursed on his travels and political ambitions. Wagg, a man of flamboyant wit and poor taste, observed the domestic details with a butcher's eye: the old gardener John, the clothes drying on gooseberry bushes, the ladies' clogs, and the widow's miniature portrait. Wagg mocked Pen's apparent poverty, noting the "thick bread-and-butter" served at six, while Pynsent remained oblivious, treating the situation with polite indifference. Wagg, however, relished the tale at dinner, describing the "old man with a beak" and the "sarving maid," much to Blanche's delight and Pynsent's growing disgust.

Back at the house, the atmosphere shifted. Laura, observing Pen's listlessness and his failure to engage with the local captains or even the new arrivals, grew concerned. "He wastes his life here," she told her mother, Helen. "He is not clever enough for us. He ought to go away." Helen, realizing Pen's unhappiness, agreed. After much deliberation, they decided to give him £200 to travel to London and study law. When they revealed this plan to Pen, he was transformed. He embraced his mother with fervor, tears in his eyes, declaring his resolve to redeem his past and honor them. He sang and laughed, happier than she had seen him since his university days.

Meanwhile, Laura was visiting the Dowager Countess of Rockminster at Baymouth, preparing for a ball. Pen, delighted by the prospect of seeing her, arranged to ride over with Captain Strong. As they approached the Ballroom at the Baymouth Hotel, the room buzzed with aristocrats and commoners alike. Mirobolant, sporting a blue ribbon and three gold crosses, mingled with the crowd, while Colonel Altamont, a man of purple-dyed whiskers and immense jewels, nodded familiarly to the Chevalier.

The music swelled with "See the Conquering Hero Comes." The Dowager Countess, Mr. Pynsent, Sir Francis Clavering, and the rest of the elite filed in. Pen rushed through the crowd, bypassing Blanche, and seized Laura's hand. "God bless you!" he cried. "I must speak to you."
"Not for three dances, dear Pen," she smiled, pulling away gently.
He fell back, biting his nails in vexation, forgetting to salute Pynsent, as the ball spun on around them.

A Ball of Ball-practising

Arthur Pendennis elected to adopt a visage of profound gloom and to stand concealed behind a curtain of calico draperies, peering into the shaded recess of a window. From this vantage point, he observed Miss Bell executing her inaugural quadrille with Mr. Pynsent as her partner. The gentleman, a model of English solemnity on such occasions, traversed the floor with the rigid gravity of a man approaching his pew at church; his face was unsmiling, his attention fixed with the severity of a judge presiding over a court of law, refusing to let any outward circumstance distract him from his grave duty. Conversely, Miss Laura's countenance was radiant with pleasure and good nature. The interplay of lights, the throng, and the music enlivened her spirits. As she unfurled her white robes and executed her steps, smiling broadly with her brown ringlets cascading over her fair shoulders from her honest, rosy face, she became the object of admiration for many a gentleman in the room. Lady Fogey, who maintained a London residence and affected considerable airs of fashion whenever visiting the country, consulted Lady Rockminster regarding the identity of this young person. Comparing her to a reigning beauty in London, Lady Fogey opined that Laura possessed a certain resemblance and, in her ladyship's judgment, "would do."

Lady Rockminster, who would have been astonished if any of her protégés failed to "do," marveled at Lady Fogey's audacity in rendering a verdict. Surveying Laura through the lens of her eyeglass, the Dowager bestowed majestic glances upon the girl. She was charmed by Laura's artless appearance and gay, innocent demeanor. "Her manner is very good," Lady Rockminster mused internally. "Her arms are rather red, but that is merely a defect of youth." Her tone, she noted, was vastly superior to that of the little pert Miss Amory, currently dancing opposite her.

Indeed, Miss Blanche stood as Miss Laura's *vis-à-vis*. She smiled with killing effect upon her dearest friend, nodding and conversing with her whenever their paths crossed during the quadrille's intricate evolutions, lavishing her upon her with obvious favoritism. Miss Blanche's shoulders were the whitest in the entire assembly; they seemed restless within her frock, shifting constantly. Her eyes rolled incessantly, and her petite figure seemed to broadcast a message to everyone present: "Behold me! Not that pink, healthy, bouncing country lass, Miss Bell, who scarcely knows how to dance until I taught her. Observe this true Parisian manner—this is the prettiest little foot in the room, and the finest *chaussure* as well. Gaze upon it, Mr. Pynsent. Gaze upon it, Mr. Pendennis, you who are scowling behind the curtain; I know you are longing to dance with me."

Laura continued her dance, maintaining a vigilant eye upon Mr. Pen in the window embrasure. He did not abandon this secluded post until the second quadrille, when the benevolent Lady Clavering beckoned him forward to the dais, the seat of honor reserved for the dowagers. To this destination, Pen advanced with a blush and an air of extreme awkwardness, typical of the most conceited young fellows. He offered a haughty salutation to Lady Rockminster, who barely acknowledged his bow, before proceeding to pay his respects to the widow of the late Amory. She was resplendent in diamonds, velvet, lace, feathers, and a dazzling array of millinery and goldsmith's ware.

Young Mr. Fogey, then a student in the fifth form at Eton and ardently anticipating both a beard and a commission in a dragoon regiment, was the second partner honored with Miss Bell's hand. He was completely entranced by the young lady. "I like you much better than the French girl," he candidly confessed to her, referring to Miss Amory with whom he had previously danced. Laura laughed, appearing more good-humored than ever; in the midst of her mirth, she caught sight of Pen, who, on his side, continued to look absurdly pompous and sullen. The next dance was a waltz. Young Fogey sighed, admitting his inability to waltz, and vowed to secure a master during the ensuing holidays.

Mr. Pynsent again claimed Miss Bell's hand for this dance. Pen beheld her, in a fury, twirling around the room, her waist encircled by that gentleman's arm. He had never known anger like this before. On summer evenings at Clavering Park, when the chairs and tables were cleared and the governess descended to play the piano, he and the Chevalier Strong—a splendid performer capable of the British hornpipe, the German waltz, or the Spanish fandango if required—along with the two young ladies, Blanche and Laura, often improvised little balls. Laura enjoyed such dancing immensely, her animation sometimes infectious enough to lift even Mr. Pynsent. However, Miss Blanche, though a beautiful dancer, was plagued by an unlucky partner: Captain Broadfoot of the Dragoons, stationed at Chatteris. Though devoted to the endeavor, Captain Broadfoot lacked the requisite timing to complete his turns and, lacking an ear for music, remained oblivious to the fact that his movements were painfully slow.

Consequently, in both the waltz and the quadrille, Miss Blanche perceived that her dear friend Laura held the honors of the dance and was by no means pleased with the latter's success. After a couple of turns with the heavy dragoon, Blanche feigned fatigue, requesting to return to her place near her mother, to whom Pen was speaking. She questioned him on why he had not asked her to waltz, leaving her to the mercy of that "great odious man" in spurs and a red coat.
