"I thought spurs and scarlet were the most fascinating objects in the world to young ladies," Pen replied. "I never should have dared to put my black coat in competition with that splendid red jacket."

"You are very unkind and cruel and sulky and naughty," said Miss Amory, accompanied by another shrug of her shoulders. "You had better go away. Your cousin is looking at us over Mr. Pynsent's shoulder."

"Will you waltz with me?" asked Pen.

"Not this waltz. I cannot, having just sent away that good Captain Broadfoot. Look at Mr. Pynsent; have you ever seen such a creature? Yet I shall dance the next waltz with you, and the quadrille too. I am promised, but I will inform Mr. Poole that I have forgotten my engagement to you."

"Women forget very readily," Pendennis remarked.

"But they invariably return, repented and sorrowful for their actions," Blanche countered. "Look, here comes the Poker, and dear Laura leaning upon him. How pretty she appears!"

Laura approached, extending her hand to Pen, to whom Pynsent rendered a bow that appeared no more graceful than the domestic instrument to which Miss Amory had compared him. Yet Laura's face overflowed with kindness. "I am so glad to have come, dear Pen," she said. "I can speak to you now. How is mamma? The three dances are concluded, and I am engaged to you for the next, Pen."

"I have just engaged myself to Miss Amory," Pen declared. Miss Amory nodded and executed her customary little curtsey. "I do not intend to give him up, dearest Laura," she added.

"Well, then, he shall waltz with me, dear Blanche," the other suggested. "Won't you, Pen?"

"I promised to waltz with Miss Amory."

"Provoking!" exclaimed Laura, returning a curtsey herself before positioning herself beneath the ample wing of Lady Rockminster.

Pen was delighted with his mischief. The two prettiest girls in the room were quarreling over him. He flattered himself he had successfully penalized Miss Laura. Leaning in a dandified posture with his elbow resting on the wall, he chatted with Blanche, mercilessly quizzing every male in the room—from the heavy dragoons in their tight jackets to the country dandies in their peculiar attire, not to mention the strange toilets of the ladies. One seemed to harbor a bird's nest atop her head; another bore six pounds of grapes in her hair alongside her false pearls. "It is a coiffure of almonds and raisins," Pen observed, "and might be served up for dessert." In short, he was exceedingly satirical and amusing.

Throughout the quadrille, he sustained this conversation with unflinching bitterness and vivacity, keeping Blanche in a constant state of laughter, both at his wickedness and his jokes—which were undeniably good—and because Laura remained their *vis-à-vis*, able to witness and overhear how merry and confidential they were.

"Arthur is charming tonight," she whispered to Laura, crossing Cornet Perch's shell-jacket as Pen performed a *cavalier seul*, drawingling through the figure with a thumb tucked into the pocket of each waistcoat.

"Who?" asked Laura.

"Arthur," Blanche answered, in French. "Oh, it is such a pretty name!" And now the young ladies moved to Pen's side, while Cornet Perch, in his turn, executed a *pas seul*. Lacking a waistcoat pocket into which to conceal his hands, they appeared large and swollen, dangling before him from the tight arms of his jacket.

During the interval

The ballroom at Baymouth was a cauldron of chaos. High words flew like shattered glass, and the crowd pressed together around a corner window with the desperate energy of trapped bees. Through the open pane, the street below roared with sarcasm—"Pitch into him!" "Where's the police?"—while inside, the air crackled with tension. On one side stood Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant, surrounded by a ring of admirers including the formidable Madame Fribsby; on the other, Arthur Pendennis, flanked by a circle of concerned gentlemen and ladies. Just as Strong pushed his way through the crush, the situation escalated.

The trouble stemmed from a tiny spark of pride involving Sir Francis Clavering's angry little chef. Earlier, Mr. Pen, humiliated by a disastrous waltz and stung by Miss Amory's icy behavior, had sought solace at the window, murmuring calmly to the sparkling sea. In his attempt to cool his temper, he felt a hand land heavily on his shoulder. Turning, he encountered Mirobolant, the French artist, with eyes blazing like coals beneath his pale ringlets. Being tapped by a kitchen staff member was an affront to every fiber of Pendennis's aristocratic sensibility.

"You speak French?" Mirobolant demanded, switching seamlessly to the tongue of his ancestors.

"What is that to you?" Pen retorted, stepping back.

"At any rate, you understand it?" Mirobolant bowed, rolling his R's with the force of a gasp. "Oui, *monsieur*, vous me comprendrez... quand je vous dis que vous êtes un lâche."

"A coward?" Pen stared, stunned. "The consequences of that word among men of honor are clear: I'll fling you out of this window!"

With a roar, Pen lunged forward, intent on executing his threat. The window was inches away, and the artist, though a formidable foil-fighter, was no match for the young gentleman. But the moment dissolved into a cacophony of screams, clattering fiddles, and rushing bodies. Captain Broadfoot and a burly officer interposed themselves like human walls, while Laura, her face a mask of terror, peeked over the crowd to ask, "What is wrong?"

Enter Strong, bursting from the refreshment room like a hurricane. He found Mirobolant grinding his teeth and spitting oaths in a garbled Galleon French, while Pen looked terrifyingly wicked, trying desperately to maintain composure.

"Qué pasa?" Strong asked the chef in rapid Spanish.

"I am Chevalier de Juillet!" the tiny man snapped, slapping his chest. "He insulted me!"

"He called me 'cook'," hissed the little Frenchman.

Strong couldn't suppress a chuckle. "Come away, poor Chevalier. We must not brawl before ladies. I'll carry your message to Mr. Pendennis. The poor fellow isn't right in his head."

Gathering round Pen, the group demanded answers. Stammering, Pen admitted he had accused the man of being a cook when the chef attempted to escort a young lady, leading to the "coward" remark and the challenge. "If you gentlemen hadn't stopped me, I'd have thrown him out!" he confessed.

"Damn him! Serve him right!" the gentlemen grumbled, though some suggested a "little thrashing" wouldn't hurt the arrogant foreigners.

Then Laura stepped forward, her face softening the storm. "Pen, you must shake hands with him before you sleep. Foreigners have different manners. If you hurt a poor man's feelings, you'd be the first to apologize, wouldn't you, dear Pen?"

Her gaze was pure forgiveness, an angelic plea. Pen, touched, took both her hands and promised compliance. As she lingered, gazing at him, he thought, *How fond she is!* Yet, he needed to resolve the absurdity of the quarrel first.

Laura then asked, "Won't you stop and waltz with me, Pen? I'm not afraid."

This was the fatal slip. Pen immediately visualized himself prostrate on the floor, having tripped over Miss Roundle and the dragoon, sending Blanche flying against the wall, all while the room erupted in laughter—including Laura and Pynsent.

"I shall never dance again," Pen declared, his face darkening with resolve. "Never. I'm surprised you'd ask."

"Why?" Laura pressed, her tone turning sharp. "Is it because you can't get Blanche?"

"No," Pen lied, his vanity flaring. "Because I don't wish to make a fool of myself. Especially not for you to laugh at me, Laura. I saw you and Pynsent. By Jove! No man shall laugh at me."

"Poor Pen!" Laura cried, wounded by his morbid sensitivity and savage vanity. "Who thinks the worse of you for stumbling? Why are you so ready to think evil?"

Just then, Mr. Pynsent arrived, bowing gracefully. "Lady Rockminster commands me to inquire if I may take you to supper?"

"I was going with my cousin," Laura stammered, glancing nervously at Pen.

"Oh, pray, no!" Pen interjected hastily. "You're in excellent hands. I can't do better than leave you. I'm going home."

"Good night, Mr. Pendennis," Pynsent said dryly—a polite wrapper for a wish to box the insolent jackanapes' ears. Pen merely bowed and departed, leaving Laura pleading behind.

Outside, the night air was crisp, the sea murmuring softly. Pynsent remarked on the strangeness of the gathering, mentioning the seafaring man who had bought him champagne and the various local dignitaries. Laura feigned ignorance, claiming she had been frightened by the quarrel.

"Pynsent said your cousin was rather unlucky tonight," Pynsent noted gently. "Three or four people haven't been pleased."

"Didn't he leave me in charge to you?" Laura asked, looking up with a guilty, coquettish flicker.

"Easily forgiven," Pynsent cried, taking her arm. They walked toward the supper room, where Laura, though eager to eat, felt deeply distressed about Pen. She wondered how her mother could expect her to wed a man so capricious and jealous. Relief finally came when a waiter delivered a small pencil note from Pen: *"I met Cooky below ready to fight me; I asked his pardon. I'm glad I did. Wanted to speak to you tonight, but will wait till you come home. God bless you. Dance away all night with Pynsent. — PEN."*

Pen descended the stairs, his heart heavy with regret for his harsh words to Laura, yet he couldn't apologize in front of Pynsent. Instead, he resolved to make peace with the Frenchman. He found Mirobolant in the hall, pacing with mischief.

"I've got him in the coffee-room," Mirobolant grinned. "With pistols and a candle. Swords on the beach? Mirobolant is a dead hand with foils."

"I can't fight a cook!" Pen fumed.

"He is a Chevalier of July!" Mirobolant countered. "They present arms to him!"

"And do you ask me to duel a servant?" Pen snarled. "I'll call a policeman!"

Strong laughed. "I'm but joking. I came to settle quarrels, not fight them. I've been soothing Mirobolant. He tells me you used the word 'cook' not as an insult, but because it's customary for a hired officer not to arm himself with a lady's arm. And here's the real secret: Madame Fribsby told me the poor artist is laboring under a violent passion."

Upon hearing this, Pen burst into a hearty laugh, his rage vanishing instantly. He realized his own jealousy throughout the evening had been absurd. Remembering his own youthful infatuations, he forgave the artist easily. He entered the coffee-room, extended his hand, and delivered a fluent speech in French, declaring he was "sincerely sorry" (*sincèrement fâché*) for the expression that had caused offense, promising he would never, *jamais, jamais* intend harm.

"Bravo!" cried Strong, delighted by Pen's fluency and magnanimity. Mirobolant withdrew, regretting his words, and Arthur felt a newfound confidence. Before departing with Strong for the "Butcher Boy" tavern, Pen dispatched a final note to Laura: *Dance away all night with Pynsent, and be very happy.*

At daybreak, they reached the "Brawl," separating as the ball at Baymouth wound down. Back at Fairoaks, Pen returned home without delivering his intended confession to Laura. He told his mother, Helen, of the "calamity" at Baymouth in a gay tone, dismissing the whole affair as a mere mishap unsuitable for serious declarations.

While Pen prepared for departure—packing books, polishing his gun, dreaming of entering the Inns of Court and becoming a successful playwright to secure his future and eventually marry Laura—life continued its chaotic rhythm. Laura returned home the following evening, driven by Pynsent, a sight that darkened Pen's brow. Helen, eager for news, retired early, leaving Laura alone with Pen, though both claimed fatigue.

The next morning, Helen summoned Pen, urging him to dress and join Laura in the garden, believing it the perfect weather for romance. Pen complied, shaving his "ambrosial chin

The journey from the quiet, wooded shadows of the West to the roaring chaos of London was not merely a change of geography; it was a metamorphosis of spirit. As the 'Alacrity' coach thundered through the velvet darkness, shedding the gentle gossip of Clavering and the humble, humming routine of Fairoaks, Arthur Pendennis sat poised on the edge of destiny. With every mile the wheels churned, the young man's mind spun a kaleidoscope of futures. He was no longer the boy who had stumbled onto this road a year prior, heading to Oxford with a naive stride. Now, hardened by a single, stinging failure, he possessed a sharper edge, a deeper reservoir of courage, and a self-assurance that bloomed like a flower in frost.

Yet, beneath the ambition, a tender current flowed. His thoughts drifted, with a warmth that defied the nipping air, to his mother. She stood at home, likely awake, her prayers rising to the same stars he gazed upon. "O Heaven!" he whispered, clutching the fabric of hope. "Grant me the strength to be worthy of her love, to work honestly, and to resist the snares that wait." Her love was his talisman, a shield against the coming storm. But there was another shadow in his heart—a regret for Laura. He had pushed her away, convinced himself unworthy of her radiant, innocent nature. Like a prodigal child fleeing a church he once loved, he drew back from her spotless purity, admitting with a pang of shame that he was not a match for such a creature. Yet, strangely, he felt liberated by her rejection, as if stepping clear of a holy ground where he felt he had no right to tread.

Sleep eluded him until the pale fingers of dawn touched the windows. By the time the coach halted at the bustling breakfast stop at B--, the sun had burst forth, igniting the road with gold. The pace quickened, the horn blared, and the miles flew by in a blur. Pen smoked, joked with the guard, and mingled with the shifting throngs, his spirit buoyant. The atmosphere thickened with every instant; the final team of grey horses broke from H--, and the coach swept into the jaws of the great beast that was London.

"What a place!" Pen thought, his heart hammering a rhythm of anticipation. "Here begins my battle. Fight or fall—that is the law." From the roof of the carriage, he surveyed the sprawling metropolis with the feverish longing of a soldier on the eve of campaign.

Among the passengers, he struck up a conversation with a cheery gentleman in a shabby cloak, a reporter for *The Star* named Mr. Doolan. This man spoke of literature as if it were his native tongue, regaling Pen with tales of Tom Campbell, Tom Hood, and Sydney Smith, treating them as old friends. Passing Brompton, he pointed out Mr. Hurtle, the formidable reviewer, striding with his umbrella. "A Boniface man!" Pen declared, peering over the side. "I admire his works!" Doolan chuckled, confirming the reputation. Pen, already intoxicated by the allure of the pen, calculated wildly: if the famous novelist Wagg earned three hundred pounds a volume, perhaps he, too, could carve out a fortune of five thousand a year. For now, he believed in the magic of authors and editors, revering even Wagg, whose intellect seemed flawed to others but who was clearly a king in the realm of print.

As the coach pulled up at the gloomy precincts of the Gloster Coffee-House, the first face to greet him was that of Harry Foker. Foker emerged from behind an enormous cab-horse, a vision in white kid gloves and tight leather breeches, his chin sporting a tuft of hair that nature had generously bestowed. Beside him rode a diminutive cab-boy, knobby-kneed and vibrating with energy. Foker eyed the dusty coach, the smoking horses of the 'Alacrity,' with a twinkle in his eye. "What, Foker!" cried Pendennis. "Hullo! Pen, my boy!" shouted the other, waving his whip in a salute that spoke of old camaraderie. Pen, thrilled to be in the lion's den and surrounded by his peculiar friend, invited Doolan to dine with him at Covent Garden. He called a cab, rattled off to the coffee-house in high spirits, and was greeted with the familiar bustle of waiters and the polite bows of the landlord. He sought out the landlady, wishing he could shake hands with everyone, his pocket heavy with a hundred pounds and his heart lighter with purpose.

Dressed in his very best, Pen dined modestly on a pint of sherry, determined to exercise economy, before venturing to the adjacent theatre. The lights, the music, the sheer cacophony of the crowd enchanted him. These sights, new to the country boy, filled him with a giddy exhilaration. He laughed at the jokes, applauded the songs, and strutted through the lobbies with the confidence of someone belonging to the highest circles. What weary frequenter of London's pavements could not recall such youthful delusions?

There, amidst the revelry, was young Foker again, this time in the company of Grandy Tiptoff of the Household Brigade, Lord Tiptoff's brother, and Lord Colchicum, a venerable peer who had been a man of pleasure since the days of the first French Revolution. Foker dragged Pen into a private box, where sat a lady with the longest ringlets and the fairest shoulders—Miss Blenkinsop, the eminent actress of high comedy. Behind her, snoozing in a wig, sat her father, the "useful" and "veteran" Blenkinsop, a man who played the heavy father both on stage and in private life.

It was nearly midnight. Back at Fairoaks, Mrs. Pendennis slept, wondering if her Arthur had rested, while Laura, too, lay awake. Yesterday, as the coach rolled over silent commons, Pen had vowed to reform, his heart anchored at home. Now, however, the farce was proceeding with magnificent success. On stage, Mrs. Leary, clad in a hussar jacket and braided pantaloons, enchanted the audience with her archness and lovely figure.

Pen, new to the town, wanted to listen to Mrs. Leary, but the box was a hive of chatter. Tiptoff knew the origin of her maillot; Colchicum recalled seeing her in '14. Miss Blenkinsop informed Pen that she sang out of tune, a revelation that shocked Pen, who saw an angel and heard a nightingale. Then Hoppus appeared as Sir Harcourt Featherby. The gentlemen in the box declared him stale, and Tiptoff prepared to fling Miss Blenkinsop's bouquet to him.

"Not for the world!" cried the daughter of the veteran Blenkinsop. "Lord Colchicum gave it to me."

Pen recalled the nobleman's name, bowing and blushing as he offered thanks for being voted into the Megatherium Club at his uncle's request. "What, you're Wigsby's nephew, are you?" asked the peer, using the familiar nickname that made Pen blush. "We balloted you last Wednesday. Your uncle wasn't there."

Delighted, Pen professed his gratitude, delivering a handsome speech to which the peer listened through his double opera-glass. "Don't be always looking at that box, you naughty creature," chided Miss Blenkinsop. "She's a dev'lish fine woman, that Mirabel," said Tiptoff, adding, "though Mirabel was a d——d fool to marry her." "A stupid old spooney," agreed the peer.

"Mirabel!" cried Pendennis.

"Ha! ha!" laughed Harry Foker. "We've heard of her before, haven't we, Pen?"

It was Pen's first love. It was Miss Fotheringay. The year prior, she had been led to the altar by Sir Charles Mirabel, G.C.B., a diplomat who had negotiated the Peace of Pultusk. "Emily was always as stupid as an owl," remarked Miss Blenkinsop. "Eh! Eh! pas si bete," countered the old Peer. "Oh, for shame!" cried the actress, oblivious to the meaning.

And there he saw her again, his first love, and wondered how he could ever have loved her so deeply. Thus, on his first night in London, Arthur Pendennis found himself entangled with a club, an actress of genteel comedy, a heavy father of the stage, and a dashing society of jovial blades. Lord Colchicum, though stricken in years and bald, remained indefatigable in the pursuit of joy, boasting that he could drink as much claret as the youngest member of his circle. He patronized the arts in all languages, hosting dramatic professors from England, Italy, France, and the Opera, entertaining scores of young men of fashion who consorted with the greenroom's ladies.

The following day, Pen paid his entrance fees to the Megatherium Club, an operation that consumed exactly one-third of his hundred pounds. He took possession of the edifice, ate his luncheon with immense satisfaction, and sank into an easy-chair in the library, trying to read the magazines. He worried about the members staring at him, daring to keep their hats on in such refined rooms. Sitting down, he wrote a letter to Fairoaks on the club paper, declaring how comforting this place would be after his labors.

Then

The Knights of the Temple

In the realm of antiquated colleges, schools, and Inns of Court, a stubborn respect for the past clings like mildew to the bricks. While modern workhouses and prisons boast sanitary marvels and running water, these venerable halls remain stuck in the Stone Age of hygiene. A mechanic in Spitalfields commands a cistern; the "Gentlemen" of the Inns endure a regime where water is fetched in jugs by laundresses who seem to enjoy the chore, and where sleeping quarters are priced at the cost of suburban villas. It is a curious paradox: the ancestors of these "Knights of the Temple" were, by all accounts, the Great Unwashed, yet today they occupy rooms built before the concept of decency existed. One can almost hear the ghost of Old Grump, a veteran of the Norfolk Circuit, cursing the heavens every time a drop of shower-bath water trickled through his roof. Grump, now a grizzled relic of half-a-century, had never touched a bath in his life, declaring the whole affair a dandified folly. He dreamed of a benevolent knight devising "Hummums"—portable baths—for the benefit of the legal fraternity, but the Temple historian remains silent on such philanthropy. Instead, we have Pump Court and Fountain Court, where the water runs clear but the souls of the lawyers within seem perpetually dry.

Yet, despite the lack of plumbing, the Inns possess a rough charm that binds their inhabitants together. To the student of law, passing by chambers where Eldon lived or where Coke mused upon Littleton, offers a thrill of poetic reminiscence. The air is thick with the ghosts of Sir Roger de Coverley and Mr. Spectator, debating hoops and patches amidst the greenery of the Temple Gardens. It is a stage where the Bard, Fielding, and Johnson have all played their parts, turning the dusty corridors into a theater of literary history. If one could capture a single day in the four-story houses of the dingy courts where Pen and Warrington dwell, the result would be a volume as queer as an Asmodeus's diary.

Consider the drama unfolding on the ground floor. A parliamentary counsel, now a gentleman driving off to Belgravia, was recently a starving soul in a garret, surviving on stealthy literature and the patience of creditors. Now, the wheel of fortune has spun, and he enjoys a clerk's salary that his former self could barely afford for boot-black. Above him, on the first floor, resides a venerable recluse, a brain-full scholar who has amassed a fortune and a library of classics. He returns from the club, a godless old man, knowing that when he dies, his heirs will burn half his books. Further up, Mr. Doomsday enjoys his nightly ritual of Catullus and a steady rubber of whist with three seniors, following a heavy dinner of three bottles of Port. They are seen asleep in the Temple Church on Sundays, safe in their small fortunes, untouched by attorneys' troubles.

On the same side, where Pen and Warrington reside late into the night, sits Mr. Paley. This young man, a Fellow of his college, is a machine of industry. He reads and notes cases until two in the morning, rises at seven, and works until an hour before dinner, only to repeat the cycle until dawn. He is a fierce grasping intellect, deliberately excluding everything that is not law—from philosophy and poetry to love, beauty, and even the sound of a sweet song. Where Pen and Warrington could indulge in friendship and kindness, Paley must remain selfish, blind to the world outside his reading lamp. He shudders when meeting his old companion Warrington on the stairs, viewing him as doomed to perdition, unable to comprehend why anyone would waste time on anything other than the endless legend of the law.

Despite Paley's grim determination, Pen finds his life in the Temple to be a delightful adventure. Though his family believes he is reforming, studying law with the seriousness of a regular student, Pen is actually enjoying the rough pleasures of a London bachelor. He has shed his aristocratic airs and the dandyish pretenses of his college days. He walks in the Park watching horses instead of riding them himself; he pulls on the river and stretches up to Hampstead. His routine involves a morning's study, a tavern dinner, a bachelor night of joviality, and a quiet evening with a pipe and a humble potation of British spirits, whose quality Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress, always tests. He attends Hall regularly, finding the scene of the Upper Temple Hall fascinating. It is a microcosm of the era: stout grey-headed attorneys, dandies in rowing shirts, swarthy colonials, and lively Irishmen.

The dinner in the Hall is a spectacle of abundance. Messes of four share a joint of beef or mutton, ample pie, and a bottle of port. The "honest habitues," however, employ harmless arts to improve their feast. Enter Mr. Lowton, a Temple gourmand and captain of the mess. Lowton is a man of keen strategy, aiming to secure the thirteenth glass of port and the choice cuts of meat. With a wink and a tug on Pen's gown, he guides the newcomer toward the secrets of the hall. He points out the notables: Boosey the bencher, likely eating turtle; Balls and Swettenham, the "regular swells"; and the mysterious trio who sit opposite—the "High Table" guests who claim to dine but merely pretend, avoiding wine so that the messmates can help themselves to the bottle.

"Why do you like to mess with them if they don't eat?" Pen asks, puzzled.
"How green you are!" exclaims Lowton. "They don't drink any wine, and a fellow gets the bottle to himself if he likes it."

As the meal proceeds, a strange figure enters the fray. Dressed in old, faded clothes and a gown that hangs behind him like a tattered sail, he strides across the table to sit beside Pen. His shirt is clean but ragged, a stark contrast to the magnificent pink raiment of Molloy Maloney in the adjacent mess. He smiles at Pen, recognizing a kindred spirit. "We ought to know each other," he says. "My name's Warrington."

Arthur Pendennis is delighted. "Are you St—— Warrington?" he asks, recalling the hero who thrashed the bargeman Bill Simes.
"Warrington—yes," laughs the stranger. "I recollect you in your freshman's term. But you appear to have quite cut me out."

Pen speaks of college legends, mentioning the bargeman and the Miss Notleys, before Warrington hushes him gently. "Glad to make your acquaintance, Pendennis. Heard a good deal about you."

An instant bond forms. The two young men fall deep into college talk, ignoring the formalities of the Hall. Seeing Warrington take his share of the port with gusto, Pen abandons his earlier pretense of dislike for the vintage, much to Lowton's dismay. After dinner, Warrington invites Pen back to his chambers in Lamb Court for a short pipe. Pen agrees, leaving Lowton sighing behind them.

Back in Lamb Court, Pen and Warrington become sworn friends. Warrington's cheerful temperament, his good sense, and his rough welcome charm Pen far more than the silent politeness of the Polytechnic. Soon, Pen moves out of his expensive lodgings in St. James's, finding it far more economical to share Warrington's room. Even Pidgeon and Flanagan divide their allegiance between the two friends. Together, they navigate the chaotic, humorous, and sometimes grim world of the Temple, bound by shared memories and the simple joy of a pipe and a joke.

Eager with the intoxicating prospect of real life, Pen embarked on a tour of London's stranger quarters. He took a peculiar pride in believing himself to be mingling with the broad spectrum of humanity—he observed coal-heavers in their taverns, pugilists in their lodging-house parlors, and respectable burghers enjoying themselves by the river or in the suburbs. In truth, he would have loved nothing more than to hob-nob with a gang of celebrated pickpockets or share a tankard of ale with a crew of burglars, provided fate offered him the chance. There was a certain gravitas in watching Warrington listen intently to the tales of the "Tutbury Pet" or the "Brighton Stunner" at the Champion's Arms, and a genuine fascination in observing his attention toward the coal-heavers gathered at the Fox-under-the-Hill. Pen's familiarity with the metropolis's pubs and their diverse patrons grew prodigious. He was the personal confidant of landlords and landladies alike, welcomed at the bar as readily as in a private club. He confessed a far greater affection for this rough society than for his own, finding the manners of his peers annoying and their conversation tedious. "In polite society," he would remark, "everyone is a carbon copy: same clothes, same food, same words. One young dandy at a club mimics another, and one debutante at a ball looks like her neighbor. But *character* exists here. I'd rather speak to the strongest man in England, or the man who can consume the most beer, or that magnificent republican hatter who believes Thomas Hardy was the greatest figure in history. I prefer gin-and-water to claret. I love a sanded floor in Carnaby Market better than a chalked one in Mayfair. And yes, I admit it, I prefer snobs." Indeed, this gentleman was a social republican; and while conversing with Jack and Tom, it never crossed his mind that he was superior to them, though perhaps the deference they showed him pleased him secretly.

Pen trailed behind Warrington to these various refuges with great glee and diligence. Yet, he was considerably younger, and consequently, much more pompous and stately than his companion. He was, in essence, a young prince in disguise, touring the impoverished provinces of his father's kingdom. These men respected him as a high-flier, a fine fellow, a regular swell. About him hung an air of imperious good humor, a royal frankness, and a majesty, despite the fact that he was merely the heir-apparent to twopence-halfpenny and descended from a galleypot. If life places us in such positions, we accept them with ease and are quick to assert superiority over those who are equal to us. Pen's condescension at this stage of his life was a spectacle worth witnessing. Among capable men, this assumption and impudence pass unnoticed due to extreme youth; but it is curious to observe the conceit of a generous and clever lad—there is something almost touching in that early display of simplicity and folly.

After spending mornings engrossed in heavy reading—fearfully not limited to law, but encompassing politics, general history, and literature, deemed necessary for a young man's advancement alongside dry legal texts—they would apply diligently to letters, reviews, elementary lawbooks, and above all, the newspapers. By the time dinner approached, these young gentlemen would sallied out into town with boundless spirits and appetites, determined to enjoy a merry night following a pleasant forenoon. It was the jovial era of twenty-four, when every faculty of mind and body operated in healthy vigor, when the world felt new, and one moved forward spurred by good cheer and a delightful capacity for enjoyment. If we ever feel young again later in life, it is with the companions of that time; the tunes we hum in old age are those we learned then. Sometimes the festivities of that period revive in memory, but how dingy the pleasure gardens have grown, how tattered the garlands appear, how sparse and aged the company, and how many lights have extinguished since that day! Gray hairs have arrived like daylight streaming in—bringing daylight and a headache with it. Pleasure has retired to bed with rouge still on her cheeks. Very well, friend, let us walk through the day, sober and somber, yet friendly.

One wonders what Laura and Helen would have said, could they have witnessed what they might occasionally have seen if they had been in London during the early morning when bridges began to blush in the sunrise and the tranquil city streets shone in the dawn. They would have seen Mr. Pen and Mr. Warrington rattling over echoing flags toward the Temple, following one of their wild nights of carousing. Nights wild, but not so wicked as they sometimes are, for Warrington was a woman-hater; and Pen, as noted, was too lofty to stoop to vulgar intrigue. Their Young Prince of Fairoaks could never address anyone of the sex without respectful courtesy and shrank from coarse words or gestures with instinctive delicacy. Though we have seen him fall in love with a fool, as superiors and inferiors have done, and likely more than once in his life, for the duration of any delusion he always regarded the object as a Goddess, choosing to wait upon her. Men serve women kneeling; when they stand, they depart.

"That," an acquaintance of Pen's declared to him in his hard, homely fashion—"that is what happened." This speaker was an old friend with whom Pen had reunited in London, none other than the honest Mr. Bows of the Chatteris Theatre, now employed as a pianoforte player accompanying the eminent lyrical talents nightly delighting the public at the Fielding's Head in Covent Garden, where the little club known as the Back Kitchen resided.

Numerous friends of Pen frequented this merry gathering. The Fielding's Head had been a house of entertainment almost since the time when the famous author of 'Tom Jones' presided as magistrate in the neighboring Bow Street; his seat was pointed out and remained occupied by the president of the night's entertainment. The worthy Cutts, landlord of the Fielding's Head, generally filled this post when not disabled by gout or other ailments. His jolly appearance and fine voice were memorable to many male readers; he sang profusely during the harmonic meetings, his songs belonging to what may be termed the British Brandy-and-Water School—such as 'The Good Old English Gentleman,' 'Dear Tom, this Brown Jug,' and so forth. Songs blending pathos and hospitality, chanting praises of good liquor and social affections in a baritone voice. The charms of our women, the heroic deeds of naval and military commanders, were often sung in these ballads. On many youthful occasions, I have admired how Cutts the singer, after working everyone up to patriotic enthusiasm by describing how Abercrombie received his death-wound, or bringing tears to our eyes as he shed them liberally himself, recounting how autumn's falling leaf "proclaimed the old man he must die"—how Cutts the singer transformed into Cutts the landlord, and before the applause, struck by fistfuls on his table, faded away, he would call out, "Now, gentlemen, give your orders, the waiter's in the room—John, a champagne cup for Mr. Green. I think, sir, you said sausages and mashed potatoes? John, attend on the gentleman."

"And I'll thank ye give me a glass of punch too, John, and take care the wather boils," a voice would cry, familiar to Pen, causing the lad to blush and start upon first hearing it—that of the venerable Captain Costigan, now established in London and one of the great pillars of the harmonic meetings at the Fielding's Head.

Captain Costigan's manners and conversation attracted many young men. He was a character, his fame spreading rapidly after his arrival in the metropolis, particularly after his daughter's marriage. He was great in his conversation regarding "me daughther." He recounted her marriage, the events surrounding it, the carriages she kept, Mirabel's adoration for her and him, and the hundreds of pounds he was at perfect liberty to draw from his son-in-law whenever necessity urged. Having stated his firm intention to "draw next Saturday, I give ye me secret word and honor next Saturday, the fourteenth, when ye'll see the money will be handed over to me at Coutts's, the very instant I present the cheque," the Captain would frequently propose to borrow a half-crown from his friend until the arrival of that day of Greek Calends, when, on the honor of an officer and gentleman, he would repay the trifling obligation.

Sir Charles Mirabel did not possess that enthusiastic attachment to his father-in-law which Costigan sometimes boasted of (though in other emotional stages, Cos would inveigh, with tears in his eyes, against the ingratitude of the child of his bosom and the stinginess of the wealthy old man who had married her). Nevertheless, the pair had acted kindly toward Costigan, settling a small pension on him, paid regularly, and forestalled with even greater regularity by poor Cos. The payment dates were always well known to his friend at the Fielding's Head, to whom the honest Captain repaired, bank-notes in hand, calling loudly for change amidst the full harmonic meeting. "I think ye'll find that note won't be refused at the Bank of England, Cutts, my boy," Captain Costigan would say. "Bows, have a glass? Ye needn't stint yourself to-night, anyhow; and a glass of punch will make ye play con spirito." For he was lavishly free with his money

In Which the Printer's Devil Knocks at the Door

Arthur Pendennis, amidst the humdrum delights of his bachelor existence, suddenly sensed a shadow lengthening over his feast. His coin was running dangerously thin. A substantial chunk had vanished into the realm of the club subscription, followed closely by the heavy price tags on his new furniture. Now, standing before the final five-pound note in his wallet, he faced a terrifying silence: there was no replacement currency on the horizon. For a boy raised to believe he was a prince—or at the very least a cherished infant fed by a loving mother—such a fiscal cliff was a shock akin to falling from a tree.

His companion, Warrington, remained blissfully ignorant of the depth of this abyss. To Warrington, Pen appeared a creature of pure aristocracy: an only child, the heir to a country estate and the whims of a dandy Uncle who dined daily with high society. Pen possessed gold chains and a dressing case worthy of a Duke. Yet, his economy was not that of a prudent merchant, but of a spoiled child. He could not be bothered to tip a waiter twopence; he would hail a cab regardless of rain or wind, invariably paying double the fare; he despised "cleaned gloves" and the concept of saving pennies. If he had been born to a fortune of ten thousand a year, he likely would have been indistinguishable from his current self, a man who would happily hand his last shilling to a beggar with a pitiful face or a pair of crying children. It was a sumptuous nature, perhaps, that refused to look twice at a ledger, driven by a generous heart and a vanity that relished the praise of even the lowest cab driver.

Pen had not deemed it necessary to confess his financial ruin to Warrington. While his friend knew of his wild college days, the specifics of his mother's dwindling resources and his own catastrophic spending habits had been kept in the dark.

The truth finally spilled out as Pen stared grimly at the change for the last five-pound note lying on the pub tray beside Warrington's pot of ale.

"It is the last rose of summer," Pen lamented, his voice heavy with theatrical despair. "Its blooming companions have fled long ago; and behold, the last flower of the garland has dropped its leaves!" He recounted the tale of his mother's failing funds, his own follies, and Laura's boundless generosity. As he spoke, Warrington smoked his pipe, listening with intense gravity.

"Poor impecuniosity will do you good," Warrington declared, tapping ash onto the floor. "For an honest man, nothing is more wholesome than a state of tick. It is an alterative and a tonic. It keeps your moral constitution in a perpetual state of alertness. Think of a rider facing a fence, or a swordsman staring down an opponent; he is forced to look the obstacle squarely in the eye and brace himself to strike or parry. A pinch of necessity brings out your pluck if you have any, and nerves you to wrestle with Fortune. You'll soon discover what you can do without when you've no money to buy it. You won't need new gloves, varnished boots, eau de Cologne, or cabs. You've been bred a molly-coddle, Pen, and spoiled by the ladies. A single man with health and brains who cannot find a livelihood in this world doesn't deserve to breathe its air. Pay your last halfpenny and leap over Waterloo Bridge. Steal a leg of mutton, get transported, and leave the country—you're not fit to live in it."

"You have certainly spoken," Pen admitted, "but how is one to live? There is beef and bread aplenty in England, but it costs money or work. And who will hire my labor? What trade can I perform?"

Warrington erupted in laughter. "Suppose we advertise in the *Times*," he suggested, "seeking an usher's post at a classical and commercial academy. 'A gentleman, B.A. of St. Boniface College, who was plucked for his degree,'"—Pen groaned, "Wishes to impart lessons in classics and mathematics, plus the rudiments of French. He can cut hair, supervise the younger pupils, and play the piano second chair to the Principal's daughters. Address A.P., Lamb Court, Temple.'"

"Go on," Pen muttered, though his curiosity piqued.

"Men adapt to all sorts of vocations," Warrington continued, amused. "Consider Bloundell, a professional blackleg who tours the Continent, picking up young fellows of fashion and fleecing them. Then there is Bob O'Toole, my school chum, who now drives the Ballynafad mail, ferrying honest Jack Finucane's correspondence. I know a doctor's son—forgive the offense—who argued with his guardian over finances. When he reached his last five-pound note, what did he do? He let his mustaches grow, migrated to a provincial town, introduced himself as Professor Spineto, chiropodist to the Emperor of All the Russians, and by performing a miraculous operation on the local newspaper editor, established a reputable practice for three years. He has since reconciled with his family and inherited his father's gallopots."

"Hang the gallopots!" Pen cried. "I cannot drive a coach, trim corns, or cheat at cards. That is all you propose."

"Yes," Warrington insisted, "there is our own correspondent. Every man has his secrets. Before you revealed your finances, I believed you a man of fortune; your airs and demeanor suggested it. Clearly, you cannot draw further from your mother. You must settle accounts with that trump card of a girl, Laura. Here is to Laura! And you must carry a hod rather than ask for a shilling from home."

"But how does one earn?" Pen asked, desperate.

"How do I live, pray?" Warrington countered. "On my younger brother's allowance, Pendennis? I have secrets of my own, my boy." His expression sobered. "I squandered that allowance five years ago. If I had taken my own life a little sooner, it might have been better. I have played off my own bat ever since. I require little money. When my purse is empty, I return to work to fill it, then lounge like a serpent or an Indian until I have digested the wealth. Observe," Warrington added, displaying a long, gaunt purse containing but a few sovereigns. "I begin to feel hollow."

"And how do you replenish it?" Pen pressed.

"I write," Warrington whispered, blushing slightly. "I prefer not to announce it openly. I dislike having it said that George Warrington writes for bread. However, I contribute to the *Law Reviews*; examine these articles." He flipped through several sheets. "Occasionally, I write for a newspaper where a friend serves as editor." Leading Pen to the club later, he requested a file of the *Dawn*, pointing silently to a few pieces. Pen read them with delight, recognizing immediately the strong thoughts, curt periods, biting satire, and scholarly depth of his friend's prose.

"I am not up to this," Pen confessed, admiring his friend's talents. "I know little of politics or history, and my Latin is merely a smattering. I cannot soar on wings like yours."

"But you can on your own, my boy, which are lighter and perhaps soar higher," Warrington replied kindly. "Those little scraps and verses I've seen display a rare natural gift. Need not blush, you conceited young jackanapes. You've harbored this thought for a decade. You possess the sacred flame—a touch of real poetic fire, I suspect. Our oil lamps are mere candles compared to it. You are a poet, Pen, my boy." Stretching out his broad hand, he clapped Pen on the shoulder.

Arthur was so overwhelmed by the compliment that tears welled in his eyes. "How kind you are, Warrington!" he exclaimed.

"I like you, old boy," Warrington said. "I was terribly lonely in chambers, craving someone. Your honest face somehow pleased me. I liked the way you laughed at Lowton—that poor, good little snob. And, frankly, the reason I cannot tell—it is simply that I am alone in the world, sir, and I needed someone to keep me company." A glimmer of profound kindness and melancholy passed through Warrington's dark eyes.

Pen was too elated by his newfound identity to notice his friend's sorrow. "Thank you, Warrington," he said, "for your friendship and your words. I have often suspected I was a poet. I will be one—I am one, as you say, though the world may disagree." He hesitated. "Was it *Ariadne in Naxos* you favored? I wrote it at eighteen."

Warrington roared with laughter. "Young goose! Of all the miserable, weak rubbish I've ever attempted, *Ariadne in Naxos* is the most mawkish and disgusting. The Prize Poem is so pompous and feeble I'm surprised it didn't win the medal. Do you imagine you are a serious poet, preparing to supplant Milton and Aeschylus? Do you intend to become a Pindar, absurd little tom-tit, believing you possess the strength and pinions of the Theban eagle soaring supreme through the azure fields? No, my boy, I think you can manage a magazine article and compose a decent stanza; that is my verdict

The imprisoned Captain Shandon, a man whose prospects were as tight as his waistcoat, announced with the crisp, emphatic cadence of a man selling ice cream in July that the hour had finally arrived for the gentlemen of England to unite. He spoke of defending their "glorious order" against a tempest of foreign revolutions, domestic radicals, and the treachery of mill-owners and cotton lords who, he claimed, gulled the masses.

"The ancient monarchy was insulted!" the Captain proclaimed, his voice rising in a crescendo that echoed off the damp prison walls. "By a ferocious republican rabble! The Church was deserted by envious dissenters and undermined by stealthy infidels!" He painted a dire picture where good institutions, once the pillars of glory, now sat defenseless against men who believed in nothing holy, knew no history, and followed no law save the signal to plunder given by their leaders.

As the Captain ranted, Mr. Shandon interjected with a flourish, citing the fall of Saint Louis's monarchy as proof that kings mistrust their gentlemen, while England's survival owed everything to the belief the people held in their class. "It was because we were headed by gentlemen," Shandon declaimed, "that the Eagles retreated before us from the Donro to the Garonne! It was a gentleman who broke the line at Trafalgar and swept the plains of Waterloo!"

At the mention of Waterloo, Bungay, the publisher, nodded with a knowing smirk and winked, clearly enjoying the spectacle. Warrington, however, couldn't hold back; he burst into laughter.

"You see how our venerable friend Bungay is affected?" Shandon whispered slyly, glancing down from his papers. "That's your true sort of test. I've used the Duke of Wellington and the Battle of Waterloo a hundred times, and I never knew the Duke to fail."

The Captain then confessed, with a candour that bordered on the absurd, that until now, the gentlemen of England had left their political interests to professionals, just as they left their estates to stewards. "That time," Shandon insisted, "is gone by. The gentlemen must be their own champions. The enemies of our order are brave, strong, numerous, and uncompromising. We must meet them in the field, not be belied by hireling advocates from Grub Street publishing gazettes from Whitehall."

"That's a dig at Bacon's people, Mr. Bungay," Shandon noted, turning to the publisher.

Bungay immediately clapped his stick on the floor with a sharp *clack*. "Hang him! Pitch into him, Captain!" he cried with exultation. Turning to Warrington, he wagged his dull head vehemently. "For a slashing article, sir, there's nobody like the Captain—not-o-body like him."

The prospectus writer continued, declaring that certain gentlemen, whose names were withheld for "obvious reasons," intended to launch a journal. These men, proud and loyal to their Sovereign and Church, sought to uphold the traditions of the "Gentlemen of England"—a phrase Bungay insisted be printed in large capitals.

"What's that?" asked Mr. Bungay, confused.

"An ancestor of mine sealed it with his sword-hilt," Pen declared with grave solemnity.

"It's the Habeas Corpus, Mr. Bungay," Warrington corrected. To which the publisher merely yawned, muttered "All right, I dare say," and urged the Captain to proceed.

Shandon rallied on, speaking of Runnymede and the readiness to defend liberty with sword and pen. "Bravo!" cried Warrington. Even the little child standing nearby seemed entranced, while the lady worked silently beside her, looking at the scene with fond admiration.

"Come here, little Mary," Warrington said, patting the child's fair curls with his large, rough hand. But the girl shrank back, preferring to seek refuge at Pen's knee to play with his fine watch-chain. Pen, a man of simple heart concealed beneath a shy and pompous exterior, was deeply pleased. She clambered onto his lap, allowing her father to finish reading the program.

"You were laughing," the Captain chided Warrington, "about 'the obvious reasons' I mentioned. Now, I'll show ye what they are, ye unbelieving heathen." He flourished his paper, explaining that they numbered influential friends in both Houses of Parliament and possessed sources of intelligence unavailable to any other journal. "The Pall Mall Gazette," he declared, "will be written by gentlemen for gentlemen. Its conductors speak to the classes in which they live and were born."

Shandon then descended with modesty upon the literary and fashionable departments of the proposed paper, conducted by men famous at the Universities and known at the Clubs. He pointed out delicately to potential advertisers that there would be no medium like the *Pall Mall Gazette* for promoting sales, nor any space for the "higher circles" to be ignored. He called upon the nobility, the baronetage, the clergy, and the matrons of England to rally around the "good old cause." At the conclusion of this grandiose speech, Bungay, waking from a second snooze, simply grunted, "It's all right."

Once the reading ended, the gentlemen discussed the political and literary management of the paper. Bungay sat listening, nodding as if understanding, though his opinions were remarkably simple: he believed the Captain could write the best "smashing article" in England. Whether the Captain wrote a letter of Junius or copied the Church Catechism, Bungay considered it a smash. He pocketed the manuscripts with satisfaction, paid for them, and even gave little Mary a penny as he departed.

Afterwards, Shandon, leading the conversation with a jaunty, fashionable air, complimented his two guests, presuming them to be of the *beau monde*. He spoke of the characters of the day and great personages with easy familiarity, telling anecdotes of private lives and entertainments as if he lived among them. Pen was amused to hear the shabby prisoner in a tattered dressing-gown talk so fluently of the great. Mrs. Shandon, delighted, believed every tale he spun, including one about an invitation to the Earl of X, which she treasured in her workbox.

Eventually, Mr. Bungay rose to take his leave. Warrington and Pen followed, though the publisher wished to linger. Shandon, with a rueful grin, assured Pen he was always at home and delighted to see him. "I'll see you to my park-gate, gentlemen," the Captain said, seizing his hat despite a deprecatory look from his wife.

Clad in shabby slippers, the Captain shuffled out before his guests, leading the way through the dismal passages of the prison. As he reached the wicket, his hand fiddled nervously with his waistcoat pocket, where Bungay's five-pound note lay. He took his leave of the three gentlemen, leaving Pen greatly relieved to be out of the horrid place and back on the flags of Farringdon Street.

Inside, Mrs. Shandon resumed her work by the window, watching the Captain run rapidly with two men toward the prison tavern. Her hopes of sharing a dinner of meat and salad with her husband and little Mary had vanished. Instead, he would spend the night playing cards or drinking in someone else's room, returning later with glazed eyes and a slight reel, requiring her nursing.

Despondent, Mrs. Shandon made herself some tea from the cupboard. In those moments of varied pain, the tea-pot played a significant role as a confidante, witnessing countless tears and secret sorrows. Finally, she and little Mary sat down to drink their tea together while the Captain went out to take his pleasure.

Just then, Mr. Jack Finucane, a townsman of Captain Shandon's, appeared. He found the wife and child, bringing a sweetmeat for the little girl. Jack, who thought Shandon the greatest of geniuses, was always ready to run errands, manage finances, or handle debts for the embarrassed Irish gentleman. He had never missed a day visiting his patron, acting as an aide-de-camp to a ragged staff of insolvent dependents who shared in the chief's rough fortunes.

Outside, as the group walked away, Bungay remarked, "He won't have that five-pound note very long, I bet a guinea." And the publisher proved right. When Mrs. Shandon emptied her husband's pockets later, she found only a couple of shillings and a few halfpence. Shandon had given a pound to one follower, sent a leg of mutton and potatoes to a poor acquaintance, paid a tavern bill, and lost several half-crowns at cards. The night had left him as poor as the morning.

Later, after quitting Shandon, the gentlemen conversed. Warrington reiterated to Bungay that Pen was a high fellow of great genius, well-connected with the peerage. Bungay agreed, expressing happiness to do business with Mr. Pendennis and hoping to dine with him soon. With mutual politeness, they parted.

Back in the lodging-house, Pen mused on the strange figure of Shandon—a man of multifarious accomplishments, undeniable talent, and humour, yet confined to a gaol for half his time and hanging on a bookseller's coat-tails when free.

"I am a bookseller's hanger-on," Warrington laughed, "and you're going to try your p

Captain Shandon, spurred on by his formidable wife—who rarely interfered with business matters but struck fear into the hearts of debt collectors—had successfully lobbied for John Finucane, Esquire, of the Upper Temple, to become the sub-editor of the *Pall Mall Gazette*. The spirited proprietor of the *Daily Journal* promptly conferred this honor upon Mr. Finucane. Truly, Finucane deserved such kindness; his devotion to the Captain and his family was legendary, matched only by his desperate eagerness to render a service. It was within Finucane's chambers that Captain Shandon, in days gone by, hid whenever danger lurked or bailiffs prowled. Those rooms became so famous a sanctuary that the sheriff's officers began visiting the Captain on Finucane's staircase with the same regularity as they did at his own front door. It was likewise to these chambers that poor Mrs. Shandon flocked repeatedly to unburden herself of her sorrows and scheme ways to rescue her adored husband. How many meals Finucane provided for her and the little child! To visit his modest rooms with such a distinguished lady was an immense honor. As she descended the staircase, her veil drawn low, Fin would lean over the balustrade, watching her retreat, perhaps secretly wishing that some rogue "Temple Lovelace" might ambush her, so he could rush forward to break the villain's bones—a fantasy born of genuine, if slightly theatrical, affection. It was a sincere delight to Mrs. Shandon that arrangements were finally made for her champion to serve as her husband's aide-de-camp in the newspaper world.

Finucane would have sat with Mrs. Shandon past the hour when prisons usually closed, often witnessing the tucking away of little Mary, who slept in a crib in the room. Before saying grace, Finucane, though a Roman Catholic himself, had offered a sympathetic "Amen" to the child's prayers asking for her father's blessing. However, destiny intervened: he had an appointment with Mr. Bungay concerning the future of their paper, necessitating a quiet dinner discussion. At precisely six o'clock, he departed from Mrs. Shandon, yet immediately appeared at the Fleet Prison the following morning. For this occasion, he had dressed in his finest garb and ornaments; while cheap in price, they blazed with brilliant colors. In his pocket lay four pounds and two shillings—the entirety of his weekly salary at the *Daily Journal*, minus two shillings spent on a pair of gloves purchased en route to the prison.

Having "cut his mutton" with Mr. Bungay, as the latter grandiloquently termed it, Finucane had previously met with Mr. Trotter, Bungay's reader and business manager, at Dick's Coffee-House. They had delved deeply into the strategy for conducting the *Pall Mall Gazette*. With masterful flair, Finucane outlined the sub-editorial architecture: the precise typography required for various articles, the reporters needed for the markets, the turf, the ring, church intelligence, and fashionable gossip. He knew intimately the gentlemen cultivating these diverse fields of knowledge and knew their weekly earnings. Furthermore, he was a connoisseur of a thousand dodges and ingenious economic contrivances by which spirited capitalists could save coin. He simultaneously dazzled and mystified Mr. Bungay, who was notoriously slow of comprehension, by the rapidity of his calculations scribbled on paper as they sat in the box. Bungay later confessed to his subordinate, Mr. Trotter, that the Irishman seemed remarkably clever.

Now, having impressed Mr. Bungay, the faithful Finucane maneuvered subtly toward his ultimate objective: the liberation of his admired friend and chief, Captain Shandon. He knew to the exact shilling the amount of detainers against the Captain at the Fleet Porter's Lodge and, indeed, professed to know all his debts, though this was impossible, for no man in England—not even the Captain himself—truly knew his liabilities. Finucane pointed out Shandon's existing engagements and argued persuasively that the Captain would work far better if released from confinement. Though Mr. Bungay initially denied this, claiming that a locked-up Captain was always "sure to be found at home," whereas a free one vanished like smoke, Finucane played his final card. He painted a vivid picture of Mrs. Shandon pining away and the child falling ill within the gloom of the prison. This emotional appeal proved decisive; the publisher agreed to consider the matter if Mrs. Shandon visited him in the morning. The colloquy concluded with a second round of brandy-and-water. Although Finucane, holding four guineas, would have happily settled the tavern bill, Bungay declared, "No, sir, this is my affair, sir, if you please. James, take the bill, and eighteenpence for yourself," handing the necessary funds to the waiter. Thus, when Finucane retired to his chambers in the Temple after dinner, he found himself actually possessing his full week's salary on the following Saturday morning.

He bestowed a wink upon Mrs. Shandon, so knowing and joyful, that the kind creature sensed imminent good news. Hastening to retrieve her bonnet and shawl, she asked if Finucane might accompany her for a walk, granting her some fresh air. Little Mary leaped for joy at the prospect of a holiday, for Finucane never neglected to procure toys or tickets to shows, carrying newspaper orders for London diversions in his pocket to amuse the child. Indeed, he loved them with all his heart and would willingly have sacrificed his rambling brains to serve them.

"May I go, Charley?" Mrs. Shandon asked, referring to the Captain, "or shall I stay with you? You're poorly, dear, this morning. He's got a headache, Mr. Finucane. He suffers from headaches, and I persuaded him to stay in bed."

"Go along with you, and Polly," the Captain replied with perfect good humor, despite writing. "Jack, take care of 'em. Hand me over Burton's Anatomy, and leave me to my abominable devices." He frequently drew upon that wonderful repertory of learning, extracting Greek and Latin quotations from it for his public writings.

So Fin gave his arm to Mrs. Shandon, and Mary skipped down the passages, through the gate, and into the free air. The journey from Fleet Street to Paternoster Row was short. Upon reaching Mr. Bungay's shop, Mrs. Bungay was entering via the private door, clutching a paper parcel and a manuscript volume bound in red—an account of her butcher transactions. She was attired in a gorgeous shot-silk dress flamed with red and purple, wearing a yellow shawl, red flowers in her bonnet, and a brilliant light-blue parasol.

Mrs. Shandon wore an old black watered silk; her bonnet had seen fewer brilliant days of prosperity than its owner, yet she possessed an innate dignity that made her look like a lady regardless of her attire. The two women curtsied to one another, each according to her fashion.

"I hope you're pretty well, mum?" said Mrs. Bungay.
"It's a very fine day," said Mrs. Shandon.
"Won't you step in, mum?" said Mrs. Bungay, staring so intently at the child that she nearly frightened her.

"I—I came about business with Mr. Bungay," stammered timid Mrs. Shandon, "I—I hope he's pretty well?"
"If you go to see him in the counting-house, couldn't you leave your little gurl with me?" Mrs. Bungay asked in a deep, tragic voice, extending a finger toward the child.
"I want to stay with mamma!" cried little Mary, burying her face in her mother's dress.
"Go with this lady, Mary, my dear," said the mother gently.
"I'll show you some pretty pictures," Mrs. Bungay cooed, her voice momentarily softening before turning to the ominous tone of an ogress. Opening her brown-paper parcel, she revealed choice sweet biscuits, a treat Mrs. Bungay savored after her wine. Little Mary, entranced, followed the attraction, and the whole party entered the private entrance, leading to a side door into Mr. Bungay's commercial apartments. Here, as the child prepared to separate from her mother, her courage faltered, and she clung again to the maternal petticoat. Seeing the look of disappointment in Mrs. Bungay's face, the kind Mrs. Shandon graciously offered, "If you will let me, I will come up too, and sit for a few minutes." Thus, the three females ascended the stairs together. A second biscuit charmed little Mary into perfect confidence, and within moments she prattled away without restraint.

Meanwhile, Faithful Finucane found Mr. Bungay in a mood far sterner than the previous night, when two-thirds of a bottle of port and two large glasses of brandy-and-water had warmed the publisher's soul into generosity. His impetuous wife had rebuked him upon his return home. She ordered that no relief be given to the Captain, dismissing him as a "good-for-nothing fellow" whom money could not help. She disapproved of the entire plan for the *Pall Mall Gazette*, predicting that Bungay would lose his money as they had lost it over the way at the Whitehall Journal. Her decree was absolute: let Shandon remain in prison and do his work; it was the best place for him. In vain did Finucane plead, promise, and implore; his friend Bungay had received an hour's lecture in the morning

On the appointed day, Pen and Warrington presented themselves at Mr. Bungay's door in Paternoster Row. They avoided the public entrance, where booksellers' lads swarmed with sacks of volumes and timid aspirants clutched virgin manuscripts hoping for a sale to Sultan Bungay. Instead, they sought the private door, the exit for the splendid Mrs. Bungay, who would soon mount her chaise, settle comfortably on cushions, and cast looks of defiant superiority at Mrs. Bacon's windows directly opposite—a residence currently occupied by a chaise-less woman.

On such occasions, usually wroth at her sister-in-law's ostentation, Mrs. Bacon would fling up her drawing-room sash and stare out at the chaise with her four children, sending a silent message akin to, "Behold these four darlings! Flora Bungay! This is precisely why I cannot drive in a carriage; you would give a coach-and-four just to have the same excuse." It was with these verbal arrows that Emma Bacon shot at Flora Bungay as she sat in her chariot, envying the motherhood and despising the childlessness.

As Pen and Warrington approached Bungay's threshold, a carriage and a cab pulled up simultaneously before Bacon's door. Old Dr. Slocum descended heavily from the first vehicle; his equipage was as ponderous as his style, yet both produced a fine, sonorous effect upon the local publishers. From the cab emerged a couple of dazzling white waistcoats.

Warrington chuckled. "You see, Bacon has his dinner-party as well. That is Dr. Slocum, author of 'Memoirs of the Poisoners.' You would hardly recognize our friend Hoolan in that gallant white waistcoat. As for Doolan, he is one of Bungay's men, and faith, here he steps out." Indeed, Messrs. Hoolan and Doolan had arrived from the Strand in the same cab, tossing a coin to decide who should pay the shilling. Mr. Doolan emerged from the other side, arrayed in black with a large pair of white gloves spread wide upon his hands, which he regarded with evident pleasure.

Inside the passage of Mr. Bungay's house, porters in evening coats and gentlemen sporting gloves as large as Doolan's, yet crafted from the famous Berlin web, bustled about receiving hats and coats and shouting names up the stairs. Some guests had already arrived, but there was only one representative of her own charming sex present: Mrs. Bungay, clad in red satin and a turban. She curtsied to each newcomer as they entered the drawing-room, though her mind was clearly preoccupied by extraneous thoughts. The fact was, Mrs. Bacon's rival dinner-party was disturbing her equilibrium. As soon as she received each individual of her own company, Flora Bungay retreated to the embrasure of the window, from where she could rake over the carriages of Emma Bacon's friends as they rattled up the Row. The sight of Dr. Slocum's large carriage, drawn by gaunt job-horses, crushed Flora completely; no hack cabs had dared approach her door that day.

The gathering comprised several literary gentlemen, though Pen remained unaware of their specific identities. There was Mr. Bole, the real editor of the magazine wherein Mr. Wagg served as nominal chief; Mr. Trotter, who, having burst onto the world as a poet of a tragic and suicidal cast, had since subsided into one of Mr. Bungay's back shops as a reader; and Captain Sumph, an ex-beau reader about town, related in some indistinct manner to Literature and the Peerage. He was said to have written a book once, to have been a friend of Lord Byron, and to be related to Lord Sumphington. Anecdotes of Byron formed his staple fare, and he seldom spoke without invoking that poet or his contemporaries. "I remember poor Shelley, at school being sent up for good for a copy of verses, every line of which I wrote, by Jove!" he would proclaim, or recall offering to bet gamba with Byron at Missolonghi. Pen observed that this gentleman was listened to with great attention by Mrs. Bungay; his tales of the aristocracy, of which he was a middle-aged member, delighted the publisher's lady, making him seem almost a greater man than the great Mr. Wagg in her eyes. Had he arrived in his own carriage, Mrs. Bungay would have ordered Mr. Bungay to purchase any volume from his pen.

Mr. Bungay circulated among his guests with much cordiality. "How are you, sir? Fine day, sir. Glad to see you year, sir. Flora, my love, let me have the honour of introducing Mr. Warrington to you. Mr. Warrington, Mrs. Bungay; Mr. Pendennis, Mrs. Bungay. Hope you've brought good appetites with you, gentlemen. You, Doolan, I know have, for you've always had a deuce of a twist."

"Lor, Bungay!" exclaimed Mrs. Bungay.

"Faith, a man must be hard to please, Bungay, who can't eat a good dinner in this house," Doolan retorted, winking and stroking his lean chops with his large gloves, while making flattering appeals to Mrs. Bungay that the honest woman refused with scorn. "She couldn't abide that Doolan," she confided to her friends. Indeed, all his flattery failed to win her favor.

As they conversed, Mrs. Bungay surveyed the street from her window when a magnificent vision of an enormous grey cab-horse appeared, nearing rapidly. Visible behind it were a pair of white reins held by small white gloves, a pale face richly decorated with a chin-tuft, and the bobbing head of an exiguous groom. These bright things delighted Mrs. Bungay. "The Honourable Percy Popjoy's quite punctual, I declare," she announced, sailing to the door to await the nobleman's arrival.

"It's Percy Popjoy," said Pen, looking out the window as an individual in extremely lacquered boots descended from the swinging cab. Indeed, it was that young nobleman, Lord Falconet's eldest son, who had come to dine with his publisher of the Row.

"He was my fag at Eton," Warrington remarked. "I ought to have licked him a little more." He and Pen had engaged in some bouts at the Oxbridge Union debates, in which Pen had enjoyed considerable success against Percy, who presently appeared with his hat under his arm and a look of indescribable good-humour and fatuity in his round, dimpled face. Upon this visage, Nature had burst forth with a chin-tuft but, exhausted by the effort, had left the rest of the countenance bare of hair.

"The temporary groom of the chambers bawled out, 'The Honourable Percy Popjoy,'" causing that gentleman some discomposure at hearing his title announced.

"What did the man want to take away my hat for, Bungay?" he asked the publisher. "Can't do without my hat—wanted it to make my bow to Mrs. Bungay. How well you look, Mrs. Bungay, today. Haven't seen your carriage in the Park: why haven't you been there? I missed you; indeed, I did."

"I'm afraid you're a sad quiz," said Mrs. Bungay.

"Quiz! Never made a joke in my—" hullo! who's here? "How d'ye do, Pendennis? How d'ye do, Warrington? These are old friends of mine, Mrs. Bungay. I say, how the doose did you come here?" he asked the two young men, turning his lacquered heels upon Mrs. Bungay, who, respecting her husband's two young guests now that she discovered they were intimates of a lord's son, softened slightly.

"What! do they know him?" she asked rapidly of Mr. B.

"High fellers, I tell you—the young one related to all the nobility," said the publisher, and both ran forward, smiling and bowing, to greet almost as great personages as the young lord himself—no less characters, indeed, than the great Mr. Wenham and the great Mr. Wagg, who were now announced.

Mr. Wenham entered, wearing the usual demure look and stealthy smile with which he commonly surveyed the tips of his neat, little, shining boots, a gaze he rarely brought to bear upon the person addressing him. Conversely, Wagg's white waistcoat spread out with profuse brilliancy; his burly, red face shone resplendent above it, lighted up with the thoughts of good jokes and a good dinner. He liked to make his entry into a drawing-room with a laugh, and when he departed at night, to leave a joke exploding behind him. No personal calamities or distresses (of which that humourist had his share in common with the unjocular part of mankind) could altogether keep his humour down. Whatever his griefs might be, the thought of a dinner rallied his great soul; and when he saw a lord, he saluted him with a pun.

Wenham advanced with a smug smile and whisper to Mrs. Bungay, looking at her from under his eyes and showing her the tips of his shoes. Wagg declared she looked charming and pushed straight at the young nobleman, whom he called Pop, instantly relating a funny story seasoned with *gros sel*. He was delighted to see Pen as well,

The new journal, *The Pall Mall Gazette*, launched with a flourish that seemed to promise empire. Rumors swirled like smoke behind a carriage wheel—an "influential political party" backing the paper, and a roster of contributors heavy with "great names." Whether these tales were true remained a mystery locked behind the editor's door, but the truth, as it turned out, was far more theatrical than the gossip suggested. That stately article on foreign policy, whispered to be the brainchild of a Noble Lord intimately tied to the Foreign Office, was actually penned by Captain Shandon, slumped comfortably in a leather armchair at the Bear and Staff public house near Whitehall Stairs. The printer's boy, acting as a detective, had tracked him down, joining forces with Shandon's temporary resident, the literary Mr. Bludyer. Similarly, the fiery dispatches on finance, universally attributed to a towering figure in the House of Commons, were the work of Mr. George Warrington of the Upper Temple, a man whose pen was sharper than his sword.

Perhaps the paper did dabble with powerful patrons; Percy Popjoy, son of the Lord Falconet, was frequently spotted climbing the stairs to Warrington's chambers, suggesting a flow of information that smelled distinctly of intrigue. The *Gazette* also became a haven for poetry that was weak in thought but thunderous in delivery, signed with the initials "P.P." These verses, alongside a review of Percy's novel that praised it with an outrageously gushing fervor, set the tone for a publication unafraid to shout.

Yet, in the political trenches, Mr. Pen kept his distance, choosing instead to throw himself into the literary fray. With offices nestled in Catherine Street, Strand, the *Gazette* became Pen's playground. He arrived daily with manuscripts tucked safely in his pockets, buzzing with the electric thrill of a debutant entering a ballroom—the dizzying sensation of seeing one's words printed on page, the arrogant belief that the ink was already causing a ripple in the pond of the world.

At the helm of the operations sat Mr. Jack Finucane, the sub-editor, a man who treated journalism with the casual brutality of a butcher at a market stall. Armed with paste and scissors, he was the architect of the *Journal*. His eagle eye swept the kingdom, devouring every paragraph concerning fashion, from the death of a duke to the menu of a midnight supper. He ferreted out scandalous tidbits from obscure provincial broadsheets and distant Scottish and Irish dailies, weaving them into a tapestry of upper-class intrigue. It was a comically poignant image: Jack Finucane, Esquire, dressed in trousers so tattered they resembled a bird's nest and sleeves rolled up to reveal skin stained with porter and grease, sitting on a stool with a slab of meat and a tankard of dark beer. Yet, he recounted the feasts of ambassadors and the galas of the elite with the gravitas of a court historian, describing the brilliance of the season as if he had been dining at the center of it all. This absurd dissonance between his ragged attire and his grandiose descriptions amused Pen immensely. Having fled his humble native village, Jack knew little of the world outside the tavern parlors he frequented, yet his writing made it look as though he lived in the bow-windows of White's Club. While occasional errors crept into his copy, the London chiefs wisely restricted his contributions to the *Ballinafad Sentinel*, leaving the delicate *Gazette* to his colleagues' careful hands.

Pen, meanwhile, applied himself with the zeal of a convert. Possessing a mind for desultory reading and a sharp, mischievous sense of humor, his reviews delighted both his boss and the paying public. He prided himself on earning his keep, and the *Gazette* found a loyal readership among the ladies at Fairoaks and the literary-minded young spinster at Clavering Park. Even Old Doctor Portman, the widow's stern guardian, offered his seal of approval, declaring that the lad possessed spirit, taste, and the air of a gentleman, if not quite the scholarship of a university professor.

The real turning point for Pen came when he stepped into the Regent Club. There, amidst the clinking of glasses and the puffing of cigars, he walked in on a gathering of the titled and the wealthy: Wenham, Lord Falconet, and others of high standing. They were not discussing state affairs or horse racing; they were dissecting the latest issue of the *Pall Mall Gazette*. Specifically, they were laughing over an article by "Pen" that savaged the recent travelogue of the Countess of Muffborough. Written in a mix of French and English, her book was a mess of blunders, and Pen, with the joy of a schoolboy finding a typo in a teacher's essay, had torn it apart with delightful mockery. He danced around his subject, exposing the Countess's faults with a mock solemnity that was nothing short of surgical. Every word was polite, every insult wrapped in velvet, yet the Countess was laid low.

Wenham's face, usually a mask of bilious indifference, twisted with malicious glee as he read the critique. Lord Falconet, who hadn't been invited to the Countess's parties in a year, roared with laughter. Lord Muffborough, Wenham's lifelong rival, joined in, and together they toasted Pen. For the first time, the Major felt a strange warmth in his chest. Until then, the letters from Fairoaks warning of "Arthur's constant and severe literary occupations" undermining the boy's health had seemed beneath his dignity. But here, surrounded by the great, Pen was celebrated. When the oracle Wenham pronounced the boy a genius, and Lord Falconet nodded in agreement, and even the notorious Lord Steyne declared the piece "capital"—promising the Countess would writh like a whale under a harpoon—the Major's attitude shifted instantly. "By gad!" he boomed, "the young rascal had stuff in him! He'll do something!" Trembling with pride, he sat down to write to the widow, relaying the accolades, and then addressed Pen directly, inviting him to eat chops and promising an introduction to Lord Steyne, who, the Major noted, enjoyed all manner of human follies, wits, and oddities equally. Pen tossed the letter to Warrington, wondering if the other man cared as much as he did.

Young critics, it seems, possess a prodigious courage. They climb the judgment seat as if it were a ladder to heaven and deliver verdicts on works they barely understand. If Pen had been presented with Macaulay's *History* or Herschel's *Astronomy* at this moment, he likely would have glanced through the volumes, lit a cigar, and given an "august approval" as if he were their superior master.
