With the aid of the *Biographie Universelle* or a quick dip into the British Museum, he could recite dates and names with a mastery that would leave his mother gaping, wondering where the knowledge came from. Two months later, when he read those articles again, the memory of the books would vanish, replaced by a vague confidence. Fortunately, Warrington stood ready to curb this impertinence with a steady dose of ridicule. Without him, Pen might have become a conceit-ridden tyrant of the pen. Shandon, for his part, loved the dash and flippancy of his young aide-de-camp, preferring Pen's light, brilliant flashes over the heavy, ponderous arguments of his older partner.

Though guilty of premature judgment and impertinence, Pen was, above all, an honest critic—a trait that grated on Mr. Bungay, who grumbled bitterly at the lack of bias. A dispute erupted one afternoon between Pen and Captain Shandon. "What have you been doing?" Shandon demanded, his voice rising. "Praising one of Mr. Bacon's books? Bungay is in a frenzy over it!"

Pen's eyes widened. "Are we to praise no book of Bacon's? Or if it is good, must we call it bad?"

"Benevolent publishers do not start critical journals to harm their rivals," Shandon retorted, leaning back. "To benefit themselves, yes, but surely not to lie?"

"To tell the truth, Captain, *ruat coelum*!" Pen insisted.

"And my prospectus?" Shandon sneered. "Was that written with mathematical precision?"

"Pardon me," Pen replied, laughing. "I admit the prospectus was poetic and the 'giant' on the showboard looked larger than the man inside the caravan. But we agreed we need not be too scrupulous. We are the fiddlers playing our tunes; you are the showman."

"And leader of the van," Shandon agreed. "I'm glad your conscience allowed you to play for us."

"But," Pen interjected, straightening his spine with a sense of noble duty, "we are all party men in England. I will stick to my side like a Briton. A fool quarrels with his own nest, but I will hit the enemy as hard as you wish—with fair play, Captain! One cannot tell all the truth, I grant you, but one must tell *nothing* but the truth. I'd rather starve by Jove, never to earn another penny by this redoubted instrument of mine, than strike an unfair blow or rank an opponent below his honest desert."

Shandon, with a fatal good nature, shook his head. "Well, Mr. Pendennis, when we want Bacon smashed, we'll find another hammer." Inside, he

Let us kindly skip ahead a few months in the biography of Mr. Arthur Pendennis. During this interval, many thrilling adventures surely occurred that would have made his autobiography a bestseller, but for the weary reader of *his* memoirs, they would likely prove as dry as dust. Last time we met him, he had officially signed up as a professional "hack"—a term Mr. Warrington, his bosom buddy, uses with relish. Now, imagine the life of a hack, whether legal, literary, clerical, or mercantile. It is a monotonous treadmill of boredom. One day's labor mirrors the next with terrifying precision. A literary man often battles time, his own will, his failing health, his crippling laziness, and his deep-seated hatred for the subject matter he is forced to tackle, much like any other daily drudge. When you need to cash in on Pegasus (assuming he owns no other marketable assets), goodbye to soaring poetry and celestial flights. Pegasus now rises only on schedule, like Mr. Green's balloon, strictly adhering to advertised times and ticket prices. He trots in harness over cobblestones, pulling a cart or cab behind him. Often, Pegasus works with panting sides and trembling knees, and frequently receives a sharp cut from his driver.

Do not, however, pour too much pity on this unfortunate steed. There is no divine exemption for animals—or humans—from labor, sickness, or decay. If Pegasus gets whipped, he often deserves it. I, for one, am prepared to defend my friend George Warrington against the sentimental notion held by poetic enthusiasts: namely, that letters and "genius" are entitled to a tax-free, duty-free existence, exempt from the prosaic grind of bread-winning and paying taxes like everyone else.

So, the *Pall Mall Gazette* was duly launched, and Arthur Pendennis's merits were recognized as those of a flippant, witty, and amusing critic. He toiled away every week, churning out reviews of whatever fell into his department. He certainly wrote with flippancy, but with honesty and to the best of his ability. Perhaps a historian of seventy, who had spent twenty-five years composing a magnum opus, was not treated fairly by a young gentleman who disposed of the material in a couple of days' reading at the British Museum. Or perhaps a poet, sweating over sublime sonnets intended for immortality, was annoyed by two or three dozen snappy lines in Mr. Pen's review where the critic acted like a lord on the bench and the author a trembling suitor. The theatergoers complained bitterly, and he likely was too harsh on them. Yet, little harm was done. Times have changed since then; back then, there were so few truly great historians, poets, or actors that almost none appeared before his critical desk. Those who received a little whipping generally got what was good for them. Not that the judge was any wiser than those he sentenced, nor did he ever fancy himself superior. Pen possessed a robust sense of humor and justice, meaning he lacked an overbearing reverence for his own work. Furthermore, he had his friend Warrington at his elbow—a terrifying critic if Pen grew conceited, and far savagier toward Pen than toward anyone else he tried at his literary assizes.

Through these critical labors and occasional contributions to leading articles, when conscience allowed him to speak his mind without offending the paper, Mr. Pendennis earned four pounds, four shillings weekly, with considerable pain and effort. He also supplied magazines and reviews with his compositions and was rumored, though he preferred silence on the matter, to be the London correspondent of the *Chatteris Champion*, which at the time featured some brilliant metropolitan letters. These endeavors enabled the fortunate youth to earn nearly four hundred pounds a year. On the second Christmas after arriving in London, he actually brought his mother a hundred pounds as a dividend on the debt he owed Laura. Mrs. Pendennis read every word of her son's works, considering him the profoundest thinker and most elegant writer of the day. She viewed his repayment of the hundred pounds as an act of angelic virtue. She feared he was ruining his health and delighted when he described the society he met and the great men of letters and fashion he encountered. This is how all readers understand the worship of sons by mothers, and that charming, simple love with which country women track the careers of their darlings in London. Whether John holds a brief, Tom is invited to a ball, or George meets a famous man at dinner, what joy fills the hearts of mothers and sisters at home in Somersetshire! How eagerly young Hopeful's letters are read and remembered! What a topic for village chatter and friendly congratulations! In the second winter, Pen visited for a very brief spell, cheering the widow's heart and lighting up the lonely house at Fairoaks. Helen had her son entirely to herself; Laura was visiting old Lady Rockminster; the folks of Clavering Park were away; the few old friends, led by Dr. Portman, called on Mr. Pen and treated him with marked respect. Between mother and son, it was pure fondness, confidence, and affection. It was the happiest fortnight of the widow's life, perhaps both theirs. The holiday vanished far too quickly, and Pen returned to the busy world, leaving the gentle widow alone again. She sent Arthur's money to Laura. I do not know why this young lady chose to leave home precisely when Pen was coming, nor whether he felt more piqued or relieved by her absence.

By this time, through his own merits and his uncle's introductions, Pen was reasonably well integrated into London, known in both literary and polite circles. His fashionable reputation served him well; he was considered a gentleman of good present means and better prospects, who wrote for pleasure rather than necessity—a supreme recommendation for a young literary aspirant. Bacon, Bungay & Co. proudly accepted his articles. Mr. Wenham invited him to dinner. Mr. Wagg regarded him favorably. They recounted how they met him at fashionable houses, where he was quite welcome, as they cared little for his current financial standing or future; his appearance and address were impeccable, and he had cultivated a character as a clever fellow. Ultimately, he was invited to one house because he had been seen at another. Thus, a vast array of London life unfolded before the young man. He became familiar with all sorts of people, from Paternoster Row to Pimlico, feeling as much at home at Mayfair dining tables as at the tavern boards where some of his pen-friends assembled.

Brimming with high spirits and curiosity, easily adapting to everyone he met, the young fellow delighted in this strange variety and jumble of humanity, making himself welcome or at least at ease wherever he went. For instance, he might breakfast at Mr. Plover's in the morning alongside a Peer, a Bishop, a parliamentary orator, two blue ladies of fashion, a popular preacher, the author of the latest sensation novel, and the newest lion imported from Egypt or America. Then, he would depart this distinguished society for the back room of the newspaper office, where pens, ink, and wet proofs awaited him. Here sat Finucane, the sub-editor, with the latest news from the Row. Soon, Shandon would arrive, nodding to Pen, and begin scribbling his leading article at the other end of the table, flanked by a pint of sherry—which, when the attendant boy spotted him, was always silently produced for the Captain. Or Mr. Bludyer's roaring voice would echo from the front room, where that truculent critic would seize books from the counter despite the timid protests of Mr. Midge, the publisher. After examining the volumes, he would sell them at his stall, drink and dine on the proceeds in a tavern box, then demand ink and paper to proceed to "smash" the author of his dinner and the novel. Towards evening, Mr. Pen would stroll toward his club to pick up Warrington for a constitutional walk. This exercise cleared the lungs and stimulated an appetite for dinner, after which Pen enjoyed the privilege of bowing at some delightful houses open to him, or exploring the town for amusement. There was the Opera, the Eagle Tavern, a ball in Mayfair, a quiet night with a cigar, a book, and a long chat with Warrington, or a wonderful new song at the Back Kitchen. At this stage of his life, Mr. Pen beheld all sorts of places and men; very likely, he did not realize how much he enjoyed himself until long afterwards, when balls gave him no pleasure, farces failed to make him laugh, tavern jokes produced no excitement, and the loveliest dancer ever to show her ankles failed to stir him from his chair after dinner. At his present mature age, all these pleasures are past, and the times have passed away. It has been but a very few years since... but the time is gone, and most of the men. Bludyer will no longer bully authors or cheat landlords of their scores. Shandon, the learned and thriftless, the witty and unwise, sleeps his last sleep. They buried honest Doolan the other day; never again will he cringe or flatter, nor pull a long bow or empty a whisky-noggin.

The London season was now blooming in full vigor. Fashionable newspapers teemed with information about grand banquets, routs, and balls enlivening the polite world. Our gracious Sovereign was holding levees and drawing-rooms at St. James's. The bow-windows of the clubs were crowded with the heads of respectable, red-faced, newspaper-reading gentlemen. Thousands of carriages trailed along the Serpentine. Squadrons

Few individuals in London possessed a sharper eye for financial nuance than Morgan, the valet, yet even he was somewhat outmatched by the sheer magnitude of rumors surrounding Lady Clavering's estate. Before her actual descent upon the capital, the metropolis buzzed with a legend of unimaginable wealth. Her fortune was whispered to be anchored in indigo factories, opium clippers, and banks swollen with rupees, adorned with diamonds and jewels plucked from the coffers of native princes. These treasures, allegedly secured through loans contracted by the princes' ancestors to Lady Clavering's father, formed a tapestry of debt so vast that her London banker's account reportedly contained so many zeros as to induce a collective gasp of awe from any listener. The air also crackled with the presence of Colonel Altamont, an envoy from the Nawab of Lucknow—a man described as having embraced Mahometanism after surviving a thousand wild adventures—who was currently negotiating the sale of the Nawab's legendary nose-ring diamond, 'the Light of the Dewan.'

This fame preceded the Begum herself, a phenomenon that seemed to validate the boasts of Delolme and Blackstone regarding the British Constitution's admission of merit. If one could not buy a seat among the peers, surely one could buy a seat in polite society. Haughtily exclusive circles, usually guarded by invisible barriers, proved remarkably porous to the influx of gold. The upper orders, far from being the stuffy aristocrats the ignorant imagined, were ready to extend hands, serve dinners, and dance at balls for anyone sufficiently wealthy.

While the Claverings prepared for their arrival, our friend the Chevalier Strong was busy orchestrating the transformation of their town house. His soul rejoiced with a fervor akin to ownership as he directed fashionable upholsterers, rearranging pictures and positioning sofas with the precision of a conductor. Simultaneously, he stocked his own modest chambers in Shepherd's Inn, where a former Spanish legionary, whom he had rescued from a breach at a fort, now served as his body-servant. This loyal man happened to be none other than the favorite of the Nawab, the valiant Colonel Altamont.

Ned Strong, however, was a man of profound discretion. Though he suspected a mysterious connection between Sir Francis Clavering and the Colonel—a link forged perhaps during Clavering's irregular days in India—he refrained from prying. Strong viewed his patron as a man of weak purpose and shifting principles, a poltroon who had likely fallen under the Colonel's sway. Indeed, the Colonel held Clavering in his grip. On one occasion, Altamont, reduced to a state of ill humor, snatched a letter from Clavering and crushed it, declaring, "A hundred, be hanged! I'll have no more shilly-shally. Tell Clavering I'll have a thousand, or by Jove I'll split, and burst him all to atoms... If the money ain't here next Friday at twelve o'clock, I'll have a paragraph in the newspaper on Saturday, and next week I'll blow up the whole concern."

Strong conveyed this ultimatum, and true to his word, Clavering scrambled together the sum on the appointed day. Altamont, behaving with the decorum of a gentleman, settled his bill at the inn and departed for a foreign tour, leaving Strong to suspect that forgery lay at the root of Clavering's predicament. Yet, fate had a cruel sense of timing. Within the year, the Colonel returned, ruined by a disastrous streak at Baden-Baden where the color rose fourteen times in succession. Obligated to draw upon Sir Francis for his return, Clavering complied, though with evident reluctance and private curses wishing the Colonel imprisoned in a German debtor's goal forever.

To manage these subsidies, Clavering relied heavily on Strong, who procured funds and negotiated debts in the shadows of his Shepherd's Inn lodgings. For a man accustomed to trading promises for cash, prosperity often brought the money-lender back sooner than expected. Clavering found it expedient to conduct these financial gymnastics at Strong's address, where bills of exchange were drawn and discounted. Strong, despite his own lack of coin, stood as the drawer for nearly all of Clavering's obligations, parleying with holders to secure extensions or installments. It was a life of gallantry amidst the looming threat of the bailiff and the rope, a existence Strong endured with the ease of a seasoned soldier.

Meanwhile, the stage was set within the Claverings' newly acquired Grosvenor Place. The family toured the "gorgeous" dining room, a space furnished in a "middle-aged style"—a choice Lady Clavering attributed to her own maturity. Despite offering only the remnants of lunch, the room was a study in ostentatious "chastity." High-backed Dutch chairs, a sculptured buffet, a sideboard looted from a Low Country church, and a brass cathedral lamp dominated the space, surrounded by portraits, tapestries, and cardboard battle-axes. Adjacent lay a library filled with busts and solemn bronzes, a sanctuary where Sir Francis smoked cigars and read *Bell's Life* before drifting off to sleep or venturing into the gambling halls of Saint James's.

But the drawing rooms were the true marvels. Carpets so fluffy they silenced footsteps, carpets blooming with giant roses and tulips, and an array of seating ranging from heavy bandy-legs to delicate frames so thin only a sylph could occupy them. The room was cluttered with marvellous gimcracks, porcelain poodles and ducks, and paintings of nymphs and shepherdesses. Gilded cages held squawking cockatoos, while clocks sang tunes and boomed the hours. It was a display of elegance that rivaled the finest tastes of the Lower Roman Empire or the Court of Louis XV.

However, Lady Clavering, for all her wealth, possessed a singular lack of reverence for the splendor surrounding her. To Major Pendennis, she noted the exorbitant cost of the furnishings and warned him against attempting to sit in a gossamer gilt chair, recounting how she had nearly broken one the previous evening. Blanche, the sharp-tongued Sylphide, seized the moment to mock Pen for chatting with his mother, accusing him of enjoying the chaos. Pen, flustered by the mention of Laura Amory and the public knowledge of his past rejection, retreated into a feigned adoration of the furniture, praising Lady Clavering's taste with exaggerated deference.

When the topic of social invitations arose, Lady Clavering revealed that Lady Rockminster had "taken them up." While initially delighted by the prospect of having her balls planned by a figure of such high standing, Lady Clavering quickly asserted her independence, refusing to allow Lady Rockminster to dictate her guest list or sit silently at her own head of the table. She proposed a "snug little party" for the 14th, a compromise that elicited a sigh of relief from Blanche and a diplomatic acceptance from the Major, who preferred quiet gatherings to grand displays.

Later, strolling through the park, the Major questioned Pen about his recent entanglement with Miss Amory. With a dandified air, Pen admitted that "used to" and "is" were vastly different terms regarding a woman's heart. The Major, a veteran of the Cape, advised caution, noting how quickly fortunes and affections changed. He urged Pen to avoid the forward and affected Miss Amory, whose character was, in his view, questionable, and instead focus on the pragmatic path of marriage. "Marry a woman with money," the Major counseled, dismissing the romantic notion of a cottage love in favor of the comfort of a well-cooked dinner. He suggested Pen aim for a "coup," perhaps targeting the daughters of Lady Catherine Martingale, whose mother had once been renowned for her beauty.

Pen, quick to adapt, playfully quoted the ballad of the Devil's Walk about a cottage of gentility, a jest that flew over the Major's head. Warrington, the grumbler, scoffed at Pen's growing puppyhood, but the truth was that the Major found genuine pleasure in watching Pen bloom under the influence of better fortune. The young man's former sulkiness had vanished, replaced by a spirit that glowed like the morning sun, a delightful spectacle for an older man who had long ago tired of clowns and harlequins.

On the appointed afternoon, Major Pendennis and Arthur Pendennis materialized at Sir Francis Clavering's residence with the same lack of premeditation that usually characterized their social engagements. They found the drawing-room occupied only by Sir Francis, his wife, and Captain Strong, whom Arthur received with genuine delight despite his Uncle's profound disdain. The Major, however, viewed Strong with the icy glare reserved for household stewards, muttering internally about the indignity of dining with Clavering's "d—— house-steward."

Tensions, however, dissolved with the arrival of Mr. Welbore Welbore, Clavering's dull but anciently wealthy neighbor. Though he contributed less to the conversation than the footman trailing behind his chair, his status as a seven-thousand-a-year country gentleman instantly calmed the Major's frayed nerves. The company swelled further with the Dowager Lady Rockminster, Lady Agnes Foker, and her son Harry, alongside the absent Mr. Pynsent. Finally, Miss Blanche Amory made her entrance, radiant in a white silk gown that showcased her pearly shoulders to perfection.

"Fairy!" whispered Foker to Pen, who returned the gaze with obvious admiration. Blanche treated Arthur with unusual warmth, extolling the virtues of Fairoaks and expressing a longing return to the country, appearing entirely simple and affectionate. For Harry Foker, unused to the company of women and typically tongue-tied, Blanche was a revelation. Where he had been mute before other ladies, he now spoke incessantly, becoming lively and talkative before the dinner bell even sounded.

While descending the stairs, Harry found himself separated from his object of desire, assigned instead to escort Mrs. Welbore Welbore due to his superior rank as an Earl's grandson. Yet, fate—or maneuvering—placed him back at Blanche's side at the dinner table, sandwiched between her and Pen. Both young men vied for her favor, though the conversation drifted quickly to the arts.

"Have you seen Taglioni in *La Sylphide*, Miss Amory?" Harry asked, ordering soup from the waiter and humming the famous ballet tune. "She clips in the air, doesn't she?"

"Miss Amory is a sylph herself," Pen interjected smoothly.

"What a delightful tenor voice you have, Mr. Foker," Blanche replied, turning her attention to the younger gentleman. "I'd love to sing with you."

Pen recalled similar sentiments from his own youth, wondering with a sneer how many partners she had since taken, but kept his thoughts private. "I should like to hear you again, Miss Blanche. I've never heard a voice I preferred so much."

"I thought you liked Laura's," she teased.

"Laura's is a contralto, often lost in the mix," Pen countered, adopting a tender tone. "I'm weary of London professionals; they sing too loud. One ages quickly here. I only care for the songs of my youth."

"My favorite is English music," Blanche declared. "Get me a saddle of mutton, Mr. Foker."

"I adore English ballads!" agreed Miss Amory. "Sing me one after dinner, will you?"

"Shall I sing you an English song?" she asked, turning to Foker with a look that nearly knocked him over. "If you promise to come up shortly."

"I'll be up fast enough," Foker assured her, accepting his share of claret with gusto. "I'm a domestic character, Miss Amory; simple habits. When I'm pleased, I'm in good humor, aren't I, Pen?" He paused, pointing at a jelly dish. "Pass the jelly with cherries inside. How do they manage those cherries?"

As the ladies retired, Blanche dropped her gloves near Foker and her handkerchief near Pen, distributing her attentions evenly, though perhaps with a slightly warmer encouragement toward the former. As she fled the room, her final glance swept over Strong's broad white waistcoat before locking onto Harry, leaving the charmer to sigh and swallow a bumper of wine.

Dinner concluded early, allowing the ladies to ascend to the drawing-room while the gentlemen lingered over wine. Through the open windows, passersby on the street gazed in at six men in white waistcoats surrounded by decanters and fruit. A policeman patrolled the sidewalk, while a man in the street, gorgeously attired in chains and a shiny boot, leaned against the railings. To the policeman, X, the man appeared dignified, perhaps a Member of Parliament, until he lurched and reeled under the influence of strong liquor.

Inside, Captain Strong was laughing heartily. As his laughter spilled through the window, the street man echoed it with a snigger, slapping his thigh and winking at Jeames, the footman standing guard at the portico. Jeames, initially mesmerized by the moon, was disturbed by the man's disheveled state and shiny attire. Realizing the stranger was not on duty, Jeames signaled Policeman X.

X approached the figure, demanding he move on. The stranger, however, ignored the command, nodding wildly at Strong and shouting, "Hullo! keep your hands off a gentleman!" He accused the house of hiding his friends, identifying Sir Francis, Strong, and a "young chap" involved in a recent row.

"It's that d—— Altamont!" cried Sir Francis from within, springing up with a guilty look. Strong followed suit, rushing out to seize the stranger's arm. A crowd gathered as Strong dragged the intoxicated man away, intending to soothe him in the private sitting room. However, Altamont, in a towering rage, refused entry, insisting, "That ain't the door—that's the dining-room door... I'll go and have some!"

The butler barred the path, but Sir Francis emerged, forcing a smile and welcoming the intruder. Colonel Altamont, revealed to be the Ambassador of the Nawab of Lucknow, reeled into the room, glaring triumphantly at Jeames before sinking into a chair. He alternated between frowning and smiling, hiccupping encomiums on the vintage.

"He's a singular man," Strong noted gravely. "Long resident in a native court; acquires singular habits."

Major Pendennis, dry-eyed, wondered what sort of company he had stumbled into. Harry Foker, meanwhile, whispered to Pen, "It's the man who sings the Malay song at the Back Kitchen." Trying to ingratiate himself, Foker offered a slice of pineapple to the Colonel.

"Pines? I've seen 'em feed pigs on pines," Altamont retorted.

"All the Nawab's pigs are fed on pines," Strong corrected quietly.

As the Colonel settled in, he locked eyes with Pen. "I know you, too, young fellow. Baymouth Ball, by Jingo. Wanted to fight the Frenchman." Laughing in the depths of his intoxication, he squatted his fists, recalling the memory. "What's his name?" he demanded. "Pendennis!"

"Pendennis! Pendennis be hanged!" Altamont roared, thumping the table.

"My name is also Pendennis, sir," the Major replied, his dignity wounded by the intrusion of a drunkard. "Please do not curse it so loudly."

Altamont turned, his eyes suddenly clearing as if sobriety struck him mid-sentence. He adjusted his black wig, staring fiercely at the Major, who met him with the steady gaze of a veteran. After a tense moment, the Colonel began buttoning his coat, stood abruptly, and tottered toward the door, shouting, "Captain Beak! Captain Beak, by jingo!" Strong hurried after him.

Within twenty minutes, the bizarre visitor vanished. Sir Francis appeared pale and agitated, asking the Major if he knew the man. Pendennis identified him as a deserter from the Horse Artillery who had ended up in Indian service. Relief washed over the baronet as the cab, driven by Strong, whisked the Colonel away.

Coffee was served, and the group ascended to the drawing-room. Foker confessed to Pen that the episode was the "rummest go" he had ever witnessed, a judgment Pen chuckled at. True to her promise, Miss Amory played the piano. Foker was enraptured, joining in whenever he recognized the tunes, while Pen feigned conversation with others before Blanche summoned him to the instrument. She sang verses of his own composition, setting them to music herself, or perhaps with the aid of Signor Twankidillo. Pen turned the pages assiduously, wishing he possessed her talent for verse.

Before the ladies departed, the curious incident belowstairs was omitted from conversation, though Arthur noticed a flicker of alarm in Sir Francis's face when Blanche requested Captain Strong. The evening proved dull overall; Welbore slept soundly, and the Major, contrary to his usual habit of endless anecdote, sat silently, watching Blanche perform.

At the conclusion of the evening, the Major expressed regret at the swift passage of time, offering Blanche a high compliment on her vocal prowess and pronunciation. "Your daughter, Lady Clavering, is a perfect nightingale..." he exclaimed, praising her linguistic abilities and beauty. Blanche, though astonished, accepted the praise gracefully.

Walking home with the young men, the Major eyed Foker's cigar smoke with distaste, wishing for the lad to depart. Foker, however, hung stubbornly on, finally releasing the pair at his home in Bury Street.

Since that fatal but delightful night in Grosvenor Place, Mr. Harry Foker's heart had been in a state of agitation so profound that you would hardly believe a man of his supposed philosophical stature could endure it. Consider his history: a boy of early wisdom, a cynic by trade, a gentleman of means who, by all rights of nature and finance, should have grown harder and colder with every passing year. Yet here he stood, a victim to Love, the All-Conqueror, having fallen for a woman.

That night, after bidding farewell to Arthur Pendennis at Lamb Court, the Back Kitchen held no charms for him. The gin-twists and deviled turkeys tasted like ash; the jokes of his cronies fell flat on his ears. Even when Mr. Hodgen, the singer of 'The Body Snatcher,' launched into a new chant even more dreadful and hilarious than the original, Foker offered the requisite "Bravo" as a polite gentleman must, yet he heard not a syllable of the song titled 'The Cat in the Cupboard.' Exhausted, he fled to his private apartments, but the downy pillow offered no respite. Before his eyelids could close, the fever of his soul erupted, and the image of Miss Amory materialized, declaring, "Here I am, I am your princess and beauty; you have discovered me, and shall care for nothing else hereafter."

Suddenly, his former pursuits seemed stale and distasteful. For years, he had barely tolerated the company of women of his own rank, dismissing them as "modest women"—a virtue he found tedious compared to the lively, theatrical charm of the *Mesdemoiselles*. His mother, though tender, failed to amuse him; his cousins, the daughters of the Earl of Rosherville, bored him beyond endurance. One was a blue geologist; another, a cigar-smoking horsewoman; the third, a High Church fanatic who turned her room into an oratory and fasted Fridays. Their father, the Earl of Gravesend, regarded Harry as a vulgar blasphemer, recalling the infamous incident where young Harry poked his uncle in the side with a billiard cue and called him an "old cock." Though the Earl played on with angelic sweetness, the insult lingered. To save his sister Agnes's heart and maintain a profitable financial exchange of IOUs, the Earl kept quiet, but the shame remained.

Furthermore, Harry was technically engaged. From infancy, Lady Ann Milton had been destined for him, a union ordained by her parents and his own father, who, despite being a man of few words, trusted the arrangement implicitly. "She won't have any money, but she's good blood," the old gentleman had reasoned, allowing Harry a final "fling" before the wedding. Consequently, Harry hung a tiny portrait of his cousin amidst his gallery of French prints, racing posters, and actresses, a modest token of a future that now felt like a cage. While Lady Ann stayed mostly at home in Gaunt Square, avoiding the whirlwind social scene her sisters enjoyed, her engagement to Harry was the only story about her that mattered.

When Foker awoke the next morning, Blanche's image glared at him from the bedclothes, her tune echoing in his ears. The room, usually a sanctuary of French art and the charming signatures of opera divas like Miss Calverley and Miss Rougemont, now seemed garish. A letter from Miss Calverley, written in a degage style of spelling and calling him "Hokey-pokey-fokey," lay beside a pile of tickets for Ben Budgeon's night at the Three-cornered Hat. These trivialities, once sources of joy, now filled him with dreary disgust. The prospect of standing dinner with the seventy-seven-year-old Mrs. Clavering at the Elephant and Castle repelled him utterly.

His mother, Lady Agnes, noted the pallor of his cheeks and the gloom of his countenance. She worried about the wicked Spratt's billiard hall and the Back Kitchen, suggesting he limit his wine intake. Harry, however, poured his heart out. He admitted it wasn't the billiards or the Back Kitchen—it was the fact that he was in love. He spoke of the Claverings' magnificent furniture and Sir Francis's agreeable nature, arranging an invitation for them to Grosvenor Street. But as he handed the card to his mother, he noticed a glaring omission.

"You have not asked Miss Whatdyecallem—Miss Emery, Lady Clavering's daughter."
"Oh, that little creature!" Lady Agnes cried. "No! I think not, Harry."
"We must ask Miss Amory," Foker insisted, stammering slightly. "I—I want to ask Pendennis; and—and he's very sweet upon her. Don't you think she sings very well, ma'am?"
Lady Agnes, trusting her son's judgment completely, added Miss Amory's name to the card.

Triumphantly, Harry embraced his mother and retreated to his chambers. There, he lay on his ottoman, brooding silently, devising wild schemes to woo the fair Miss Amory. His valet, Anatole, a polyglot of dubious origin who spoke all languages poorly, observed the disturbance in his master's spirits. As Harry prepared for the day, he became notoriously difficult to please, cursing pantaloons of every stripe and vowing his shirts were too loud. In a moment of sudden vanity, he asked Anatole if he could dress his hair, leading to a comical scramble for tongs that resulted in Harry sporting a head of curls as curly as a negro's.

Dressed in his finest, Harry set off, intending to cancel his appointment with Miss Calverley. However, upon reaching Pelham Corner, he realized he couldn't abandon the gathering of Poyntz and Rougemont. Instead, he commandeered his own carriage, driving straight to Lamb Court to spy on Miss Amory. Peering upward at her pink window curtains in Grosvenor Place, he felt a surge of satisfaction before rushing to Pen's chambers. But Pen was gone, attending to proofs at the printing office. Desperate, Harry raced to the *Pall Mall Gazette*, only to find Pen had left there as well.

Disconsolate, Harry decided to visit Lady Clavering at her own house, delivering two engraved cards to her footman, Jeames, in a speechless agony. The doors closed, shutting him out from the beloved object who was likely within earshot. Defeated, he rode off to Tattersall's, mounting his pony. Just as he was about to depart, Lady Clavering's splendid barouche arrived. Dodging around the Green Park, Harry followed the carriage, hoping to catch a glimpse of his enchantress. He spotted her emerging, clad in gossamer with a pink bonnet and light-blue parasol, accompanied by Miss Amory.

They visited Madame Rigodon's lace shop, Mrs. Wolsey's worsted emporium, and eventually stopped for ices at Hunter's. Harry, riding his pony alongside the carriage, watched them eat large pink ices in the sunshine, enduring the heat and the exertion until they finally departed for the Park. Following them, Harry dashed forward, desperate for a nod of recognition, a mere glance across the ditch where the horsemen assembled. Yet, despite the proximity, he exchanged not a single word with his charmer. Mute, he watched her return to the carriage, driven away amid ironic salutes from other gentlemen.

Back at the club, surrounded by friends like Mr. Eales and Mr. Poyntz, Harry indulged in gossip about Lady Clavering's finances and the scandalous rumors surrounding Sir Francis. When the topic turned to Miss Amory's singing, comparing her to a mermaid, Harry grew flushed and tearful, admitting his lack of scholarly knowledge in Homer and feeling inferior to the poets and musicians who surely surrounded her. "What am I," he thought, "compared to her? She's all soul... I am a poor, ignorant little beggar, good for nothing but Foker's Entire."

Just as his soliloquy reached its nadir, a cheery voice cried out, "Harry, Harry!" Looking up, he beheld his aunt, Lady Rosherville, and two of her daughters. Sitting in the back seat of a large carriage was Lady Ann, his betrothed. A terrible truth struck him: his fate, his arranged future, sat literally behind him. Pale and scared, he muttered greetings about a pressing engagement, waving goodbye as the great carriage rolled away, leaving the little man and his pony to vanish instantly. Inside, the Countess attended to her spaniel, Lady Lucy read sermons, and Lady Ann focused on a new novel, none aware of the storm raging in the hearts of the departing young gentleman.

Carries the Reader both to Richmond and Greenwich

Poor Foker found the dinner at Richmond to be the most dreary entertainment upon which ever mortal man wasted his guineas. "I wonder how the deuce I could ever have liked these people," he thought in his own mind. "Why, I can see the crow's-feet under Rougemont's eyes, and the paint on her cheeks is laid on as thick as Clown's in a pantomime! The way in which that Calverley talks slang, is quite disgusting. I hate chaff in a woman. And old Colchicum! that old Col, coming down here in his brougham, with his coronet on it, and sitting bodkin between Mademoiselle Coralie and her mother! It's too bad. An English peer, and a horse-rider of Franconi's!—It won't do; by Jove, it won't do. I ain't proud; but it will not do!"

"Twopence-halfpenny for your thoughts, Fokey!" cried out Miss Rougemont, taking her cigar from her truly vermilion lips, as she beheld the young fellow lost in thought, seated at the head of his table, amidst melting ices, and cut pineapples, and bottles full and empty, and cigar-ashes scattered on fruit, and the ruins of a dessert which had no pleasure for him.

"Does Foker ever think?" drawled out Mr. Poyntz. "Foker, here is a considerable sum of money offered by a fair capitalist at this end of the table for the present emanations of your valuable and acute intellect, old boy!"

"What the deuce is that Poyntz a talking about?" Miss Calverley asked of her neighbour. "I hate him. He's a drawlin', sneerin' beast."

"What a droll of a little man is that little Fokare, my lor'," Mademoiselle Coralie said, in her own language, and with the rich twang of that sunny Gascony in which her swarthy cheeks and bright black eyes had got their fire. "What a droll of a man! He does not look to have twenty years."

"I wish I were of his age," said the venerable Colchicum, with a sigh, as he inclined his purple face towards a large goblet of claret.

"C'te Jeunesse. Peuh! je m'en fiche" said Madame Brack, Coralie's mamma, taking a great pinch out of Lord Colchicum's delicate gold snuff-box. "Je m'aime que les hommes faits, moi. Comme milor. Coralie! n'est-ce pas que tu n'aimes que les hommes faits, ma bichette?"

My lord said, with a grin, "You flatter me, Madame Brack."

"Taisez-vous, Maman, vous n'etes qu'une bete," Coralie cried, with a shrug of her robust shoulders; upon which, my lord said that she did not flatter at any rate; and pocketed his snuff-box, not desirous that Madame Brack's dubious fingers should plunge too frequently into his Mackabaw.

There is no need to give a prolonged detail of the animated conversation which ensued during the rest of the banquet; a conversation which would not much edify the reader. And it is scarcely necessary to say, that all ladies of the corps de dance are not like Miss Calverley, any more than that all peers resemble that illustrious member of their order, the late lamented Viscount Colchicum. But there have been such in our memories who have loved the society of riotous youth better than the company of men of their own age and rank, and have given the young ones the precious benefit of their experience and example; and there have been very respectable men too who have not objected so much to the kind of entertainment as to the publicity of it. I am sure, for instance, that our friend Major Pendennis would have made no sort of objection to join a party of pleasure, provided that it were en petit comite, and that such men as my Lord Steyne and my Lord Colchicum were of the society. "Give the young men their pleasures," this worthy guardian said to Pen more than once. "I'm not one of your strait-laced moralists, but an old man of the world, begad; and I know that as long as it lasts young men will be young men." And there were some young men to whom this estimable philosopher accorded about seventy years as the proper period for sowing their wild oats: but they were men of fashion.

Mr. Foker drove his lovely guests home to Brompton in the drag that night; but he was quite thoughtful and gloomy during the whole of the little journey from Richmond; neither listening to the jokes of the friends behind him and on the box by his side nor enlivening them as was his wont, by his own facetious sallies. And when the ladies whom he had conveyed alighted at the door of their house, and asked their accomplished coachman whether he would not step in and take something to drink, he declined with so melancholy an air, that they supposed that the Governor and he had had a difference or that some calamity had befallen him; and he did not tell these people what the cause of his grief was, but left Mesdames Rougemont and Calverley, unheeding the cries of the latter, who hung over her balcony like Jezebel, and called out to him to ask him to give another party soon.

He sent the drag home under the guidance of one of the grooms, and went on foot himself; his hands in his pockets, plunged in thought. The stars and moon shining tranquilly overhead, looked down upon Mr. Foker that night, as he in his turn sentimentally regarded them. And he went and gazed upwards at the house in Grosvenor Place, and at the windows which he supposed to be those of the beloved object; and he moaned and he sighed in a way piteous and surprising to witness, which Policeman X did, who informed Sir Francis Clavering's people, as they took the refreshment of beer on the coach-box at the neighbouring public-house, after bringing home their lady from the French play, that there had been another chap hanging about the premises that evening—a little chap, dressed like a swell.

And now with that perspicuity and ingenuity and enterprise which only belongs to a certain passion, Mr. Foker began to dodge Miss Amory through London, and to appear wherever he could meet her. If Lady Clavering went to the French play, where her ladyship had a box, Mr. Foker, whose knowledge of the language, as we have heard, was not conspicuous, appeared in a stall. He found out where her engagements were (it is possible that Anatole, his man, was acquainted with Sir Francis Clavering's gentleman, and so got a sight of her ladyship's engagement-book), and at many of these evening parties Mr. Foker made his appearance—to the surprise of the world, and of his mother especially, whom he ordered to apply for cards to these parties, for which until now he had shown a supreme contempt. He told the pleased and unsuspicious lady that he went to parties because it was right for him to see the world: he told her that he went to the French play because he wanted to perfect himself in the language, and there was no such good lesson as a comedy or vaudeville,—and when one night the astonished Lady Agnes saw him stand up and dance, and complimented him upon his elegance and activity, the mendacious little rogue asserted that he had learned to dance in Paris, whereas Anatole knew that his young master used to go off privily to an academy in Brewer Street, and study there for some hours in the morning. The casino of our modern days was not invented, or was in its infancy as yet; and gentlemen of Mr. Foker's time had not the facilities of acquiring the science of dancing which are enjoyed by our present youth.

Old Pendennis seldom missed going to church. He considered it to be his duty as a gentleman to patronise the institution of public worship and that it was quite a correct thing to be seen at church of a Sunday. One day it chanced that he and Arthur went thither together: the latter, who was now in high favour, had been to breakfast with his uncle, from whose lodging they walked across the park to a church not far from Belgrave Square. There was a charity sermon at Saint James's, as the Major knew by the bills posted on the pillars of his parish church, which probably caused him, for he was a thrifty man, to forsake it for that day: besides he had other views for himself and Pen. "We will go to church, sir, across the Park; and then, begad, we will go to the Claverings' house and ask them for lunch in a friendly way. Lady Clavering likes to be asked for lunch, and is uncommonly kind, and monstrous hospitable."

"I met them at dinner last week, at Lady Agnes Foker's, sir," Pen said, "and the Begum was very kind indeed. So she was in the country: so she is everywhere. But I share your opinion about Miss Amory; one of your opinions, that is, uncle, for you were changing the last time we spoke about her."

"And what do you think of her now?" the elder said.

"I think her the most confounded little flirt in London," Pen answered, laughing "She made a tremendous assault upon Harry Foker,

Mr. Pen had long since occupied himself, during his stay at home following his defeat at Oxford, with various literary pursuits. Among these, he had composed the greater part of a novel. Written under the heavy influence of youthful embarrassments—both romantic and financial—the book was a fiercely gloomy and passionate affair. It was steeped in Byronic despair, Wertherian despondency, and the mocking bitterness of Mephistopheles from *Faust*. For our young protagonist had recently been studying German and, like many clever lads, had imitated his favorite poets. Passages in the volumes he once loved, now read so rarely, still bore the distinct marks of the pencil with which he had annotated them in those turbulent days. Tears had fallen onto the leaves, perhaps blistering the pages as the impassioned youth dashed his thoughts down. Yet, when he revisited the books later, he possessed neither the ability nor the wish to sprinkle those leaves with the "early dew" of former times. His pencil was no longer eager to score its marks of approval. However, as he scanned the pages of his manuscript, memories of the overflowing emotions that had caused him to blot out lines returned, along with the pain that had inspired them. If one could write the secret history of books, noting the author's private thoughts alongside the story, how many insipid volumes would become interesting, and how many dull tales would excite the reader! A bitter smile often crossed Pen's face as he read his novel, recalling the specific time and feelings that gave it birth. Some grand passages seemed pompous now; others, where he thought he had poured out his full heart, appeared woefully weak. He recognized that certain pages were merely imitations of a then-favorite author, whereas he had convinced himself he was writing entirely original material. As he mused over specific lines, he recollected the exact place and hour of their creation; the ghost of the dead feeling returned, causing him to blush at reviewing the faint image. And what of those blots on the page? Like coming across camel tracks in desert clay, traces of withered herbage, you know water was once there; so the place in Pen's mind was no longer green, and the fountain of tears was thoroughly dried up.

One morning, he used this simile to explain his state to Warrington, who sat over his pipe and book. Pen, gesticulating with his wonted excitement when stirred, thumped his manuscript onto the table, rattling the tea-things and making the blue milk dance in the jug. The previous night, he had unearthed the manuscript from a long-neglected chest filled with old shooting jackets, scribbled Oxbridge notebooks, his ancient surplice, and a battered cap and gown—all memorials of youth, school, and home. He had read the volume in bed until sleep overtook him, for the beginning of the tale was somewhat dull, and he had returned home weary from a London evening party.

"By Jove!" exclaimed Pen, slamming down his papers. "When I reflect that these were written but a few short years ago, I am ashamed of my memory. I wrote this believing myself eternally in love with that little coquette, Miss Amory. I used to carry verses down to her and hide them in the hollow of a tree, dedicating them 'Amori.'"

"That was a sweet little play upon words," Warrington remarked, puffing. "'Amory'—'Amori.' It showed proof of scholarship. Let us hear a bit of the rubbish." Stretching over from his easy-chair, he snatched Pen's manuscript with the fire-tongs he had been using to feed his pipe. Holding the volume, he began to read aloud from the *Leaves from the Life-book of Walter Lorraine*:

"'False as thou art beautiful! heartless as thou art fair! mockery of Passion!' Walter cried, addressing Leonora; 'what evil spirit hath sent thee to torture me so? O Leonora...'"

"Cut that part!" cried Pen, lunging at the book, though his comrade held fast. "Well! Don't read it out at any rate. That's about my other flame, my first—Lady Mirabel, who is now. I saw her last night at Lady Whiston's. She invited me to a party at her house, saying that, as old friends, we ought to meet oftener. She has been seeing me around town for two years without inviting me, but seeing Wenham talking to me, and Monsieur Dubois, the French literary man who had a dozen orders and might have passed for a Marshal of France, she condescended to ask me. The Claverings are to be there that evening too. Won't it be exciting to meet one's two flames at the same table?"

"Two flames! Two heaps of burnt-out cinders," Warrington observed. "Are both beauties in this book?"

"Both, or something like them," Pen replied. "Leonora, who marries the Duke, is the Fotheringay. I drew the Duke from Magnus Charters, with whom I was at Oxford; it's a little like him. And Miss Amory is Neaera. By gad, that first woman! I thought of her as I walked home from Lady Whiston's in the moonlight, and the whole early scene came back to me as if it were yesterday. When I got home, I pulled out the story I wrote about her and the others three years ago. Do you know, outrageous as it is, it has some good stuff in it, and if Bungay won't publish it, I think Bacon will."

"That's the way of poets," said Warrington. "They fall in love, get jilted, or jilt someone; they suffer and cry out that they suffer more than any other mortal. Then, having experienced enough feelings, they note them down in a book and take it to market. All poets are humbugs; all literary men are humbugs. Directly a man begins to sell his feelings for money, he becomes a humbug. If a poet gets a pain in his side from eating too well, he bellows 'Ai Ai' louder than Prometheus."

"I suppose a poet has a greater sensibility than another man," Pen countered with spirit. "That is what makes him a poet. He sees and feels more keenly; that is why he speaks of what he feels and sees. You speak eagerly enough in your leading articles when you spot a false argument in an opponent or detect a quack in the House. Paley, who cares for nothing else in the world, will talk for an hour about a legal question. Give another the privilege you take yourself, the free use of his faculties, and let him be what nature has made him. Why shouldn't a man sell his sentimental thoughts as well as you sell your political ideas, or Paley his legal knowledge? Each is a matter of experience and practice. It isn't money that causes you to perceive a fallacy or Paley to argue a point, but a natural or acquired aptitude for that kind of truth. A poet sets down his thoughts and experiences upon paper just as a painter paints a landscape or a face upon canvas, to the best of his ability, according to his particular gift. If I ever think I have the stuff in me to write an epic, by Jove, I will try. If I only feel that I am good enough to crack a joke or tell a story, I will do that."

"Not a bad speech, young one," Warrington admitted, "but that doesn't prevent all poets from being humbugs."

"What—Homer, Aeschylus, Shakespeare, and all?"

"Their names are not to be breathed in the same sense as yours, you pigmies," Mr. Warrington declared sternly. "There are men and men, sir."

"Well, Shakespeare was a man who wrote for money, just as you and I do," Pen answered, which confused Warrington momentarily. The publisher resumed his pipe and his manuscript.

There was no doubt that the document contained a vast amount of Pen's personal experiences, and *Leaves from the Life-book of Walter Lorraine* would never have been written but for Arthur Pendennis's own private griefs, passions, and follies. Since we have become acquainted with these in the first volume of his biography, it is unnecessary to make large extracts from the novel of *Walter Lorraine*, wherein the young gentleman depicted those aspects of his life he deemed likely to interest the reader or suitable for his story.

Though he had kept it in his box for nearly half the period required, according to the Horatian maxim, for a work of art to lie ripening—a maxim whose truth may be questioned—a man who pens a composition with the intention of burying it for ten years before giving it to the world, or exercising his own mature judgment upon it, had best be very sure of the original strength and durability of the work. Otherwise, upon withdrawing it from its crypt, he may find, like small wine, that it has lost its flavor and is only tasteless when opened. There are works of all tastes and smacks, the small and the strong, those that improve by age, and those that won't bear keeping at all, being pleasant only at the first draught when they refresh and sparkle.

Pen had never, even in the time of his youthful inexperience and fervor of imagination, thought that the story he was writing was a masterpiece of composition, nor that he was the equal of the great authors he admired. Now, however, as he reviewed his little performance, he was keenly alive to its faults and modest regarding its merits. It was not very good, he

Alsatia

Nestled somewhere behind the soot-stained gables and grimy chimneys of Wych Street and Holywell Street, hidden away like a secret from the bright outer world, lay the courtyard of Shepherd's Inn. It was a place where the sun rarely ventured, accessible only through winding, smoke-laden alleys that smelled of damp pavement and endless mud. Here, amidst the clutter of slop-sellers, brandy-ball hawkers, and peddlers of dingy furniture, life moved in a chaotic, noisy rhythm. Ballad singers chanted bitter satires against Whigs, bishops, and German royals in guttural tones, while Punch set up his tiny theatre, hoping to extract a halfpenny from the swarming tenants. Inside the courtyard, however, lay a tranquil, old-fashioned quadrangle. In its center stood a mangy grass plat guarded by iron railings, protecting the statue of Shepherd from the assault of local boys.

Though the Inn had once housed serious lawyers, the legal giants had long since vacated its precincts for grander chambers. Now, it was home to the whimsical and the weary. One ground-floor suite belonged to the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum Copper Mines, another to the "Union of Genius and Capital Company." Yet, among these fantastical enterprises, the only gentleman listed in the Law List was Mr. Campion. He wore distinctive mustachios and arrived in his cab twice or thrice a week. While known as Mr. Somerset Campion in the West End, where his wife entertained the gentry, within these walls he operated as Campion and Co. His cab-horse's harness blazed with heraldic bearings, and the groom's white breeches gleamed in the dim light, casting a temporary sunshine over the shadowy square.

Captain Costigan, our old friend, was a regular fixture here. Dressed in carpet slippers, a dressing-gown, and an old hat cocked over his eye, he trailed about the court, examining Campion's cab and horse with practiced interest. After breakfast, he would visit the porter's lodge, patting the heads of the children and chatting with Mrs. Bolton about theatres and her daughter, Leedy Mirabel. Mrs. Bolton, a former dancer at the Wells, remembered the glory days well.

Costigan lived in the third floor of No. 4, formerly the chambers of Charley Podmore, the pleasing tenor singer. Now shared with Mr. Bows, the room echoed with the sounds of a piano whenever the windows were open. Fanny Bolton, the porteress's daughter, was one of Mr. Bows's pupils. With a good voice and a pretty figure, she dreamed of emulating her mother's theatrical glories, preparing meals for the gentlemen in exchange for musical lessons. Though she suspected the Captain indulged in his "unfortunate propensity to liquor," she considered him the finest gentleman in the world, believing every tale he spun. Her fatherly fondness for the shy, kind-hearted Captain was mutual; indeed, his heart was so full of love that he felt uneasy without someone to care for.

Captain Costigan had once hosted carriages of distinction, boasting of the select nobility visiting the "old man, the honest old half-pay Captain." In truth, Lady Mirabel had left her husband's card on the mantelpiece months prior, coming personally to see her father only on rare occasions. Upon marrying Sir Charles, she had settled a small pension on him, admitting him to her table. Initially, Costigan's behavior, though absurd, was harmless. Clad in his best attire, he employed the longest, richest words in his vocabulary, adopting a solemnity that baffled everyone present. "Was your Leedyship in the Pork to dee?" he would ask, demanding to know if his daughter's chariot had appeared in town. He thanked the servants and toasted the company with champagne, treating the domestics with the same magnificence as the masters.

However, familiarity bred disaster. After a few visits, Costigan's caution vanished. During his evening sessions at the Back Kitchen, he would brag of his son-in-law's wine until his speech failed him following his sixth tumbler of whisky-punch. Eventually, he lamentably disgraced himself at Sir Charles's table by premature inebriation. A carriage was summoned, and the hospitable door shut upon him. Heartbroken, Cos spoke to his friends at the Kitchen of his resemblance to King Lear, complaining of his thankless child and describing himself as a poor, worn-out old man driven to drinking by ingratitude.

When his funds ran dry, Costigan would frequently beg money from his daughter, sometimes making statements inconsistent with the truth. Once, when a bailiff threatened imprisonment, he wrote a desperate plea for three pounds and five shillines to liberate his "grey hairs" from gaol. Lady Mirabel, naturally good-natured, sent the sum with a stern warning to be more economical. Later, after breaking a plate-glass window in the Strand, she again advanced the necessary funds. If one had followed the remittance, they would have seen her servant trailing behind the Captain's "aide-de-camp"—an equally impoverished Irishman—to the Roscius Head in Drury Lane, where the Captain was currently in pawn for rum-and-water.

On a third occasion, writing of an illness requiring a physician, Lady Mirabel, alarmed and perhaps guilt-ridden, drove to Shepherd's Inn. The porteress directed her to "No. 4, third floor, name of Podmore." She found the door opened by poor Cos in his shirt-sleeves, ready to receive mutton-chops on a gridiron purchased by Mrs. Bolton. Meanwhile, Sir Charles Mirabel suffered greatly. Letters addressed to him at Brookes's announced that Captain Costigan was in the hall waiting for an answer. Playing whist at the Travellers', he feared his father-in-law would seize him, stalking the streets with a cocked hat and a steady gaze fixed on the club windows. Weak, old, and infirm, Sir Charles cried to his wife, vowing to move abroad or to the country, certain that seeing Costigan again would kill him.

It required a stern second visit from Lady Mirabel to restore peace. Representing to Costigan that further harassment or loan requests would result in the withdrawal of his allowance, she managed to keep her papa in order. She also severely rebuked Mr. Bows, accusing him of abetting her father's conduct and failing to watch him properly. "Papa's conduct is bringing me to the grave," she declared, though she looked perfectly healthy. These were the thanks honest Bows received for his lifelong devotion, a burden he likely bore with little complaint.

Two floors above, at No. 3, lived two other acquaintances: Colonel Altamont, agent to the Nawaub of Lucknow, and Captain Chevalier Edward Strong. Their door bore no name. Strong preferred to conceal his address, using a Jermyn Street hotel card instead, while Altamont, the "Ambassador Plenipotentiary," conducted a confidential mission independent of the East India Company. They sought to sell the principal diamonds and rubies of the Lucknow crown to private capitalists, avoiding official scrutiny.

Strong's chambers, comfortably furnished since Sir Francis Clavering's arrival, boasted three rooms below—an office retaining the desks of departed officials, Strong's bedroom, and a sitting-room. A private stair led to two upper apartments: one for Altamont, the other a kitchen and bedroom for Mr. Grady, the attendant. From Grady's casement floated the most fragrant odors imaginable. The three old soldiers forming the garrison of No. 3 were culinary masters. Grady excelled at Irish stew; the Colonel was famous for pilau and curry; and Strong, when his purse was full, could cook anything from French dishes to omelettes to perfection. He was the most hospitable man in England when credit was good, offering friends a good dinner, a good glass of wine, and a good song. Poor Cos often heard with envy the roar of Strong's choruses and the musical clinking of glasses, wishing he were closer to those festivities, though he wisely avoided inviting Costigan due to his drunken tendencies.

The company surrounding the Chevalier was a strange, motley assembly, each member carrying a history of tide-like fortunes and misfortunes. Jack Holt, formerly in Don Carlos's army, organized a tobacco-smuggling scheme promising thirty thousand a year. Tom Diver, ex-Mexican navy, claimed knowledge of a sunken specie-ship holding hundreds of thousands in gold. Keightley, manager of the underwater copper mines, eyed a Smyrna Sponge Company and quicksilver operations. Then there was Filby, a former field-preacher and actor, who inherited a mysterious property from his deceased father, yielding no rents. And finally, Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., who, despite lacking convivial powers, was cherished for his wealth and position, having weathered his own share of prisons and stamped papers.

When Altamont first returned from Paris, stripped of his wealth but entrusted with the jewels, Strong was sent to accompany him by Sir Francis. Strong grumbled to Clavering about housing Altamont, complaining that the fellow dressed like a "nigger on a holiday" and behaved wildly at the theater. Altamont had once screamed and wept over a fictional death in *The Brigand*, causing a scene that nearly turned the audience out. Strong pressed Clavering for the truth of their connection, suspecting a robbery or some dark secret, urging the Baronet to confess before it was too late. Clavering, however, merely replied, "Hang him

Early the following morning, after the grand dinner at Grosvenor Place where Colonel Altamont had made his dramatic entrance, the Colonel emerged from his attic chamber at Shepherd's Inn. He stepped into Captain Strong's sitting room, where the Chevalier sat comfortably in his chair, a newspaper spread before him and a cigar glowing lazily in his hand. Captain Strong was a man who managed to make his temporary quarters feel like home wherever he pitched his tent. Long before Altamont arrived, he had already conquered the breakfast table, devouring a copious serving of fried eggs and broiled rashers prepared by Mr. Grady *secundum artem*.

Good-natured, talkative, and possessing a constitution that defied the rigors of the road, Strong preferred any company to solitude. Though he harbored no affection for his fellow lodger and would not have mourned the misfortune Sir Francis Clavering wished upon him, he maintained a civil exterior. Having tucked Altamont into bed with friendly gestures the night prior and snatched away his candle to prevent fires, Strong had later discovered a spirit bottle unexpectedly empty. Satisfied with a glass of water over his pipe, he had drifted soundly into sleep, emerging next morning with a clear head and a hearty appetite, ready for whatever struggles or pleasures awaited.

The Colonel's first act was to roar for Grady to fetch a pint of pale ale. Grady obliged, pouring it into a pewter flagon before transferring it to the Colonel's lips. The tankard returned empty, the Colonel drew a massive breath, wiped his mouth with his dressing gown, and declared himself "immensely refreshed."

"Nothing like that beer," the Colonel remarked, his eyes gleaming, "when the coppers are hot. Many a day I've drunk a dozen of Bass at Calcutta, and—and..."

"And at Lucknow, I suppose?" Strong interjected with a chuckle. "I fetched the beer specifically for you, anticipating your needs after last night."

Thus encouraged, the Colonel launched into a rambling account of his exploits from the previous evening. "I cannot help myself!" he exclaimed, thumping his temple with a large hand. "I'm a madman once the liquor gets on board. Ain't fit to trust a spirit bottle. Once I start, I can't stop 'til it's empty, and then Lord knows what I say or what I don't say. I dined quietly at home; Grady gave me just two tumblers, and I intended to spend the evening at the Black and Red as sober as a parson. Why did you leave that confounded sample-bottle of Hollands out in the cupboard, Strong? Grady had to step out, and I couldn't keep away from it. I washed it all down, sir, by Jove! And I'm convinced I had more later at that infernal thieves' den."

"What, were you there too?" Strong asked, lighting a fresh cigar. "Before you reached Grosvenor Place? That was beginning betimes."

"Early hours for drinking and clearing out before nine, eh? But so it was. Like a great big fool, I went there. Found the fellows dining—Blackland, young Moss, and two or three other rogues. If we'd tried Rouge et Noir, I surely would have won. But we stuck to hazard on the dining table. They were too cunning for me. That rascal Blackland got the bones out, and I dropped every penny I had from you this morning. It set me wild, and I suppose I was burning with fever in the head, for I went off determined to get more money from Clavering. Then—and then I don't much remember waking up until this morning, hearing old Bows playing on his pianoforte."

Strong mused silently as he lit his cigar. "I should like to know, Colonel," he said finally, "how you manage to draw money from Clavering."

The Colonel burst into a laugh. "Ha, ha! He owes it to me," he declared.

"I don't know that that is a sufficient reason for Frank to pay," Strong countered. "He owes many more debts to others."

"Well, he pays me because he is so fond of me!" the Colonel retorted with a grinning sneer. "He loves me like a brother, you know he does, Captain. —No? Oh, well, perhaps he doesn't. And if you ask me no questions, perhaps I'll tell you no lies, Captain Strong—put that in your pipe and smoke it, my boy."

"But I'll give up that confounded brandy bottle," the Colonel continued after a pause. "I must give it up, or it'll be the ruin of me."

"It makes you say queer things," Strong observed, fixing Altamont with a hard stare. "Remember what you said last night at Clavering's table."

"Say? What did I say?" the other asked hastily. "Did I split anything? Dammy, Strong, did I split anything?"

"Ask me no questions, and I will tell you no lies," the Chevalier replied. Strong recalled the words Altamont had used and his abrupt departure from the Baronet's house upon recognizing Major Pendennis. Yet, Strong resolved to investigate these matters separately and chose not to dredge the memory of them from Altamont. "No," he said, "you didn't 'split,' Colonel. It was merely a trap of mine to see if I could make you speak. But you said not a word anyone could comprehend—you were far too far gone."

So much the better, Altamont thought, heaving a sigh of relief. Strong noted the emotion but took no notice. Being in a communicative mood, the Colonel continued.

"Yes, I own to my faults," the Colonel confessed. "There are three things I simply cannot resist, no matter what I try: a bottle of brandy, a box of dice, and a beautiful woman. No man of pluck and spirit, no man worth his salt, ever could. There's hardly a country in the world where those three haven't gotten me into trouble."

"In truth?" Strong asked.

"Yes, since the age of fifteen, when I ran away from home to become a cabin-boy on an Indiaman, until now, at nearly fifty, these women have always been my ruin. Why, it was one of them—with such black eyes and jewels around her neck, dressed in ermine like a duchess—that swept off the best part of the thousand pounds I carried off from Paris. Didn't I ever tell you of it? Well, I don't mind. At first, I was cautious, keeping my money close and living like a gentleman—Colonel Altamont at Meurice's Hotel, and so forth. I rarely played, except at public tables, and usually won more than I lost.

"Well, sir, I spotted a chap at the hotel and the Palace Royal, a regular swell fellow with white kid gloves and a tuft to his chin. His name was Bloundell-Bloundell, and somehow we became acquainted. He invited me to dinner and took me to Madame the Countess de Foljambe's soirées. Such a woman, Strong! Her eye! Her hand at the piano! Lor bless you, she'd sit down and sing to you, gazing at you, until she warbled your soul out of your body almost. She asked me to attend her evening parties every Tuesday. Didn't I buy opera boxes and treat her to dinners at the restaurateur? But I had a streak of luck at the tables, and it wasn't in the dinners or boxes that poor Clavering's money vanished. No, be hanged to it, it was swept away another way.

"One night at the Countess's, several of us were at supper—Mr. Bloundell-Bloundell, the Honourable Deuceace, the Marquis de la Tour de Force—all tip-top nobility, the height of fashion. After supper, champagne flowed freely, followed by some of that confounded brandy. I demanded it—I pressed for it. The Countess mixed punch for me, and after supper we played cards as well as grog. I played and drank until I knew not what I did. I was like last night. I was taken away and put to bed, never waking until the next day to a roaring headache and seeing my servant inform me that the Honourable Deuceace wished to see me.

"'How are you, Colonel?' he said, entering my bedroom. 'How long did you stay last night after I departed? The play was getting too high for me, and I'd lost enough to you for one night.'"

"'To me?' I said. 'How's that, my dear fellow? For though he was an Earl's son, we were as familiar as you and me.' He told me he had borrowed thirty louis of me at *vingt-et-un*, giving me an I.O.U. the night before, which I had placed in my pocket-book before he left. I produced my card-case—which the Countess had worked on—and there lay the I.O.U., sure enough. He paid me thirty louis in gold upon the table at my bedside. So I said he was a gentleman and asked if he needed anything, when my servant should bring it. But the Honourable Deuceace didn't drink in the morning and departed for some business.

"Presently, another ring at the outer door. This time it was Bloundell-Bloundell and the Marquis. 'Bonjour, Marquis,' I said. 'Good morning—no headache

Every day, following the entertainments at Grosvenor Place and Greenwich, the worthy Major Pendennis's friendship for the Clavering family seemed to deepen with a fervor that bordered on the absurd. His calls were incessant, and his attentions to the lady of the house unremitting. As an elderly gentleman of the town, he possessed the rare fortune of being received wherever a lady of Lady Clavering's distinction appeared. "Would her ladyship not like to grace the grand entertainment at Gaunt House?" he would suggest, or extoll the "very pretty breakfast ball" at Viscount Marrowfat's in Fulham. "Everybody will be there, including august personages of the highest rank," he'd assure her, promising a Watteau quadrille where Miss Amory would surely look charming. To these and other diversions, the obsequious old gentleman kindly offered to conduct Lady Clavering, positioning himself as a utility ready to serve the Baronet in any agreeable capacity.

Yet, despite his current station and fortune, the world maintained a frosty demeanor toward Clavering, trailing strange suspicions in his wake. He was blackballed from two clubs in quick succession. Within the House of Commons, he confined his conversations to a select circle of the most disreputable members, possessing a natural knack for choosing bad society and adapting to it with the ease others reserve for the company of their superiors. To name every senator with whom Clavering consorted would be invidious; suffice it to say, there was Captain Raff, the honorable member for Epsom, who retired after the last Goodwood races under the guise of a mission to the Levant; Hustingson, the patriotic member for Islington, whose voice had fallen silent on corruption since his appointment as Governor of Coventry Island; and Bob Freeny of the Booterstown line, a marksman of no small repute. Of all these gentlemen, with whom Mr. Hotspur, the Whip, was professionally compelled to associate, none inspired in him a deeper contempt or dislike than Sir Francis Clavering, the representative of an ancient race who had sat for their borough time out of mind.

"If that man is wanted for a division," Hotspur declared, "ten to one he'll be found in a hell. Educated in the Fleet, he hasn't heard the end of Newgate yet, take my word for it. He'll muddle away the Begum's fortune at thimble-rig, get caught picking pockets, and finish on board the hulks." Yet, if the high-born Hotspur could maintain civility towards Clavering for professional reasons, why should not Major Pendennis, armed with his own motives, attend to this unlucky gentleman?

"He has a very good cellar and a very good cook," the Major remarked. "As long as he remains silent, he is not offensive, and he very seldom speaks. If he chooses to frequent gambling tables and lose his money to blacklegs, what matters to me? 'Don't look too curiously into any man's affairs, Pen, my boy,' I say. Every fellow has some cupboard in his house, begad, which he wouldn't like you or me to peep into. Why should we try when the rest of the house is open to us? And a devilish good house, too, as you and I know." He paused, admiring the domestic arrangement. "If the man of the family isn't all one could wish, the women are excellent. The Begum is not over-refined, but as kind a woman as ever lived and devilish clever. And as for the little Blanche, you know my opinion, you rogue. You know my belief is that she is sweet on you and would have you for the asking. But you're growing such a great man, I suppose you won't be content under a Duke's daughter—Hey, sir? I recommend you to ask one of them and try."

Perhaps Pen was somewhat intoxicated by his recent successes, and it may have entered the young man's mind, encouraged by his uncle's perpetual hints, that Miss Amory was tolerably well-disposed to renew the flirtation carried on in the early days by the banks of the rural Brawl. However, he was little disposed to marriage at that moment. Adopting some of his uncle's worldly tone, he spoke rather contemptuously of the institution and in favor of a bachelor life.

"You are very happy, sir," said he, "and you get on very well alone, and so do I. With a wife at my side, I should lose my place in society; and I don't, for my part, much fancy retiring into the country with a Mrs. Pendennis, or taking my wife into lodgings to be waited upon by a servant-of-all-work. The period of my little illusions is over. You cured me of my first love, who certainly was a fool, and would have had a fool for her husband, and a very sulky discontented husband too if she had taken me. We young fellows live fast, sir; and I feel as old at five-and-twenty as many of the old fellows—the old bachelors—whom I see in the bow-window at Bays's. Don't look offended, I only mean that I am blasé about love matters, and that I could no more fan myself into a flame for Miss Amory now than I could adore Lady Mirabel over again. I wish I could; I rather like old Mirabel for his infatuation about her, and think his passion is the most respectable part of his life."

"Sir Charles Mirabel was always a theatrical man, sir," the Major said, annoyed that his nephew should speak flippantly of anyone of Sir Charles's rank and station. "He has been occupied with theatricals since his early days. He acted at Carlton House when he was Page to the Prince; he has been mixed up with that sort of thing: he could afford to marry whom he chooses; and Lady Mirabel is a most respectable woman, received everywhere—everywhere, mind. The Duchess of Connaught receives her, Lady Rockminster receives her—it doesn't become young fellows to speak lightly of people in that station. There's not a more respectable woman in England than Lady Mirabel:—and the old fogies, as you call them, at Bays's, are some of the first gentlemen in England, of whom you youngsters had best learn a little manners, and a little breeding, and a little modesty." And the Major began to think that Pen was growing exceedingly pert and conceited, and that the world made a great deal too much of him.

The Major's anger amused Pen. He studied his uncle's peculiarities with a constant relish, and was always in a good humor with his worldly old mentor. "I am a youngster of fifteen years' standing, sir," he said adroitly, "and if you think that we are disrespectful, you should see those of the present generation. A protégé of yours came to breakfast with me the other day. You told me to ask him, and I did it to please you. We had a day's sights together, and dined at the club, and went to the play. He said the wine at the Polyanthus was not so good as Ellis's wine at Richmond, smoked Warrington's cavendish after breakfast, and when I gave him a sovereign as a farewell token, said he had plenty of them, but would take it to show he wasn't proud."
