"Did he?—did you ask young Clavering?" cried the Major, appeased at once—"fine boy, rather wild, but a fine boy—parents like that sort of attention, and you can't do better than pay it to our worthy friends of Grosvenor Place. And so you took him to the play and tipped him? That was right, sir, that was right:" with which mentor he quitted Telemachus, thinking that the young men were not so very bad, and that he should make something of that fellow yet.

As Master Clavering grew into years and stature, he became too strong for the authority of his fond parents and governess; rather governing them than permitting himself to be led by their orders. With his papa he was silent and sulky, seldom making his appearance in the neighborhood of that gentleman; with his mamma he roared and fought when any contest arose regarding the gratification of his appetite; and in his disputes with his governess over his books, he kicked that quiet creature's shins so fiercely that she was entirely overmastered and subdued. He would have so treated his sister Blanche, too, and did on one or two occasions attempt to prevail over her; but she showed an immense resolution and spirit, boxing his ears so soundly that he forbore from molesting Miss Amory, as he did the governess and his mamma's maid.

At length, when the family came to London, Sir Francis gave forth his opinion that "the little beggar had best be sent to school." Accordingly, the young son and heir of the house of Clavering was despatched to the Rev. Otto Rose's establishment at Twickenham, where young noblemen and gentlemen were received preparatory to their introduction to the great English public schools.

It is not our intention to follow Master Clavering in his scholastic career; the paths to the Temple of Learning were made more easy to him than they were to some of us of earlier generations. He advanced towards that fane in a carriage-and-four, so to speak, and might halt and take refreshment almost whenever he pleased. He wore varnished boots from the earliest period of youth, and had cambric handkerchiefs

Noble Henry Foker, whom we had briefly lost track of, had not idled away his time. A man of constancy, naturally, turns his full attention to whatever passion consumes him, and Foker's all-absorbing obsession was love. I earnestly suggest to my youthful readers, particularly those prone to such "amusements," that they pause to calculate the staggering cost of this occupation. Consider the hours sacrificed to it! One loses precious sleep, tossing in bed thinking of the adored object, emerging late for breakfast just as noon advances and the family scatters. Once engaged in the day's necessary labors, one performs them with a listless spirit, all mental faculties diverted elsewhere. The work is slurred over; friends and relatives, one's natural companions, are neglected in favor of a fleeting glance at the beloved, a peek through her window, or a spy on her carriage in the Park. By night, the blandishments of home become tedious; Mother's chatter grows stale, and the meal she prepares with such care remains largely untouched, save for one particular dish that somehow retains its flavor. Life, business, and family ties, once sources of comfort, become intolerable burdens, rendering one uneasy anywhere except in the active pursuit of one's flame.

I believe this is frequently the state of mind among ill-regulated young gentlemen, and surely it was Mr. H. Foker's condition. Having been raised to indulge every inclination, he surrendered himself to this one with his customary, selfish enthusiasm. Nor should the world-beaten be surprised that Mr. Foker, despite offering Arthur Pendennis sound advice on a prior occasion, became passion's slave in return. Who among us hasn't preached the virtues of temperance to friends, only to practice them less consistently ourselves? To be sure, you, Madam, may be a perfect being, devoid of wrong thoughts in your frigid, irreproachable existence; or you, Sir, may be so strong-minded that foolish passions cannot disturb your equanimity in chambers or at the 'Change. Such serenity renders you immune to sympathy, for you require none. We reserve our compassion for the humble and weak, those who stumble and rise again to march with the rest of mortals. What need have angels of a hand to lift them? Your serene virtue casts no shadow of passion, nor stirs by temptation, nor darkens with remorse; thus, companionship becomes unbearable. You are, by virtue of your elevation, necessarily lonely. We cannot converse familiarly with such potentates, so we bid them goodbye. Our path lies with humble folk, not serene highnesses like you. And let us note that there are no perfect characters in this history, save perhaps one little one, who is far from perfect, for she never realizes it, believing with a deplorable misapprehension that she is as great a sinner as any.

This young lady is currently absent from London, and it is certainly not for her that Mr. Henry Foker's mind is currently agitated. Yet, who cares for minor failings? Must we all be angels, male or female, to be worshipped as such? Let us admire the kaleidoscope of human tastes. From the oldest and ugliest to the stupidest and most pompous, from the silliest and most vapid to the greatest criminals, tyrants, and boobies—we need never despair. I have read of a transported pickpocket's passion for a female convict, both aged and repulsive, ignorant and quarrelsome, their love as magnificent as Cleopatra's for Antony or Lancelot's for Guinevere. The flame Count Borulawski, the Polish dwarf, inspired in the bosom of the most beautiful Baroness at Dresden, is common knowledge. The fever that burned in young Cornet Tozer's heart, causing him to flee and wed Mrs. Battersby, who was old enough to be his mother, is chronicled in newspapers. Should we be ashamed or pleased to know our hearts are constructed so that the highest Ajax may find himself prostrate before the pattens of his kitchen-maid? That no poverty, shame, or crime will be endured, hugged with delight, and cherished more closely than virtue itself, by the perverse fidelity and admirable, constant folly of a woman?

Thus, Henry Foker, Esquire, longed for his love and cursed the fate separating them. While Lord Gravesend's family retreated to the country, leaving his proxy with the venerable Lord Bagwig, Harry lingered in London, certainly not to Lady Ann's sorrow, to whom he was affianced and who missed him not at all. Whichever direction Miss Clavering moved, this infatuated young man followed. Aware that his engagement to his cousin was public knowledge, he was forced to conceal his passion, locking it within his breast until it was compressed so tightly it threatened to explode, a stormy secret poised to collapse him.

A grand entertainment had taken place at Gaunt House on a beautiful evening in June. The following day's journals listed nearly two columns of the most closely printed nobility and gentry honored with invitations. Among the guests were Sir Francis and Lady Clavering, and Miss Amory, for whom the indefatigable Major Pendennis had secured an invite, alongside our two young friends, Arthur and Harry. Both youths exerted themselves, dancing extensively with Miss Blanche. As for the worthy Major, he assumed charge of Lady Clavering, ensuring her introduction to that department of the mansion where her ladyship particularly excelled: the refreshment-room. Amidst pictures of Titian and Giorgione, regal portraits of Van Dyck and Reynolds, enormous salvers of gold and silver, pyramids of flowers, and constellations of wax candles—in a manner perfectly regardless of expense—a supper was served all night. Of the countless creams, jellies, salads, peaches, white soups, grapes, pâtés, galantines, cups of tea, champagne, and sundries Lady Clavering consumed, it is not proper for us to speak. How much the Major suffered as he followed the honest woman about, summoning solemn male attendants and lovely servant-maids, administering to Lady Clavering's various needs with admirable patience, no one knows—he never confessed. He never allowed his agony to mar his countenance in the least; yet, with constant kindness, he brought plate after plate to the Begum.

Mr. Wagg attempted to tally every dish Lady Clavering consumed, though his powers of calculation were compromised by his own free consumption of champagne throughout the evening. Consequently, he recommended Mr. Honeyman, Lady Steyne's physician, to monitor the Begum's health closely and inquire of her status the following day.

Sir Francis Clavering made his appearance, skulking about the magnificent rooms for a while. However, the company and splendor surrounding him failed to suit the Baronet's taste. After tossing off a tumbler or two of wine at the buffet, he departed Gaunt House for the neighborhood of Jermyn Street, where his friends Loder, Punter, little Moss Abrams, and Captain Skewball were gathered around a familiar green table. In the rattling of their boxes and the agreeable hum of their conversation, Sir Francis's spirits rose to their accustomed point of feeble hilarity.

Mr. Pynsent, who had previously requested Miss Amory's hand, approached to claim her again. However, having exchanged scowls of recognition with Mr. Arthur Pendennis in the dancing-room, Arthur suddenly rose and claimed Miss Amory as his partner for the present dance. Mr. Pynsent, biting his lips and scowling savagely, withdrew with a profound bow, stating that he relinquished his claim. Some men seem destined to cross one's path in life; Pynsent and Pen held this mutual view, regarding each other accordingly.

"What a confounded conceited provincial fool!" thought the one. "Because he has penned a twopenny novel, his absurd head is turned, and a kicking would take his conceit out of him."

"What an impertinent idiot!" remarked the other to his partner. "His soul resides in Downing Street; his neckcloth is foolscap; his hair is sand; his legs are rulers; his vitals are tape and sealing-wax; he was a prig in his cradle and has never laughed since birth, save three times at the same joke of his chief. I share the same disdain for that man, Miss Amory, as I do for that cold boiled veal." Upon which Blanche, of course, remarked that Mr. Pendennis was wicked, méchant, and perfectly abominable, wondering what he would say when her back was turned.

"Say! Say that you possess the most beautiful figure and the slimmest waist in the world, Blanche—Miss Amory, I mean. I beg your pardon. Another turn; this music would make an alderman dance."

"And have you ceased tumbling when you waltz now?" Blanche asked, archly lifting her gaze to her partner's face.

"One falls and rises again in life, Blanche; you know I used to call you so in old times, and it is the prettiest name in the world. Besides, I have practiced since then."

"With a great number of partners, I'm afraid," Blanche said, feigning a slight sigh and shrugging her shoulders. Indeed, Mr. Pen had practiced considerably in this life and undeniably improved his dancing.

If Pendennis was impertinent in his talk, Foker, usually so bland and communicative, was entirely mute and melancholy when dancing with Miss Amory. To clasp her slender waist

Major Pendennis, having pushed himself to the absolute limit the previous night at Gaunt House, found his weary bones demanding immediate relief. With a groan that echoed the exhaustion of a long campaign, he dragged himself to Buxton, seeking solace in the region's famous healing waters. By the time Parliament adjourned and Sir Francis Clavering's family had departed town, the affairs of the moment had not progressed a single step. Yet, the city itself had undergone a dramatic transformation. The season had drawn to a close; the lawyers had vanished onto the circuits, and the fashionable set had packed their trunks for the Continent or fled to the Scottish Highlands for health and thrills. The bow-windows of the clubs stood empty, and Pall Mall was a ghostly street. The scarlet coats of the Guards had melted away from the Palace gates, and the tradesmen of St. James's wandered the countryside. Even the tailors, sporting sudden mustaches, had traveled up the Rhine, while the bootmakers were found at Ems or Baden, flushing crimson whenever they encountered their former clients amidst their debts and dice tables. The clergy of St. James's preached to a mere handful of souls, devoid of any sinners of consequence. The brass bands of Kensington Gardens had silenced their silver trumpets, leaving the Serpentine banked with only a few straggling carriages. Clarence Bulbul, trapped in town by his tedious Treasury duties, rode Rotten Row and lamented the silence, comparing the scene to the vast, dusty solitude of an Arabian desert where he was the lone Bedouin. Meanwhile, Warrington stuffed his carpet bag with Cavendish tobacco and retreated to his brother's estate in Norfolk.

Left behind, Arthur Pendennis found himself the sole occupant of his chambers. Though he styled himself a man *blasé* and worn out by life, the truth was a different matter entirely. Pen was a robust young fellow with a voracious appetite and an insatiable hunger for company. He was far from a misanthrope; a mediocre dinner satisfied him as readily as a feast, and the lack of wit or beauty in a gathering mattered little if conversation flowed freely. Whether in a tavern parlour, aboard a Greenwich steamboat, on a Hampstead excursion with his colleague Finucane, or at the summer theaters across the river, Pen found joy. At Vauxhall, he rubbed shoulders with the great Simpson, shaking hands with the principal comic singer of the arena with a satirical humor that never lost its thread of genuine sympathy. He watched the grimaces and graces of the performers with a keen eye, equally appreciative of the roystering youth, the delighted parents, and the poor outcasts who danced away their shame and cares until dawn. To Pen, anthropology—the study of human variety—was his true passion. From the coquetting dowagers to the innocent children feeding ducks, he observed humanity with an unfailing delight, finding deep, secret pleasure in watching the endless tapestry of human nature, a pursuit that required a heart both clean and lonely.

"You see," Pen would boast to Warrington, "I loved fiercely in my youth and burned that flame out forever. Any future marriage will be purely of reason, with a sensible, good-looking woman with a bit of money to cushion our carriage ride through life. Romance is exhausted; I'm old before my time!"

"Balderdash!" Warrington would grunt. "You thought you were going bald yesterday and bragged about it. But you started using bear grease the moment the hairdresser warned you, and you smell like a fresh shave."

"You're a modern-day Diogenes," Pen retorted, blushing despite himself, "demanding everyone live in a tub. Violets smell better than stale tobacco, you grizzled cynic." Yet, Pen cared deeply for his appearance, ornamenting himself with careful attention to ensure he remained agreeable to the world, wearing tight boots and bright varnish like a weary pilgrim on a pilgrimage to vanity.

It was on a shining Friday night in late autumn, following a brilliant editorial piece penned by Pen for the *Pall Mall Gazette*, that he decided to cross the water to Vauxhall. Having secured his admittance ticket and paid the necessary fare, he strolled across Waterloo Bridge under a canopy of stars, heading toward the Royal Gardens. Before entering the enchanted grounds, illuminated by twenty thousand additional lamps, visitors traversed a dark, dreary passage guarded by ticket collectors. Pen presented his order, only to find himself blocked by a gentleman in a short, shabby cloak and a lady accompanied by her daughter.

The gentleman, whose hat leaned precariously to one side, was Captain Costigan, a man of distinct Hibernian dignity who spoke with a heavy, rolling accent. He was loudly arguing with the check-taker, insisting that a "most unlucky contraction"—a lost ticket obtained from Mr. Hodgen, a renowned singer of "The Body Snatcher"—should allow entry for three instead of two. The check-taker refused, leading to a theatrical display of distress from the Captain regarding the fair ladies, particularly the younger one, who had never been to Vauxhall.

"My poor child!" Costigan cried, feigning outrage. "Sir, surely you cannot deny this innocent creature?"

"It isn't my business," the peevish doorkeeper grumbled.

Just then, Arthur Pendennis appeared. Recognizing the Captain, he bowed politely to the ladies and extended a warm hand to Costigan. "Don't you know me, Captain? Pendennis!"

"My dear boy! My dear friend!" Costigan exclaimed, rushing forward to embrace him. "Mr. Pendennis, you've been my preserver! I lost the order, and now these ladies are doomed to disappointment. Especially this young miss; she's never been inside."

Pen, possessing a heart full of good nature, immediately offered assistance. His first impulse was to pay for the entire party, but he quickly realized that doing so would make him appear like a grand *seigneur*. Instead, he handed his purse to Costigan and, with a charming smile, produced his own ticket, inviting the elder lady to follow.

"Fanny, take the gentleman's arm," the lady instructed. "Since you'll be so kind..."

Fanny curtsied, placing her hand, clad in a shabby little glove, under Arthur's arm. She was hardly a woman yet, but her cheeks flushed with youthful blushes and her eyes glistened with gratitude. Arthur, protective and kind, placed his other hand over hers. "Fanny's a very pretty little name," he said. "Do you know me?"

"We keep the lodge at Shepherd's Inn," Fanny replied, shrinking back slightly as she took in the dazzling spectacle of the gardens. "I've never been here, sir... Oh, law, how beautiful!" She stared at the hundreds of lamps, awestruck. Pen felt a surge of pleasure at seeing her delight, pressing her small hand to his side. "What would I not give for a little of this pleasure?" he murmured, feeling suddenly less *blasé*.

Behind them, Captain Costigan's voice boomed, "Pendennis, my dear boy, you've been my preserver! I've known him since childhood, Mrs. Bolton; he's the proprietor of Fairoaks Castle, where I've drunk copious quantities of claret with the nobility!"

While Pen displayed magnificence in his courtesy, Costigan performed a different sort of splendor, bowing and calling "Bravo" to the singers with the dignity of a king descending from a line of Hibernian monarchs. However, unlike Pen, Costigid felt compelled to maintain a distance from the ladies, viewing them as his housekeepers rather than equals. Pen, conversely, had no such scruples. He walked closely beside Miss Fanny Bolton, who, comparing him to the other gentlemen around her, declared him the handsomest and grandest.

They were soon joined by the eccentric party of Lord Viscount Colchicum and his companion, Mademoiselle Caracoline, a French rider who spoke with idiomatic force. Nearby, young Tom Tufthunt, a guard, acted as a loyal lapdog to the elderly viscount, carving poultry and making salads. When Pen and Fanny approached, the viscount merely leered, but Tufthunt greeted Pen with a knowing nod.

"How beautiful they are, sir!" Fanny cried later, as they paused near a concert stage. Her eyes turned toward a pale, gloomy figure standing nearby, Henry Foker Esq., who looked utterly miserable.

"I think he's crossed in love," Pen suggested, looking down at Fanny with the protective air of Egmont guarding Clara. "Isn't that enough to make any man dismal, Fanny?"

"Poor gentleman," Fanny sighed, her eyes filled with kindness. But Harry Foker, lost in his melancholy, failed to notice the beautiful dark eyes fixed upon him.

Suddenly, a voice interrupted the quiet. "How dy do, Mr. Pendennis!" It was Mr. Samuel Huxter, a young surgeon dressed in a flamboyant white coat, a dingy shirt collar, and trousers that seemed to scream "Look at me!" He held a lady in pink satin on his arm and produced a card-case with his teeth.

"Saw your name in the Morning Post," Huxter chirped, handing Pen a card. "Supper tomorrow night at Charterhouse Lane? Some devilish good fellows from Bartholomew's. Stunning gin-punch. Come along, Mary."

Pen accepted the card with lofty courtesy, noting the audacity of the invitation. "You are exceedingly kind,

Costigan left Pen sleeping soundly, undisturbed by any hostile missives from Mr. Huxter. When Pen finally stirred, it was not with the weary grogginess typical of a Londoner dragged back from dreams by care and consols. Instead, he awoke with a brisk, electric vitality, the kind that usually belongs to a man just beginning a fresh adventure. For a City man, the moment sleep departs is instantly seized by thoughts of 'Change and ledgers; for a lawyer, by looming cases and unkept promises. But Pen, the literary hack, found his mind drifting elsewhere. His labor, unlike the tireless grind of commerce or law, lacked a permanent grip on his soul. The thrill of creation faded quickly, leaving behind the prosaic drudgery of hitting deadlines for the *Pall Mall Gazette*. He was a Pegasus forced into harness, running a stage daily, content enough with a whip or a feed of corn, but rarely finding true joy in the plodding rhythm of the newspaper page.

Society, too, offered him little sustenance. He was, in his own estimation, a man out of step. Too young to command the respect of the older, established gentlemen whose company he admired but from which he was excluded, and too old for the frivolous lads of his own generation who spent their days chasing pleasures. He was destined to walk a solitary path, a fate that many men of taste embrace, though Pen, faithful to his nature, would undoubtedly complain about it later. Yet, for now, he carried his solitude with a surprising equanimity.

"What a lovely, artless little creature!" Pen murmured to himself as the morning light filtered through the window, recalling the Vauxhall affair. "How refreshing her natural manners are compared to the calculated coquetry of the ballroom belles! Who would imagine such a beautiful rose growing in the grim shadow of a porter's lodge, blooming in that dreary pot at Shepherd's Inn? Does she learn to sing from old Bows? If her voice is as sweet as her speech, it must be divine. I adore those low, veiled tones. 'What would you like me to call you?' she asked—poor little Fanny! It tugged at my heartstrings to treat her with the grand air, to bid her call me 'Sir.' But surely, no Faust and Margaret drama for me. That old Bows! Is he teaching her to sing? He's a dear old soul, dressed in those antique garb, a philosopher with a kind heart. He showed me kindness during the Fotheringay business; he, too, knows sorrow. I must cultivate Old Bows. One sees people of all stripes. Besides, the polite world is becoming tiresome, and there's hardly anyone left in town anyway. Yes, I shall visit Bows and Costigan. Costigan is a treasure trove of character! Begad, I'll study him and put him into a book." Thus, our young anthropologist chattered to himself, deciding to take advantage of Saturday's lull in the press. With the *Gazette* waiting for contributions and his brain free from immediate demands, he resolved to pay a call at Shepherd's Inn, primarily to see Old Bows.

In truth, if Arthur Pendennis had ever been a scheming rogue like Lovelace, he could not have devised a more effective strategy for captivating poor Fanny Bolton than the gentle charm he displayed the previous night. His dandified demeanor, his feigned generosity, his good humor, and even the honest hesitation he showed when checking her trembling advances—all worked in concert to win her affection. Her faults and virtues were indistinguishable to him, and that ambiguity only deepened her admiration. If one could peer into Fanny's bed—a cramped space shared with two little sisters where Mr. Costigan administered gingerbread and apples—one would find her tossing restlessly, disturbed by her companions' movements, yet lost in a reverie of that eventful night. Every word, glance, and gesture of Arthur played in her mind like a scene from a novel. She had devoured countless tales of romance and seduction, though the market for cheap, sensational fiction was not yet fully developed. Still, the circulating library kept by Miss Minifer, alongside her school and millinery shop, had fed her imagination. To Fanny, Arthur was the living embodiment of all those heroes in greasy, three-volume books.

Arthur, known for his obsession with fine shirts and haberdashery, had certainly made an impression. Fanny gazed with wonder at the crisp whiteness of his linen, the sparkle of his studs, the elegance of his pocket-handkerchief, and the gleaming polish of his boots. The Prince had arrived, subduing the humble handmaid. His image haunted her restless dreams: the blue light of his eyes, the generous, pity-laden smile, the manly, protective grin, the frank, winning laugh. She still felt his arm around her, saw him filling that delicious glass of champagne. Then, her thoughts drifted to the girls who used to sneer at her—Emma Baker, proud of her engagement to a cheesemonger near Clare Market, and Betsy Rodgers, fussing over her young man, an attorney's clerk who carried a bag.

By two o'clock in the afternoon, the Bolton family had concluded their meal. Mr. B., the porter, was away in the countryside attending to the Countess of Estrich's hearse for Messrs. Tressler, the eminent undertakers of the Strand. Just then, a gentleman in a white hat and white trousers appeared beneath the inn archway, stopping at the porter's wicket. Fanny was not merely surprised; she was delighted, blushing furiously. She knew it was Him. There stood His Royal Highness, beaming upon her from the gate. She called to her mother, who was busy above stairs, "Mamma, mamma!" and rushed to open the wicket, shoving past her siblings. How she blushed as she handed him her hand! How affably he removed his white hat, allowing the children to stare up at him in awe. He asked Mrs. Bolton if she had slept well after last night's fatigue and expressed hope that she suffered no headache. Then, turning to the lodger, he remarked that since he was passing that way, he simply could not leave without asking news of his little partner.

Mrs. Bolton, initially shy and suspicious of these advances, was melted by Pen's inexhaustible good humor. He seemed entirely welcome. Looking around for a seat, finding only dishes covering tables and workboxes cluttering surfaces, he perched himself on one of the children's chairs. An uncomfortable eminence, yes, but it launched a fit of laughter among the children, especially Fanny, who was more amused than terrified. She marveled at His Royal Highness's condescension; actually sitting down in a child's chair! Many times afterward, she would regard that piece of furniture, and perhaps others in her room, filling them with the sweet, smiling faces of those who might never look upon her again.

Pen sat down and talked with great volubility to Mrs. Bolton. He inquired about the undertaking business, how many mourners had descended with Lady Estrich, and the state of the Inn. He expressed keen interest in Mr. Campion's cab and horse, noting he had met the gentleman in society. He speculated on shares in the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum mines and asked if there were chambers to let, remarking that it was preferable to the Temple. He wanted to move into Shepherd's Inn. Naturally, he turned his attention to Captain Strong and Colonel Altamont. The Captain, he noted, was an old friend from home, with whom he had dined in those chambers before the Colonel arrived. Describing the Colonel, Pen became immensely waggish, eliciting hysterical giggles from the ladies. "Wasn't he a stout man?" Pen asked, describing a large quantity of jewellery, a wig, and whiskers so black they were almost "blue black"—a rich greenish-purple hue. "I've met him at Sir F—— in society."

"Oh, we know," the ladies replied. "Sir F—— is Sir Francis Clavering; he's here often, two or three times a week with the Captain. My little boy has gone out fetching bill-stamps for him." Mrs. Bolton, talking herself into good nature, suddenly blurted, "O Lor! I beg pardon, I shouldn't have mentioned no secrets." But she assured Pen that they knew he was a gentleman, for he could behave like one. "Hasn't Mr. Pendennis, Fanny?"

Fanny, loving her mother for this defense, cast up her dark eyes to the low ceiling and confirmed, "Oh, that he has, I'm sure, Ma," her voice thick with emotion.

Pen, curious about the bill-stamps and the transactions in Strong's chambers, asked if Altamont was also out for stamps, who he was, and what circles he frequented. These questions, posed with considerable adroitness by Pen—who was personally invested in Sir Francis Clavering's affairs due to private motives—were answered artlessly by Mrs. Bolton, within the limits of her knowledge, which, to be fair, were not extensive.

Once these inquiries were satisfied, and Pen finding himself at a loss for further topics, he remembered his privileged status as a journalist. He asked the ladies if they would like any theatre orders. The play was their delight, as it is the universal delight of theatrical folk. When Mr. Bolton was away on professional duties—which lately involved a

Arthur Pendennis spotted Mr. Bows and immediately adopted the persona of the boisterous host, shouting a "How d'ye do!" in a voice loud enough to wake the dead. He offered a dashing, offhand salute, though a crimson flush betrayed him, instantly mirrored by Fanny Bolton, whose cheeks erupted in a fluttering red signal. After shaking hands, the atmosphere shifted abruptly. An ironical acceptance from Bows regarding Pen's imminent visit to his chambers was followed by a heavy, guilty silence that settled over the group like a damp woolen blanket.

To break the tension, Pen attempted to rattle the air with a story of the previous night's escapades. He tried to mimic Costigan pleading with the Vauxhall ticket-taker, but the performance was a disaster. No laughter rippled through the room. Mrs. Bolton stared blankly, confused whether the Captain or the ticket-taker was being roasted; Fanny wore a mask of alarm, attempting a timid giggle that sounded more like a squeak; and old Mr. Bows sat as glum as a violinist playing a funeral dirge on a broken piano. Realizing his narrative had failed to ignite joy, Pen's voice dwindled to a whisper, flickering out until the room plunged back into somber darkness. Even the rhythmic *clink* of the ticket-porter's boots echoed with undue solemnity.

"You were coming to see me, sir?" Mr. Bows asked, breaking the stalemate. "Won't you have the kindness to walk up to my chambers with me? You do them a great honor, I'm sure. They're rather high up, but—"

"Oh! I live in a garret myself," Pen interjected, trying to lighten the mood, "and Shepherd's Inn feels twice as cheerful as Lamb Court."

"I knew you resided on the third floor," Bows replied, his tone shifting to a melancholic gravity. "And may I gently suggest, without meaning offense, that the air up three flights of stairs is far more wholesome for a gentleman than the stagnant breath of a porter's lodge."

"Sir!" Pen snapped, his candle flame suddenly flaring in indignation. "May I choose my company without your interference?"

"You were so polite to mention your intention to honor my humble domicile," Bows said softly, his voice dripping with a theatrical sadness that Pen recognized as genuine, not an act. "Shall I show you the way? Mrs. Bolton, Mr. Pendennis and I are ancient friends; our paths crossed at the dawn of our lives."

Pointing a trembling finger toward the exit, holding a hat with a gesture slightly too dramatic for a quiet afternoon, Bows spoke with words borrowed from the orators of the theater. Yet, despite his melodramatic flair, Pen sensed the depth of the old man's sincerity. "Come along, sir," Bows urged, "since you are so pressing. Mrs. Bolton, I wish you a prosperous day. Goodbye, Miss Fanny; I shall forever cherish our night at Vauxhall, and rest assured, I will remember the theatre tickets." He took Fanny's hand, pressed it warmly, and departed, leaving the room in a flurry of unspoken emotions.

"What a delightful young man, truly!" exclaimed Mrs. Bolton.

"Do you think so, Mother?" Fanny whispered.

"I was thinking who he reminded me of," Mrs. Bolton mused, gazing out the curtained window after Pen and Bows as they ascended the courtyard. "Back at the Wells, with Mrs. Serle, there was a gentleman from the City who rode in a tilbury, wearing a white hat. He was the exact image, save for his whiskers, which were black instead of Pen's fiery red."

"Laws, Mother! They are the finest hair I've ever seen," Fanny agreed.

"He frequented the house of Emly Budd, who danced Columbine in 'Arlequin Ornipe,' even when Miss De la Bosky was indisposed," Mrs. Bolton continued, busying herself with wiping mugs and stacking plates. "Emly was a baker with a country estate near Omerton. He drove Emly down Goswell Street, and eventually, they wed privately at St. Bartholomew's, where the band played just for them. Now, she drives a carriage and is listed in the papers as a patroness of the Manshing-House Ball for the Washwomen's Asylum. Look at Lady Mirabel, Captain Costigan's daughter; she, too, has turned professional."

As the women folded the dinner cloth, placing it carefully in the drawer, a sudden intimacy occurred. In the final fold, Fanny and her mother were forced close together. Mrs. Bolton slipped a finger beneath Fanny's chin and bestowed a kiss. Once again, the red signal flashed across Fanny's cheeks, but this time it was not fear. It was a sweet, fluttering pleasure. Was love a sin because it tasted so sweet at the start and so bitter at the end?

Mrs. Bolton announced she needed to attend to business and left, instructing Fanny to guard the lodge. As soon as her mother vanished, Fanny moved to the window overlooking Bows's door, refusing to look anywhere else. Nearby, her sisters, Betsy-Jane and Ameliar-Ann, buzzed around a picture book held upside down, pretending to read a grave tract from their father's collection. Fanny ignored them, her gaze fixed solely on the street below.

Suddenly, she gave a sharp shake. He had emerged. He would pass the door. But her hope shattered instantly. Pen did step out, yet Bows followed closely in his wake. Under the archway, they paused. Bows merely removed his hat and bowed, offering a glance inward before continuing. He did not stop to speak. Within moments, Bows returned alone, entering the porter's room, leaving Fanny bewildered.

"Where is your Ma, dear?" Bows asked Fanny later.

"I don't know," Fanny retorted with an angry toss of her head. "I don't follow Mother's footsteps wherever she wanders, Mr. Bows."

"Am I to be my mother's keeper?" Bows sighed, his usual bitterness returning. He called over his nieces, presenting them with gingerbread medals based on their reading skills. While the girls discussed their treats in the courtyard, Fanny retreated to her work, stitching furiously as if her needle were a weapon against her rising emotions. Bows positioned himself to watch the street entrance, waiting for someone who never appeared. Soon, Mrs. Bolton returned from market, finding Bows seated in the spot where she had hoped to find Pen.

Inside the room above, the meeting between Bows and his guest was far from harmonious. Pen was sullen and withdrawn. Bows, having matters on his mind, refused to share them in the presence of Captain Costigan, who had hastily vacated his bedroom mere moments prior. Costigan, however, lingered in the room, exuding a cloying scent of whisky and possessing a handshake that trembled violently, a testament to his nervous constitution.

Bows's chamber stood in stark contrast to the Captain's chaos; it was immaculate, with manuscripts neatly shelved and a lithograph of Miss Fotheringay hanging above the bed. Bows cherished the old handwriting of Lady Mirabel, though he treasured a specific note from her recent era above all others. He was currently teaching Fanny to sing and write, channeling the same devotion he once poured into Emily. To lose Emily, he had taken a substitute, much like a man seeking a crutch after losing a limb. It was cruel fate that Pen should appear again, pursuing the object of Bows's latest affection.

Costigan, meanwhile, believed his presence was entirely welcome. He chatted affably, regaling Pen with tales of Sir Charles and Lady Mirabel's trip to Baden-Baden, promising invitations for Pen to join them. Pen, feigning interest, nodded gravely, recalling the Captain's past columns in the *Sporting and Theatrical Newspapers*. They discussed the hospitality of the Grand Duke and the festivities at Dublin Castle, topics Pen had once revered. As the Captain spoke, memories of Emily, the small room at Chatteris, and their conversations on the bridge flooded back. For a moment, Pen felt a warm kinship for both old friends, shaking their hands cordially before departing.

However, the moment the Captain began speaking, Pen forgot about little Fanny Bolton, lost in his own self-absorbed reveries. He only recalled her when Bows began hobbling down the stairs, clearly intending to shadow Pen outside. This precaution proved ill-advised. The sight of the old man trailing behind ignited a surge of wrath in Pen. "What does he mean by dogging me?" Pen fumed as they reached the Strand.

Isolated on the street, Pen burst into a laugh, though it lacked honesty. Perhaps the thought struck him then, causing a fresh blush, realizing the absurdity of his reaction to the elder's stratagem. He sought distraction, climbing the dizzying stairs of the Panorama only to find Care accompanying him every step. He visited the Club, composing a long, witty, sarcastic letter home, deliberately omitting any mention of Vauxhall or Fanny, fearing his mother and Laura would find the subject unpalatable. Neither novels nor the library table, nor the staid Mr. Hawkins, could anchor his wandering mind.

On his way home, passing a Comedy Theatre, he saw billboards advertising "Stunning Farce," "Roars of Laughter," and "Good Old English

In or near the Temple Garden

Fashion had long ago abandoned the verdant, charming Temple Garden. As Shakespeare imagined it, York and Lancaster once plucked innocent white and red roses there, which later became the emblems of their bloody wars; and the learned author of the *Handbook of London* informs us that "the commonest and hardiest kind of rose has long ceased to put forth a bud" in that smoky atmosphere. Few of the current residents of the surrounding buildings bother to notice whether roses still grow there or bloom past the ancient gate, save perhaps when hurrying to chambers. The attorneys' clerks certainly do not carry bouquets in their bags or posies tucked under their arms as they dash to the lawyers' offices—the handful of lawyers who take constitutional walks hardly ponder York and Lancaster anymore, especially since the railway business is concluded. Only antiquarians and literary dilettantes linger with genuine interest, conjuring up Sir Roger de Coverley and the sharp-faced Mr. Spectator pacing the path, or the beloved Oliver Goldsmith in the summer-house, perhaps contemplating his next 'Citizen of the World,' his new suit being fitted by Mr. Filby, or the collection letters from Mr. Newbery.

Treading heavily on the gravel, rolling majestically along in a snuff-colored suit and a wig desperately lacking the barber's powder and irons, one observes the Great Doctor approaching. Behind him follows his Scottish lackey, slightly tipsy from port wine consumed at the Mitre. Mr. Johnson invites Mr. Goldsmith to return home for a dish of tea with Miss Williams. Oh, kind fantasy! Sir Roger and Mr. Spectator feel as vivid to us now as the two physicians and the intoxicated, loyal Scotsman. These poetic figures inhabit our memory just as surely as the real characters. Since young Mr. Arthur Pendennis possessed a romantic and literary bent, entirely unaccustomed to the drab legal pursuits prevalent in the neighborhood, we may safely assume he was indulging in similar poetic reveries when, on the evening following the events of the last chapter, he selected the Temple Gardens for exercise and meditation.

On a Sunday evening, the Temple is typically tranquil. The chambers are mostly empty: the senior lawyers are hosting grand dinners in the Belgravia or Tyburn districts; the promising young barristers are absent, attending these parties or paying respects to Mr. Kewsy's excellent claret or Mr. Justice Ermine's accomplished daughters. Those not invited are enjoying the economical joint and modest half-pint of wine at the Club, amusing themselves and their fellows with circuit jokes and legal wit. No one occupies a chamber except poor Mr. Cockle, who is ill and subsisting on gruel prepared by his laundress; or Mr. Toodle, a flute enthusiast, whose solo piping drifts from his second-floor lodgings; or young Tiger, the student, whose open window belches clouds of cigar smoke, surrounded by discarded plates and covers marked with the insignia of Dicks' or the Cock. But wait! Where does Fancy lead us? It is vacation time, and with the sole exception of Pendennis, no one inhabits Chambers.

Perhaps it was solitude that drove Pen into the garden. Though he had never previously passed the gate, nor truly admired the neat flower beds or the groups of contented citizens strolling the trim lawns and broad gravel paths by the river, on this particular evening, after dining alone at a nearby tavern, the young gentleman took a whim to stroll through the gardens, breathe the fresh air, and gaze upon the gleaming Thames. After wandering briefly and observing various peaceful, happy clusters around him, weariness overtook him. He retired to one of the summer-houses flanking the ends of the main walk and sat down modestly. What were his thoughts? The evening was delightfully bright and still; the sky was cloudless; the chimneys on the opposite bank remained silent; the wharf warehouses glowed rosily in the sunlight, clear as if freshly scrubbed for the holiday. Steamers raced rapidly up and down the stream, loaded with holidaymakers. The bells of the countless city churches rang for evening prayers—such serene Sabbaths as Pen might have recalled in his youth, pacing with his arm around his mother's waist on the terrace before the lawn at home. The sun illuminated the little village of Brawl as well as the broad Thames, sinking majestically behind the Clavering elms and the tower of the familiar parish church. Were these thoughts, or merely the sunset, causing the flush on the young man's face? He tapped time on the bench to the choir of bells outside, flicked dust from his polished boots with his pocket-handkerchief, then sprang up, stamped his foot, and declared, "No, by Jove, I'll go home." With this resolve—which indicated an internal struggle regarding the propriety of staying or leaving—he stepped out of the summer-house.

He nearly collided with two small children who, standing no taller than his knees, trotted along the gravel path, casting long blue shadows toward the east. One cried out "Oh!" while the other burst into laughter, uttering a knowing infantile chuckle, "Missa Pendennis!" Arthur looked down and beheld his two little friends of the previous day, Mesdemoiselles Ameliar-Ann and Betsy-Jane. He blushed furiously at the sight. Seizing the child he had nearly knocked over, he lifted her into the air and kissed her. At this sudden assault, Ameliar-Ann began to weep in great alarm.

Her cry instantly summoned two ladies dressed in crisp collars, fresh ribbons, and magnificent shawls: Mrs. Bolton, adorned in a rich scarlet Caledonian Cashmere and a black silk gown, and Miss F. Bolton, wearing a yellow scarf and a sweet sprigged muslin, holding a parasol—truly the lady. Fanny uttered not a single word; however, her eyes flashed a welcome, shining as brightly as the most blazing windows in Paper Buildings. Yet Mrs. Bolton, after scolding Betsy-Jane, exclaimed, "Lor, sir—how very odd that we should meet you, yer! I ope you ave yer health well, sir.—Ain't it odd, Fanny, that we should meet Mr. Pendennis?"

"What do you mean by sniggering, Mesdames?" Arthur retorted. "When young Croesus stayed at a country house, have you never, by some singular coincidence, been walking with your Fanny in the shrubberies? Have you and your Fanny never happened to be listening to the heavies' band at Brighton when young De Boots and Captain Padmore clinked down the Pier? Have you and your darling Frances never chanced to visit old Widow Wheezy at the cottage on the common when the young curate arrived with a tract suitable for rheumatism? Do you suppose that if singular coincidences occur at the Hall, they don't also happen at the Lodge?"

"It was a coincidence, no doubt: that was all," Mrs. Bolton conceded. During the conversation the prior day, Mr. Pendennis had simply stated, in the simplest terms possible, in response to Miss Bolton's inquiry, that while some courts were gloomy, parts of the Temple were very cheerful and agreeable, particularly the chambers overlooking the river and surrounding the gardens, noting that the gardens made for a delightful Sunday evening promenade frequented by many. And here, by mere chance, all their acquaintances had reunited, just like so many folks in polite society. What could be more artless, good-natured, or natural?

Pen appeared extremely grave, pompous, and dandified. His attire was unusually smart and brilliant: white duck trousers, a white hat, a multi-colored neckcloth, a light waistcoat, gold chains, and shirt studs gave him the air of a prince of the blood. How splendidly his magnificence suited his figure! "Was anybody ever like him?" someone thought. "He blushed—how becomingly his blushes became him!" the same individual murmured. The children, having seen him the day before, had been so struck by him that after he departed, they played at being him. Ameliar-Ann, sticking her little chubby fingers into the armholes of her pinafore as Pen used to do with his waistcoat, had declared, "Now, Bessy-Jane, I'll be Missa Pendennis." Fanny had laughed until she cried, burying her sister in kisses for that feat. How joyful she seemed to see Arthur embrace the child!

If Arthur was crimson, Fanny, conversely, was very wan and pale. Arthur noticed and kindly inquired why she looked so fatigued.

"I was awake all night," Fanny replied, beginning to blush slightly.

"I put out her candle and ordered her to go to sleep and cease reading," the affectionate mother interjected.

"You were reading! And what captivated you so?" Pen asked, amused.

"Oh, it's so beautiful!" Fanny sighed. "'Walter Lorraine.' How I do hate that Neaera—I don't know the pronunciation. And I love Leonora, and Walter, oh, how dear he is!"

How had Fanny discovered the novel *Walter Lorraine*, and that Pen was the author? This little soul remembered every word Mr. Pendennis had spoken the night before, recalling how he wrote in books and newspapers. Which books? So eager to know, she almost considered being civil to old Bows, who suffered under her displeasure since yesterday, but resolved first to apply to Costigan. She began by coaxing the Captain and smiling upon him in her most winning manner as she helped arrange his dinner and tidied

Long ago, in the opening pages of this chronicle, we paused to glance at the tiny hamlet of Clavering, nestled beside Pen's ancestral home of Fairoaks. We noted its inhabitants, though we barely spared them a moment for amusement, for the social circle there was, to put it mildly, lacking in sparkle. Yet, amidst this drab backdrop, Mr. Samuel Huxter, the dashing spirit we last encountered at Vauxhall, remained a beacon of liveliness. During his vacations, he transformed the local gatherings into raucous affairs, regaling friends with the sharp wit of Bartholomew's Coffee House and the scandalous gossip of high London society.

Then there was Mr. Hobnell, the young gentleman Pen had once beaten soundly following the Fotheringay squabble. While a schoolboy at Clavering's Grammar School, Hobnell was a favorite at Mrs. Huxter's table, a master of the local apothecary's shop knowing exactly where to find the tamarind pots and how to scent his handkerchiefs with rosewater. It was during those school days that he fell hopelessly in love with Miss Sophy Huxter. Upon his father's passing, he wed her and moved into "The Warren," a few miles from Clavering, a property his ancestors had farmed for generations.

His father, a man of grand visions, demolished the humble farmhouse to erect a blinding white mansion complete with sprawling stables, a drawing-room piano, and a pack of hunting dogs. With these additions, the Hobnells ascended from yeomen to the respectable county gentry. When the old Squire died, his son inherited the throne. Young Sam Huxter, meanwhile, delighted in boasting to his London cronies at Bartholomew's about his brother-in-law's new status, his hounds, his horses, and his hospitality. Every year, whenever Mrs. Hobnell's nursery duties allowed, Hobnell would dash to London, secure a room at the Tavistock Hotel, and indulge in every pleasure the city offered alongside his learned brother. From Ascot and the theaters to Vauxhall and the taverns near Covent Garden, the vivacious squire lived life to the fullest, declaring that when in London, one must "go it a bit." Returning to the West Country, he would gift his wife new bonnets and shawls, ready to trade the elegance of town life for the rustic sports of the next eleven months.

Sam and Hobnell maintained a lively correspondence, exchanging metropolitan news for baskets of game and clouted cream. To Sam, Hobnell was a genius; to Hobnell, Sam was the life and soul of the Warren. When Sam arrived, the house erupted in laughter, fueled by songs, jokes, and sheer fun. He was even credited with saving the eldest child's life by extracting a fishbone from her throat. Truly, he was the delight of the circle.

However, fate, in its mischievous way, arranged for Pen to encounter Mr. Huxter merely three days after their disastrous meeting at Vauxhall. True to his vow, Pen had avoided seeing little Fanny, attempting to drown his thoughts in relentless work. As a critic for the *Pall Mall Gazette*, he savaged poems and romances with savage indifference. After slaying these literary victims, he retired to the lonely Polyanthus Club, where vast silences only deepened his gloom. Theater outings offered no relief; instead, he watched an ignoble farce that filled him with sorrow, his dismal countenance surely dampening the spirits of the actor on stage.

Exhausted from walking twenty miles over Hampstead Common seeking rest, Pen decided to visit the Back Kitchen, his old haunt with Warrington. He wasn't tired, merely in need of distraction. There, Bows presided calmly over the piano, leading a session of comic songs that cracked the room with laughter. To Pen, these melodies sounded strangely alien, yet he found himself fixated solely on Bows. Within what he claimed was an extinct volcano of emotion, a sudden flame ignited—a result of two days' indulgence followed by two days of abstinence. Just as he mused on this internal turmoil, drinking glass after glass, Arthur's gaze landed on Mr. Huxter. Like Pen, Huxter had attended the theater and now sat with two companions. Huxter whispered to his friends, clearly causing Pen discomfort, before working his way around the room until he seated himself opposite Pen, offering a familiar nod and a dirty handshake.

Pen accepted the hand, feeling a pang of regret for his previous savagery. Huxter, however, wore a mask of perfect good humor, utterly unaware that he could ever be disagreeable. To him, the Vauxhall altercation was mere "chaff," a trifling jest easily forgotten.

Seeking a suitable topic, Huxter and his party decided to strike a chord most painful to Pen.
"Jolly night at Vauxhall—wasn't it?" Huxter asked, winking knowingly.
"I'm glad you liked it," Pen groaned inwardly.
"I was dev'lish cut," Huxter continued, feigning concern. "Uncommon. Been dining with some chaps at Greenwich. That was a pretty bit of muslin hanging on your arm—who was she?"

The question broke Pen. "Have I asked you any questions about yourself, Mr. Huxter?" he snapped.
"I didn't mean any offence—beg pardon—hang it, you cut up quite savage!" Huxter exclaimed, astonished.
"Do you remember what took place between us the other night?" Pen demanded, his wrath rising. "You forget? Very probably. You were tipsy, as you observed, and very rude."
"Hang it, sir, I asked your pardon," Huxter looked red.
"You did certainly, and I granted it with all my heart," Pen retorted. "But if you recall, I begged you to omit me from your list of acquaintances forever. And when we meet in public, I implore you not to take the trouble to recognize me. Will you please remember this henceforth? And since the song begins, permit me to leave you to enjoy the music without restraint."

Raising his hat, Pen bowed to the stunned Mr. Huxter and departed the table. As Pen left, his companions, after a brief pause of awe, let out a roar of laughter at Huxter's expense, prompting the room's president to bark, "Silence, gentlemen! Do have silence for the Body Snatcher!"—the popular song that was just starting. Pen prided himself on commanding his temper, though he secretly wished Huxter had been pugnacious; he craved a fight. He went home, unsettled by the day's work, the dinner, the play, the whisky, and the quarrel. Sleep eluded him completely.

Days later, Mr. Sam Huxter penned a scathing letter to his brother-in-law, Mr. Hobnell, detailing Arthur Pendennis's exploits. Sam described Arthur as a confounded insolent rogue, an "abandoned criminal" and a "regular Don Juan." He accused Arthur of turning an innocent girl from the lower ranks into a victim, noting that he had identified her from an Irish gentleman formerly in the army who frequented their club. Huxter vowed to warn her father, concluding his missive with thanks for recent parcels of game and a hint of his eagerness for future favors.

About once a year, a christening occurred at the Warren. This particular ceremony took place the day after Hobnell received the letter. The infant, a darling little girl named Myra Lucretia after her godmothers, became the center of attention. Naturally, Hobnell shared Sam's letter with his wife, and Mrs. Hobnell, in turn, disseminated the horrid contents to her gossip network. By afternoon, the entire village of Clavering knew the story.

Myra, too shocked to speak, remained silent, but Mrs. Pybus was another matter. She discussed the scandal not only with Mrs. Portman but also with the Honorable Mrs. Simcoe, Mrs. Glanders, Madame Fribsby, and indeed the whole of Clavering society. Madame Fribsby, eyeing her portrait of a dragoon and reflecting on her own wounded memories, mused that "men will be men" and that deception was inherent to their nature, quoting lines from *Marmion* regarding where deceitful lovers should rest. Mrs. Pybus, however, lacked words strong enough to describe a villain capable of such baseness. She attributed Arthur's downfall to early indulgence, insolence, extravagance, and "aristocratic airs"—noting specifically that Pen had refused to drink tea with her. Blaming the "corrupt and horrid parties" of modern Babylon, she lamented the ruin of a promising boy.

Mrs. Portman feared that her own fatal partiality had spoiled the child, suggesting that literary success had turned his head and his "horrid passions" caused him to forget the principles instilled by Dr. Portman. Captain Glanders, the "atrocious" Dragoon, whistled and made jocular allusions when informed by Mrs. Glanders, earning a rebuke as a brute and the removal of the girls from the room. Mr. Simcoe remained calm, perhaps even pleased, viewing the incident as confirmation of his low opinion of the "wretched young man." He admitted he hadn't read a line of Pen's dangerous works, but questioned what could be expected from such a lack of seriousness. Consequently, Pen became the subject of a second sermon

Doctor Portman dispatched his stern missive to London, attempting to calm Mrs. Pendennis's nerves until a reply could surface—a response he optimistically believed would vindicate his son's morals. For now, Helen's plan to travel to London and confront Pen personally remained on hold. The local apothecary forbade her even a trip to Fairoaks for the first day, leaving her confined to her sofa, attended by the devoted, though taciturn, Laura.

Unfortunately for everyone involved, Pen never encountered the Doctor's homily until weeks later. Meanwhile, the widow waited day after day for an answer to the charges laid against her son, her own health deteriorating with each passing hour. For Laura, bearing this anxiety was a heavy burden; witnessing her dearest friend's suffering, compounded by the pain of Helen's estrangement and the unspoken grief between friends, was a trial she endured with saintly fortitude. Through her private, silent prayers, she supported them both, a virtue so noble it demands silence, much like a flower blooming quietly in a garden rather than strutting in a ballroom. Yet, alas, what tragedy befalls those pure spirits when grief or death strikes them down, wasting them in sickness or cutting them short in their prime? Surely, Heaven chastens those it loves most, refining their souls through repeated trials.

As for Pen, the letter was dutifully posted and delivered to his box in Lamb Court, eventually reaching his table amidst a pile of laundry brought by Mrs. Flanagan, who was currently draining a glass of gin in the room where a portrait of Pen standing outside his chamber hung. Those who have tracked Mr. Arthur's career thus far likely suspect his cardinal flaw: his greatest enemy is himself. Like many, Pen harbors a "rascal" within—a spoiled servant who grows insolent and rude at the slightest hint of coercion. While Laura, accustomed to self-denial, handles sacrifice with ease, Pen, unaccustomed to restraint, reacts with savage grumbling whenever asked to forego a pleasure.

Consequently, Pen resolved to see Fanny no more. He attempted to purge her image from his mind through relentless work, exercise, and dissipation. He overworked, over-walked, over-rode, and consumed excessive quantities of food, drink, and tobacco. Yet, despite the cigars and punch, the vision of Fanny haunted his fever-brightened brain. After a week of this harsh regimen, the young gentleman collapsed into bed with a high fever. Let anyone who has never suffered a fever in a garret pity the wretched soul forced to endure such solitude. One might imagine a series of Cruikshank engravings depicting the terrors of bachelor life: the lonely breakfast with a cold kettle in midsummer, the return from holiday to find the fire extinguished and the landlord's bill awaiting, the discovery of a shirt missing its buttons, and finally, the horror of falling ill alone. There is no kind hand to offer water, no gentle smoothing of the hot pillow, only the companion of sickening fancies and fevered delirium.

Such a fate befell Arthur Pendennis. One night he succumbed to illness, waking the next day in a terrible state. His sole visitors, aside from the laundress, were a printer's devil from *Pall Mall Gazette*, whom Pen struggled to entertain, and Mr. Finucane, the Irish editor. Finucane, rushing to assist, filled the blanks in the newspaper with magnificent tributes to the greatness of Ireland and the genius of its inhabitants, delighting the absent Chief Editor, Shandon, who was relaxing at Boulogne. Finucane, a warm-hearted partisan, even offered to skip his weekend holidays to nurse Pen, though Arthur, prideful and stubborn, insisted on facing his malady alone.

Later, Captain Costigan, alerted by Finucane, visited Pen on a Sunday afternoon. He found the laundress, Mrs. Flanagan, weeping in the sitting-room, having resorted to brandy to cope with the shock of her charge's condition. Her attentions became so intrusive to the invalid that he begged her to leave, prompting further tears and alcohol consumption. However, Pen was relieved to hear Costigan's voice. Lying in his fever-stricken bed, haggard and unshaven, he managed to summon a lively chatter about Vauxhall, the Back Kitchen, and Fanny. "How is little Fanny?" he asked.

Indeed, Fanny was far from well. Having witnessed Arthur lighting his lamp in his chambers after meeting Captain Costigan, she returned home in a deep melancholy. Restless through the night, she clutched 'Walter Lorraine' under her pillow, unable to read it aloud due to her father's strict rules. The following day, she visited the musician, Mr. Bows. When he mentioned seeing Pen, Fanny's defensiveness flared, fearing he would speak ill of her beloved. Bows, however, assured her that Pen was a good and honest young man, but explained that their paths were mismatched. He recounted Pen's past, unrequited romance with Lady Mirabel, noting that Pen, now a man of the world, realized he must marry someone of equal rank and fortune. A penniless girl, regardless of virtue, could not sustain the future Pen envisioned.

Fanny's emotions shifted from anger to dismay. She pleaded ignorance, insisting she meant no harm, but Bows gently guided her toward accepting reality. He advised her to forget Pen for her own reputation's sake. Though she promised to obey, her heart remained torn. Later, she confided in her mother, weeping over the "inequality" of their conditions. She idolized Pen, imagining him as a noble poet hiding ancient wealth, vowing to work and study to become worthy of him. Her mother, however, warned her against such thoughts, suggesting that if Pen did not pursue her, he was unworthy. Yet, as soon as her father, Mr. Bolton, entered the lodge for supper, the mother and daughter instantly masked their sorrow with feigned cheer, a testament to the remarkable hypocrisy with which women often conceal their hearts.

Ironically, fate seemed determined to fuel Fanny's passion. Every circumstance around her, including Bows's warnings, only heightened her longing. Pen, meanwhile, was not a seducer; he was simply trying to crush a foolish, imprudent passion born of his youth, terrified of the shame and scandal it would bring to his family. He viewed marriage to a girl of low degree as impossible, a sentiment solidified by his worldly experience. Thus, he waited, hoping the feeling would fade, while Fanny waited, praying for a sign.

Her vigil lasted a full week until Captain Costigan informed her of Arthur's critical illness. That same evening, after Costigan's visit, Arthur's uncle, the Major, arrived in town from Buxton. He sent his valet, Morgan, to inquire for his nephew and arrange a breakfast. Morgan returned with a pale face, reporting that Arthur was extremely ill, burning with fever, and that no doctor had been summoned. The Major, horrified, immediately despatched Morgan to fetch Dr. Goodenough. The physician arrived promptly, confirming the severity of the case. He bled the patient instantly and ordered the Major to send for Mrs. Pendennis, warning that the situation was precarious.

The Major, though plagued by his own ailments, felt compelled to act. He wrote a frantic letter to Helen and traveled to Lord Steyne's house, where he sat in gloomy silence, losing his money at whist and dreaming of his dying nephew. The next morning, a mysterious note arrived from Mr. Bows, confirming Arthur's fragile state. Just as the Major prepared to hunt, a shabby, grey-headed figure emerged from a nearby carriage—a fly driven up from the station. It was Mr. Bows himself, seeking the Major in urgent haste. Seeing the alarm on the old gentleman's face, Bows whispered gravely, "It's... it's my boy that's dead."

Before the shock could fully register, a britzka pulled up. Lord Steyne, impatient for the mail train, shouted for the Major to jump in. As they sped away, another carriage approached, carrying two ladies asking directions. The Major recognized the panel of the vehicle: the worn crest of the Eagle looking at the Sun, the motto "Nec tenui penna." It was his sister's old carriage. Helen and Laura were inside, racing to Pen's room.

The Major ran to meet them, kissing his sister's hand, and together they hastened down Temple Lane, ascending the gloomy stairs to the door marked with Arthur's name. They knocked softly, and the door swung open. Standing there, in the doorway, was Fanny Bolton.

Fanny, trembling with a chill that went deeper than the damp air of the passage, saw the two ladies approaching. The elder, whose countenance wore a mask of inscrutable alarm, fixed her gaze upon the poor girl with a look that promised no solace. Instantly, Fanny knew the truth: Pen's mother was there. A terrible resemblance haunted the widow's haggard eyes, mirroring the fever-dream visage of Arthur as he tossed in his bed. Beside her stood Laura, a statue carved from ice and malice; her face held no flicker of mercy, only a cold, stony hardness. Hard-heartedness and gloom clung to both women like a heavy shroud. Fanny cast a desperate glance toward the Major behind them. Old Pendennis, usually so steady, lowered his lids, peering up from beneath them with a stealthy, worried look at the little nurse who had done so much for his son.

"I—I wrote to you yesterday, if you please, ma'am," Fanny stammered, her voice cracking as every limb shook with fear. "I'm pale as Laura," she whispered to herself, noting the menacing shadow on the younger woman's face looming over the older one.

"Did you?" Mrs. Pendennis replied, her tone sharp and cutting. "I suppose I may now relieve you from nursing my son. I am his mother, you understand."

"Yes, ma'am. I—" Fanny faltered, then suddenly cried out, "Wait a minute! I must prepare you for his..."

The widow, whose features had been etched into something hopelessly cruel and ruthless, recoiled with a startled gasp and a stifled cry.

"He's been so since yesterday," Fanny murmured, her teeth chattering violently.

From within the open door of Pen's room, a horrid shriek of laughter erupted. After several frantic shouts, the poor wretch began to sing a college drinking-song, then hurrahed and shouted as if surrounded by a riotous wine party, thumping his fist against the wainscot. He was utterly delirious.

"He does not know me, ma'am!" Fanny sobbed.

"Indeed. Perhaps he will know his mother," the widow said, pushing past the weeping girl with hurried steps. "Let me pass, and go in to him." Laura sailed by without a word, and the Major followed them into the dark passage leading to the sick chamber. Left alone, Fanny sank onto a bench, crying and praying as best she could. She would have died for him, yet they hated her. These fine ladies offered not a word of thanks or kindness. She sat there in the gloom, unsure of the passing time, until Doctor Goodenough arrived for his second visit.

He found the little maid at the door. "What, nurse? How's your patient?" the good-natured doctor asked gently. "Has he had any rest?"

"Go and ask them. They're inside," Fanny replied, pointing toward the door where the widowed mother and Laura sat.

"Who? His mother?" the doctor queried, his brow furrowing slightly. Fanny nodded silently, unable to speak.

"You must go to bed yourself, my poor little maid," the doctor advised, his voice softening. "You will fall ill if you don't."

"Oh, mayn't I come and see him? Mayn't I come and see him! I—I love him so," the little girl pleaded, falling to her knees and clutching the doctor's hand with an agony that melted his heart and blurred his spectacles.

"Pooh, pooh! Nonsense! Nurse, has he taken his draught? Of course you must come and see him. So must I."

"They'll let me sit here, won't they, sir? I'll never make no noise. I only ask to stop here," Fanny insisted. The doctor, chuckling at her earnest simplicity, called her a "stupid little thing," tapped her pale cheek with his finger, and bustled into the farthest room, leaving her to wait.

Inside, Mrs. Pendennis sat pale and solemn in a great chair by the bedside. Her watch lay on the table beside Pen's medicines, her bonnet and cloak draped in the window. Her Bible rested in her lap, a constant companion. Upon entering, her first act had been to retrieve Fanny's shawl and bonnet from the drawers and discard them onto the study table, effectively closing the door on the Major and Laura to claim possession of her son.

Her greatest terror—that Arthur would not recognize her—was partially assuaged. Pen knew his mother intimately, smiling and nodding familiarly. As soon as she entered, his mind, wandering in fever, snapped back to the comfort of home at Fairoaks. He talked and chattered and laughed wildly, unaware of the poison his laughter shot into Laura's heart. It was true, then: guilt, an intrigue with a servant-maid, a betrayal of love, and now, a dying raving unrepentant. The Major occasionally hummed a word of consolation, but Laura barely heard him.

It was a dismal sitting for all parties. When Doctor Goodenough finally appeared, he walked in like an angel, bringing a light that banished the shadows. For the sick man, yes, but also for the friends surrounding him, his presence was a balm. How we watch for him! How the thrill of his carriage wheels makes our hearts leap! We hang on his words, seeking a smile to lighten our darkness. Who has not seen the mother pry into his face, searching the eyes of the silent infant battling fever? Ah, how she looks! What thanks if there is hope; what grief if he casts his eyes down, fearing to utter the word. Whether it be the father or the son, the physician stands as Fate, the dispenser of life and death, bearing the crushing weight of responsibility. To let a patient live when he should die, or vice versa, is a cruelty that haunts the conscience. Yet, amidst this gravity, there arose a strange contrast.

After a hasty introduction to the new arrivals, the doctor examined the patient. High fever demanded immediate action; antiphlogistic remedies were necessary. He consoled the mother with comforting assurances of youth and strong constitution, doing his utmost to calm her alarms before stepping aside into the vacant room (formerly Warrington's) for a private consultation with the Major.

"The case is critical," the doctor noted seriously. "If not stopped, the fever will carry him off. He must be bled forthwith. You must inform her of this necessity." He paused, his expression shifting to one of mild amusement. "Why was that other young lady brought with her? She is out of place in a sick-room."

"And there was another woman still, be hanged to it!" the Major interjected, his voice rising. "The—the little person who opened the door! My sister-in-law brought out the poor little devil's bonnet and shawl and flung them upon the study table. Did Goodenough know anything about the—the little person?" The Major leaned forward, his eyes twinkling despite the grim situation. "I just caught a glimpse of her as we passed in, and begad, she was uncommonly nice-looking."

The Doctor looked queer, then smiled—a grave, satirical smile that somehow made the gloom darker, mocking the seriousness of life and death.

"I have it," the doctor said eventually, re-entering the study. He wrote two notes hastily at the table, sealing one. Taking up Fanny's discarded shawl and bonnet along with the notes, he stepped back into the passage. "Quick, nurse," he commanded, addressing the little figure waiting outside. "You must carry this to the surgeon and bid him come instantly. Then go to my house in Hanover Square, ask for my servant Harbottle, tell him to compound this prescription immediately, and wait until I return. It may take a little time."

Poor Fanny trudged away with her two notes, finding the apothecary in the Strand, who came straightaway with his lancet, and then hastened to the doctor's house. While the prescription was being compounded by Harbottle, who took a long time, the doctor returned home before it was finished. During the remainder of Arthur's illness, Fanny never again appeared in the chambers as his nurse. But for those two days, a little figure might be seen lurking about Pen's staircase, a sad, sad face interrogating the apothecary, his boy, the laundress, and the kind physician as they passed.

On the third day, the doctor's carriage stopped at Shepherd's Inn. The good, honest, benevolent man entered the porter's lodge and tended to a little patient there. It was on this day, when he could finally tell Fanny Bolton that the crisis was over and hope remained for Arthur Pendennis, that the tide turned.

J. Costigan, Esquire, late of Her Majesty's service, observed the carriage and critiqued its horses. "Green liveries, bedad!" the General remarked, admiring the high-stepping bees. "There's no end to the pride and arrogance of doctors nowadays—not but that is a good one, a scientific character, and a right good fellow, bedad; and he's brought the poor little girl well through her fever." Mr. Costigan was so pleased with the doctor's behavior that whenever he met Dr. Goodenough thereafter, he saluted him with the courtesy due a Lord Lieutenant.

The widow's gratitude knew no bounds—or perhaps scarcely any.

The duty of a chronicler, even when recording a shame that would stain the cover of any novel, is to speak the truth. Here lies a fact concerning Pendennis: after recovering from a severe fever, which had subjected him to bleeding, blistering, shaving, and a regimen of medicinal horrors, his "mental malady" vanished as completely as the physical one. The passion he had held for Fanny Bolton evaporated. He was no longer in love with her, not with the fervor of a lover, but with the detached wisdom of a man who knows better than to chase porters' daughters.

Lying on his pillow, Pendennis chuckled at this second cure. He felt no regret for Fanny; indeed, he marveled at his own stupidity in caring for her so intensely just months prior. He performed a mental autopsy on his dead feelings, dissecting the anatomy of his former obsession for the benefit of his nurse. Was it her wit? Her breeding? Her beauty? No; there were countless women superior in every regard. The fire had simply burned out within him, leaving the girl unchanged while the observer was altered. The fever, quelled by pills and lancets, had seemingly cured his infatuation. It was a source of immense relief to Pendennis to realize he could now resist temptation without guilt, viewing the "Fanny Bolton snare" as a trap from which he had miraculously escaped. Yet, beneath the surface, a humbling satisfaction lingered—an uncomfortable admission that the world's greatest passions are often fleeting fancies.

While Pendennis oscillated between amusement and shame, a different atmosphere prevailed at his bedside. His mother, the Countess, offered a warmth that filled him with profound security. Her unwavering attention and gentle smiles were the only things she required; executing his slightest whim was her supreme joy. Surrounded by her love, the young man felt a gratitude akin to that of a child in infancy. However, shadows clouded this paradise. Though Pen suspected vaguely that Fanny Bolton might have nursed him during his delirium, these memories were so foggy he dismissed them as fever dreams. Consequently, he remained silent, refusing to confide in his mother about Fanny, a precaution that ultimately proved disastrous.

Misinterpretation flourished where communication failed. Seeing Miss Bolton as nurse, Mrs. Pendennis constructed a narrative of scandal in her mind, assuming the worst of the accusations against her son. A man's wife is often the first to suspect treachery, and the Countess, steeped in protective jealousy, accepted the possibility of sin without inquiry. Meanwhile, the innocent nurse, believing her charge was suffering from a spiritual as well as physical plague, masked her internal terror with a cheery exterior, bearing the weight of false suspicion alone.

Across the Channel, at Boulogne, Captain Shandon read the *Pall Mall Gazette* with a grim satisfaction. He noted the absence of Jack Finucane's handwriting and the return of Mr. Warrington. To Shandon, journalism was a battlefield; Finucane fought like a butcher, mangling subjects, whereas Warrington laid his cuts neatly, straight down the spine of the page, drawing blood with surgical precision. "My dear," Shandon remarked, describing the "crack of the whip," "it is savage to the men." Mrs. Shandon, a gentle soul, protested, defending Mr. Warrington's kindness to the children, but Charles merely smiled, noting that savagery was reserved for the professional arena, not the domestic sphere. Where the wines were cheap and the brandy flowed freely, the Captain dreamed of returning to harness, muttering Latin about crossing terrible seas tomorrow.

Thus, Warrington stepped into the void left by his prostrate friend, tackling Pen's journalistic duties with ferocious energy. He wrote literary critiques and theatrical reviews with his characteristic intensity. When he visited the sick man's family, he lavishly praised Arthur's style, claiming it was the most graceful and elegant in the land. He spoke of Arthur's loyalty and gentleness, dismissing minor flaws like "wilfulness" and "dandification." Helen and Laura, though deeply grieved by Pen's condition, found solace in Warrington's high regard for their brother. Even Major Pendennis, usually taciturn, erupted in praise of the younger man, extolling his lineage from the Suffolk Warringtons and his connection to the Prince of Wales.

As the Major spoke, Laura blushed, her mind racing with speculations about Warrington's own romantic entanglements. Soon, the Major granted permission for Laura to accompany Warrington for walks in the Temple Gardens, reasoning that if the great arbiter of morals approved, no harm could come of it. Laura, grateful for the fresh air and the chance to escape her solitude, accepted. A tentative understanding existed between the two women, forged in the fires of Pen's near-death experience; Laura had confessed to Helen the depth of her love for Arthur, a secret that softened the edges of their collective grief.

Yet, fate was about to judge another soul. The question of what Fanny Bolton expected stood stark and terrifying. Society, that brutal jury, rarely offers leniency. Women are fierce guardians of morality, ready to tear apart any female who strays from the path. The narrator, adopting the persona of a Turk, warned the "spotless" accusers of their power to condemn: be cautious before ordering the execution, lest you strike the wrong soul. If Fanny were guilty, justice must be swift; if innocent, mercy was due. But the judgment hung in the balance, waiting for evidence that might never arrive.

Back in Lamb Court, the atmosphere shifted from tragedy to a peculiar, vibrant convalescence. The Major, ever the benevolent host, encouraged the budding friendship between Warrington and Laura. He arranged outings to exhibitions and concerts, insisting that their honor was "entirely spotless."
