To the Major, Laura represented purity and natural charm, a stark contrast to Pendennis, whom he viewed as a spoiled dandy, weary of the servitude that had raised him. While Pen, languid and indifferent, treated the attentions of women with bored resignation, Laura, driven by a natural instinct to love, found herself captivated by Warrington. She saw in him a vitality and genuine kindness that Pen lacked, a man unburdened by the vanity that defined his cousin.

Why did Laura love him? The narrator posits a universal truth: young ladies fall in love not with abstract ideals, but with the person standing before them, constantly praised and observed. Having formed an image of Warrington in her heart, she tended to it with devotion. When she traveled back to London, her conversations with Helen were a litany of Arthur Pendennis's virtues—from his refusal to extract a tooth to his generosity toward beggars. These tales were less about the reality of the man and more about the projection of the ideal, a hero figure constructed by the adoration of those around him.

Life in Lamb Court became a theater of quiet joys. The Major, enchanted by London's autumn, turned his lodging into a sanctuary. He orchestrated elaborate meals, securing delicacies like jellies and aspics from a renowned chef, and even procuring rare Amontillado via the Marquis of Steyne, a gift from the King of Spain. The room transformed into a place of mirth. Warrington taught Laura to cry "Hear! Hear!" during dinner toasts, tapping the table with his knuckles, while the Major delivered speeches on the "present occasion." The servant, Pidgeon, grinned, and Dr. Goodenough joined in the levity.

The evenings were punctuated by music and games. Warrington, despite his lack of formal training, sat entranced by Laura's contralto voice as she played a simple piano and sang old songs. Below, a mysterious "pale little girl in a black bonnet"—presumably Fanny or a neighbor—stood at the lamp post, listening to the melodies drifting from the open windows. As twilight deepened, the Major and Warrington would play cards, while Laura worked wool for a pair of slippers, likely intended for Arthur, George, or the Major himself. The Major joked about the cost of the materials, his tone laced with affectionate teasing.

Before ten o'clock, the St. Clement's clock chimed, signaling the hour. Martha from Fairoaks appeared, lighting candles for departure, and the group bid farewell to the "sporting door" behind which the young mistress and her maid locked themselves away. Later, Warrington walked the Major home, shaking hands on the doorstep, discussing Laura's charming blushes and her modest fortune. As they parted, Warrington's face fell, the Major's joviality replaced by a somber reflection on his own unremarked life. He returned to his chambers, smoking pipes and writing savage articles, haunted by the distance between his rugged existence and the polished world he admired.

Pen improved rapidly, his appetite voracious, devouring roast chickens with the ferocity of an ogre. But amidst the recovery, a tender, confidential event unfolded. During his delirium, Dr. Goodenough had ordered ice applied to Pen's head and commanded the removal of all his hair. This catastrophe was revealed to the Major, who, with a blush and a wink, offered to send a coiffeur to restore the "ravaged beauty." Laughter ensued; Warrington and the Major jokingly proposed wigs, culminating in a gentleman from Burlington Arcade delivering a box containing Pen's own hair, which they paraded with flowers to create a ridiculous, festive crown. For ten days, the house rang with laughter, a stark contrast to the gloom that had preceded

Fanny's Occupation's Gone

Since Arthur's illness, Mrs. Pendennis, known to the world as "good Helen," had effectively taken command of her son's private quarters, turning his drawers and closets into a bureau of domestic surveillance. While she ostensibly managed his laundry—repairing shirts missing buttons and stockings needing mends—she inevitably discovered the papers hidden among the linens. These were letters requiring answers, a burden she assumed during Arthur's weakened state. Though Mrs. Pendennis never explicitly broached the "dreadful Fanny Bolton mystery" to her son, it haunted her mind with inexpressible anxiety. To shield him further, she had removed the brass knocker from his inner door, ensuring that the postman's jarring double rap would not disturb his convalescence. Consequently, no mail reached Arthur, not even urgent notes from bootmakers or hatters demanding payment.

Mrs. Pendennis, however, possessed a modest fortune saved through frugality and self-denial. She delighted in using these funds to settle Arthur's debts, viewing it as a moral imperative. Indeed, many a dutiful parent would welcome the chance to intercept a son's correspondence, yet few find the same joy in paying off a youthful indiscretion. For Mrs. Pendennis, the silence of the postbox was a blessing; she spared Pen the trouble of hearing the dreaded *rat-tat*.

Yet, the widow's vigilance was not absolute. When at Richmond, she sought counsel from her brother-in-law, Major Pendennis. Sitting on a terrace bench with Pen and Mr. Warrington, she poured out her terrors regarding Arthur's conduct, though naturally omitting the grosser details of her son's financial ruin. She confessed her confusion about the "odious transaction" involving Fanny Bolton, asking the Major for guidance.

The Major, a man whose morals were far less rigid than his sister's, adopted a theatrical demeanor. He puckered his face, casting sidelong glances at the carpet, and finally declared, "My dear, good creature, I don't know anything about it... Young men will be young men." When Helen, looking stately, protested against hearing such sins spoken lightly, the Major feigned offense, claiming he spoke only to spare her feelings. He suggested that Arthur might simply be "flirting with a little grisette," a notion that caused Helen to sob violently, fearing her honorable boy had fallen low.

"The most unhappy mother in the world!" she cried.

The Major, amused by her distress, gently chided her for treating her son like a hermit. He warned that suppressing letters could breed resentment, urging caution. Then, the critical moment arrived. Helen produced a single, tiny letter, sealed with wax, which she claimed was all that existed. The Major, despite his outward gravity, struggled to maintain composure as he examined the document. It appeared to be written in a timid, sprawling hand, containing nothing more than prayers for Arthur's health and a desire to see him.

"Morgan, my man, has made inquiries," the Major explained, adopting a conspiratorial tone. "It seems this Fanny was greatly smitten, paid for her own pleasures at Vauxhall, and her father is a violent drunkard. But here is the crux: the girl's mother swears to her innocence, while the girl herself claims Arthur treated her like a brute. Yet, the facts suggest otherwise. A laundress named Flanagan confirmed that Fanny arrived only when Arthur was delirious, accompanied by an elderly gentleman, Mr. Bows, who kindly transported the Major from Stillbrook."

Helen, relieved by this revelation, hurried to the terrace where Arthur sat with Warrington. She intended to prove his innocence. However, the Major, anticipating disaster, intercepted her. He reasoned that if Helen confronted Arthur immediately, the truth would shatter everything. Instead, he proposed a grand scheme: a trip abroad to the Rhine. "We must occupy him," the Major insisted, envisioning a journey where Arthur could recover and forget his troubles under the care of a nurse and Miss Bell.

The plan galvanized Helen. She rushed to her desk, counting her meager savings and eyeing her diamond cross, desperate to fund the escape. Meanwhile, the Major, now a "fatigued and happy warrior," dozed on the sofa, dreaming pleasantly of his diplomatic triumph.

Back on the terrace, Pen and Warrington discussed future literary endeavors, Pen boasting of a new tragedy. Suddenly, Miss Bell arrived, bringing game for Pen and flowers for his mother. She reported that Helen was deeply worn out, her health failing under the strain of watching over Arthur. The Major, overhearing her concern, seized the opportunity to manipulate the narrative. He hinted that Helen's agitation stemmed from the recent scandal, proposing that they travel to Europe where painful memories could be left behind.

Helen, moved by the prospect of relief, agreed instantly. She resolved to borrow money if necessary, eager to whisk Arthur away to a distant watering-place. The Major, pleased with his strategy, turned his attention to securing Warrington's participation. Though Warrington initially hesitated, citing lack of funds and personal engagements, the Major offered to act as guarantor, promising to cover expenses and inviting him to join the party.

At the tea-table, with Helen momentarily absent, the Major renewed his plea. "Isn't it unfriendly?" he coaxed, addressing Miss Bell. "Here we have been the happiest party in the world." Under the gaze of the Major and the shy glance of Laura, Warrington blushed. When Laura softly expressed her hope that he would join, Warrington yielded, murmuring, "I will go anywhere you ask me."

Just as the Major prepared to celebrate the agreement, shouting "Bravo!" and shaking hands, Helen reappeared, candle in hand, her face pale with fright. Laura quickly withdrew her hand, crimson with embarrassment.

"What is it?" Helen asked.

"It's a bargain we have been making," the Major announced smoothly, his voice dripping with false sweetness. "We have just bound over Mr. Warrington in a promise to come abroad with us."

Helen stared, stunned, realizing that the Major's machinations had inadvertently trapped her son in a web of deception before he was even well enough to walk.

Could Helen have foreseen that as Pen's strength returned, his foolish infatuation for little Fanny would reignite? Though she never uttered a syllable concerning the girl after her talk with the Major, and though she appeared to treat Fanny as non-existent, Mrs. Pendennis kept a hawk-like eye on Master Arthur. Under the guise of his fragile health, she barely allowed him out of her sight and was determined to spare him the trouble of any correspondence for the present. Likely, Arthur trembled as he glanced at his own letters; likely, receiving them at the family table while feeling his mother's gaze fixed intently on her teacup or book, he waited daily for a scrap of handwriting he had never seen, his heart pounding with anticipation. Was he more pleased or annoyed that day after day his hopes were unfulfilled? Was his mind relieved that no letter from Fanny arrived? Though, no doubt, when Lovelace tires of Clarissa (or vice versa), it is best for both to break at once and pursue separate paths through life, solitude notwithstanding, our self-love, pity, or sense of decency cannot stomach such sudden bankruptcy. Before announcing to the world that "Lovelace & Co." cannot meet its obligations, we strive for compromises; we hold mournful partner meetings; we delay putting up the shutters and making the dreary announcement of failure. It must come, however: we pawn our jewels merely to keep the show running a little longer. On the whole, I suspect Pen was rather irritated that he received no remonstrances from Fanny. What! Could she depart from him and not even glance back? Could she sink without holding out a small hand or crying, "Help, Arthur?" Well, well: not everyone who ventures on that voyage goes down. A few drown when the ship founders, but most are merely dunked before scrambling ashore. As for whether A. Pendennis, Esquire, of the Upper Temple, belonged to the class of persons likely to sink or swim, the reader's experience will surely decide.

Though Pen was still too weak to walk half a mile and could not be trusted to take a carriage drive alone without a nurse, Helen could not keep watch over Mr. Warrington. She lacked the authority to prevent him from traveling to London if business called, and indeed, if he had stayed, perhaps the widow, for reasons of her own, would have been content. However, she quelled these selfish wishes as soon as she acknowledged them, remembering Warrington's great regard and constant friendship for her boy, and treating him as a member of her family with her usual melancholy kindness. Yet, one morning when his affairs summoned him to town, she divined his errand: he had gone to London to gather news about Fanny for Pen.

Arthur had discussed his adventures with Fanny—which the reader already knows—and his feelings regarding her, with his friend. He was thankful he had escaped the great dangers to which Warrington assented heartily. He confessed he had no great fault to reproach himself with regarding his behavior, but if they parted, as they must, he wished to say a God bless her and hope she would remember him kindly. Speaking with so much gravity and emotion, George Warrington—who had strongly advocated for the separation—began to fear his friend was not so well cured as he boasted, worrying that if they reunited, the danger and temptation might have to be fought again. With what result? "It is hard to struggle, Arthur, and easy to fall," Warrington observed. "And the best courage for us poor wretches is to fly from danger. I would not be what I am now had I practiced what I preach."

"And what did you practice, George?" Pen asked eagerly. "I knew there was something. Tell us about it, Warrington."

"There was something that can't be mended, and that shattered my whole fortunes early," Warrington answered. "I said I would tell you about it some day, Pen: and will, but not now. Take the moral without the fable now, Pen, my boy; and if you want to see a man whose whole life has been wrecked by an unlucky rock against which he struck as a boy—here he is, Arthur: and so I warn you."

Meanwhile, we have seen how Mr. Huxter, writing home to his Clavering friends, mentioned a fashionable club in London where he met an Irish officer of distinction who provided intelligence regarding Pendennis. This club was none other than the Back Kitchen, where the disciple of Saint Bartholomew met the General. The peculiarities of the General's brogue, appearance, disposition, and conversation greatly amused the young gentlemen who used the Back Kitchen for nightly entertainment. Huxter, possessing a fine natural genius for mimicry, whether of actors, cocks, or corkscrews, studied the General with gusto, twanging his poor old long bow whenever drink, a hearer, and an opportunity occurred. A bait of sixpennyworth of brandy-and-water was always enough to make the worthy old man swallow, and under its influence, who was happier than he to tell stories of his daughter's triumphs and his own exploits in love, war, drink, and polite society? Thus, Huxter presented many pictures of Costigan to his friends: of Costigan fighting a jewel in the Phoenix, of his interview with the Duke of York, of his son-unlaw's table surrounded by the nobility of his country, and of Costigan, when crying drunk, lamenting his daughter's ingratitude and stating that his grey hairs were hastening to a premature grave. Through these tales, Huxter attracted a number of young fellows to the Back Kitchen, who consumed the landlord's liquors while relishing the General's peculiarities, so that the host pardoned many of the latter's foibles in consideration of the good they brought to his house. Not the highest position in life was this, certainly, nor one we would wish for an old man if we revered him, but of this aged buffoon it may be mentioned that he had no particular idea his condition was low, nor did he harbor a black drop in his whiskied blood or a bitter feeling in his muddled brains against any mortal being. Even his cruel child, Emily, he would have taken to his heart and forgiven with tears; and what more can one say of Christian charity than that a man is ready to forgive those who have done him every kindness and with whom he is wrong in a dispute!

Some among the young men who frequented the Back Kitchen believed the Captain made a mystery regarding his lodgings, fearing debt collectors or desiring privacy, and lived in some wonderful place. Nor would the landlord, when questioned, answer inquiries, maintaining his maxim that he only knew gentlemen in that room, and when they quit, his communication with them ceased, making it impertinent curiosity to ask where any other gentleman lived. Costigan, in his most intoxicated and confidential moments, also evaded replies. There was no particular secret, as we have seen who have entered his apartments, but in the vicissitudes of a long life, he had resided in houses where privacy was necessary to his comfort, where the appearance of visitors would bring nothing but displeasure. Hence, legends arose: that he slept habitually in a watch-box in the City; in a cab at a mews, where a proprietor gave him shelter; in the Duke of York's Column, etc.—wild theories put abroad by the facetious and imaginative Huxter. For Huxey, when not silenced by the company of "swells" and when in the society of his own friends, was a very different fellow from the youth cowed by Pen's impertinent airs, and adored by his family at home, was the life and soul of the circle he met, either round the festive board or the dissecting table.

On one brilliant September morning, as Huxter regaled himself with coffee at a stall in Covent Garden, having spent a delicious night dancing at Vauxhall, he spied the General reeling down Henrietta Street, with a crowd of hooting blackguard boys at his heels, who had left their beds under the river arches betimes and were prowling for breakfast. The poor old General was not in a condition where the sneers of these young beggars had little effect. The cabmen and watermen at the stand knew him and commented, the policemen gazed after him and warned the boys off with looks of scorn and pity. What did the scorn and pity of men, the jokes of ribald children, matter to the General? He reeled along with glazed eyes, having just sense enough to know whither he was bound and to pursue his accustomed beat homewards. He went to bed not knowing how he had reached it, as often as any man in London. He woke and found himself there, asked no questions, and continued tacking about on this daily though perilous voyage, when, from his station at the coffee-stall, Huxter spied him. To note his friend, to pay his twopence (indeed, he had but eightpence left, or he would have had a cab from Vauxhall), was with the eager Huxter the work of an instant—Costigan dived down the alleys by Drury Lane Theatre, where gin-shops, oyster-shops, and theatrical wardrobes abound, the proprietors of which were now asleep behind their shutters as the pink morning lighted up their chimneys; and through these courts Huxter followed the General until he reached Oldcastle Street, wherein lies the gate of Shepherd's Inn.

Just as he was within sight of home,

Major Pendennis kept his word to Warrington as well as his conscience allowed, successfully convincing his poor wife Helen that the connection between her son and the detestable little gatekeeper was severed forever. Helen, in turn, could lay aside her fears of an imprudent attachment or a humiliating match for Pen. Young Arthur, having shaken off the initial blow to his vanity, breathed easier knowing that Miss Fanny was not destined to die of love for him and that no dire consequences awaited their brief, unfortunate liaison.

With these anxieties dispelled, the entire party was free to execute their planned journey to the Continent. Arthur Pendennis, a *rentier* and *voyageant*, traveled alongside Madame Pendennis and Mademoiselle Bell, accompanied by George Warrington, a *particulier* of thirty-two years, six feet tall, of ordinary features, black hair, and a black beard. They secured passports from the Consul of His Majesty the King of the Belgians at Dover and crossed the channel to Ostend. From there, they leisurely toured Bruges and Ghent on their way to Brussels and eventually the Rhine.

It is not the purpose of this narrative to chronicle this well-trodden circuit, nor to detail Laura's delight in the tranquil, ancient cities she encountered for the first time, nor Helen's wonder at the Beguine convents. Yet, the sight of black-veiled nuns kneeling with outstretched arms before illuminated altars filled her with almost terror. She observed strange pomp and ceremony in Catholic worship, barefoot friars roaming the streets, crowned images of saints and virgins before which crowds bowed in open defiance, as she believed, of the written law. Priests in gorgeous robes lurked in dark confessionals; theaters were open, and people danced on Sundays. These new sights and manners shocked and bewildered the simple country lady.

Whenever the young men returned from their evening drives or walks, they found the books of devotion laid out on the table. Upon entering, Laura would typically pause in reading the Psalms or sacred pages, which Helen above all others cherished. The recent events concerning her son had cruelly shaken her spirit. Laura watched every movement of her dearest friend with intense, hidden anxiety. Poor Pen remained constant and affectionate in his attentions to his mother, whose wounded bosom still yearned for him. Yet, a secret barrier existed between them, accompanied by an anguish bordering on rage on Helen's part. She felt dispossessed of her son's heart, sensing recesses within it that she neither dared nor could enter. She longed for the sacred days of boyhood when Arthur's heart held no secrets, when she was his all in all, when he poured his hopes, pleasures, childish griefs, vanities, and triumphs into her willing and tender embrace. Now, however, fate, selfishness, and nature had driven him forth on wayward wings to range his own flight, sing his own song, and seek his own home and mate.

Watching this devouring care and racking disappointment in her friend, Laura once remarked to Helen, "If Pen had loved me as you wished, I should have gained him, but I should have lost you, Mamma; I know I should. And I like you to love me best. Men do not know what it is to love as we do, I think." Helen, sighing, assented to this portion of the speech, though she protested against the former part. For my part, I suppose Miss Laura was right in both statements, particularly regarding the latter assertion, which is an old and received truism: love is an hour with us, but it is all night and all day with a woman. Damon has taxes, sermons, parades, tailor's bills, parliamentary duties, and the devil knows what else to contend with; Delia has to think about Damon. Damon is the oak—or the post—that stands upright, while Delia is the ivy or honeysuckle whose arms twine about him. Is it not so, Delia? Is it not your nature to creep about his feet and kiss them, to twine round his trunk and hang there, while Damon stands like a British man with his hands in his breeches pockets, tolerating the pretty fond parasite clinging around him?

Old Pendennis had merely accompanied his friends to the water's edge before leaving them aboard the boat, entrusting the chief charge of the little expedition to Warrington. Old Pendennis himself was bound for a brief visit to the residence of a great man and friend. After this sojourn, he proposed joining his sister-in-law at the German watering-place to which the party was bound. The Major felt that his long attentions to his sick family had earned him a little relaxation. Although the best of the partridges had been thinned off, the pheasants remained at Stillbrook, where the noble owner still resided. Old Pendennis betook himself to that hospitable mansion, finding great comfort within. A royal duke, some noted foreigners, illustrious statesmen, and pleasant people visited it. It did the old fellow's heart good to see his name listed in the *Morning Post* amidst the distinguished company entertained by the Marquis of Steyne at his country-house. He proved a very useful and pleasant personage in a country-house, entertaining young men with queer little anecdotes and risqué stories during shooting parties and in the smoking-room, where they laughed at him and with him. He was obsequious with the ladies in the morning rooms, walking the new arrivals about the park and gardens, showing them the *carte du pays*, pointing out the best views of the mansion and the lake, indicating where timber was to be felled, tracing the old roads before bridges were built, and recounting where old Lord Lynx discovered Sir Phelim O'Neal on his knees before her ladyship. He called lodge-keepers and gardeners by name, knew exactly how many domestics sat in the housekeeper's room and how many dined in the servants'-hall. He had a word for everybody, about everybody, and a little against everybody. Invaluable in a country-house, he richly merited and enjoyed his vacation after his labors. Perhaps, while thus deservedly enjoying himself with country friends, the Major was not displeased at transferring the command of the family expedition to Warrington, thereby keeping him in the service of the ladies—a servitude George was only too willing to undergo for his friend's sake and for the sake of a society he found increasingly delightful.

Warrington, a proficient German scholar, willingly gave Miss Laura lessons in the language, who was eager to improve, whereas Pen, for his part, was too weak or lazy to resume his studies. Warrington acted as courier and interpreter, managing baggage, money matters, and putting the little troop into marching order. He located the English church and walked with great decorum alongside Mrs. Pendennis and Miss Laura if they chose to attend. He walked beside Mrs. Pendennis's donkey on evening excursions, arranged carriages for her, procured copies of *Galignani*, and even devised comfortable seats under lime-trees for her when guests paraded after dinner near the Kursaal band, where our tired friends stopped to listen to their pleasant music. Many a fine-whiskered Prussian or French dandy, come to the baths for the game of *Trente-et-quarante*, cast longing glances toward the pretty, fresh-colored English girl who accompanied the pale widow and would have longed to dance the galop or waltz with her. But Laura rarely appeared in the ballroom, save once or twice when Pen condescended to walk with her. As for Warrington, that rough diamond lacked the polish of a dancing-master and did not know how to waltz, though he would have liked to learn had he had such a partner as Laura. Such a partner! Psha, what had a stiff bachelor to do with partners and waltzing? What was he about, dancing attendance here, drinking in sweet pleasure at a risk he knew not of what after-sadness, regret, and lonely longing? Yet, he stayed on. To the unobservant eye, one might say he was the widow's son, watching her with constant care and watchfulness; or perhaps an adventurer seeking to marry her fortune, or at least secure some great treasure or benefit from her. Very likely he did, for ours, as the reader has possibly already discovered, is a Selfish Story, and almost every person, according to his nature and the way of the world, is occupied about Number One. Thus, Warrington selfishly devoted himself to Helen, who selfishly devoted herself to Pen, who in turn selfishly devoted himself to himself at this present period, having no other personage or object to occupy him except his mother's health, which gave him serious and real disquiet. Though they sat together, they spoke little, and a cloud always hung between them.

Every day Laura looked for Warrington and received him with increasing frankness and eager welcome. He found himself talking to her as he did not know he could, performing acts of gallantry that astonished him afterward. He found himself staring blankly into the glass at the crow's-feet around his eyes, at streaks of white in his hair, and at intrusive silver bristles in his grim, blue beard. He found himself looking at the young bucks at the bath—the bland, tight-waisted Germans, the capering Frenchmen with their lacquered mustaches and trimmed varnished boots, the English dandies, including Pen, with their calm, domineering airs and insolent languor. He envied each of these youths some excellence or quality of youth, or good looks,

The modest feast prepared by the poor widow, aided by her faithful servant Martha—who chuckled at the German customs and managed the household with a cheerful efficiency—was attended only by Major Pendennis and his two younger companions. Helen had declined the honor, sending word that she was too unwell to dine, leaving Laura to bear her company in silence. During dinner, the Major chattered loudly, oblivious to—or perhaps deliberately ignoring—the heavy, suffocating gloom that clung to the two silent sisters.

It was not until evening that Helen and Laura emerged from their confinement into the sitting-room. Helen entered leaning heavily on Laura, her back turned to the dying light, shielding her pallid, woe-stricken countenance from Arthur's view. Yet, as she approached Pen, whom she had not seen throughout the day, she collapsed into him. She wrapped her fond, trembling arms around his shoulders and pressed a kiss to his cheek. Immediately, Laura withdrew, retreating to a distant corner of the room. Pen observed the scene with a mixture of shock and irritation; his mother's voice and entire frame vibrated with a terrible tremor. Her hand, clammy and cold as ice, clutched his forehead, and she embraced him with a piteous intensity. The sight of her misery, rather than softening him, seemed to inflame the young man's inherent wrath and testiness. He barely returned her kiss, meeting her desperate appeal with a hard, cruel expression. *"She persecutes me,"* he thought bitterly, *"and she comes to me with the air of a martyr!"*

"You look very ill, my child," she whispered, her voice failing. "I cannot bear to see you looking so." She tottered onto a sofa, still gripping one of his passive hands with her thin, freezing fingers.

"I have had much to annoy me, mother," Pen said, his chest heaving. As the words left his lips, Helen's heart hammered so violently against her ribs that she sat almost dead with terror.

Warrington, Laura, and the Major stood frozen, sensing the approaching storm.

"I have received letters from London," Arthur continued, his voice rising, "and one in particular has caused me more agony than anything in my life. It informs me that previous letters of mine have been intercepted and stolen—that a young woman who has shown me nothing but love and care has been cruelly betrayed by—you, Mother!"

"For God's sake, stop!" cried Warrington. "She is ill! Don't you see?"

"Let him go on," the widow murmured faintly.

"Let him go on and kill her!" Laura shouted, rushing to her mother's side. "Speak on, sir, and watch her die!"

"It is *you* who are cruel!" Pen roared, his heart revolting indignantly at the injustice laid at his door. "It is you who attribute all this pain to me! It is you who wield your wicked reproaches, your wicked suspicions, and your wicked persecutions against those who love me—against those who brave everything for me, whom you despise and trample upon solely because they are of lower birth! Shall I tell you what I will do? What I am resolved to do now that I know your conduct? I will return to that poor girl whom you cast out of my doors and ask her to share my home with me! I will defy the pride that torments her and the pitiless suspicion that insults us both!"

"Do you mean, Pen, that you—" Helen began, her eyes wide and hands outstretched, but Laura silenced her instantly. "Silence, hush, dear mother," she cried, and the widow obeyed. Though Pen spoke savagely, she hung on his every word, nearly swooning with eagerness.

"By Gad, I say he shan't go on, or I won't hear him, by Gad!" the Major thundered, trembling with rage. "If you choose, sir, after all we've done for you, after all I've done for you personally, to insult your mother and disgrace your name by allying yourself with a low-born kitchen-maid, go and do it, by Gad—but let us, ma'am, have no further connection with him. I wash my hands of you, sir! I wash my hands of you! I am an old fellow, and I haven't long for this world. I come of an ancient and honorable family, by Gad, and I hoped, before I went off the hooks, by Jove, that the fellow I liked, raised, and nursed through life, by Jove, would do something to prove that our name—the name of Pendennis, by Gad—was left undishonoured behind us. But if he won't, damny, I say, amen. By G—, both my father and my brother Jack were the proudest men in England, and I never thought there would come this disgrace to my name—never—and I'm ashamed that it's Arthur Pendennis."

The old fellow's voice broke into a sob, the second time Arthur had drawn tears from those wrinkled lids. The sound of his broken voice instantly stilled Pen's fury. He ceased pacing the room. Laura stood by Helen's sofa, and Warrington, who had remained a silent observer, watched the family storm with intense interest. Darkness had fallen as the parties spoke. Following the passionate outburst of the Major, a deep, trembling voice cut through the twilight.

"Will you let me tell you something about myself, my kind friends?" the voice asked. "You have been so good to me, ma'am, so kind to me, Laura—I hope I may call you so sometimes—my dear Pen and I have been such friends that I have longed to tell you my story as it is, though I would have sooner if it were not so sad and contains another's secret. However, it may do good for Arthur to know it—it is right that everyone here should. It will divert you from thinking about a subject which, born of a fatal misconception, has caused so much pain to all. May I please tell you, Mrs. Pendennis?"

"Pray speak," Helen whispered, her mind already racing with a terrifying hope that Pen's words had ignited.

George filled a bumper of wine, drained it, and began. "You all know me as a man with no desire to advance in the world: careless of reputation, living in a garret from hand to mouth, though I have friends and a name, and surely the capabilities for greatness if I had the mind. But mind I have none. I shall likely die in that garret, alone. I nailed myself to that doom in early life. Shall I tell you what fascinated me about Arthur years ago, and inclined me toward him when I first saw him? The men from Oxford and Cambridge told me tales of that early affair with the Chatteris actress—a topic Pen has discussed with me since—which, but for the Major's generalship, might have made you his daughter-in-law, ma'am. I cannot see Pen in the dark, but he blushes, I'm sure; and I dare say Miss Bell does too; and my friend Major Pendennis, I dare say, laughs as he ought, for he won. What would Arthur's lot have been had he been tied at nineteen to an illiterate woman older than himself, with no qualities in common to make companions? No equality, no confidence, no love quickly? He would have been most miserable. And when he spoke just now of threatening a similar union, be assured it was but a threat occasioned by anger, which you must allow was very natural on his part. After a generous, manly, self-denying conduct—which is rare—he has met with unkind suspicion from friends and complained of unfair treatment toward an innocent person, toward whom he and you all stand deeply indebted."

The widow rose, but Warrington, seeing her attempt, asked, "Do I tire you, ma'am?"

"Oh no—go on, go on," Helen said, delighted, and he continued.

"I liked him because of that early history, which reached my ears in college gossip, and because I like a man, if you'll pardon me, Miss Laura, who shows he can have a great, unreasonable attachment for a woman. That is why we became friends—and remain friends here, aren't we?" he added, leaning closer to her in a lower tone. "And Pen has been a great comfort and companion to a lonely, unfortunate man."

"I am not complaining of my lot," George continued softly. "No man's lot is what he wishes it to be. Up in my garret, where you left the flowers, with my old books and pipe for a wife, I am content, occasionally envying men with brighter careers or the ability to solace ill fortune with the affection of a woman or a child." A sigh escaped from near Warrington, and a hand extended toward him, only to be instantly withdrawn by prudish modesty. Checking the motion, kindly friendship shrank back, ashamed, and Warrington resumed his tale.

"My fate is what I made, and not lucky for me or others involved. Before college, as a boy of eighteen, I was at a private tutor's. Like Arthur, I became attached, or fancied I was, to a woman of much lower degree and greater age. You shrink from me..."

"No, I don't," Laura said. Resolutely, she placed her hand in his. She had divined his story from earlier hints.

"She was a yeoman's daughter in the neighborhood," Warrington said, his voice faltering. "I fancied what all young men fancy.

The Great Festival of Epsom Downs, where London takes its annual holiday, proved to be a veritable circus of the elite. Amidst the throngs, a comfortable open carriage, drawn by a pair of spirited horses, carried Mrs. Bungay of Paternoster Row, resplendent in attire that suggested she was a modern Solomon in all her glory. Beside her sat modest Mrs. Shandon, a steadfast friend since the dawn of their acquaintance, while the worthy publisher, Mr. Bungay, recovered from a copious luncheon by furiously shying at nearby sticks until sweat poured down his bald pate. Nearby, Finucane attended the ladies with dutiful vigilance, intercepting gentlemen connected to the publishing house who sought to pay their respects.

Mr. Archer, a man of boundless gossip, approached to pay his compliments. With the enthusiasm of a tour guide, he regaled Mrs. Bungay with the location of the Prime Minister and urged her to back "Borax," though Archer privately favored "Munmeer." He then launched into a tale of international diplomacy involving the Chinese Ambassador. Archer described how, despite bringing his own cook, the Ambassador had once suffered a culinary tragedy at Archer's own table. The cook, having seized the opportunity while Archer was absent, had slaughtered Mrs. Archer's beloved Blenheim spaniel—a dog inherited from the Duke of Marlborough and whose lineage traced back to the Battle of Malplaquet—to stuff him with forcemeat for the second course.

"Law!" gasped Mrs. Bungay, horrified.

Archer detailed the aftermath: the cook screaming up the stairs, the discovery of Fido's skin in the scullery, and the immediate severing of ties between the Archers and the Ambassador. He noted that the Lord Mayor, however, had enjoyed the dish immensely, remarking that it tasted remarkably like duck when paired with green peas. The conversation drifted to the tragic fate of Lady Flamingo, who Archer claimed was squeezing her feet down for marriage to the Ambassador, a prospect he deemed destined to cripple her permanently. Comparing her plight to Mrs. Archer's own tiny feet, Archer concluded with a sigh regarding the absurdities of cross-cultural matrimonial customs.

Suddenly, Mrs. Bungay spotted a carriage containing Mr. Pendennis and Mr. Warrington. She noted Pendennis's somber demeanor and his association with "tip-top company." Archer revealed that Pendennis possessed an annual income of eighteen hundred from land and twenty-two thousand five hundred in consols, explaining that his Uncle Major had wisely tied up the assets to prevent squandering. As they identified Lord Rochester, Lord John, and Sir James, Archer proudly declared them his "intimate friends." He singled out the Duke of Hampshire as a model of antiquarian virtue, recounting how the Duke had attended sixty-five Derbies, having first ridden a piebald pony alongside the future Prince of Wales at age seven.

Further down the line, Mrs. Bungay identified a yellow carriage surrounded by parasols. Archer explained this was Lady Clavering, accompanied by her tipsy son and Miss Amory, Lady Clavering's daughter from a first marriage. Archer hinted that Miss Amory held a secret affection for Pendennis, though he suspected her heart was elsewhere, citing the recent suicide attempt of the brewer's son, young Mr. Foker, who had been rescued from hanging by his valet due to a fatal infatuation for Miss Amory.

While the aristocracy marveled, an hour prior, a battered stagecoach known as Wheeler's "Harlequin's Head" drag had arrived from Bow Street, carrying a motley crew of "choice spirits" and shabby swashbucklers. As the Derby race thundered past, the passengers shouted wild predictions—"The Cornet!" "Muffineer!" "Blue Sleeves!"—until the signal announced the winner: Podasokus. One of the passengers, a Colonel, leaped onto the roof in jubilation, only to crash back down, cracking the wood under his weight. He roared for supper, declaring that everyone should join him regardless of cost, prompting whispers among the "dusky dandies" that he stood to win fifteen hundred pounds and urging caution lest anyone get him into debt.

In stark contrast to the Coach of the Spirits, Cab No. 2002 rolled gently along, transporting a gentleman and two ladies. Inside, a repast of lobster salad and bitter ale was enjoyed by a young lady, Fanny Bolton, and her mother. Fanny, fresh and rosy, attracted the gaze of many a dandy strolling near the "Sticks," the gambling tables where sticks were thrown for amusement. Her escort, Mr. Sam Huxter, a young surgeon, was a whirlwind of activity, filling the cab with trophies from the Sticks—including pincushions, wooden apples, and jack-in-the-boxes. He even brought a gypsy to tell fortunes, though the prophecy that Fanny must beware of a "fair man" who would prove false caused a momentary chill in the otherwise sunny atmosphere.

Then, the scene shifted. Arthur Pendennis, looking dandified in black crape and jet buttons, suddenly encountered Cab No. 2002. His mood instantly soured, replaced by a "severe demeanour" born of alarmed morality. He viewed the scene through the lens of a prude, suspecting impropriety between the ladies and the rough-looking Mr. Sam Huxter, whose bare hands and heavy boots seemed worlds apart from Pendennis's refined lavender gloves.

Inside the cab, chaos erupted. Mrs. Bolton spoke with frantic volubility, listing her knowledge of Mr. Pendennis and praising Mr. Warrington's height, nearly breaking his head on gate posts. Fanny's heart pounded as she compared Pendennis's immaculate appearance to Huxter's disheveled state, noting the blackened glove mark on Pendennis's hand versus Huxter's dirty fist. Yet, when Huxter extended his hand, the gesture softened the air. Pendennis, seeing the offer of refreshment, melted his stern features into a genial smile. He accepted Huxter's handshake, leaving a black stain on his pristine glove, and toasted the party with frothy, lukewarm beer, declaring it "delicious."

As they parted ways, the dynamic shifted again. A young lady in a dove-colored dress, Miss Amory, leaned on the arm of a stalwart gentleman with a military mustache, Captain Strong. She exchanged playful French insults with Pendennis, calling him a "monster," which he dismissed with a laugh, pointing out her safety between her mother and her guardian. However, the mention of lobsters and laughter haunted Pendennis later, as Miss Amory warned that if Huxter attended to him when sick, he would kill him.

By evening, the carriage of Lady Clavering departed, leaving behind a trail of complications. The "Begum" (Lady Clavering) was in high spirits, boasting of a lottery win, but her husband, Sir Francis, had been secretly gambling. Young Hopeful Clavering, the heir, confessed to having backed both Borax and Podasokus, revealing that his father had lost seven thousand pounds. The revelation shattered the mood; Lady Clavering wept, vowing to separate from her "blackleg" husband, while Captain Strong, trapped by his own tongue, confirmed the betrayal.

As the carriage rolled toward Grosvenor Place, the scene transformed from festive hilarity to grim domestic reality. The Major, observing the trajectory of the Clavering fortune, feared the inevitable bankruptcy that plagued the family line. Meanwhile, inside the carriage, the young heirs slept off their excesses, while Miss Amory plotted future balls and dances, oblivious to the financial abyss opening beneath her family's wheels. The day ended not with the triumph of the race, but with the sobering calculations of debt, the tears of a betrayed wife, and the silent, heavy burden of inheritance falling upon the shoulders of the innocent.

Nearly a year has slipped by since the last time we saw Arthur Pendennis in a state of sartorial distress. Now, the black coat is retired to make way for a dashing blue frockcoat, and the wig—a heavy burden of the past—is finally laid aside. Though his natural hair, having suffered a slight thinning, has been allowed to re-enter the public sphere, it looks far less like a broom-brush than it did previously. Most significantly, Arthur has earned the honor of appearing at Court in the uniform of a Cornet in the Clavering troop of the Yeomanry Cavalry, presented to the Sovereign by the formidable Marquis of Steyne.

This elevation was a measure vigorously, and somewhat pathetically, pushed by Arthur's uncle. The Major simply refused to let a single year go by without this ceremony of "gentlemanhood." To the old gentleman, it seemed imperative that his nephew abandon the rather unsavory Megatherium Club for something far more select. Indeed, the Major has loudly proclaimed his disappointment across the globe regarding the financial prospects of his nephew, noting dryly that the estate has failed to materialize into the windfall he had hoped for, currently yielding a modest income of just under fifteen hundred a year.

And so, this is the figure by which the world measures Pendennis. Where once publishers treated him with suspicion, they now regard him with a newfound, if slightly condescending, respect. Even mothers have ceased to be outright rude. The logic is sound: if the prettier daughters are destined to marry men of vastly superior expectations, then at least the plain ones remain available. Since the dazzling Myra is rumored to be snaring an Earl, it follows that poor little Beatrice—who possesses a distinct asymmetry in her shoulders—must settle for a boor throughout her life. Why, then, should not Mr. Pendennis serve as her support?

In the first winter following his mother's inheritance, Mrs. Hawxby, residing in a country house, arranged for Beatrice to learn billiards from Arthur. Furthermore, she insisted on driving exclusively in his pony carriage, reasoning that both she and Beatrice shared a "literary" sensibility. She publicly declared that the young man, allegedly instigated by his "horrid old uncle," had behaved with infamous treachery toward Beatrice's affections. The reality, however, was that the elderly Major, possessing a keen understanding of Mrs. Hawxby's predatory nature and her habit of preying on unwary youths, had personally intervened. He had rescued Arthur from the immediate clutches of the lady at the country house, saving him from her claws, if not from the sharp sting of her tongue. While the elder Pendennis had wished for his nephew to spend Christmas at Clavering, Arthur lacked the fortitude. Clavering was simply too close to the desolate ruins of poor old Fairoaks, a place saturated with memories too sad to revisit.

Our view of the Claverings has waned until their sudden reappearance at the Epsom racecourse, necessitating a brief retrospective on their tumultuous year. The world, it seems, had not treated any member of the family with particular kindness. Lady Clavering, historically renowned for her excellent digestion and impeccable grammar, found her appetites and temperaments severely tested by relentless domestic strife. Her grievances were so profound that they rendered the finest French cuisine unpalatable and the softest sofa cushions intolerably hard. "I'd sooner eat a turnup for dessert," she lamented to her faithful companion, staring vacantly at a table laden with pineapples and Muscatel grapes, "than endure that pineapple. If only I could enjoy my meal in silence. How infinitely happier I was when I was a widow, before all this money descended upon me!"

The Clavering family had, in essence, made a disastrous start. They offered hospitality without guidance, position, or gratitude, receiving no return of kindness from those they entertained. Their initial London season was questionable, followed swiftly by a notorious failure. Gossip ran rampant: "Human patience was not sufficient to tolerate Sir Francis Clavering." He was deemed hopelessly low, dull, and disreputable. Whispers abounded regarding the Begum, a woman of immense wealth lacking the ability to pronounce 'h', and her origins remained a mystery. Her daughter was criticized for a bizarre blend of Gallic affectation and conceit, unfit for association with well-bred English girls. The circle surrounding them was filled with strangers. Sir Francis, a confirmed gambler, was seen in the company of blacklegs and profligates. Even Hely Clinker, serving in his regiment, was accused of cheating at cards and displaying the "white feather." When Lady Rockminster eventually abandoned Lady Clavering, the great ladies refused to bring their daughters to her parties. The young men attending these gatherings behaved with an odious freedom and scornful familiarity. Consequently, Lady Clavering was forced to admit she had opened the door to "the canal"—the lower sort of society—in her drawing-room, as the elite simply would not visit.

Yet, Lady Clavering harbored no malice toward "the canal," nor did she suffer from excessive pride. She had blindly followed the social dictates given to her by her godmother upon entering the world: to know whom they knew, and ask whom they asked. While "the canal" was often pleasanter than high society, leaving a mistress is easy, whereas being cast off by her is cruel. One can forsake society with a sense of relief, but if society casts you out, the resulting mortification is severe.

Among the fashionable youth, Harry Foker, Esquire, stood out as potentially faithful amidst the faithless. However, he had mishandled his affair with Priscilla Pen. What began as a secret passion became notorious, reaching the ears of his weak and doting mother, and ultimately landing him before the stern gaze of the bald, inflexible Mr. Foker Senior. When the father learned of this scandal, a violent confrontation ensued, culminating in the younger gentleman's banishment from England for a year. He was ordered to return and either marry his cousin or retire to private life with an allowance of three hundred a year, forever barred from seeing his parents or the brewery. Mr. Henry Foker departed, carrying a sorrow and anxiety that passes freely through customs and is proverbial among exiles. To him, even the Parisian Boulevards appeared gloomy, and the Italian skies turned black.

For Sir Francis Clavering, that same year proved calamitous. As our sporting acquaintances recall, Lord Harrowhill's horse, a classical nobleman named Podasokus, won the Derby, to the dismay of the wise who had backed the non-existent favorite, Borax. Sir Francis, intimately connected with the rascally characters of the turf and possessing "valuable information," had heavily wagered against the winner and freely supported the favorite. The result, as his son later admitted to the stricken Lady Clavering, was a staggering loss of seven thousand pounds.

This was a devastating blow to the lady, who had already discharged her husband's debts countless times. Having received numerous oaths of reform, paid his money-lenders and horse-dealers, and furnished both town and country residences, she was now summoned to meet this colossal sum, the penalty for her husband's cowardice and extravagance.

Earlier, the Major Pendennis had acted as an advisor to the Claverings, inspecting every room of their home, including the dreaded closet where, per the proverb, the family skeleton is locked away. Regarding the Baronet's finances, the Major admitted ignorance, not due to lack of inquiry, but because Sir Francis himself hid them in a hopeless labyrinth of lies, making it impossible for any attorney or adviser to discern the truth. However, concerning Lady Clavering, the Major was well-informed. Upon the misfortune of the Derby, he had meticulously investigated her means, learning of the vast sacrifices the widow Amory had made for her current husband.

He openly expressed, winning considerable favor from Miss Blanche in doing so, his belief that Lady Clavering's daughter had been unfairly treated financially by her second marriage. He gently suggested to the lady that Miss Blanche deserved a better provision. The Major had previously indicated to the widow that he was fully aware of her early, tragic history in India, specifically the circumstances leading to her separation from her first husband. He could direct her to the Calcutta newspaper detailing Amory's trial. Moreover, he demonstrated to the Begum his forbearance in keeping this knowledge silent, maintaining friendship with the family throughout.

"My dear Lady Clavering," he remarked, "interest may drive me, and surely we all act from interest. My motive, which I need not hide, is to arrange a marriage between my nephew and your daughter." To this, the surprised Lady Clavering readily agreed.

Frankly, however, the Major continued, "My dear lady, my boy earns merely five hundred a year. A wife with a fortune of ten thousand pounds would barely improve his situation. I am convinced we could do far better for him. He is a shrewd, cautious young man who has sown his wild oats; he possesses good parts and ample ambition. His objective in marrying is clearly self-improvement. If you and Sir Francis agree—and trust me, Sir Francis will not refuse anything—you could place Arthur in a position to advance considerably. Show the stuff within him! What use is that parliamentary seat to Clavering, who rarely appears in the House or utters a word? Gentlemen who heard him at Oxford assure me he is a famous orator. Give him a stirrup, and I guarantee he will not be

Our benevolent Begum, initially so incensed by yet another instance of her husband's duplicity and foolishness, flatly refused to assist Sir Francis Clavering in meeting his debts of honor. She declared her intention to separate from him, leaving him to face the dire consequences of his incorrigible weakness and squandering ways. Following that disastrous day at the Derby, the unfortunate gambler was in such a state of mental turmoil that he sought to avoid everyone. He fled from his turf associates, whom he feared he might not be able to pay, and especially from his wife, his long-suffering banker, whose credit he now doubted.

When Lady Clavering inquired the following morning if Sir Francis was home, she learned he had not returned. Instead, he had dispatched a messenger to his valet, instructing him to forward clothes and letters via the bearer. Strong, knowing that his patron would soon require assistance, secured a note begging him to visit his "distracted" friend F.C. at Short Hotel, Blackfriars, specifically asking for "Mr. Francis." The Baronet possessed a peculiar mental trait: he would rather utter a falsehood than admit defeat, and invariably began a contest with fortune by fleeing and hiding.

The Bootboy of Mr. Short's establishment, who delivered Clavering's message to Grosvenor Place and retrieved his carpet-bag, instantly recognized its owner. He whispered this intelligence to the footman setting the breakfast table, who relayed it to the servants' hall, where it reached Mrs. Bonner, the lady's housekeeper and confidante, who finally informed her mistress. Thus, every soul within the Grosvenor Place establishment knew that Sir Francis was in hiding under the alias of "Francis" at an inn in the Blackfriars Road. Furthermore, Sir Francis's coachman spread the news to other gentlemen's drivers, who shared it with their masters and the neighboring Tattersalls, where gloomy rumors circulated that Sir Francis Clavering was preparing for a lengthy tour in the Levant.

Throughout the day, the volume of correspondence addressed to Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., that accumulated on his hall-table was remarkable. The French cook submitted his account to the lady; tradesmen supplying her table, along with Messrs. Finer and Gimcrack, the mercers and ornamental dealers, and Madame Crinoline, the eminent milliner, all forwarded their bills, alongside Miss Amory's substantial private accounts from each establishment.

By the afternoon of the day after the Derby, when Strong visited Grosvenor Place to conduct business as usual, he found his principal's study littered with these suspicious documents. After a brief, tearful colloquy with the manager at Short's Hotel, whom he found sobbing and drinking Curacao, Strong opened the papers with a rueful countenance.

Just as he was examining them, Mrs. Bonner descended upon him. As a devoted part of the family, essential to her mistress as the Chevalier was to Sir Francis, she stood firmly on Lady Clavering's side, angering far more deeply than her ladyship herself due to her sense of duty.

"He won't pay if I advise it," Mrs. Bonner declared. "Go back to Sir Francis, Captain—and tell him we refuse to pay his debts anymore. He's skulking around a low public house and doesn't dare face his wife like a man! We raised him from gaol, paid his debts repeatedly, set him up in Parliament, gave him a town and country house. Yet here he is, a shabby sneak, where he dares not show his face! We've provided the horse he rides, the dinner he eats, and the clothes on his back. From now on, we give nothing more. Our remaining fortune belongs to ourselves; we shall not waste another penny on this ungrateful man. We'll provide enough to keep him alive and then let him go. Tell him that from Susan Bonner."

Hearing of Strong's arrival, Lady Clavering summoned him, hoping to find her factotum less rigid than Mrs. Bonner. Often before, the Chevalier had successfully softened his mistress's heart with pleas for his client. This time, however, he painted a dismal picture of Sir Francis's plight, fearing terrible consequences if his engagements could not be met.

"Let him kill himself," Mrs. Bonner laughed. "Dying would be the finest thing he could do." Strong swore he had found the Baronet with razors on the table, but Lady Clavering laughed bitterly at this. "He'll do himself no harm as long as there's a shilling left for him to rob a poor woman. His life is quite safe, Captain; you may depend on it. Ah! What a bad day it was when I first set eyes on him!"

"He's worse than a man," cried the lady's aide-de-camp. "He was a wild devil, yes, but he had the courage of a man. Whereas this fellow? What use is it for my lady to pay his bills, sell her diamonds, and forgive him? Next year, the moment he gets a chance, he'll be cheating her again, robbing her. Her money goes to keep a pack of rogues and swindlers—I don't mean you, Captain; you've been a good friend, barring the wish we'd never have met you."

The Chevalier noticed from Mrs. Bonner's slip of the tongue regarding the diamonds that the kind-hearted Begum was willing to relent, at least temporarily, offering hope for his principal.

"Upon my word, ma'am," he said, feigning deep sympathy and admiration for her untiring generosity—"any criticism of Clavering or complaint from Mrs. Bonner is deserved, and it was indeed an unlucky day for you when you encountered either. He has behaved cruelly. If you were not the most generous and forgiving woman in the world, there would be no hope for him. But you cannot let the father of your son be disgraced, sending little Frank into the world with such a stain. Bind him; tie him down with any promises you like. I vouch for him; he will sign them."

"And break 'em," retorted Mrs. Bonner.

"And keep 'em this time!" cried Strong. "If you could have seen how he wept, ma'am! 'Oh, Strong,' he said to me, 'it's not for myself I feel now: it's for my boy—it's for the best woman in England, whom I have treated basely—I know I have.' He didn't intend to bet on this race, ma'am—he didn't. He was cheated into it; the entire ring was taken. He thought he could gamble safely, without risk. And it will be a lifelong lesson to him. To see a man cry—oh, it's dreadful."

"He doesn't think much of making my dear missus cry," said Mrs. Bonner, pointing to the ceiling. "Poor dear soul! Look if he does, Captain."

"If you have the soul of a man, Clavering," Strong told his principal later, recounting the scene, "you will keep your promise this time. So help me Heaven, if you break word with her, I will turn against you and tell all."

"What all?" cried Mr. Francis, to whom his ambassador brought the news back at Short's Hotel, where Strong found the Baronet weeping and drowning his sorrows in Curacao.

"Pshaw! Do you suppose I am a fool?" burst out Strong. "Do you suppose I could live so long in this world, Frank Clavering, without keeping my eyes open? You know I have but to speak, and tomorrow you are a beggar. And I am not the only man who knows your secret."

"Who else does?" gasped Clavering.

"Old Pendennis does, or I am very much mistaken. He recognized the man the first night he saw him, when he came drunk into your house."

"He knows it, does he?" shrieked Clavering. "Damn him—kill him."

"You'd like to kill us all, wouldn't you, old boy?" said Strong, sneering and puffing his cigar.

The Baronet dashed his weak hand against his forehead; perhaps the other had interpreted his wish correctly. "Oh, Strong!" he cried, "if I dared, I'd put an end to myself, for I'm the damnedest miserable dog in all England. It's that misery that makes me so wild and reckless. It's that which drives me to drink"—and he drained a bumper of his fortifier with a trembling hand—"and to live about with these thieves. I know they're thieves, every one of 'em, damned thieves. And how can I help it? And I didn't know it, you know—and, by Gad, I'm innocent—and until I saw the damned scoundrel first, I knew no more about it than the dead—and I'll fly, and I'll go abroad out of reach of these confounded hells, and I'll bury myself in a forest, by Gad! and hang myself up to a tree—and, oh!I'm the most miserable beggar in all England!" With more tears, shrieks, and curses, the impotent wretch vented his grief, deplored his unhappy fate, and, amidst groans, despair, and blasphemy, vowed his miserable repentance.

The honored proverb that an ill wind blows no good to anyone proved true in the case of Sir Francis Clavering, as well as for another occupant of Mr. Strong's chambers in Shepherd's Inn. The man was "good," by

Not long after Colonel Altamont's stroke of luck at Epsom, he set sail for Europe aboard the *Soho*, bound for Antwerp. The gossip columns of London Bridge duly noted his departure alongside Sir Robert and Lady Hodge, the Kewsyes, Major Coddy, and others of the fashionable fraternity. Colonel Altamont traveled in high style, clad in a sumptuous traveling suit, immune to seasickness thanks to his steady consumption of brandy-and-water, and accompanied by his devoted Irish body-servant, a legionary who had served him faithfully since the days of their shared lodging at Shepherd's Inn.

Before boarding, the Chevalier treated his departing friend to a lavish dinner at Blackwall. Amidst the clinking glasses and toasts to Altamont's health, the Chevalier's companion leaned in with a conspiratorial grin. "Strong, old boy," he urged, "if you're in need of a trifle, now is your hour. I'm your man. You've always been a true friend, and a twenty-pound note wouldn't hurt me a whit."

Strong, however, waved the offer away with a nonchalant shrug. "No, I'm flush," he declared, puffing his chest out. "Quite flush—though not quite flush enough to repay your last loan, Altamont, but sufficient to keep me moving for a while." With a greeting that was cordial enough but lacked deep warmth, they parted ways. One might wonder if money truly had softened Altamont's edges, making him more honest and amiable, or if it simply made him appear so in Strong's eyes. Perhaps it was the sheer radiance of wealth that blinded Strong to the Chevalier's flaws. Inside himself, Strong muttered a defense of his friend: *"This poor devil, this unlucky outcast returned from the penal colonies, is ten times the gentleman that Sir Francis Clavering ever was. He possesses pluck and a strange sort of honesty. He sticks to friends and faces enemies. The other fellow lacks the courage to do either."*

Strong harbored a lingering guilt about the lie he'd told Altamont—that he wasn't desperate for cash. It was a "truthful lie," after all; Strong could manage, albeit precariously. Besides, Clavering had promised funds, and the Chevalier, being of an optimistic disposition, pinned his hopes on intercepting stray payments meant for his patron, where Mr. Strong's business skills would surely shine.

Life at Shepherd's Inn grew gloomy without Altamont. The solitude grated on Strong's social nature, and he found himself indulging in extravagant habits, employing a servant to polish his boots and prepare his meals. It became a somewhat tragicomic sight to watch the portly gentleman meticulously painting his own leather and broiling his own chops. The Chevalier, who had recently returned from Spain after a brief and turbulent union with a lady from Vittoria—who had famously ended their engagement by breaking his skull with a dishware item—began to ponder returning to Juanita. His spirits sank, a condition the Chevalier termed being "down on his luck." He reflected on how every hero, from Marius to Napoleon, faced periods of ill fortune.

Meanwhile, Sir Francis Clavering remained in dire straits. The twenty-five pounds ("the pony") he had extracted from Altamont had vanished as quickly as his previous loans. Downriver, he had joined a party of sportsmen who evaded the police to land in Essex, where they staged a boxing match between Billy Bluck and a cabman. The betting favored the cabman three to one, yet Clavering, confident in his backing, lost miserably. Worse still, his debt to a man named Lightfoot mounted to fourteen pounds, a sum compounded by Lightfoot's relentless demands. Clavering, cursing the situation, begged Strong for help, promising half of whatever he could squeeze from his wife, or anyone else.

"Ned," Strong responded sternly, "I gave my word of honor as a gentleman never to participate in any further bill transactions involving your husband. My wife and I have given ours, too." The Chevalier, true to his word, vowed he would polish his own boots for the rest of his days rather than break his promise. He further declared his intention to warn Lady Clavering immediately if Clavering showed any sign of betraying his marital vows.

True to form, Sir Francis erupted in a torrent of curses and tears, swearing that death was his only escape. As he descended the stairs of Shepherd's Inn, he transformed from a sobbing beggar into a furious invective, calling Strong the "infernal villain," "traitor," and "blackguard" under the sun, wishing him buried in hell, yet paradoxically wishing him alive to endure Clavering's future vengeance.

Inside Strong's chambers, however, the atmosphere shifted to something lighter. Enter Mr. Samuel Huxter, a young gentleman of Clavering's acquaintance. Huxter, who had once poached walnuts in Clavering Park and witnessed the Baronet's ostentatious display of four-horse carriages, approached Strong with immense respect and trepidation. He introduced himself as a "Clavering man," detailing his father's service to Sir Francis's keeper, Coxwood, whose gun accident had cost him three fingers. Sir Francis received this tribute with affability, and Huxter delighted in recounting tales of the Baronet's magnificence to the lads at Bartholomew's.

At the lodge, a young maid named Fanny listened with wide-eyed skepticism. "Sir Francis is an ojus creature," she declared, shaking her head. "He's wicked, low, and mean!" When Sam insisted that the Baronet was affable and had kindly borrowed half a sovereign from her, Fanny burst into laughter. She tugged at Sam's long, unwashed hair, patted his chin, and labeled him a "stoopid, old foolish stoopid," noting that the Baronet had been turned away twice by her mistress, Mar, only to return three months later for seven shillings.

"Don't say 'er' but 'her'," Sam corrected, pointing out grammatical errors.
"Well then, 'her,' and 'borrow,' and 'haftually'..." Fanny retorted, and the grammar lesson concluded with Sam's face twisted in a pretty grimace, leaving the grammar master appeased and eager for more tuition.

Mrs. Bolton, ever present, observed the scene with a mixture of amusement and resignation. Time, she felt, had brought Fanny certain consolations, softening the bitter regrets of her youth, reducing them to the level of a toothache—a temporary pang, but ultimately transient.

Strong sat reflecting on the ironies of life. Surrounded by respect and affection in his old age, he mused on the inevitable transfer of glory to the successor. The servants bowed to the son as they bowed to the father; the tenants shouted for the heir. Prosperity and flattery, like sunlight, followed the estate. A great man must keep his heir close, a living *memento mori*. If life held too much sway, the presence of the heir became a constant sting and reminder of mortality. "Make ready to go," the successor seemed to whisper, "I am waiting. And I could hold it as well as you."

Yet, amidst these reflections, Strong turned to the thoughts of Pen and Warrington. They often spoke of Pen's mother, a saint canonized in their memories. Lucky was the man who knew such a woman, a providential gift of innocence, love, and beauty. But just as they drifted into sentimentality, the public intruded, stepping in to halt their confidential chat and remind them of the gritty realities of the world they inhabited.

Arthur Pendennis, newly arrived with his inheritance, initially behaved with modesty and equanimity, earning Warrington's praise. However, his Uncle Major Pendennis disapproved of his nephew's lack of pomp. The Major wanted Arthur installed in handsome quarters, riding showy hacks in cabriolets. "I am too absent-minded," Arthur laughed, "to drive a cab in London; the omnibus would cut me in two. And you wouldn't have me driven about by a servant like an apothecary, uncle?"

"No, never!" the Major protested indignantly. "The representative of the House of Pendennis must not demean himself. You're like the fellow in Sterne—the Marquis demanding his sword back. Your father got it restored for you. You are a man of landed estate, by Gad, sir, and a gentleman—never forget you are a gentleman."

Arthur playfully turned the tables, asking who cared about his "paltry means" in the society he frequented. "It would be absurd to compete with the great folks; all they require is a decent address and good manners."

"But I should belong to a better club," the uncle argued, "give occasional dinners, and escape that horrid garret in the Temple." Compromised, Arthur moved to the second floor of Lamb Court, remaining close to Warrington, who still occupied his old quarters. The two friends resolved never to separate, sharing chambers and a servant, a bond forged in the generosity of youth.

Later, discussing the Gentry of Grosvenor Place, particularly Sir Francis and Lady Clavering, Warrington expressed his disdain. "The world is right about those people," George grumbled. "Young men laugh and talk freely before them. The girl sees people she has no right to know." He accused them of being contaminated by bad company.

"The Begum is the

The revelations concerning the Clavering household, gathered by Major Pendennis through the diligence of Mr. Strong and his own direct interventions, served as a heavy anchor for the old gentleman's ambitions. To betroth his nephew, Arthur, to a lady burdened by fathers-in-law of such dubious repute—the "guileless" and ill-starred Lady Clavering and her companion, the notorious convict Altamont—was a recipe for disaster. While these two fathers-in-law might theoretically cancel each other out, the practical reality was far less diplomatic. Any public sighting of Altamont would trigger his immediate flight and subsequent execution; a rope was his inevitable fate under British law. Yet, no guardian wished to secure a wife whose lineage required the disposal of a parent via hanging. Furthermore, the Major's strategy relied on the threat of exposure to keep Sir Francis Clavering in check, turning the Baronet into a willing slave to anyone who held the fatal secret of Altamont's return.

However, the financial calculus presented a grim alternative. If the Begum, driven by love, liquidated her entire fortune to bail out this "irreclaimable reprobate," her heirs would inherit an empty treasury. Worse still, Miss Amory would arrive at her husband's side not with a dowry or a parliamentary seat, but with her person alone and a pedigree tarnished by the infamous legal abbreviation *sus*.

Yet, amidst these dire prospects, the Major's scheming mind identified a final avenue. This opportunity lay within a conversation that was about to unfold between the Major and the Honourable Baronet, the Member for Clavering.

When a man finds himself drowning in pecuniary difficulties, he vanishes from the flock of his former peers, diving into strange, distant nooks to catch his breath. One knows a gentleman of fashion who, vanished from the clubs and parks, resurfaced happy and affable at a shabby tavern in Billingsgate; another, a man of learning, sent his address from a cellar in the Strand when outrunning a constable. Under such misfortunes, a man may have a house, but he is rarely in it; he possesses an address for letters, but only fools expect to find him there. Only the faithful possess the key to his hiding place.

Thus, despite the disputes with his wife and the resulting chaos, finding Sir Francis Clavering at home proved impossible. "Since I last fetched him for my book—which cost fourteen pounds—he doesn't return until three o'clock," Mr. Lightfoot remarked to his friend Morgan. "Then he pretends to sleep when I bring his water in the morning and slips out when I'm downstairs." Announcing his intention to become Butler to My Lady and marry his old woman, Lightfoot described the Baronet's current state. Similarly, following his altercations with Strong, Clavering had fled to other haunts, escaping the Chevalier's reproaches and, perhaps, the reach of his own conscience.

Though the elder Pendennis, bent on seeing his country neighbour and parliamentary representative, was determined to engage the shy Baronet, it required considerable effort. Since being called in as a family friend, Clavering had treated the Major like a plague, avoiding him whenever accounts needed rendering or business discussed—a behavior mirrored by all lawyers and agents when a bill was due. The Major's attempts to lure the bird failed repeatedly: an innocent dinner invitation at Greenwich resulted in the Baronet suspecting a trap and leaving the Major to eat his whitebait alone; a request for ten minutes' talk at Bays's was acknowledged with precise punctuality ("four o'clock... precisely"), yet Clavering never materialized. The Major realized that seeking a man was easy, but finding him was another matter entirely.

Just before the close of the day, a critical transaction occurred. Sir Francis Clavering, having sworn to his wife's advisers to accept no further bills and to subsist solely on her allowance, had nonetheless signed his name to a piece of stamped paper. Carried off by his friend, Mr. Moss Abrams, it promised to be "done" by a party intimately connected with Abrams. Meanwhile, Mr. Santiago's cigar-shop, located near St. James's and Bury Streets, served as a hive of activity. Strong, constantly frequenting the back-parlour, heard of this transaction.

"He is at his old work again," Mr. Santiago informed a customer. "He and Moss Abrams were in my parlour. Moss sent my boy for a stamp. It must have been a bill for fifty pounds. The Baronet told Moss to date it two months back. He will claim it is an old bill, forgotten during his recent settlement with his wife. I dare say they will grant him more funds now that he is 'clear.'" A man who habitually signs "promises to pay" at six months enjoys the satisfaction of knowing his affairs are scrutinized and his signature circulated among London's worst knaves.

As the sun set, Major Pendennis walked daintily toward his lodgings in Bury Street, only to encounter Mr. Strong, the Chevalier, striding along the same pavement, burning with wrath and smelling faintly of Havana tobacco.

"Confound these young men," the Major thought, wrinkling his nose. "How they poison everything with smoke. Here comes a fellow with mustachios and a cigar. Every fellow who smokes and wears mustachios is a low fellow." Realizing who it was, he offered a dignified bow. "I hope you are well, Mr. Strong?"

Strong stopped, extending his hand. Old Pendennis offered a couple of ungracious fingers, maintaining a patronising air. "What is your good news?" he asked, condescending to address the other as if he were merely a common acquaintance. "Still in town, Mr. Strong? I hope I see you well."

"My news is bad news, sir," Strong replied gravely. "It concerns our friends at Tunbridge Wells, and I should like to talk to you about it. Clavering is at his old tricks again, Major Pendennis."

"Indeed!" The Major's interest awakened immediately. "Pray do me the favour to come into my lodging."

Inside the drawing-room, Strong unburdened himself of his indignation. "No promises will bind him, sir," Strong declared. "Remember when we met with my lady's lawyer? He wouldn't be satisfied with giving his honour; he wanted to take his oath on his knees, ring for a Bible, and swear perdition on his soul if he ever gave another bill. Yet, he signed one today, sir. He will sign as many more as you please for ready money. He will deceive anybody—his wife, his child, or his old friend who has backed him a hundred times. Why, there's a bill of his and mine due next week."

"I thought we had paid all."

"Not that one," Strong admitted, blushing slightly. "He asked me not to mention it, and—and I had half the money for that, Major. And they will be down on me. But I don't care; I'm used to it. It's Lady Clavering that riles me. It's a shame that that good-natured woman, who has paid him out of gaol a score of times, should be ruined by his heartlessness. A parcel of bill-stealing boxers and rascals get his money, and he don't scruple to throw an honest fellow over. Would you believe it, sir, he took money of Altamont—you know whom I mean."

"Indeed?" The Major feigned an impenetrable countenance. "Of that singular man, who I think came tipsy once to Sir Francis's house? Who is Altamont, Mr. Strong?"
