"I am sure I don't know, if you don't know," Strong answered, his expression shifting to one of surprise and suspicion.

"To tell you frankly," the Major said, adopting a tone of grave wisdom, "I have my suspicions—I suppose, mind, I only suppose—that in our friend Clavering's life, who, between you and me, Captain Strong, is about as loose a fish as any in my acquaintance, there are queer secrets and stories he would not like known. Very likely this fellow, Altamont, knows some story against Clavering and holds him to ransom. I know some of the best men in England paying through the nose in that way. But their private affairs are no business of mine, Mr. Strong. And it is not to be supposed that because I dine with a man, I pry into his secrets or am answerable for his past life. As for Clavering, I am most interested for his wife's sake, and her daughter, who is a most charming creature. When her ladyship asked me, I looked into her affairs and tried to set them straight. And if I am called upon—and, by the way, this Mr. Altamont, Mr. Strong? How is he? Is he in town?"

"I don't know that I am called upon to know where he is, Major Pendennis," Strong said, rising and taking up his hat in dudgeon. The Major's patronising caution and impertinent inquiries had offended the honest gentleman.

Pendennis's manner shifted instantly from hauteur to knowing good-humour. "Ah, Captain Strong, you are cautious too, I see; and quite right, my good sir. We don't know what ears walls may have, or to whom we may be talking. As a man of the world, and an old soldier—an old and distinguished soldier, I have been told—you know very well there is no use throwing away your fire. You may

Lady Clavering, having finally escaped the social minefields of London, retreated to a charming villa nestled in the picturesque commons near Tunbridge Wells. In tow came Miss Amory, naturally, and Master Clavering, fresh from his holidays. For Blanche, the return home was a mixed bag: her favorite pastime remained the art of fighting and quarreling with her younger brother, though he, alas, possessed zero interest in domestic squabbles. Instead, he threw himself into cricket, horseback riding, and the company of a dozen young gentlemen aged thirteen who lived at the Begum's estate. These boys were a raucous crew, consuming tarts and champagne with alarming gusto, racing across the lawns, and generally frightening the good-natured Begum until she coughed violently enough to make herself ill. The result was a dining room so unbearable for Blanche that she fled it whenever possible.

Yet, for the fair Miss Amory, "change" itself was a delightful drug. For a fortnight, she would have traded silk dresses for bread-and-cheese, and perhaps even a dungeon for a night. The move to Tunbridge was, therefore, a welcome respite. She wandered the woods sketching trees and cottages, devoured French novels, and drove into town to catch any conjurer, musician, or ball that crossed her path. Her mornings were a battlefield of quarrels with Mamma and Frank, followed by a chaotic stint at the local village school where she alternately cooed over the girls, defied the mistress, and then scolded the students while laughing at the teacher.

She also became a fixture at the little ancient church, a tiny Anglo-Norman jewel adorned with painted windows and gilded scriptures. Eager to prove her piety, Blanche designed a strictly correct high-church altar cover. To the local clergyman, she appeared suddenly transformed into a saint. She charmed him with such artful coaxing that poor Mrs. Smirke, the vicar's wife, descended into madness. Mrs. Smirke, whose husband had recently cut off his collar and grown his hair out to pursue a higher calling, watched Blanche with jealous fury. The vicar, a harmless soul who had gone without dinner on Fridays and read the Roman Hours, was now accused by the dissenters and the orthodox alike of being a dangerous Jesuit plotting with the Pope. Yet, despite the rumors of correspondence with Rome and the display of candlesticks upon his makeshift altar, he meant no earthly harm.

Belinda, the vicar's wife, spent her time pacing the grass near two small graves, wishing desperately for a third spot so she could bury her hopes alongside them. She feared that if Blanche stayed another moment, her husband would offer to that "creature" who had infatuated him in a fortnight. "I shall retreat!" Belinda declared, vowing to enter a convent and leave him to his errors. Her neighbors echoed these fears, whispering that he was bewitched by errors far more odious than any he had previously confessed. Still, the vicar thought nothing of it; he saw only Blanche's piety and grace. His music, however, soon grew stale to him. What began as a fascination turned to doubt, then irritation, when she lost her temper and began tapping the fingers of his schoolchildren. Blanche, it seemed, inspired a cycle of admiration and satiety in many a man; she threw her charms wide, dazzling them with jewels and smiles, only to drop them as quickly as they grew tired of her.

It was a joyous evening for Belinda when Blanche departed. The vicar, blushing and sighing, admitted to her that he had been deceived, fearing that Blanche's gifts were merely "tinsel" and her piety just amusement. "There is none like thee, my Belinda," he murmured, lifting his fine eyes to the ceiling, "precious among women!" And Blanche? From the moment she lost sight of him, she ceased to care for either him or his wife.

However, the dynamic shifted entirely when Arthur Pendennis arrived at Tunbridge Wells to spend a few days with the Begum. At this stage, neither Blanche nor the simple clergyman had reached the point of indifference. Smirke still regarded Blanche as an angelic wonder, sitting in silent, rapt adoration as she played the piano in the summer evenings, forgetting tea or bread-and-butter in her ecstasy. He told Arthur stories of her, describing her as a being of rare talent and a heavenly disposition.

The reunion between Arthur and the curate was warm and cordial. Arthur, who loved anyone who loved his mother, listened with amusement to Smirke's affected airs, his long hair, and his blank stock. He found the vicar's devotion both ridiculous and touching. Blanche, equally glad to see Arthur, welcomed him with the zest of a country lass hearing news from the big city. She laughed for hours after dinner, sang her songs with renewed spirit, and even forgave her mother, kissing her with surprising affection. Before bed, she sighed, "Deja!" with a regretful air, squeezing Arthur's hand fondly before retreating.

Later that night, Pen stood at his window, watching the moonlight bathe the little Saxon church. "She has improved immensely," he mused, lighting a cigar despite the open window. "That stupid young cub of a boy ought not to be allowed to get tipsy, but the old woman is jolly, and Blanche is... well, she's devilishly nice." Just then, a voice drifted from a nearby clematis-covered casement—"Oh, heavenly! The moon! But I must close, *les moeurs* forbid!" It was the voice of the author of 'Mes Larmes.' Pen laughed, exchanging pleasantries before singing "Goodnight to Don Basilio."

The following day, they strolled through the fields, a pair of gay friends reminiscing about Laura and the golden days of their youth. Blanche spoke sentimentally of Laura's voice and wondered if she would join them. Then came the walk to Penshurst, followed by the conversation with the curate that lingered in Arthur's mind.

*"Is she truly all this perfection?"* Arthur asked himself, walking beside the vicar. *"Does she teach the poor? Is she kind? Yes, I saw it."* Later, alone with Blanche, he questioned her about the country life.

"I would never see that odious city again," Blanche declared, her voice full of longing. "My good thoughts grow here in these sweet woods like flowers that refuse to bloom in London. The gardener changes our balconies weekly, but I cannot bear the brazen, smoky face of London anymore."

"Why do you sigh, Blanche?" Arthur pressed gently.

"Never mind why."

"Yes, I do mind why. Tell me everything."

"I wish you hadn't come down," she replied, a second edition of *'Mes Soupirs'* suddenly manifesting. "I don't want you to go away. This house won't be happy without you, and that is precisely why I wish you had never arrived."

*Mes Soupirs* were laid aside, replaced by *'Mes Larmes.'*

What answer does a young woman give to the gaze of a lover? What method dries tears? What transpired between these two battered London rakes, momentarily forgetting their world and imagining themselves as Phillis and Corydon? Ah, what a fine night! How jolly a cigar at midnight! And how beautiful that little church looked under the moon. Perhaps, indeed, when one contemplates country houses and country walks, one begins to understand why so many men remain unmarried.

It seemed a peculiar irony of friendship that, despite Pendennis's usual frankness with Warrington, Arthur withheld the specific details of his recent escapades at the villa near Tunbridge Wells. While he freely discussed the rediscovery of his tutor Smirke, the eccentricities of his wife, and the absurdity of his Anglo-Norman church, the subject of Blanche remained a carefully guarded silence. When pressed, Arthur offered vague, diplomatic generalizations. He called her "a good-natured, clever little thing" who, *if* properly guided, might not ultimately prove a disastrous match, but he firmly declared his days of romance were over and that he intended no immediate marriage. To Warrington, however, the evidence spoke otherwise.

Indeed, pretty little satin envelopes arrived at Lamb Court, Temple Street, bearing the neatest handwriting and sealed with an admirable cipher. If Warrington had bothered to scrutinize the script—or if the code were ever broken—he would have discovered that Mr. Arthur corresponded with a young lady whose initials were B.A. These missives were met with Pen's finest gallantry: a blend of witty town gossip, playful jests, and, most dangerously, original verse. It was highly probable that the love-poems which later adorned the pages of *Roseleaves*, the annual edited by Lady Violet Lebas, were first conceived as private couriers to Blanche, serving as musical prefaces to Pinkney's pictorial garlands.

"Don't let the poetry go to your head," warned the elder Pendennis, watching his nephew scratch out another artless effusion at the club. "Letters are fine if Mamma permits them, and if they're to old country friends, but mind you, Pen, don't commit yourself. Who knows what the 'doose' may happen?"

Old Pendennis, growing garrulous with age, launched into a lecture on the perils of indiscretion. He recounted how young Spoony had been forced into a marriage with the Widow Naylor merely because his poetical notes had been too ardent, leading to a confrontation with the widow's brother, Colonel Flint. He detailed how Hopwood, of the Blues, had been compelled to withdraw from Louisa Salter after her letters surfaced, resulting in his union with the unsuitable Miss Stickney of Lyme Regis. "I've read the Great Book of the World, and it teaches caution," the Major proclaimed.

Pen listened, blushing slightly—not necessarily from shame, but from a sense of prudent instinct. He recalled the lessons of Lady Mirabel's school and promised to be circumspect. "I'm not to be caught again, Uncle," he said with mock humility. Old Pendennis beamed, pleased to see his nephew maturing into a sensible man of the world. Yet, if Warrington had been consulted, he would likely have argued that the boy's foolish, open-hearted letters were superior to the slick, slippery compliments of a hardened adult. To truly win a woman, one often needs the cover of subterfuge and a secure retreat, but Pen, bound by a certain youthful honesty, chose a different path.

Meanwhile, fortunes elsewhere were shifting violently. Colonel Altamont had returned from his foreign tour, and Sir Francis Clavering had retired to the country, leaving the sole partner of Shepherd's Inn firm, Captain Strong, to the "ills of fate." Strong, who had previously refused a generous loan from Altamont due to a misplaced sense of conscience, now faced a sudden deluge of creditors. Tradesmen, once awestruck by Strong's jolly demeanor, now besieged his chambers with bills and demands. The doors of Shepherd's Inn became a fortress, battered by tailors, bootmakers, and the silent, deadly threats of lawyers' clerks carrying writs.

Trapped within his castle, Strong relied on a network of spies and allies. Huxter and Miss Fanny Bolton acted as his messengers, using coded whistles and jodels to signal the arrival of provisions and comfort. But when the siege tightened, Strong employed a more dramatic tactic. Having previously demonstrated to Altamont how to climb a leaden water-pipe from his kitchen window to the room above, he utilized this secret passage to escape the "enemy lines." One afternoon, he burst into the neighboring apartments of Messrs. Costigan and Bows, bursting through their windows with a jolly grin, claiming the streets were mined with debt collectors. Thus, he slipped past the sentries, dined at the Albion, attended the play, and returned home at midnight, baffling Mrs. Bolton and Fanny.

For weeks, Strong held out with admirable spirit, sustained by the visits of his friends and the camaraderie of the "all gentlemen"—Costigan, Bows, and eventually, the returning Altamont. When the Colonel finally arrived from Baden-Baden, having won a "lucky month" at the gaming tables, the mood shifted from desperate survival to raucous celebration. They toasted the recovery of the Chevalier, discussing Altamont's "infallible system" for winning at cards—a method he claimed would restore fortunes if backed by sufficient capital.

"The devil take me if I don't stand by you," Strong enthused, pressing Altamont's foot under the table. "But what do you think of little Fanny? She lent two pounds to me!" Altamont proposed a grand scheme: he would return to Germany to gamble, take Strong along as his aide-de-camp, and use Strong's steadiness to execute his system. He even hinted at a romantic future for Fanny, proposing to marry her and thereby secure Strong's loyalty forever.

Later, at a dinner in Richmond, Pen recounted this wild saga to his uncle, describing Altamont as a tipsy, lawless rogue who didn't care for the name of Pendennis. The Major, ever the pragmatist, advised Arthur to avoid such characters, suggesting that Altamont and his associates operated under some dark, mysterious influence over men like Clavering. "Keep yourself straight, my boy," the Major counseled, warning that once a man gave someone power over him, it was hard to shake free. Pen, however, dismissed the warnings with a laugh, noting that Altamont's lack of scruples suited his own rebellious streak.

Back at Tunbridge Wells, Pen sought to entertain Miss Blanche with the thrilling tale of the Chevalier's imprisonment and Altamont's heroic rescue. He dwelt particularly on the generosity of little Fanny Bolton, praising her selflessness. However, the narrative carried an unintended sting. Blanche, naturally jealous and curious about Fanny, heard the story with a mixture of admiration and suspicion. Pen, seeking to draw a moral, suggested that women recovered from first loves as easily as men, using Fanny's sacrifice to Huxter—a man lacking in looks, manners, or wit—as proof.

"You wicked, odious creature!" Blanche exclaimed, her cheeks flushing. "I believe you are actually jealous of Mr. Huxter!"

Perhaps she was right. A blush betrayed Pen's hidden irritation at being supplanted by such a "rival." Yet, beneath the sarcasm lay a genuine regret. He lamented the cynical ending of Fanny's story, wishing that life followed the fairy-tale logic where heroes and heroines became perfectly happy and virtuous instantly. "Is a life a compromise?" he mused. "Do we have to settle for a husband who smells of tobacco and gallypots?"

"And don't you intend to be good and happy, pray, Monsieur le Misanthrope?" Blanche teased, challenging his cynicism. "Will your marriage be a compromise? Are you discontented with your lot?"

Pen, stung by the accusation, admitted he possessed little of what people called "heart." He confessed that his venture into love at eighteen had yielded a "vulgar dancing-woman," and that failure before marriage was preferable to failure after. "I don't profess to have heart," he declared, trying to maintain his satirical mask. "I'll buy you a hatful of bonbons from Fortnum and Mason's, and I'll sing you silly songs. I won't flatter you, Blanche. I'll tell you the truth: you are not peerless, you are not a goddess, and I am not a prince. But I think you are good-looking, clever, and good-natured."

"But if I bring you a sum of money and a seat in Parliament..." Blanche countered, playing the role of the ambitious suitor. "...will you condescend to throw your royal pocket-handkerchief at me?"

"If thieves are about the house," Pen retorted, adopting a grim persona, "my Morgiana will dance around me with a tambourine and kill them all." But his expression softened, revealing that he doubted his ability to protect her from the real dangers of the world.

"Ah, Blanche," he said gently, sensing her distress. "Don't be angry at my truth-telling. I always take you at your word. If you say you need money and position, I will seek them. Why add useless deception to the necessary ones of life? If I offer myself because I think we might be happy together, why force me to wear a Prince Prettyman's dress from a masquerade warehouse?"

"Merci du choix, Monsieur," the Sylphide curtsied, looking pitiful.

"I can buy bonbons," Pen assured her, trying to soothe her. "And it shall have its sugar-plums."

"Nay, my dear, dry those eyes," Pen coaxed, offering the standard consolations required by the situation. "I

In Which Pen Begins His Canvass

If Clavering Park had ever known a gloom darker than the days preceding Pen's marriage, it was not nearly so cheerless now as it became under the tenure of Sir Francis Clavering. The vast majority of the mansion stood locked and silent, a monument to a bankrupt proprietor currently exiled to foreign shores. Inside, the Baronet was confined to a few parlors on the ground floor, where his housekeeper and her assistant from the lodge waited upon him with the dutiful servitude of forced exile. Here, amidst the dust of unoccupied halls, he spent his dreary mornings shooting at game, which they then boiled into a meal fit for a refugee.

Lightfoot, his former man, had wisely crossed over to serve "My Lady," and as Pen learned via a letter from Mr. Smirke—who had officiated at the ceremony—the young fellow had successfully executed his prudent design of marrying Mrs. Bonner, My Lady's woman. This union was a triumph of nature; Mrs. Bonner, though of mature years, possessed a charm that struck the youth with irresistible force, and she endowed him with both her savings and her substantial frame. To become joint landlords of the Clavering Arms was the shared ambition of the pair, and it was settled that they would remain in service until quarter-day, at which point they would take possession of their hotel. Pen graciously pledged to hold his election dinner there once the Baronet vacated his seat, and indeed, as arranged by his Uncle, Arthur descended upon Clavering Park in September. The owner, desperate for companionship to alleviate his solitude—and perhaps hoping to borrow some ready cash—was delighted to have a visitor.

Pen, true to his word, furnished his host with a couple of pounds within days of Arthur's arrival. The moment these funds landed in Sir Francis's pocket, however, the Baronet found urgent business waiting for him at Chatteris and various neighboring watering-places. He promptly departed to attend to affairs that were likely transacted at county racecourses and billiard rooms. Arthur, possessing ample mental resources, managed quite well alone. He walked with the gamekeeper in the mornings and consumed books and cigars by the fire in the evenings, proving to be a literary genius who needed little else to pass the night. Yet, within two or three days, the society of Sir Francis Clavering became utterly intolerable to Arthur. With a mischievous eagerness, he offered the Baronet the little pecuniary aid the latter customarily solicited, thereby supplying him with the perfect excuse to flee his own house.

Moreover, Pen found himself tasked with ingratiating himself with the townsfolk of Clavering and the voters of the borough he hoped to represent. Remembering how unpopular he had previously been among the simple folk, he resolved to vanquish the odium he had inspired. A natural humorist, Pen relished this task. Transforming overnight from a reserved, somewhat sullen dandy into a figure of boisterous joviality, he became as frank and easy as Captain Strong. He laughed with everyone willing to share a chuckle, shook hands with dexterous cordiality, and made appearances at market-days and farmers' ordinaries. He acted as a consummate hypocrite, precisely the sort of high-born, spotless gentleman who feigns warmth to win over constituents for personal gain. How easily do we allow ourselves to be fooled? We know it is false coin, flattery distributed at no cost, yet we prefer it to silence. Friend Pen moved through Clavering laboriously simple and adroitly pleased, a stark contrast to the scornful young man the villagers recalled from ten years prior.

Meanwhile, the Rectory stood shuttered. Dr. Portman had fled to Harrogate with his gout and family—an event Pen deplored deeply in a letter, expressing regret for missing his old friend whose advice he might need. Consoled by this absence, Pen turned his attention to Mr. Simcoe, the opposition preacher, the partners of the local cloth factory, and the Independent minister, all of whom he encountered at the Clavering Athenaeum. Established by the Liberal party as a beacon of modern spirit, this new institution stood in proud opposition to the aristocratic old reading-room, where even the *Edinburgh Review* had struggled for entry and tradesmen were strictly forbidden. Pen propitiated the younger cloth-partner by inviting him to dine at the Park, complimented the Honorable Mrs. Simcoe with game from the estate, and requested permission to read her husband's latest sermon. When he fell slightly unwell one day, the rascal Simcoe seized the opportunity to display his tongue to Mr. Huxter, who duly sent medicines and called the following morning. Old Pendennis would have been thrilled by his pupil's antics, and Pen himself found the sport delightful, inspiring a wicked good-humor.

Yet, as Pen walked out of Clavering at night, fresh from presiding over an Athenaeum meeting or entertaining Mrs. Simcoe—who, along with her husband, was awestruck by the young Londoner's reputation—he crossed the familiar bridge over the rushing Brawl. Hearing the well-known roar of the waters beneath and glimpsing his cottage of Fairoaks silhouetted against the starlit sky, different thoughts stirred within him, awakening pangs of grief and shame. A faint light still burned in the window of the room he remembered so well, where the Saint who loved him had spent countless hours in care, yearning, and prayer. He turned his gaze away from the wan, reproachful glow, feeling as though his mother's spirit watched and warned from beyond. The night air was keen, the stars bright, and the waters flowed ceaselessly. The old trees whispered, waving their dark branches over the cottage roof. Beyond lay the terrace where, as a boy, he had wandered in summer evenings, ardent and trustful, unspotted and ignorant of passion or doubt, sheltered in the pure bosom of love. Suddenly, the town clock tolled midnight with a clang, shattering his reverie and sending him onward toward his lodging through the lodge and the dark lime avenues.

By the next afternoon, the cottage smiled in the sunset. The bedroom windows were open, where a light had burned the night before. Pen's tenant, Captain Stokes of the Bombay Artillery—whose mother, old Mrs. Stokes, resided nearby—received his landlord with great cordiality. Stokes showed Pen over the grounds and a new pond he had constructed in the back garden using materials from the stables. They spoke confidentially of repairs to the roof and chimneys, and Stokes begged Pen to name a date for visiting them both. Pen, having been in the country for a fortnight, excused his delay by frankly admitting he lacked the heart to call sooner. "I understand you, sir," Stokes said. Mrs. Stokes, who had slipped away at the ring of the bell—a gesture that seemed oddly formal to Pen—descended in her best gown, surrounded by her children. The youngsters clambered over Stokes; the boy jumped into an armchair that had belonged to Pen's father. Arthur recalled days when he would have thought sitting in that chair as absurd as mounting the King's throne. He asked if Miss Stokes, who was the image of her mother, could play the piano. She did, playing notes on an instrument enfeebled by age. Yet Pen did not listen to the music; he listened to Laura singing as in their youth, and saw his mother bending, beating time over the girl's shoulder.

The dinner at Fairoaks, hosted in Pen's honor by his tenant, proved initially stupid and melancholy for him. However, the mood shifted when the waiter from Clavering—a street boy Pen remembered, now a barber there—dropped a plate over Pen's shoulder. Mr. Hobnell, who also employed the lad, chuckled, "I suppose, Hodson, your hands are slippery with bear's-grease. He's always dropping the crockery about, that Hodson is—haw, haw!" Hodson blushed, looking disconcerted, and Pen burst into laughter. Good humor and hilarity became the order of the evening. For the second course, a hare and partridges were served, and when Pen later told the Vicar of Tinckleton, "I think, Mr. Stooks, you should have asked Hodson to cut the hare," the joke was instantly caught. Minutes later, Captains Stokes and Glanders joined in, followed by Mr. Hobnell, who arrived late but with an immense guffaw.

While Pen engaged in these country schemes, the lady of his choice, if not his deepest affection, came up to London from Tunbridge Wells on shopping expeditions. Accompanied by old Mrs. Bonner, her mother's maid—who had lived and quarreled with Blanche since infancy but now, preparing to quit Lady Clavering's service for the hymeneal state, wished to bestow tokens of kindness before departing to become the wife of Lightfoot and landlady of the Clavering Arms—the pair ventured into the city.

The honest woman utilized Miss Amory's taste to make her purchases, requesting that Blanche choose something she liked, serving as a reminder of a nurse who had attended her through wakeful nights, teething, and childhood fevers, loving her almost as a child of her own. An immense Bible was purchased for Blanche, prompting the suggestion that Mrs. Bonner buy a large Johnson's Dictionary for her mother, a gift both women could surely profit from. Mrs. Bonner then invested in linens for the future Clavering Arms and bought a red-and-yellow neck-handkerchief, which Blanche immediately

While Pen was busy weaving his selfish parliamentary schemes within his own county, news arrived that Lady Rockminster had returned to Baymouth, bringing with her the object of his deepest dread and fascination: Laura.

At the announcement that his sister was near, Pen felt a sudden, sharp pang of guilt. His ambition soared higher than any other desire—he wished to stand supreme in her esteem, to be the worthy patron and protector she had inherited from their mother. Yet, how could he possibly brave the news he carried? How would he explain his meditated plans to that spotless judge? He imagined neither he nor Blanche could withstand Laura's dazzling, calm scrutiny. To reveal his worldly hopes and ambitions to her seemed like confessing a crime to an angel.

Driven by a mix of panic and hypocrisy, Pen wrote a letter filled with flowery phrases, grand protests of affection, and easy, biting satire. Yet, beneath the ink, he felt like a rogue caught in the act, terrified of his own duplicacy.

"How could a simple country girl inspire such trembling in an accomplished gentleman?" he wondered, shaking his head as he rode from Clavering to Baymouth. His worldly tactics, his diplomatic wit, and his cynical knowledge of human nature crumbled before her purity. He realized with a sinking heart that his affairs were in such a precarious position that he could not tell the truth to that honest soul. Riding along, he felt as guilty as a schoolboy forgetting his lesson before facing the dreaded master. Was truth not the ultimate authority? Did she not hold the book of judgment?

Meanwhile, Laura had blossomed under the charge of her "wayward" and absolute patroness, Lady Rockminster. Though she had seen enough of the world to gather some accomplishments, her spirit remained unbroken. While many girls, coddled from infancy, would have withered in the great careless world, Laura adapted with a trustful smile. She possessed a peculiar virtue—a "sparkling Kohinoor" in her bosom. It was not merely wealth, but a talisman of innocence and cheerful confidence. Like Cogia Hassan's famous stone, it fetched a price and lit up the darkness of life. Before a year had passed, she had won the hearts of everyone in Lady Rockminster's household, from the ancient, irritable dowager to the grumbling Swiss major-domo. She had softened the harsh mistress, charmed the kitchen staff, and even converted the stern footmen, Calverley and Coldstream, into enthusiastic supporters of her convalescence. She achieved this not through art or flattery, but simply by the force of her good nature and her capacity to please.

When Pen finally arrived, he expected the worst. He braced himself for Laura to treat him as a clumsy, ambitious outsider. Instead, she rushed down the stairs, embracing him so warmly that Pen nearly forgot the presence of the footmen. She kissed him, her cheeks flushed with health and beauty, her eyes beaming with a frank kindness that smote him deeply.

"My goodness, how kind you are to me!" Pen stammered, holding her hand. "I don't deserve it!"

"Mamma left you to me," Laura replied, brushing his forehead. "We made a pact, Arthur, last year. You were to come to me when troubled or happy. Are you happy now, or in trouble?"

Her arch, kind glance pierced him. "Do you know about the Parliament plan?" Pen asked.

"Everyone knows!" she exclaimed, her spirits soaring. "The doctor discussed it today. The *Chatteris* paper will print it tomorrow. Sir Francis Clavering retires, and Mr. Arthur Pendennis steps in! And, oh, Arthur, you mentioned Miss Blanche Amory? Is it true? 'Tout se sait,' as they say!"

"What! That too?" Pen gasped.

"Yes, dear Arthur. I've heard the whole tale. I received a letter from Blanche; she speaks so warmly of you! I hope... I know she feels what she writes. When is it to be, Arthur? Why didn't you tell me? May I not come to live with you?"

"My home is yours, dear Laura," Pen said, trying to sound firm. "Everything I have is yours. I didn't tell you because... nothing is decided. No words have passed between us. But you think Blanche could be happy with me?"

"Not a romantic fondness, I know," Pen hurried on, seeking to dismiss the thought. "Only a sober attachment. I need my wife on one side of the fire and my sister on the other. Parliament in the session, Fairoaks in the holidays, and my Laura never to leave me until someone with a right comes to take her away."

"Somebody with a right!" Pen muttered the phrase, feeling a strange, hot jealousy rise in his chest. Why did he feel angry at the invisible entity with the legal right to remove Laura? He was hurt that she received his news so lightly, as if his happiness were already guaranteed.

"I'll stay home and be Aunt Laura," Laura laughed, performing a perfect Parisian curtsey. "I'm an excellent housekeeper. I've learned to sing and dance with the money you sent me. When you become a Minister of State, Blanche shall present me!"

Just then, Lady Rockminster entered, extending a finger for Pen to shake. He bowed clumsily.

"So you are going to marry, sir?" the old lady declared. "And go into Parliament to replace that good-for-nothing Sir Francis. I wanted him to give my grandson his seat! I hope you're getting a great deal of money with Miss Amory. I wouldn't take her without a fortune."

"Sir Francis is tired of Parliament," Pen winced. "And I wish to try that career. The rest is premature."

"I wonder," Lady Rockminster sniffed, "when you had Laura at home, you could take up with such an affected little creature as that."

"I am very sorry Miss Amory does not please your ladyship," Pen smiled weakly.

"You mean it's no affair of yours! Well, I'm not marrying her, and I'm very glad. A little odious thing! When I think a man could prefer her to my Laura..." The old lady rambled on about the selfishness of modern youth, comparing the current generation of suitors—who wanted money—to the gallantry of her own time. She berated the young men for not falling in love with Laura, citing the three attaches at the Embassy who had dismissed her solely due to financial reasons.

"Where is your friend Bluebeard?" she demanded. "Warrington! Why doesn't he marry Laura? They all marry for money now. You are all selfish cowards!"

Pen defended himself with some heat, noting that she hadn't consulted Laura before making accusations.

"But you haven't," Laura interjected gently, taking Pen's hand. "He is going to marry somebody else. I intend to be very fond of her and live with them, provided he doesn't ask every bachelor why he doesn't marry me."

With her conscience cleared and Pen's examination passed without reproach, Pen found himself drawn constantly to Baymouth. Lady Rockminster, delighted to have a captive audience, ordered him to dine there daily to improve his manners. "Don't blush and stammer!" she chided, serving him champagne. "Almost all young men are selfish. My sons and grandsons were too, until I cured them. Come here, and let us teach you to behave properly."

Despite the Dowager's tyrannical ordering of the household, she was beloved, and her small circle included the local vicar and his family. After dinner, when the old lady napped, Pen and Laura would sit together, listening to her soft humming.

"If my music gives her a nap," Laura said kindly, "am I not glad?"

"Why didn't you write to me when you were ill?" Pen asked, blushing.

"What good could you do? I had Martha and the doctor. You are too busy with your books and politics," she teased gently. "I wrote when I was well."

Pen looked at her, ashamed to remember that during her entire illness, he had never written to her.

Through their frequent rides, Pen grew to appreciate Laura's frankness and the simplicity of her heart. Their mother's lingering shadow still influenced them; Laura had held back from Pen previously, fearing her mother's disapproval of their closeness. Now, with Arthur engaged to another, that barrier vanished. They spoke often of their mother, recalling the constant, silent devotion she had shown.

One day, Pen confessed his guilt. "Other people's children play on the grass where we used to play," he said, his voice hard. "Look at the magnolia we planted; it has grown huge. I've visited the cottages where Mother used to help. It's barely a year since she died, and the people care no more for her than for Queen Anne. We are all selfish. There are but few exceptions, like you, to shine like good deeds in this naughty world."

Laura looked down, touching the honeysuckle on her breast. "When you gave me that flower, you weren't selfish, Arthur."

"But it was a sacrifice!" Pen sneered, half-jesting.

"But your heart was kind," Laura insisted. "If you think humbly of yourself, the love diminishes, doesn't it? Perhaps your mother spoiled you by worshipping you. And as for the world, men must fight for themselves. If the world is wicked, why pursue it?"

"And what would you have me do?" Pen asked.
