There is a certain public house known as the Wheel of Fortune, a haunt where the air is thick with the dust of commerce and the clink of coins. It is here, within the shadow of Mr. James Morgan's club, that Sir Francis Clavering once held a private audience with Major Pendennis. Any gentleman who has ever stepped inside knows of the layout: a public parlour for the general riff-raff, a second room reserved for those in liveried service, and a third, strictly forbidden chamber marked "Private" in bold letters. This final sanctum belongs to "The Confidentials," an exclusive circle of which both Morgan and Lightfoot are members.

James Morgan, a man of profound silence, had been eavesdropping on a heated exchange between Strong and Major Pendennis at the Major's lodgings. His curiosity, piqued by the weight of the conversation, had led him to trail his master to the Wheel of Fortune, slipping quietly into the Private Room while the two gentlemen debated matters of state and scandal in the adjacent parlour. From a specific vantage point, hidden behind a curtain of shadows, Morgan could hear nearly everything. The voices rose in a high, angry key, and what he gathered only hardened the suspicions forming in his mind.

"He knew Altamont instantly, didn't he?" Morgan murmured to himself, piecing together the fragments. "Clavering isn't married to my Lady! Altamont is the man. A convict. Young Harthur gets into Parliament, and the Governor promises not to split. By Jove, what a sly old rogue that Governor is! No wonder he's eager to wed Blanche to Harthur. Why? Because she'll bring a hundred thousand guineas and secure a seat for him. A trade, pure and simple."

A physiognomist would have marveled at the transformation on Morgan's face. "If it weren't for my haggard looks and the prejudices of polite society," he muttered, eyeing his reflection in a broken mirror, "damn me, James Morgan, I'd marry her myself!" Yet, marriage seemed out of reach. Instead, Morgan calculated that this secret could serve as a ladder to elevate his own fortunes. Most of those involved would prefer to bury the truth. Sir Francis Clavering, whose fortune hung in the balance, would scream to keep it quiet; Colonel Altamont, whose neck was on the line, would do anything to hush it; and the upstart Mr. Harthur, who believed himself a duke despite having no more than a convict's daughter and a bought seat in Parliament, would pay any price to hide his origins. Even Lady C., Morgan reasoned, whether weary of Clavering or terrified for her son, would pay handsomely to be freed from this web. And Miss Blanche? She would surely reward the man who restored her stolen rights.

"Darn me," concluded the valet, his fingers twitching as he imagined the pile of gold, "with such a hand as luck has dealt me, James Morgan, I'm a made man. It might be a regular eternity of profit. Every single soul involved must subscribe. With what I've already banked, I could quit business, warn my old Governor, turn gentleman, and finally hire a servant of my own, begad." Despite these feverish calculations, Morgan maintained a mask of icy composure, ensuring his duties to the Major remained flawless.

However, one crucial player, Colonel Altamont, was currently missing from London. Morgan knew of Sir Francis's favorite tavern, Shepherd's Inn, but arrived there too late. The Colonel had already cashed his Derby winnings and fled to the Continent. The news sent a jolt of frustration through Morgan, though he masked it with his usual equable smile. "He'll squander that money in the gambling dens of the Rhine," Morgan mused. "It's confounded annoying he slipped away before I could lay my trap." Whether hope triumphed or deferred, victory or defeat, Morgan wore his countenance like iron. While the Major polished his boots, curled his hair, and endured the Major's senile satire with obsequious fidelity, Morgan secretly felt richer than his employer, knowing the secrets of everyone around him.

Meanwhile, the Major, Major Pendennis, spent an autumn wandering from country house to country house, seeking the warmth of old friendships. But the world had changed. Many of the men of his time had died, and their heirs knew him not. The traditions of "the Wild Prince" and the heroes of fashion were forgotten. Walking past the doors of his former haunts, the Major felt a creeping melancholy. He realized he was no longer of the present age. The young men laughed at him. The grand courtliness of the old days had vanished, replaced by a utilitarian drabness.

"The late Lord," he mused, comparing the past to the present, "came to London with four post-chaises and sixteen horses. The whole North Road rushed out to gaze upon his cavalcade. Now? The present Lord travels in a railway carriage with five porters, sneaking away from the station in a brougham, smoking a cigar." He sighed, thinking of Lady Lorraine. Once, she had been a goddess of diamonds and velvet, the wits of the world bowing at her feet. Today, she was a little woman in a black silk gown, talking of laboring classes and emigration, lurking in churches at eight in the morning.

"And the other young men," the Major raged internally, "those lounging guardsmen and lazy dandies sprawled over sofas, stealing off to smoke in each other's bedrooms, reverencing nothing—not even an old gentleman who knew their fathers! They're replaced by cotton-spinners and parsons with hair combed down their backs. The breed is gone. I'm getting old; they're laughing at us." And he was right. The times had shifted, and the new generation mocked the old ways with a gravity that stung the Major's sensitive pride.

By the dismal end of October, when the fogs rolled in and the lawyers returned to town, the Major came back to London. He found himself surrounded by the familiar chaos: loaded cabs, piled boxes, and crowds of returning families. For the Major, however, there were no bills to settle and no domestic sorrows to soothe him. He had money enough in his desk, and his indifference to others' needs usually kept him insulated. Yet, a gentleman may be wealthy and still feel dispirited.

His recent country visit had been trying. Gout had twinged his joints; the birds were wild and shy; the ploughed fields had worn him down. Worse still, the young men had laughed at him, and he had been forced to endure their mockery at table. He hadn't even managed his whist game. Sulky and discontented, he had been unbearable to Morgan, swearing at him and abusing him for days. He had scalded his mouth with bad soup at Swindon and lost his umbrella in a train carriage, a grievance that had driven him to curse his valet with renewed fervor.

Back in his lodgings, the atmosphere was stifling. The chimneys smoked furiously, and when the Major flung open the windows to vent his spleen, he swore so acrimoniously that Morgan, standing nearby, felt an impulse to hurl the old gentleman out of the casement himself. As the Major marched down the street to the Club, Morgan trailed behind, muttering curses of his own.

Bays's Club was not a sanctuary. The house smelled of fresh varnish and turpentine, and a large streak of white paint marred the back of the Major's fur-collared coat. The dinner was mediocre, and the company was odious. Old Hawkshaw, with his hacking cough, sat beside old Colonel Gripley, who devoured newspapers like a starving animal, joined by the irrepressible bore Jawkins, who detailed every inn-bill paid during his latest foreign tour. Each incident added to the Major's misery. As the waiter trod on his toe delivering coffee, the Major felt the Furies hunting him in tandem—from home to the Club, and back again.

While the Major suffered, Morgan sat in the landlady's parlour, drowning his troubles in hot brandy-and-water. He poured out his grievances to Mrs. Brixham, the landlady, who was, in effect, his slave. Morgan had bought the lease of her house, secured her son's acceptance, and held a bill of sale over her furniture. Her son, a clerk, was easily manipulated, and Mrs. Brixham, a clergyman's widow, was reduced to fetching his bootjack and slippers. The portraits of her dead children, tiny black profiles, hung over the mantelpiece of his back-parlour, now his property. Morgan drank deeply, paying for the liquor with the poor woman's own coin, and ate his supper with a relish born of dominance.

Just as Morgan finished a third tumbler, the Major returned from the Club, heavy with ill humor. Hearing the bell, Morgan swore savagely, finishing his drink before answering the summons. He received the Major's subsequent abuse in silence, kneeling to remove the boots while growling maledictions beneath his breath. As the Major demanded his coat be taken off, Morgan approached close enough for the scent of brandy to betray him.

"I'm a drunken beast!" the Major accused, pointing an accusing finger. "And you smell of it!"

Patience snapped. "Am I a beast, am I?" Morgan roared, throwing off all subordination

In Which the Major Neither Yields His Money Nor His Life

Early the following morning, the heavy iron shutters of the lodging house at Bury Street clicked open. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of boiled cabbage and stale tobacco, broken only by the sharp, metallic tang of a razor being oiled. Standing by the window, a figure moved with the stiff, jerky precision of a clockwork toy that had seen better days. This was Mr. James Morgan, the old gentleman's valet, though "valet" felt like a generous title for a man whose soul seemed to have been replaced by a ledger book. He held a silver dressing-case in one hand and a can of water in the other, his face a mask of grave, unblinking respect.

From the bed, where he lay swathed in blankets like a mummy, came the voice of Old Pendennis. It was a gravelly rumble, the sound of a cannon that had fired too many times in its day. "It's you, is it?" the Major croaked. "I shan't take you back again, you understand."

Morgan turned slowly, his movements deliberate, as if calculating the cost of every step. "I ave not the least wish to be took back agin, Major Pendennis," he declared, his voice dripping with a dignity that bordered on the absurd. "Nor to serve you nor hany man. But as I wish you to be comftable as long as you stay in my house..." He trailed off, clearing his throat, "...I came up to do what's nessary." With a theatrical flourish, he laid out the silver case, unscrewed the top, and strapped the shining razor with a tension that suggested it might explode if not handled with absolute reverence.

"These offices concluded," Morgan murmured, addressing the Major with an indescribable solemnity, "thinkin' that you would most likely be in want of a respectable pusson, until you suited yourself, I spoke to a young man last night, who is 'ere.'"

"Indeed!" grunted the warrior in the tent-bed.

"He ave lived in the fust famlies," Morgan continued, his eyes darting around the room as if expecting witnesses, "and I can wouch for his respectability."

"You are monstrous polite," grinned the old Major, a spark of amusement lighting his weary eyes. The truth was, after the chaotic events of the previous evening, Morgan had retreated to his own club, *The Wheel of Fortune*. There, amidst the clinking of glasses and the hum of gossip, he had encountered Frosch, a courier and valet fresh from a foreign tour with young Lord Cubley. Morgan, feeling the tremors of retirement approaching, had pitched his story to Frosch with dramatic flair: he claimed to be having "a devil of a blow hup with his own Gov'rner," planned to retire entirely, and needed a temporary replacement immediately. If Frosch wanted a job, he was told to apply in Bury Street.

"You are very polite," said the Major, feigning admiration. "And your recommendation, I am sure, will have every weight."

Morgan flushed red, feeling the sting of his former master's mockery. "The man have awaited on you before, sir," he stammered, regaining his composure. "Lord De la Pole... gave him to his nephew, young Lord Cubley... He have been with him on his foring tour... Not wishing to go to Fitzurse Castle, which Frosch's chest is delicate, and he cannot bear the cold in Scotland, he is free to serve you or not, as you choose."

"I repeat, sir, that you are exceedingly polite," the Major said, straightening up. "Come in, Frosch—you will do very well—Mr. Morgan, will you have the great kindness to—"

"I shall show him what is nessary, sir," Morgan interjected, stepping forward with a stern air. "What is customry for you to wish to ave done. Will you please to take breakfast 'ere or at the Club, Major Pendennis?"

"With your kind permission, I will breakfast here, and afterwards we will make our little arrangements."

"If you please, sir."

"Will you now oblige me by leaving the room?"

Morgan bowed deeply and withdrew. The excessive politeness of his ex-employer, coupled with the Major's biting sarcasm, had ignited a small fire of anger within him, though he masked it quickly with a grim smile. As the old gentleman began his mysterious toilet, brushing his hair with the fervor of a man preparing for battle, we will also modestly retreat to observe the unfolding drama.

***

After breakfast, the atmosphere in the room shifted from domestic tranquility to military efficiency. Major Pendennis and his new aide-de-camp, Frosch, set about packing for departure. The establishment of the old bachelor was surprisingly streamlined. He possessed no useless wardrobe; his collection of worldly goods was curated with the eye of a collector. There sat a Bible belonging to his mother, a road book, Pen's novel bound in calf skin, and the Duke of Wellington's Despatches. Scattered around were prints, maps, and portraits of illustrious generals, sovereigns, consorts, and specifically the General under whom the Major had served in India. He was a man always ready to march at a few hours' notice.

The cases in which he had brought his property into his lodgings fifteen years prior, originally stored in the lofts, were miraculously still intact and ample enough to hold everything again. A young woman named Betty, who performed the duties of a housekeeper, assisted under the watchful gaze of the "terrible" Mr. Morgan. Her demeanor was subdued, her hands moving with mechanical precision as she dusted and cleaned. Morgan stood guard, his expression guarded and solemn. He had spoken no words to Mrs. Brixham regarding his threats from the night before, but his eyes betrayed a predatory intent, and the poor widow trembled, awaiting her fate.

Old Pendennis, armed with his cane, supervised the packaging of his goods and chattels under Frosch's capable hands. Meanwhile, the "Slavey"—Betty—burned such of the Major's papers as he deemed unnecessary, flung open doors and closets until they were utterly empty. Soon, all boxes and chests were sealed tight, save for one specific item: the Major's desk, which stood ready to receive the final accounts of Mr. Morgan.

That individual now made his entrance, balancing a stack of books. "As I wish to speak to you in privick," he announced, "peraps you will ave the kindness to request Frosch to step downstairs."

"Bring a couple of cabs, Frosch, if you please—and wait downstairs until I ring for you," commanded the Major. Morgan watched Frosch descend the stairs, vanish onto the street, and return with the vehicles. Then, producing his books and accounts—which were simple, clean, and easily settled—he cleared his throat.

"And now, sir," he said, pocketing the cheque his former employer handed him and signing his name with a flourish, "and now that accounts is closed between us, sir," he raised his voice, "I porpose to speak to you as one man to another." Morgan loved the sound of his own voice; whenever an opportunity arose, whether at the Club or in the housekeeper's room, he indulged in public speaking. "And I must tell you, that I'm in possession of certing infamation."

"And may I inquire of what nature, pray?" asked the Major, feigning ignorance.

"It's valuble information, Major Pendennis, as you know very well," Morgan intoned. "I know of a marriage as is no marriage—of a honourable Baronet as is no more married than I am; and which his wife is married to somebody else, as you know too, sir."

At once, the pieces fell into place for Pendennis. "Ha! this accounts for your behaviour. You have been listening at the door, sir, I suppose," the Major said, looking very haughty. "I forgot to look at the keyhole when I went to that public-house, or I might have suspected what sort of a person was behind it."

"I may have my schemes as you may have yours, I suppose," Morgan countered, his chin lifting. "I may get my information, and I may act on that information, and I may find that information valuble as anybody else may. A poor servant may have a bit of luck as well as a gentleman, mayn't he? Don't you be putting on your aughty looks, sir, and comin' the aristocrat over me. That's all gammon with me. I'm an Englishman, I am, and as good as you."

"To what the devil does this tend, sir? and how does the secret which you have surprised concern me, I should like to know?" demanded the Major, with great majesty.

"How does it concern me, indeed! how grand we are!" Morgan exclaimed, pacing slightly. "How does it concern my nephew, I wonder? How does it concern my nephew's seat in Parlyment: and to subornation of bigamy? How does it concern that? What, are you to be the only man to have a secret, and to trade on it? Why shouldn't I go halves, Major Pendennis? I've found it out too. Look here! I ain't goin' to be unreasonable with you. Make it worth my while, and I'll keep the thing close. Let Mr. Arthur take his seat, and

In Which Pendennis Counts His Eggs

Arthur Pendennis had just landed in London, though his stay was meant to be fleeting. After seeing off some fellow travelers at an inn near the West End, he hurried to Chambers in Lamb Court. Even the dreariest legal chambers manage to catch a little sunlight, and for the barrister, freedom itself is the sun. Pen pushed open the door to his friend Warrington's room and found it, as ever, fragrant with pipe-smoke. George Warrington sat hunched over his newspapers and reviews, the quintessential image of a busy lawyer.

The two friends exchanged a greeting that was rough around the edges but warm at the core, a distinctly British form of affection. "Well, young one!" Warrington grinned, pulling his pipe from his mouth. "How are you, old boy?" Pen replied, shaking his hand vigorously. This was the ritual of two men who might have died for each other yesterday and merely nod at breakfast tomorrow. They shared a purse, a soul, and a fierce loyalty, yet their interaction remained a study in stoic decorum. They did not embrace like the French, nor kiss like the Germans; they simply acknowledged each other's existence with a grunt and a handshake, confident in a bond that required no words.

"Switzerland?" Pen asked, settling into the chair opposite.

"Yes," Warrington confirmed. "Couldn't find tobacco fit to smoke until Strasbourg, where I finally grabbed some Caporal."

Though Warrington's mind was clearly filled with grand sights and sublime emotions, his enthusiasm remained shy, veiled behind a cloud of tobacco smoke. He would save his true effusions for private letters later; for now, he contented himself with a list of places visited: Switzerland, Northern Italy, the Tyrol, Vienna, Dresden, and the Rhine. He spoke of them with a peculiar sulky tone, as if mentioning them made him slightly miserable.

Turning the conversation back to himself, Pen confessed to being bored stiff in the country, canvassing with agonizing slowness. He mentioned plans for Tunbridge Wells and the inevitable slowness of his friends there. "It is terribly difficult for an Englishman to admit happiness," he sighed.

"And the seat in Parliament, Pen? Is it settled?" Warrington asked, sharpening his pencil.

"As soon as Parliament sits and a new writ is issued, Clavering steps down, and I slide into his shoes," Pen answered confidently.

"Do we operate under a specific King? Are we Liberals, Conservatives, or Independents?"

"Hem!" Pen chuckled. "Politics are dead. Every man thinks alike. I haven't enough acreage to be a Protectionist, nor could I afford to be if I owned the whole county. I'll stick with the Government but push ahead on social reforms. I've been busy compiling Blue Books on sanitation and colonization. Don't look at me like that, old cynic. I'm serious."

"We reserve the right to vote against the government whenever we feel like it," Warrington noted dryly. "But we are friends of the people first. We lecture at the Clavering Institute, shake hands with mechanics, and argue for a wider franchise. Still, we're ready to accept office once the House hears our speeches and realizes we aren't nuisances."

"I am not Moses," Pen said, his voice dropping to a melancholy hum. "I have no divine laws to descend from Sinai. I don't belong to the mountains, nor do I aspire to lead humanity. My faith isn't strong enough for that, nor is my vanity. I won't lie, George, unless it's a necessary fiction to keep the wheels turning. If I hear a good idea in the House, I'll support it. If a decent spot opens up, I'll take it. But I wouldn't flatter a giant any more than a mob. As for labels, why bother with Whiggism? It's not a holy order. The Radicals and the countryside built the Liberal Conservatives. Sometimes *The Morning Post* is right, sometimes *Punch*. I don't have a calling; I just seize chances. Let's talk about something else."

"The next thing after ambition is love, surely?" Warrington pressed gently. "How are our young hearts doing? Are we going to trade our chambers for a drawing-room? Are you divorcing me, Arthur, to marry?"

"I suppose so," Pen admitted, sighing deeply. "She's kind, lively, sings well, and doesn't mind smoking. She'll have a fortune—Uncle says the Begum will shower us with gold. And Blanche seems smitten."

"That implies we accept both her caresses and her cash," Warrington observed sagely.

"Life is a transaction, isn't it?" Pen mused. "I haven't broken my heart over her. I've told her plainly what I feel, and we're engaged. Since I last saw her, especially these past two months, I think she's grown fonder. Her letters to me and to Laura prove it. Ours have been plain—no rapturous vows, nothing theatrical. Just treating it as a done deal, neither rushing nor delaying."

"And Laura? How is she?" Warrington asked, his tone shifting abruptly.

"Laura, George," Pen said, staring fixedly at his friend, "by heaven, she is the best, noblest, and dearest girl the sun has ever warmed." His voice cracked. He reached across the table, gripping Warrington's hand.

"You only realized that now, didn't you?" Warrington asked softly after a heavy pause.

"Who hasn't learned lessons too late?" Arthur cried, his impatience flaring again, gathering emotion as he spoke. "Whose life hasn't been a series of disappointments? Who buries their heart intact? We all pay a ransom to Fate. If we survive paying the fine, that's lucky. I thought I'd lost the greatest prize in the world—an angel living under my roof—and let her slip away. Am I the only fool? Does it hurt less because I admit I deserved it? She's gone. God bless her! It's like *Undine*, isn't it, George?"

"She was in this room once," Warrington whispered. "I saw her. Heard her soft voice. Saw that sweet smile and those kind eyes. I think of her in every nightmare, blessed and loved forever. Now... nothing remains but a glass that once held a bouquet and a Bible with Helen's handwriting. A brief flower of a life."

They sat in silence, the weight of loss hanging between them. Finally, Pen broke the quiet. "I must go see Uncle. He's in a terrible mood, writing nasty letters. He's getting old, and he wants me in Parliament with a house and an heir before I fade away. 'Show me these,' he wrote, 'then let old Arthur Pendennis make room for the younger fellows.' He walked the Pall Mall pavement long enough."

"There's a kindness in that old heathen," Warrington remarked. "He cares for his lineage, for us. He blesses us from the club window at Bays's and gets buried beneath the flags of St. James's, watching the cabs roll to the Levee. An edifying end."

Pen mused, "The new blood I'm bringing in is a bit tainted. If I'd chosen differently, Father-in-Law Amory wouldn't have been the ancestor I wanted. Nor Grandfather-in-Law Snell. Or those Oriental forebears. Speaking of Amory, who was he? Lieutenant of an Indiaman. Blanche wrote verses about him—the storm, the mountain wave, the seaman's grave, the gallant father. Drowned commanding a country ship between Calcutta and Sydney. Amory and the Begum weren't exactly happy. She's been unlucky with husbands, the poor old lady. Between us, Sir Francis Clavering is a despicable creature—"

"Never legislated for his country," Warrington cut in sharply. Pen flushed.

"At Baden," Warrington continued, changing the subject, "I met Chevalier Strong in full regalia. He and Clavering are at odds; Strong thinks Clavering is an utter scoundrel. Your old card teacher, Bloundell, was with Strong. Time has polished him into a more accomplished rogue. But the real king of the castle was Colonel Altamont. He was breaking banks and giving flies to everyone."

"My uncle knows a thing or two about Altamont," Pen said. "And Clavering knows something about him. There's a certain *louche* quality to the man. Anyway, I must dash off to Bury Street like a dutiful nephew."

Taking his hat, Pen prepared to leave. "I'll walk with you," Warrington offered. They stopped at Pen's chambers, now situated on the lower floor. Pen immediately began dabbing himself with eau-de-cologne, meticulously scenting his hair and whiskers.

"What's wrong? Haven't you smoked? Did my pipe poison you?" Warrington grumbled.

"I'm dining with some ladies," Pen explained, adjusting a gorgeous shirt-front and neckcloth. "They're passing through town, staying at an hotel in Jermyn Street. I slept at Bath last night. Come along, George! I can invite anyone I please, and the old lady talks about you constantly."

Warrington looked at Pen, dressed to the nines with fresh gloves and glistening boots, while he wore thick high-lows, a torn shirt, and a blue beard that had chewed through

The dinner had barely been cleared when Arthur Pendennis stumbled back into the room, his face a mask of ghastly pallor. Lady Rockminster, whose patience was as thin as her temper, immediately launched into a tirade about his tardiness. But Laura, watching her cousin, saw the terror in his eyes and cut her aunt short with a gasp of tender alarm. "Arthur!" she cried, "What has happened? Are you ill?"

To drown out the questions, Arthur downed a massive bumper of sherry, his hands trembling. "I have heard news so extraordinary I must tell you later," he stammered, casting a nervous glance at the servants. "Please, stop kicking the floor under the table," Lady Rockminster chided, pointing out that Arthur had trampled on her dog, Fido, and knocked over his saucer, whereas Mr. Warrington managed to keep his boots perfectly silent. "And you," she added, addressing the group, "you've been tramping and beating the carpet like madmen."

By dessert, the atmosphere was heavy with unspoken dread. "This dinner has been positively stupid," Lady Rockminster declared, tossing her head with theatrical indignance. "I suspect something grave has occurred, and you wish to speak to Laura. I shall retire to take a nap; I doubt whether I shall drink any tea. Good night, Mr. Warrington. Do come again, but let us hope there is no business to discuss." With a flourish of dignity, the old lady departed, followed by George and the others. As Warrington rose to bid Laura farewell, Arthur stopped him. "Pray, stay, George," he urged, his voice thick with agitation. "You must hear this news and offer your counsel. I am lost without it."

"It concerns Blanche, Arthur," Laura whispered, her heart hammering against her ribs, her cheeks flushing a color she had never known before.

"Yes," Pen confirmed, leaning forward, "and it is the strangest story imaginable. When I left you to visit my uncle's lodgings, I found his ancient servant, Morgan, standing guard at the door. He informed me that he and his master had parted ways that very morning; my uncle had fled to this hotel. Morgan insisted I enter, claiming he possessed information of vital importance. It turns out the scoundrel has hoarded a fortune while serving my uncle and is now a millionaire. He revealed a terrible secret: Blanche's father is not dead. The marriage between Clavering and the Begum was void."

"And therefore Blanche is her grandfather's heir?" Warrington reasoned.

"Perhaps," Pen said grimly, "but consider the father! Amory is an escaped convict. Clavering knows it; my uncle knows it. They used this fact to blackmail Clavering into yielding his parliamentary seat to me."

"But Blanche doesn't know," Laura interjected softly. "Nor does Lady Clavering?"

"No," Pen replied, his voice dropping. "Blanche believes her father drowned in New South Wales, a convict, not a captain as she was led to believe. Her grandmother tells me they were miserable together and that Amory was a bad man. Lady Clavering wept when she told me this, saying it was hard for a woman to admit being glad her husband was dead, and that twice in her life she had chosen terribly. Now, what is to be done? If Amory returns, Morgan will use the secret to extort money from us all. The villain assumed I knew, threatening me with an annuity and demanding I buy a seat in Parliament through Clavering. Was my uncle mad to conspire in this treachery? To treat my mother's son like a pawn?"

"I cannot imagine it, dear Arthur," Laura said, seizing his hand and pressing a kiss to it. "Her father's shame is not Blanche's fault. If you were married, would you desert her because she is innocent? You are pledged to her. If she suffers, surely you would console her? Mother would have done the same." She wrapped her arms around him, burying her face in his chest.

"Our mother is an angel to God," Pen sobbed, his composure cracking. "And you are the dearest, the kindest woman. Teach me my duty. Pray for me that I may fulfill it with a pure heart. God bless you, my sister!"

"Amen," groaned Warrington, his head bowed in his hands. "She speaks truly. That girl cannot do wrong." Indeed, she looked like an angel, radiant and kind. After a moment, she placed her small hand on the table. "And now," she said, looking at the men, "we shall have some tea."

Before this mundane resolution could settle, a servant announced that Major Pendennis had returned to the hotel and awaited his nephew. Laura exchanged a worried glance with Pen. *Behave,* her eyes seemed to say, *hold fast to the right.* She excused herself, retreating to her chambers, leaving the two gentlemen alone. Warrington, usually indifferent to tea, regretted the impending beverage deeply. *Why couldn't old Pendennis have arrived an hour later?* he mused. *An hour sooner or later matters little. The hour has struck. The inevitable moment to say farewell has come.*

As Arthur walked through the hotel corridors, his anger swelled. He was furious that the old man was using him as a puppet, compromising his honor. Yet, when he met the Major, the old gentleman's hand was cold and shaking. He coughed miserably by the fire, complaining of Morgan's drunken insolence and ingratitude, cursing the impudent scoundrel who had stolen ten thousand pounds and deserved hanging. The Major spoke of his failing health, of packing his library, and of a Bible belonging to Arthur's poor mother that he wished the younger man to keep. His age and kindness slowly disarmed Arthur's rage, filling him with guilt.

Arthur prepared to deliver the blow. "I visited your lodgings at Bury Street, Uncle," he began slowly. "I spoke with Morgan."

"The cat is out of the bag," the Major muttered, his face flushing. "A devilishly awkward affair, eh?"

"He told me a story that shocked me to the core," Arthur said. "Concerning Miss Amory's father, Lady Clavering's first husband, and his true identity."

"Hem," the Major rubbed his nose nervously. "I've suspected this circumstance for some time."

"I wish I had known sooner, or not at all," Arthur said gloomily. "There is no reason for the women to know, and I shall never tell them. Morgan intends to trade on this secret and has offered me ransom. I find myself engaged to a convict's daughter."

"That is exactly why I withheld it from you, my dear boy," the Major said, his tone shifting to a desperate cheerfulness. "Miss Amory is not a convict's daughter. She is the legitimate daughter of Lady Clavering, worth fifty or sixty thousand pounds. Her father-in-law, a reputable Baronet, approves of the match and yields his seat to her husband. What could be simpler?"

"Is it true, sir?" Arthur asked, his voice hollow.

"Begad, yes! Of course it's true. Amory is dead. The first sign of life he shows, he dies. We have him at a deadlock, like the fellow in the play 'The Critic.' A monstrous witty man, Sheridan, and so was his son. By Gad, when I was at the Cape..."

Arthur interrupted him sharply. "If you had told me this sooner, you would have spared us both immense pain. I am bound by an engagement I cannot honorably uphold."

"No, begad, we've fixed you!" the Major exclaimed, oblivious. "A man fixed to a seat in Parliament and a pretty girl with an income is fixed to no bad thing."

"Great Heavens, sir!" Arthur cried, his eyes wide with horror. "Are you blind? Can't you see?"

"See what, young gentleman?"

"See that I would rather join my father-in-law at the hulks than accept a parliamentary seat bought by Clavering's silence! See that I would rather take the spoons off the table than marry a felon's daughter for money! You have condemned me to poverty and shame, cursed my career when it might have flourished! We have played a guilty game, Uncle, and been overreached. In marrying for money and a seat, I degraded myself and prostituted my honor."

"What in Heaven's name do you mean?" shrieked the old man.

"I mean there is a limit to baseness I cannot cross!" Arthur shouted. "I have felt for months that my conduct was wicked and sordid. I am rightly punished by the loss of both the fortune and the seat."

"How do you mean you lose them?" the Major screamed. "Who the devil will take your fortune or seat away? Clavering shall give you every shilling of eighty thousand pounds!"

"I will keep my promise to Miss Amory, sir."

"And her parents shall keep theirs to you."

"Not so, please God," Arthur answered firmly. "I have sinned, but Heaven help me, I will sin no more. I will release Clavering from the bargain made without my knowledge. I will take no money from Blanche beyond her original settlement, and I will strive to make her happy. You have brought this misery upon me, sir, but you knew no better. I forgive you."

"Arthur—in God's name—in your father's name

When Pen, clad in his dressing-gown, ascended the stairs to Warrington's chambers the following morning to report on the previous evening's interview with his uncle and solicit George's counsel, he discovered only Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress. Her charge, George, had departed, having packed a carpet-bag bound for his brother's house in Suffolk. Several packages addressed to the newspaper and reviews for which he labored lay upon the table, waiting for collection.

"He found the dear gentleman at the table!" Mrs. Flanagan chirped. "He was writing at his papers, and one candle had burned quite out. Hard as his bed is, he didn't touch it all night, sir."

Indeed, having endured the Club's brawl until it became insufferable, George had returned home. He had spent the night, and then some, hammering away at a piece of work he was commissioned to complete, bending his youthful spine to the task with singular intensity. By the time the tardy November dawn crept through the window, the labor was finished. Somewhere along the line, perhaps lost in the fog of exhaustion, the young man had succumbed to slumber. Now, however, he was gone, leaving behind the relics of his genius: two or three reference books, fragments of torn manuscripts, open drawers, pens, an inkstand, lines barely visible on blotting paper, and a twist of sealing wax bitten into sundry pieces.

Pen threw himself into George's empty chair, noting the scene with his usual acumen, though perhaps also in spite of himself. There was a gap in the bookcase, right beside the old College Plato and the Boniface Arms, where Helen's Bible usually resided. *He has taken that,* Pen realized. *Of course he has.* He knew why his friend was gone.

"Oh, dear, dear old George!" Pen murmured, rubbing his hand over his eyes. *How much wiser, how much better, how much nobler he is than I!* Where was such a friend? Where could he find such a brave heart again? Where would he hear such a frank voice, or witness such kind laughter? Where would he see such a true gentleman? It was no wonder she loved him. God bless him! Compared to this titan, what was Pen? What choice did she have but to love him? They would remain brothers unto the end, as fate decreed they could never be more. They would serve as her knights, waiting in attendance; and when they grew old, they would recount how deeply they had loved her. *Dear, dear old George!*

Descending to his own chambers, Pen glanced at the letter-box of his outer door, a feature he had previously overlooked. Nestled within was a little note addressed to A.P., Esq., penned in George's familiar hand. George had slipped it in just as he departed.

"Dear Pen,—I shall be half-way home when you breakfast, and intend to stay over Christmas, in Norfolk, or elsewhere.
*I have my own opinion of the issue of matters about which we talked in J——— St. yesterday; and think my presence de trop.*
*Give my very best regards and adieux to your cousin.*"

And so, George was gone, leaving Mrs. Flanagan to rule over his empty rooms.

Naturally, Pen felt compelled to visit his uncle the day after their conversation. Being denied admission, he turned instead to Lady Rockminster's apartments. The old lady immediately demanded Bluebeard, insisting that he join them for dinner.

"Bluebeard is gone," Pen replied, producing poor George's scrap of paper and handing it to Laura. She examined it, cast no look at Pen in return, and passed the paper back, walking away silently. Pen rushed into an eloquent eulogy of his dear old George for Lady Rockminster, who was stunned by his fervor. She had rarely heard him so warm in praise of anyone; indeed, she frankly confessed that she couldn't imagine it being in his nature to care so deeply for any other soul.

Later, as Mr. Pendennis strolled through Waterloo Place on his daily pilgrimage to the hotel where Laura resided, he spotted an old acquaintance emerging from Messrs. Gimcrack's celebrated shop. This gentleman was trailed by an obsequious shopman carrying parcels. The man wore the deepest mourning, as did his brougham, driver, and horse. Grief, cushioned by ease and supported by the softest springs, was perfectly typified by the equipage and its diminutive owner.

"What, Foker! Hail, Foker!" cried Pen, recognizing Arthur's schoolfellow, the heir to the late lamented John Henry Foker, Esq., master of Logwood and co-partner in the great brewery of Foker and Co.—the greater portion of Foker's Entire.

A little hand, gloved in deep ebony and framed by a snowy wristband of three inches, extended to meet Arthur's salute. The other little hand clutched a small morocco case containing, undoubtedly, something precious recently acquired at Gimcrack's. Pen's keen eyes and satirical turn instantly discerned the errand; he recalled the heir in Horace pouring forth the gathered wine of his father's vats, realizing that human nature remained remarkably consistent whether in Regent Street or the Via Sacra.

"*Le Roi est mort. Vive le Roi!*" Arthur declared.

"Ah!" said the other. "Yes. Thank you—very much obliged. How do you do, Pen?—very busy—good-bye!" And he leaped into the black brougham, sitting like a little black Care behind the black coachman. Having blushed upon seeing Pen, he displayed other signs of guilt and perturbation, which Pen attributed to the novelty of his situation and began to speculate upon in his usual sardonic fashion.

"Yes: so wags the world," Pen mused. "The stone closes over Harry the Fourth, and Harry the Fifth reigns in his stead. The old ministers at the brewery come and kneel before him with their books; the draymen, his subjects, fling up their red caps, and shout for him. What a grave deference and sympathy the bankers and lawyers show! There was too great a stake at issue between those two for them ever to love each other cordially. As long as one man keeps another out of twenty thousand a year, the younger must always hunger after the crown, and the wish becomes the father of the thought of possession. Thank Heaven, there was no thought of money between me and our dear mother, Laura."

"There never could have been. You would have spurned it!" Laura cried. "Why make yourself more selfish than you are, Pen; and allow your mind to entertain, for even an instant, thoughts of such... such dreadful meanness? You make me blush for you, Arthur: you make me..." Her eyes completed the sentence, and she wiped them with a handkerchief.

"There are some truths which women will never acknowledge," Pen observed, "and from which your modesty always turns away. I do not claim to have known the feeling myself, merely that I am glad I lacked the temptation. Is there any harm in that confession of weakness?"

"We are all taught to pray to be delivered from evil, Arthur," Laura whispered. "I am glad if you were spared from that great crime; and only sorry to think that you could, by any possibility, have been led into it. But you never could; and you don't think you could. Your acts are generous and kind: you disdain mean actions. You took Blanche without money, without a bribe. Yes, thanks be to Heaven, dear brother. You could not have sold yourself away; I knew you could not when the day arrived, and you did not. Praise be—where praise is due. Why does this horrid scepticism pursue you, my Arthur? Why doubt and sneer at your own heart—at everyone's? Oh, if you knew the pain you give me—how I lie awake thinking of those hard sentences, dear brother, wishing them unspoken, unthought!"

"Do I cause you many thoughts and many tears, Laura?" Arthur asked. The fullness of innocent love beamed from her in reply. A smile of heavenly purity, a glance of unutterable tenderness, sympathy, and pity illuminated her face. All these indications of love and purity Arthur beheld and worshipped in her, watching them as one watches a child, or as one imagines regarding an angel.

"I—I don't know what I have done," he said simply, "to merit such regard from two such women. It is like undeserved praise, Laura—or too much good fortune, which frightens one—or a great post when a man feels unfit for it. Ah, sister, how weak and wicked we are; how spotless, and full of love and truth, Heaven made you! I think for some of you there has been no fall," he said, gazing at the charming girl with an almost paternal look of admiration. "You can't help having sweet thoughts, and doing good actions. Dear creature! they are the flowers which you bear."

"And what else, sir?" asked Laura. "I see a sneer coming over your face. What is it? Why does it come to drive all the good thoughts away?"

"A sneer, is there? I was thinking, my dear, that Nature did very well in making you so good and loving: but—"

"But what? What is that wicked 'but,' and why are you always summoning it up?"

"Dearest Blanche," Arthur wrote, "you are perpetually reading and dreaming up pretty dramas and exciting romances in real life; are you now prepared to enact a part of one? And not the pleasantest role, my dear Blanche, wherein the heroine seizes possession of her father's palace and wealth, welcoming her husband to loyal retainers and faithful vassals, greeting her happy bridegroom with 'All of this is mine and thine'—but the other character, that of the unluckiest lady imaginable. A woman who suddenly discovers she is not the Princess's wife, but Claude Melnotte's, the beggar. That of Alnaschar's wife, who bursts in just as her husband has kicked over the tray of porcelain that was to be the making of his fortune. But wait; Alnaschar, who kicked down the china, was not a married man. He had cast his eye on the Vizier's daughter, and his hopes of her crashed to the floor with the shattered bowls and tea-cups.

"Will you be the Vizier's daughter, refusing and laughing Alnaschar to scorn, or will you be the Lady of Lyons, falling for the penniless Claude Melnotte? I will act that part if you like. I will love you my best in return. I will do my utmost to make your humble life happy—for humble it will be. At least, the odds are heavily stacked against any other conclusion. We shall live and die in a poor, prosaic, humdrum way. There will be no stars and epaulettes for the hero of our story. I shall write one or two more stories, which will presently be forgotten. I shall be called to the Bar and try to climb the ladder of my profession; perhaps some day, if I am very lucky and work very hard (which is absurd), I may secure a colonial appointment, and you may become an Indian Judge's lady. Meanwhile, I shall buy back *The Pall Mall Gazette*; the publishers are weary of it since the passing of poor Shandon and will sell it for a trifling sum. Warrington will be my right hand, writing it up to a respectable sale. I will introduce you to Mr. Finucane, the sub-editor, and I know who in the end will be Mrs. Finucane—a very nice, gentle creature who has lived sweetly through a sad life. We will jog along, I say, and look out for better times, earning our living decently. You shall have the opera boxes and superintend the fashionable intelligence, breaking your little heart in the poet's corner. Shall we live over the offices?—there are four very good rooms, a kitchen, and a garret for Laura in Catherine Street in the Strand; or would you prefer a house in Waterloo Road? It would be very pleasant, only there is that halfpenny toll at the bridge. The boys may go to King's College, mayn't they? Does all this read to you like a joke?"

"Ahh, dear Blanche, it is no joke, and I am sober and telling the truth. Our fine daydreams have vanished. Our carriage has whirled out of sight like Cinderella's; our house in Belgravia has been whisked away into the air by a malevolent genius, and I am no longer a Member of Parliament any more than I am a Bishop on his bench in the House of Lords, or a Duke with a garter at his knee. You know pretty well what my property is, and your own little fortune. We may have enough with those two to live in decent comfort—to take a cab sometimes when we go out to see friends, and not to deny ourselves an omnibus when we are tired. But that is all: is that enough for you, my little dainty lady? I doubt sometimes whether you can bear the life which I offer you—at least, it is fair that you should know what it will be. If you say, 'Yes, Arthur, I will follow your fate whatever it may be, and be a loyal and loving wife to aid and cheer you'—come to me, dear Blanche, and may God help me so that I may do my duty to you. If not, and you look to a higher station, I must not bar Blanche's fortune—I will stand in the crowd, and see your ladyship go to Court when you are presented, and you shall give me a smile from your chariot window. I saw Lady Mirabel going to the drawing-room last season: the happy husband at her side glittered with stars and cordons. All the flowers in the garden bloomed in the coachman's bosom. Will you have these and the chariot, or walk on foot and mend your husband's stockings?"

"I cannot tell you now—afterwards I might, should the day come when we may have no secrets from one another—what has happened within the last few hours which has changed all my prospects in life: but so it is, that I have learned something which forces me to give up the plans which I had formed, and many vain and ambitious hopes in which I had been indulging. I have written and dispatched a letter to Sir Francis Clavering, stating that I cannot accept his seat in Parliament until after my marriage; in like manner, I cannot and will not accept any larger fortune with you than that which has always belonged to you since your grandfather's death and the birth of your half-brother. Your good mother is not in the least aware—I hope she never may be—of the reasons which force me to this very strange decision. They arise from a painful circumstance, attributable to none of our faults; but, having once befallen, they are as fatal and irreparable as that shock which overset honest Alnaschar's porcelain and shattered all his hopes beyond the power of mending. I write gaily enough, for there is no use in bewailing such a hopeless mischance. We have not drawn the great prize in the lottery, dear Blanche: but I shall be contented enough without it, if you can be so; and I repeat, with all my heart, that I will do my best to make you happy."

"And now, what news shall I give you? My uncle is very unwell and takes my refusal of the seat in Parliament in sad dudgeon; the scheme was his, poor old gentleman, and he naturally bemoans its failure. But Warrington, Laura, and I had a council of war: they know this awful secret and back me in my decision. You must love George as you love what is generous and upright and noble; and as for Laura—she must be our Sister, Blanche, our Saint, our good Angel. With two such friends at home, what need we care for the world without; or who is member for Clavering, or who is asked or not asked to the great balls of the season?"

To this frank communication came back the letter from Blanche to Laura, and one to Pen himself, which perhaps his own letter justified. "You are spoiled by the world," Blanche wrote; "you do not love your poor Blanche as she would be loved, or you would not offer thus lightly to take her or to leave her, no, Arthur, you love me not—a man of the world, you have given me your plighted troth, and are ready to redeem it; but that entire affection, that love whole and abiding, where—where is that vision of my youth? I am but a pastime of your life, and I would be its all;—but a fleeting thought, and I would be your whole soul. I would have our two hearts one; but ah, my Arthur, how lonely yours is! how little you give me of it! You speak of our parting with a smile on your lip; of our meeting, and you care not to hasten it! Is life but a disillusion, then, and are the flowers of our garden faded away? I have wept—I have prayed—I have passed sleepless hours—I have shed bitter, bitter tears over your letter! To you I bring the gushing poesy of my being—the yearnings of the soul that longs to be loved—that pines for love, love, love, beyond all!—that flings itself at your feet, and cries, Love me, Arthur! Your heart beats no quicker at the kneeling appeal of my love!—your proud eye is dimmed by no tear of sympathy!—you accept my soul's treasure as though 'twere dross! not the pearls from the unfathomable deeps of affection! not the diamonds from the caverns of the heart. You treat me like a slave, and bid me bow to my master! Is this the guerdon of a free maiden—is this the price of a life's passion? Ah me! when was it otherwise? when did love meet with aught but disappointment? Could I hope (fond fool!) to be the exception to the lot of my race; and lay my fevered brow on a heart that comprehended my own? Foolish girl that I was! One by one, all the flowers of my young life have faded away; and this, the last, the sweetest, the dearest, the fondly, the madly loved, the wildly cherished—where is it? But no more of this. Heed not my bleeding heart.—Bless you, bless you always, Arthur!"

The express train rattled past the countryside, carrying Arthur Pendennis to Tunbridge with a velocity that seemed to mock the slowness of his soul. As the miles blurred behind him, the young man's mind, usually a fertile garden of schemes, turned barren. He reviewed his life not with pride, but with a grim, self-deprecating humor. "Here," he muttered, staring vacantly at the iron seats, "is the end of hopes and aspirations! Where I yield, I'm miserable; where I obstinately refuse, I'm equally wretched."

He traced the path of his errors with a bitter smile. "I refused an angel," he thought, "because I was stubborn. If I'd taken her, Laura wouldn't have become the angel I needed. I couldn't have loved her properly if someone else had to explain her virtues to me. Instead, I listened to my uncle's wheedling, accepted Blanche on guarantee, and traded a heart for a dowry. And look at the result!" He laughed softly, a dry, hollow sound. "Fortune arrived, left me the wife without the cash, and stripped me of the 'heart' I thought I bought. My poor old uncle is furious—he can't understand my scruples. He's indignant, heart-struck, almost, because I won't take the money. I dissatisfy everyone. I'm a maimed, weak, imperfect wretch, seemingly unequal to any fortune. I make no one happy. What prospect is there for this frivolous girl taking my obscurity? I lack ambition to excite me, self-esteem to console me."

His gaze drifted to the window, then back to the passengers. "If I wrote a book that sold twenty editions," he mused, "I'd be the first to sneer at its reputation. If I succeeded at the Bar, bullying witnesses and twisting evidence, would that fame satisfy me? Is that a life well-spent?"

Beside him sat a stout gentleman holding a newspaper. "Would you care to look at the paper, sir?" the man asked, pointing to a fiery article attacking a black-coated traveler in the compartment.

Arthur nodded politely, took the paper, and resumed his reverie without reading a single line.

"And yet," Arthur pondered, "would you adopt either of those men's creeds, with their consequences?" He sighed deeply. "Ah, me! You must bear your own burden, fashion your own faith, and pray your own prayer. To what mortal ear could I tell all, if I had the tongue? Who can judge another's shortcomings, lost opportunities, or the invisible accidents—a grain of sand, perhaps—that altered the whole current of life? Only One knows the weight of our accounts."

Arthur rested his chin in his hand, his heart falling humbly before an unseen throne of wisdom and pity. "What matters fame or poverty!" he whispered to himself. "If I marry the woman I chose, may I find the strength to be true to her and make her happy? If I have children, pray God teach me to speak the truth. There are no splendours for my marriage, but does my life deserve none? I begin a new phase; may it be better than the last!"

The train hissed to a halt at Tunbridge. Arthur returned the newspaper, stood, and stepped out with a carpet-bag in hand, bracing himself to face his fortune.

A fly dashed him swiftly to Lady Clavering's house. Inside the carriage, Arthur rehearsed a speech, crafting a virtuous, honest, and well-meaning oration that he hoped to deliver to Blanche. *'Blanche,'* he planned to say, *"I cannot guess your meaning from your last letter. I know why I forgo worldly advantages; I feel I would be dishonored by accepting them without cause. If you doubt my affection, let Smirke be called, and let us marry out of hand. With all my heart, I purpose to keep my vow and cherish you through life."*

The carriage door swung open, revealing a domestic who stared at Arthur with surprise. "Her Ladyship's not at home, sir," the man said, ignoring the bag. "Lightfoot is gone."

"I am Mr. Pendennis," Arthur replied firmly. "Where is Lightfoot?"

"Lightfoot is gone, sir. My Lady is out."

Arthur heard a melodic warble issuing from the drawing room. "Take the bag to a dressing room, if you please," he ordered, bypassing the porter and marching straight toward the door. Inside, the air vibrated with music. Miss Amory, the little Siren, sang with all her fascinations at a piano. Beside her sat a gentleman, utterly enraptured by her passionate, melancholy strain. Near the piano, asleep on a sofa, lay Master Clavering, indifferent to the sounds.

As Arthur pushed the door open, the gentleman jumped up with a startled "Hullo!" The music cut off abruptly with a little shriek from the singer. Frank Clavering woke from his nap, and Arthur stepped forward, bowing. "What, Foker! How do you do, Foker?"

Arthur looked at the piano. Resting beside Miss Amory was a purple-leather box, identical to the one Harry had been eyeing in Waterloo Place three days prior. It was open, revealing a magnificent serpentine bracelet with a blazing ruby head and a diamond tail, coiled around a white satin cushion.

"How de-do, Pendennis?" said Foker, his face flushing. Blanche made several shrugging motions, signaling unrest, and covered the bracelet with a handkerchief before advancing to greet Arthur. Her hand trembled violently.

"How is dearest Laura?" she asked.

Foker's face, framed by profound mourning, looked pitiful and puzzled. Frank Clavering, watching the scene with an expression of utmost knowingness, managed only to ejaculate, "Here's a jolly go!" before disappearing with a snigger.

Arthur, restraining himself until that moment, gazed at Foker's burning cheeks and burst into a fit of wild, loud laughter. It terrified Blanche more than any serious display could have.

"And this was the secret, was it?" Arthur gasped, wiping tears from his eyes. "Don't blush and turn away, Foker, my boy. Why, man, you are a pattern of fidelity! Could I stand between Blanche and such constancy? Could I stand between Miss Amory and fifteen thousand a year?"

"It is not that, Mr. Pendennis," Blanche said with great dignity. "It is not money, it is not rank, it is not gold that moves me. It is constancy, it is fidelity, it is a whole trustful loving heart offered to me, that I treasure!" She reached for her handkerchief, hesitated, realizing what lay beneath it, and paused. "I do not disown, I do not disguise—my life is above disguise. To him on whom it is bestowed, my heart must be forever bare. That I once thought I loved you... yes, I thought I was beloved by you, I own! How I clung to that faith! How I strove, I prayed, I longed to believe it! But your conduct—your own words so cold, so heartless, so unkind—have undeceived me. You trifled with the heart of the poor maiden! You flung me back with scorn the troth which I had plighted! I have explained all—to Mr. Foker."

"That you have," said Foker, with devoted conviction.

"What, all?" Arthur asked, casting a meaningful look at Blanche. "Am I in fault? Well, well, Blanche, be it so. I won't appeal against your sentence, and bear it in silence. I came down here looking for very different things, Heaven knows, with a heart most truly and kindly disposed towards you. I hope you may be happy with another, as, on my word, it was my wish to make you so. And I hope my honest old friend here will have a wife worthy of his loyalty, his constancy, and his affection. They deserve the regard of any woman—even Miss Blanche Amory."

Shaking hands, Arthur added, "Don't look askance at me. Has anybody told you that I was a false and heartless character?"

"I think you're a——" Foker began, his wrath rising, but Blanche interceded instantly.

"Henry, not a word! I pray you let there be forgiveness!"

"You're an angel, by Jove, you're an angel!" cried Foker, his eyes shining. At this, Blanche looked up seraphically toward the chandelier.

"In spite of what has passed, for the sake of what has passed, I must always regard Arthur as a brother," the seraph continued. "We have known each other years, trodden the same fields, plucked the same flowers together. Arthur! Henry! I beseech you to take hands and to be friends! Forgive you! I forgive you, Arthur, with my heart I do. Should I not do so for making me so happy?"

"There is only one person of us three whom I pity, Blanche," Arthur said gravely. "And I say to you again, that I hope you will make this good fellow, this honest and loyal creature, happy."

A Chapter of Match-Making

Upon the platform at Tunbridge, Pen swelled with indignation and impatience until the evening express thundered into London, a full half-hour later—or six hours, if one counted by the pounding of his heart. Yet even this vast interval vanished like mist before the sun; the train roared forward, the golden lights of London pierced the gloom, and a gentleman who had forgotten his carpet-bag leaped into a cab, shouting, "Drive as hard as you can go to Jermyn Street!" The cabman, though a Hansom-man, bowed gratefully to the tip and clattered away. Upstairs, Pen burst into Lady Rockminster's apartment.

Laura sat alone in the drawing-room, reading by a lamp with a pallor that seemed to glow with its own internal light. When the door swung open, she raised her eyes. *May we follow?* The great moments of life are often decided in seconds. A glance, a touch of the hand, a whisper of the lips—it all hangs in the balance.

Lady Rockminster, having enjoyed her post-prandial siesta, rose and glided into the sitting-room. As she crossed the threshold, the narrator invites you to follow suit. "Upon my word, young people!" was her first exclamation, and her attendant, peeking over her shoulder, goggled in wonder. Indeed, the young pair were in an attitude fit for a painting; Pen stood in a posture that every maiden of the age had either heard described, witnessed, dreamed of, or felt entitled to behold.

Directly inside, Pen marched straight to Laura, whose pale face held no time for the question, *"What, back so soon?"* Seizing her outstretched, trembling hand as she rose from her chair, he dropped to his knees. "I have seen her!" he cried breathlessly. "She has engaged herself to Harry Foker—and—and Now, Laura?"

The hand pressed gently—the eyes flashed a silent answer—the lips quivered without sound. Pen sank his head into the girl's lap, sobbing, "Come and bless us, dear mother," while arms as tender as Helen's once more enveloped him.

Just then, Lady Rockminster entered, declaring, "Upon my word, young people! Beck! leave the room. What do you want poking your nose in here?"

Pen sprang upright, still clutching Laura's hand, his face alight with triumph. "She is consoling me for my misfortune, ma'am," he declared.

"What do you mean by kissing her hand? I don't know what you will be next doing."

Pen kissed her Ladyship's fingers. "I have been to Tunbridge," he explained, adopting a tragic air, "and seen Miss Amory. I found, upon my return, that a villain has transplanted me in her affections."

"Is that all? Is that what you were whimpering on your knees about?" the old lady demanded, her temper flaring. "You might have kept the news till tomorrow."

"Yes—another has superseded me," Pen continued. "But why call him a villain? He is brave, constant, young, wealthy, and beautiful."

"What nonsense are you spouting, sir?" cried the old lady. "What has happened?"

"Miss Amory has jilted me and accepted Henry Foker, Esquire," Pen recited. "I found her warbling ditties to him as he lay at her feet. Presents had been accepted, vows exchanged, within these ten days. Harry was merely old Mrs. Planter's rheumatism, a condition that kept dearest Laura out of the house. He is the most constant and generous of men. He promised the living of Logwood to Lady Ann's husband and gifted her a splendid present upon her marriage; and the moment he realized he was free, he rushed to fling himself at Blanche's feet."

"And so, since you cannot have Blanche, you settle for Laura, is that it, sir?" the old lady queried sharply.

"He acted nobly," Laura interjected softly.

"I acted as she bade me," Pen replied. "Never mind how, Lady Rockminster; to the best of my knowledge and power. And if you imply that I am unworthy of Laura, I know it, and I pray Heaven to better me. But if the love and company of the best and purest creature in the world can mend me, at least I shall have those gifts to aid me."

"Hm, hm," murmured the old lady, regarding the pair with a somewhat appeased expression. "It is all very well; but I should have preferred Bluebeard."

Seeking to divert the conversation from themes that grew painful, Pen recalled his interview with Huxter in the morning and Fanny Bolton's affairs, which he had momentarily neglected amidst his own emotional storm. He recounted how Huxter had elevated Fanny to the rank of wife and the terrors concerning the arrival of his father. He described the scene with considerable humor, dwelling especially on Fanny's coquetry and her irrepressible desire to captivate mankind. His underlying message was clear: *"See, Laura, I was not so guilty in that little affair; it was the girl who made love to me, and I who resisted. Since I am no longer present, the little siren practices her arts upon others. Let that transaction be forgotten, if you please; or visit me with a very gentle punishment for my error."*

Laura grasped his meaning beneath the fervor of his explanation. "If you did any wrong, you repented, dear Pen," she said. "And you know," she added, with meaningful eyes and blushing cheeks, "that I have no right to reproach you."

"Hm!" grunted the old lady. "I should have preferred Bluebeard."

"The past is broken away. The morrow is before us. I will do my best to make your morrow happy, dear Laura," Pen said. His heart humbled itself before the prospect of his happiness, standing awestruck in contemplation of her sweetness and purity. He loved his wife better now that she had admitted her fleeting feeling for Warrington and laid bare her generous heart. And she—perhaps thinking, *"How strange it is that I ever should have cared for another! I am vexed almost to think I care for him so little, am so little sorry that he is gone. Oh, in these past two months how I have learned to love Arthur! I care about nothing but Arthur; he is never absent from me. And to think that he is to be mine, mine! That I am to marry him, and not serve as I expected only this morning! For I would have knelt to Blanche to beg permission to live with him. And now—Oh, it is too much. Oh, mother! Mother, that you were here!"* Indeed, she felt as if Helen were there, invisibly beside her. A halo of happiness radiated from her.

She moved with a different step, blooming with a new beauty. Arthur observed the change, and the shrewd-eyed old Lady Rockminster noted it instantly.

"What a sly demure little wretch you have been," she whispered to Laura—"and how you have kept your secret!"

"How are we to help the young couple?" Laura asked. Generous lovers, naturally, feel an interest in all young couples.

"We must go and see them," said Pen.

"Of course we must," agreed Laura. "I intend to be very fond of Fanny. Let us go this instant. Lady Rockminster, may I have the carriage?"

"Go now! Why, you stupid creature, it is eleven o'clock at night. Mr. and Mrs. Huxter have their nightcaps on, I dare say. And it is time for you to go. Good night, Mr. Pendennis."

Arthur and Laura pleaded for ten more minutes.

"We will go to-morrow morning, then. I will fetch you with Martha."

"An earl's coronet," Pen remarked, clearly pleased with himself, "will have a great effect in Lamb Court and Smithfield. Stay—Lady Rockminster, will you join us in a little conspiracy?"

"How do you mean conspiracy, young man?"

"Will you please to feign illness to-morrow? And when old Mr. Huxter arrives, will you let me summon him in? If he is put into a good humour by the notion of attending a baronet in the country, what influence won't a countess have on him? When he is softened, when he is ripe, we will reveal the secret, bring in the young people, extort the paternal benediction, and finish the comedy."

"A parcel of stuff," said the old lady. "Take your hat, sir. Come away, miss. There—my head is turned another way. Good night, young people." And who knows but the old lady thought of her own early days as she departed on Laura's arm, nodding and humming to herself?

With the early morning came Laura and Martha, according to appointment, and the desired sensation, we trust, was effected in Lamb Court. From there, the three proceeded to wait upon Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Huxter at their residence in Charterhouse Lane.

The two ladies exchanged glances of great interest, not a little emotion stirring within Fanny. She had not seen her "guardian"—as she delighted to call Pen due to his bequest—since the event uniting her to Mr. Huxter.

"Samuel told me how kind you had been," she said. "

Time, that relentless editor, has moved our characters forward by a month. In this interim, a remarkable convergence of destiny has drawn the scattered figures of our story back to the little country town where they first made their entrance. Among these returning souls is Frederic Lightfoot, formerly the humble *maitre d'hôtel* to Sir Francis Clavering, now transformed into a proprietor of some standing. He has issued a bold manifesto to the nobility and gentry of the shire, announcing his takeover of "the Clavering Arms."

"This ancient and well-established house," proclaims Mr. Lightfoot, "has been repaired and decorated in a style of the greatest comfort. Gentlemen hunting with the Dumplingbeare hounds will find excellent stabling. A commodious billiard-room has been attached, and the cellars stocked with the choicest wines, selected without regard to expense by C.L. Commercial gentlemen will find this a most comfortable resort, and the scale of charges has been regulated to suit the economical spirit of the times."

Indeed, the old inn breathes a new vitality. The gateway has been splashed with fresh paint, the coffee-room windows glow with Christmas holly, and the magistrates have convened their petty sessions within the card-room of the old Assembly Hall. The farmers' ordinary draws increased crowds, enchanted by Mrs. Lightfoot's cuisine, particularly her Indian curries and Mulligatawny soup, which have earned high praise from Major Stokes, Captain Glanders, and other local gentry. These culinary triumphs were savored not only in private but at the grand dinner celebrating the incorporation of the Clavering Institute, chaired by Sir Francis Clavering himself, with the Reverend Dr. Portman as supporter and the enterprising Barker, Esq., filling the vice-chair. Barker, the head of the local ribbon factory and director of the impending Great Western Railway branch, represents the industrial future of this bustling little town.

Meanwhile, the local press—the *Chatteris Champion*, *Clavering Agriculturist*, and *Baymouth Fisherman*—offers a tantalizing glimpse into the social whirlwind awaiting the residents. They report that a significant shift in the romantic landscape of our talented townsman, Arthur Pendennis, Esq., has seen him abandon his candidacy for the borough. Rumours whisper, with the usual fervour, that Sir Francis Clavering, whose failing health may necessitate his resignation from Parliament, intends to vacate his seat in favour of a young gentleman of colossal fortune and high aristocratic lineage. This suitor is poised to wed an accomplished lady closely tied to the Clavering Park estate. Lady Clavering and Miss Amory have already arrived for the Christmas holidays, promising festivities of a peculiarly interesting nature at the commencement of the new year.

For the ingenious reader, these announcements serve as a map to the events transpiring behind the scenes. While Lady Rockminster grumbled initially at Laura's preference for Pendennis over Bluebeard, those privy to the latter's secrets understand that Laura could make no other choice. The kind old lady, acting as Miss Bell's guardian, was far from displeased at the prospect of fulfilling the ultimate purpose of young ladies: marriage. She confided in her maid, Mrs. Beck, who, being perpetually in the loop thanks to Martha of Fairoaks, was immensely delighted. "Mr. Pendennis's income is so substantial," Mrs. Beck noted, calculating the railway dividends, "and Miss Bell has her dowry. They will manage very well. I shall speak to my nephew, George Pynsent, Chief Secretary of the Tape and Sealing Wax Office, and ensure Mr. Pendennis receives a suitable position."

Thus, at one o'clock, the Dowager Lady Rockminster appeared at Major Pendennis's residence. The Major, a man of honour, was delighted to receive such a noble visitor, though he had been braced for the news that Pen's engagement to Miss Amory had been severed. He recalled his uncle for the first time that day, meeting his new servant, Mr. Frosch, in the hall. Before retreating to the hotel coffee-room to pen a half-dozen lines to his guardian, he sought the Major's health. His letter to the Major read: *"Dear uncle, if there has been any question between us, it is over now. I went to Tunbridge Wells yesterday and found that somebody else had carried off the prize... Miss A., without any compunction, has bestowed herself upon Harry Foker, with his fifteen thousand a year."*

Upon receiving this dispatch, the Major smiled with courtly benevolence. "It was a very great kindness of your Ladyship to bring me the news," he declared. "How well you look! How very good you are!" He admitted that, despite his initial wish for the union, he did not regret its failure, noting the financial improvement in Pen's fortunes. "We must console him," Lady Rockminster urged gently. "We must get him a wife."

The truth finally dawned on the Major: Lady Rockminster had arrived as an ambassador for Laura. The negotiations proved tolerably easy. There could be no valid objection to Pen marrying according to his and his mother's wishes. Lady Rockminster bolstered the agreement with intimations of profound weight, though we shall not detail them lest we offend the living memory of the Countess. Overcome by the old lady's determined graciousness and her evident fondness for Laura, the Major relented. Indeed, nothing was more bland and kind than her demeanour, save for one fleeting moment when she spoke of Pen "throwing himself away." At that instant, she revealed, with a humility acknowledged by Pen's friends, that Laura was a thousand times too good for him—a paragon of virtue fit to be the wife of a king.

When the Major, instantly smitten by the Countess's admiration for Miss Bell, received Laura, who appeared blushing and radiant, he extended a shaky hand to both the couple with unaffected cordiality. The scene was one of happy blushes and beams of love. The storyteller, turning from this tender tableau, hopes that one day the eyes of his young audience may shine with similar light.

With Pen retreated in friendship and the lovely Blanche having bestowed her affections upon Harry Foker, a blaze of happiness erupted in Lady Clavering's household. The good Begum and Blanche were on the most delightful terms. The ardent Foker, pressing onwards the happy day, was eager to abridge the mourning period that had placed him in possession of Blanche's charms. Lady Clavering, buoyed by this joy, descended from Tunbridge, and a reconciliation ensued between her and Sir Francis. Sir Francis returned to London, sat at the head of his own table, and resumed his habits at the billiard-rooms and gambling-houses, appearing flushed with money once more.

One day, as Major Pendennis and Arthur dined at Grosvenor Place, they encountered an old acquaintance serving as major-domo. The gentleman in black, offering sweet or dry champagne with perfect gravity, was none other than Mr. James Morgan. Present also was the Chevalier Strong, in high spirits, recounting his amusements abroad. Under his breath, Strong murmured to Arthur, "It was my Lady who invited me. That fellow Morgan looked as black as thunder when I came in. He is about no good here. I will go away first, and wait for you and Major Pendennis at Hyde Park Gate."

As Morgan assisted the departing Major with his great-coat, he muttered something about accepting a temporary engagement with the Clavering family. Arthur overheard the sentence and caught the look of hatred accompanying it. Excusing himself to retrieve a forgotten handkerchief, he returned to the drawing-room. Blanche was still there, lingering about her siren. Pen gave her a look full of meaning, and the siren, understanding the silent code, beckoned him out.

"What is it?" she asked, shutting the door. "Have you told Harry? Do you know that villain Morgan knows all?"

"I know it," Blanche replied. "Have you told Harry?"

"No, no," she whispered, "you won't betray me?"

"Morgan will," Pen said sternly.

"No, he won't," Blanche insisted. "I have promised him—*n'importe*. Wait until after our marriage..." Suddenly, the smiles vanished. "Oh, how wretched I am," she cried.

Arthur implored her, "Tell him now. It is no fault of yours. He will pardon you anything."

"And give her this—" Blanche handed him a note, "—*Il est la*—with my love. Please. If she will be at Madame Crinoline's at half-past three, and if Lady Rockminster can spare her, I should so like to drive with her in the park." She departed, singing and kissing her little hand, as Morgan the velvet-footed ascended the carpeted stair.

Pen heard Blanche's piano break into brilliant music as he joined his uncle. "What was to be done?" Arthur asked.

"What is to be done, begad?" the old gentleman sighed. "Leave it alone. Be thankful that we are out of the business." Yet Pen hoped to heaven she would tell Harry.

"Miss Amory is a dev'lish wide-awake girl," the Major observed. "She must play her own cards."
